This is a digital copy of a book that was preserved for generations on library shelves before it was carefully scanned by Google as part of a project to make the world's books discoverable online.

It has survived long enough for the copyright to expire and the book to enter the public domain <note>A legal status where a work is no longer protected by intellectual property laws and is free for anyone to use.</note>. A public domain book is one that was never subject to copyright or whose legal copyright term has expired. Whether a book is in the public domain may vary country to country. Public domain books are our gateways to the past, representing a wealth of history, culture and knowledge that's often difficult to discover.

Marks, notations and other marginalia <note>original: "marginalia"; handwritten notes or sketches made in the margins of a book</note> present in the original volume will appear in this file—a reminder of this book's long journey from the publisher to a library and finally to you.

## Usage guidelines

Google is proud to partner with libraries to digitize public domain materials and make them widely accessible. Public domain books belong to the public and we are merely their custodians. Nevertheless, this work is expensive, so in order to keep providing this resource, we have taken steps to prevent abuse by commercial parties, including placing technical restrictions on automated querying <term>automated querying: the use of software scripts or "bots" to automatically fetch data from a website</term>.

We also ask that you:

+ *Make non-commercial use of the files* We designed Google Book Search for use by individuals, and we request that you use these files for personal, non-commercial purposes.

+ *Refrain from automated querying* Do not send automated queries of any sort to Google's system: If you are conducting research on machine translation, optical character recognition <note>OCR; the electronic conversion of images of typed, handwritten or printed text into machine-encoded text</note> or other areas where access to a large amount of text is helpful, please contact us. We encourage the use of public domain materials for these purposes and may be able to help.

+ *Maintain attribution* The Google "watermark" you see on each file is essential for informing people about this project and helping them find additional materials through Google Book Search. Please do not remove it.

+ *Keep it legal* Whatever your use, remember that you are responsible for ensuring that what you are doing is legal. Do not assume that just because we believe a book is in the public domain for users in the United States, that the work is also in the public domain for users in other countries. Whether a book is still in copyright varies from country to country, and we can't offer guidance on whether any specific use of any specific book is allowed. Please do not assume that a book's appearance in Google Book Search means it can be used in any manner anywhere in the world. Copyright infringement liability can be quite severe.

--- Page 12 ---
<meta>This page transitions from the general outline of the work provided in the previous pages to the actual text of Aristotle's Rhetoric, beginning with the foundational definitions of the discipline in Book I, Chapter 1.</meta>

# ARISTOTLE'S
# RHETORIC.
## BOOK I.

### CHAPTER I.

**R**hetoric <note>original: "Hē rhētorikē"</note> is the counterpart <note>original: "antistrophos," suggesting a mirror image or the second half of a poetic couplet</note> to <term>dialectic</term>; <note>Dialectic is the art of logical discussion and investigation, usually through question-and-answer, aimed at discovering truth.</note> for both are concerned with the kind of things that are, in a sense, common to all people to understand and belong to no specialized science. Therefore, all people, in a way, share in both; for everyone, to a certain extent, attempts both to examine and to uphold an argument, as well as to defend themselves and to accuse others. 

Now, among the general public, some do these things at random, while others do them through a habit formed by a practiced disposition. Since both ways are possible, it is clear that one could also provide a systematic path <note>original: "hodopoiein," literally "to make a way or road"</note> for them. For it is possible to investigate the reason why some succeed through habit and others by chance, and everyone would already agree that such an investigation is the function of an <term>art</term>. <note>The Greek "technē" refers to a systematic craft or a branch of knowledge with its own rules, rather than just "art" in the aesthetic sense.</note> 

At present, however, those who have compiled "Arts of Speech" <note>Aristotle is referring to previous authors of rhetorical manuals</note> have provided only a small portion of the subject; for only the <term>modes of persuasion</term> <note>original: "pisteis"; these are the means by which a speaker wins over an audience</note> are inherent to the art, while everything else is merely an addition. These authors say nothing about <term>enthymemes</term>, <note>A rhetorical syllogism or argument based on logic and shared values; Aristotle calls it the "body" of proof because it is the core logical structure.</note> which are the very "body" of persuasion, but they occupy themselves mostly with matters outside the subject. For slander, pity, anger, and such emotions of the soul...

<summary>This page introduces Rhetoric as the logical partner to Dialectic, arguing that persuasion can be studied as a systematic "art" rather than left to chance. Aristotle criticizes previous teachers for focusing on emotional appeals rather than the logical "body" of an argument, the enthymeme.</summary>
<keywords>Rhetoric, Dialectic, Art (Techne), Persuasion (Pistis), Enthymeme, Logic vs. Emotion</keywords>

--- Page 13 ---
<meta>This page continues Aristotle's critique of contemporary rhetorical manuals. In the previous page, he argued that previous authors focused on irrelevant matters—specifically emotional manipulation—rather than the "body of proof" (the enthymeme). This page develops the argument that laws should be as specific as possible to limit the influence of a judge's personal emotions on a case.</meta>

->ARISTOTLE<-

...of the soul, is not about the facts of the matter, but is directed toward the judge. So, if this practice were applied to all trials—as it currently is in some cities, and especially in those that are well-governed—these authors would have nothing to say. For everyone believes that the laws should be phrased in this way, and some cities actually use such rules and forbid speaking "outside the matter," just as is done in the <term>Areopagus</term> <note>The Areopagus was the high court of Athens, renowned for its strictness and for forbidding speakers from using emotional appeals to influence the judges.</note>; they are right to hold this view. For one must not warp the judge by provoking them to anger, or envy, or pity; that would be like someone making a <term>ruler</term> <note>original: "kanōn," a carpenter's straight-edge or measuring tool</note> crooked before using it to measure. Furthermore, it is clear that the only task of the litigant is to show that the fact is or is not so, or has or has not happened. But whether it is great or small, or just or unjust—insofar as the lawgiver has not defined these things—the judge himself must surely decide, and not learn it from those who are disputing the case.

It is most appropriate, therefore, for well-established laws to define everything themselves as much as possible, and to leave as little as possible to those who judge. First, because it is easier to find one or a few people of good sense who are capable of legislating and judging than to find many. <margin><unclear>hard</unclear></margin> Secondly, because legislation is produced by those who have considered the matter over a long period of time, whereas judgments are made on the spur of the moment <note>original: "ex hypoguiou," meaning "at hand" or "on the spot"</note>; thus it is difficult for those judging to render what is just and beneficial correctly. But the most important reason of all is that the lawgiver's judgment is not about a specific case, [nor about present matters,] but is about what is to come and what is universal. The <term>assemblyman</term> <note>original: "ekklēsiastēs," a member of the citizen assembly who voted on policy.</note> and the judge, however, decide on matters that are already present and specific. For them, feelings of love and hatred, and their own personal interest, are already involved...

<summary>Aristotle argues that lawgivers should define as much as possible in advance because legislation is a product of long-term reflection on universal truths, whereas judges and assemblies are easily clouded by immediate emotions and personal interests.</summary>

<keywords>Aristotle, Rhetoric, Lawgiver, Judge, Areopagus, Justice, Legislation, Emotional Appeals, Universal vs Particular</keywords>

--- Page 14 ---
<meta>This page continues Aristotle's argument from page 2, where he explained that laws should be comprehensive to prevent judges from being influenced by personal feelings. He specifically argued that it is easier to find a few wise lawgivers than many impartial judges, and that legislation is made in advance with a cool head, whereas trials happen in the heat of the moment.</meta>

### RHETORICA. LIB. I. CAP. I.

A 2

...has often been compromised; the result is that they are no longer able to see the truth sufficiently, but instead, their own sense of pleasure or pain obscures their judgment. Regarding other matters, then—as we have been saying—the law should make the judge the master of as little as possible. 

However, as to whether something has happened or has not happened, or whether it will or will not be, or whether it is or is not—these matters must necessarily be left to the discretion of the judges. It is simply not possible for a lawgiver to foresee every specific instance.

If this is true, it is evident that those who define other matters are theorizing about things outside the subject. This includes those who dictate what the <term>introduction</term> <note>original: "prooimion"</note> or the <term>narrative</term> <note>original: "diēgēsis," the part of a speech where the facts of the case are laid out</note> should contain, as well as each of the other parts of a speech. In these rules, they occupy themselves with nothing else except how to put the judge into a certain frame of mind. They offer no instruction regarding <term>artistic proofs</term> <note>original: "entechnoi pisteis"; these are proofs that the speaker constructs through logic, character, or emotion, rather than external evidence like contracts or DNA.</note>. Yet, it is through these proofs that one becomes a <term>master of the rhetorical argument</term> <note>original: "enthymēmatikos," referring to the "enthymeme," which Aristotle considers the most important tool of persuasion—a sort of logical shortcut used in public speaking.</note>.

For this reason—even though the method is the same for <term>public speaking</term> <note>original: "dēmēgorika," specifically political or deliberative oratory</note> and <term>courtroom speaking</term> <note>original: "dikanika," or forensic oratory</note>, and even though the study of public speaking is a nobler and more statesmanlike pursuit than the study of private legal contracts—these authors say nothing about the former. Instead, they all attempt to systematize the art of litigation. 

This is because in public speaking, it is less useful to talk about things "outside the matter." Public speaking is also less prone to the "dirty tricks" of courtroom oratory, as it deals with more general concerns. In the political assembly, the judge (the citizen) is deciding on matters that affect his own interests; therefore, nothing more is required than to prove that things are as the advisor says they are. 

But in the courtroom, this is not enough. In legal cases, it is often useful to win over the listener. This is because the judgment concerns the affairs of others. Since the judges are looking out for their own ease and listening for their own pleasure, they often give in to the litigants rather than actually judging the case. This is why in many places, as I mentioned before, the law for-

<summary>Aristotle argues that rhetoricians wrongly focus on emotional manipulation in courtrooms rather than logical "artistic" proofs. He notes that political oratory is more honest because the audience has a personal stake in the truth, whereas jurors in courtrooms are often swayed by speakers because they are deciding someone else's fate.</summary>

<keywords>Aristotle, Rhetoric, legal vs political oratory, artistic proofs, enthymeme, judicial bias, introduction and narrative</keywords>

--- Page 15 ---
<meta>This page continues Aristotle’s critique of contemporary rhetoric teachers. On the previous page, he argued that most writers focus on emotional manipulation (outside the subject) rather than the logical core of persuasion. He now transitions into explaining why a logical foundation—specifically the "enthymeme"—is essential for a true art of rhetoric.</meta>

# ARISTOTLE

### RHETORICA. BOOK I. CHAPTER I.

->A 2<-

...forbids speaking **outside the subject**. In those courts, the judges themselves guard this rule strictly enough. Since it is clear that the systematic method of the art concerns **proofs** <term>proofs</term> <note>original: "pisteis," refers here to the means of persuasion that a speaker constructs.</note>, and proof is a kind of **demonstration** <note>original: "apodeixis"; Aristotle believes we are most convinced when we feel a point has been proven logically.</note> (for we believe most fully when we consider something to have been demonstrated), it follows that a rhetorical demonstration is an **enthymeme** <term>enthymeme</term> <note>original: "enthymēma." This is Aristotle’s "rhetorical syllogism"—an argument that starts from premises the audience already accepts.</note>. This is, to put it simply, the most sovereign of proofs. 

The enthymeme is a type of **syllogism** <term>syllogism</term> <note>original: "syllogismos," a logical argument where a conclusion is inferred from two premises (e.g., All men are mortal; Socrates is a man; therefore Socrates is mortal).</note>. It belongs to the study of **Dialectic** <term>dialectic</term> <note>The art of logical discussion and testing of arguments.</note>—either as a whole or as one of its parts—to examine every kind of syllogism. It is clear, then, that the person most capable of examining how and from what a syllogism is formed would also be the most skilled in using enthymemes, provided they also understand the subjects enthymemes deal with and how they differ from strictly logical syllogisms. 

For the capacity to see the truth and the capacity to see what resembles the truth are the same. At the same time, human beings are naturally gifted for finding the truth, and for the most part, they succeed in reaching it. Therefore, a person who can aim at **accepted opinions** <note>original: "endoxa," the common beliefs or reputable views held by the majority or the wise.</note> is also well-equipped to aim at the truth.

It is clear, then, that other authors of rhetorical manuals theorize about matters **outside the subject**, and it is obvious why they lean more toward **courtroom pleading** <note>original: "dikologein," or forensic oratory focused on accusation and defense.</note>. 

Yet, rhetoric is useful because the true and the just are by nature stronger than their opposites. Consequently, if judgments are not made as they should be, truth and justice must necessarily be defeated by their own advocates; this is a state of affairs worthy of blame. Furthermore, when dealing with certain audiences, even if we possessed the most exact **scientific knowledge** <term>scientific knowledge</term> <note>original: "epistēmē"</note>, it would not be easy to use that knowledge to persuade them. This is because a speech based on science is a form of **instruction** <note>original: "didaskalia"</note>, and in some situations, teaching is impossible. Instead, we must construct our proofs and our arguments through **common notions** <note>original: "koina," ideas or beliefs shared by the general public.</note>, just as...

<summary>Aristotle defines the "enthymeme" as the core of rhetorical persuasion and argues that rhetoric is a necessary tool for ensuring truth and justice prevail in public life, especially when formal scientific instruction is not possible.</summary>

<keywords>enthymeme, syllogism, dialectic, proof, truth, justice, accepted opinions, instruction, persuasion</keywords>

--- Page 16 ---
<meta>This page continues Aristotle’s argument that rhetoric is a tool of discovery rather than just a performance. He has just finished explaining that the "enthymeme"—a form of rhetorical argument based on logic—is the core of the art. He now moves to justify why a speaker must be able to argue both sides of an issue and why rhetoric is a necessary skill for a virtuous citizen.</meta>

# ARISTOTLE

### RHETORIC. BOOK I. CHAPTER I.

...and as we stated in the *Topics* <note>Aristotle’s work on dialectical reasoning and the "places" (topoi) from which arguments are drawn.</note> regarding how to converse with the multitude. Furthermore, one must be able to persuade on **opposite sides** of an issue, just as in syllogisms <term>syllogisms</term> <note>original: "syllogismois." Logical arguments where a conclusion is inferred from premises.</note>. This is not so that we may actually practice both—for we must not persuade people to do what is base—but so that we are not unaware of how the case stands, and so that if another uses arguments unjustly, we may be able to **refute** them.

Of all the other arts, none draws opposite conclusions; only **Dialectic** and **Rhetoric** do this, for both are equally concerned with opposites. However, the underlying subjects are not equal; rather, things that are true and better are by nature always easier to prove and more persuasive, to put it simply. 

Moreover, it is absurd if it is considered shameful for a person to be unable to defend themselves with their body, but not shameful to be unable to defend themselves with **speech** <term>logos</term> <note>original: "logō." In Greek, "logos" means both the words spoken and the reason behind them.</note>; yet the use of speech is more characteristic of a human being than the use of the body. If it is argued that one who uses such power of speech unjustly might do great harm, this is a charge that can be made against all good things (except for virtue), and especially against the most useful things, such as strength, health, wealth, and military leadership. For with these, a person might do the greatest good if they use them justly, or the greatest harm if they use them unjustly.

It is clear, then, that Rhetoric does not belong to any single, defined class of subjects, but is like Dialectic; and it is clear that it is useful. It is also evident that its function is **not to persuade**, but to identify the available means of persuasion <note>Aristotle argues that rhetoric is an investigative skill, not just a result-oriented trick.</note> regarding any given subject. This is the same as in all other arts. For it is not the function of medicine to make a person healthy, but to bring them as far toward health as possible; for even those who are unable to attain health can still be treated well. Furthermore, it is clear that it belongs to the same art to see both the persuasive and the...

<summary>Aristotle explains that rhetoric is a neutral tool that should be used to argue both sides of an issue to better find the truth and refute errors. He defends rhetoric as a uniquely human faculty of "logos" (reasoned speech) and defines its goal as finding the available means of persuasion rather than simply winning an argument.</summary>

<keywords>Aristotle, Rhetoric, Dialectic, Logos, Persuasion, Medicine analogy, Syllogism, Ethics of Speech</keywords>

--- Page 17 ---
<meta>This page concludes Aristotle's introductory argument in Chapter I regarding the moral purpose of rhetoric and begins Chapter II, where he provides his formal definition of the art and categorizes the different types of evidence or 'proofs' used in persuasion.</meta>

# ARISTOTLE

...to see the apparent persuasive argument, just as in **Dialectic** one sees both the valid syllogism and the apparent syllogism. For being a "sophist" <note>Aristotle uses "sophist" to describe someone who uses deceptive or fallacious reasoning for personal gain or to appear wise.</note> is not a matter of one's faculty <note>original: "dynamei"</note>, but of one's moral purpose <note>original: "proairesei"</note>. However, in this field [rhetoric], a person is called a "rhetorician" based either on their knowledge <note>original: "epistēmēn"</note> or on their purpose; whereas in Dialectic, a person is a "sophist" because of their purpose, while the "dialectician" is defined not by purpose, but by their faculty.

Regarding the **method** itself, let us now attempt to discuss how and from what sources we will be able to achieve our objectives. Therefore, let us define rhetoric once more from the beginning and then discuss the remainder.

## CHAPTER II.

Let **Rhetoric** be defined as the faculty <term>dynamis</term> <note>original: "dynamis." This refers to a capacity or a power to act, rather than a rigid set of rules.</note> of observing, in any given case, the available means of persuasion. This is the function of no other art; for every other art is instructive and persuasive about its own specific subject matter. For example, medicine deals with health and disease; geometry with the properties of magnitudes; arithmetic with numbers; and likewise with all other arts and sciences.

Rhetoric, however, seems to be able to observe the persuasive element in almost any given subject; therefore, we say that its technical domain is not restricted to any specific class of things.

Of the modes of persuasion <term>pisteis</term> <note>original: "pisteōn." These are the "proofs" or means by which a speaker creates conviction in an audience.</note>, some are **non-artistic** and others are **artistic**. By **non-artistic** <note>original: "atechna"</note> I mean those that are not provided by our own efforts but existed beforehand—such as witnesses, evidence given under torture, written contracts, and the like. By **artistic** <note>original: "entechna"</note> I mean those that can be constructed by us through the system of rhetoric and our own intellectual effort. Consequently, one must simply make use of the former, but **discover** the latter.

Of the proofs provided through speech <term>logos</term> <note>original: "logou." Here referring to the spoken argument itself.</note>, there are three kinds: for some reside in the...

<summary>Aristotle defines rhetoric as the unique faculty of discovering the available means of persuasion in any subject. He further distinguishes between "non-artistic" proofs, like physical evidence or witnesses, and "artistic" proofs, which the speaker must invent through logic and method.</summary>

### RHETORIC. BOOK I. CHAPTER II. 7

...the **character** of the speaker, others in disposing the listener in a certain way, and others in the speech itself, by demonstrating or appearing to demonstrate a point. 

Persuasion is achieved through **character** <note>original: "ēthous," the root of our word "ethics."</note> when the speech is delivered in such a way as to make the speaker worthy of belief. For we trust fair-minded people <note>original: "epieikesi," referring to those who are decent, honest, or reasonable.</note> more and more readily, generally on all subjects, but in matters where there is no exact certainty—only room for doubt—our trust is absolute. 

This effect, however, must come about through the speech itself, and not because of any pre-existing opinion of what kind of person the speaker is. For we should not follow those technical writers of manuals who claim that the decency <note>original: "epieikeian"</note> of the speaker contributes nothing to the power of persuasion. On the contrary, we might almost say that character is the most authoritative form of proof <term>pistis</term> <note>original: "pistis." In this context, it refers to a "means of persuasion" or a "ground of belief."</note> available.

Persuasion is achieved through the **listeners** when they are moved to an emotion <term>pathos</term> by the speech. For we do not render our judgments in the same way when we are grieving as when we are rejoicing, or when we are loving as when we are hating. This—the manipulation of feelings—is the only area that current writers of rhetorical manuals actually try to address. We will clarify these matters individually when we discuss the emotions.

Finally, people are persuaded through the **arguments** <term>logos</term> <note>original: "logōn," meaning the words or the reasoning of the speech.</note> when we show the truth, or the apparent truth, based on the persuasive facts of each specific case.

Since persuasion is achieved through these three means, it is clear that mastering them belongs to someone who is capable of logical reasoning <term>syllogism</term>, someone who can contemplate characters and virtues, and thirdly, someone who understands the emotions—what each emotion is, what its nature is, and from what sources and in what manner it arises. 

Consequently, it happens that **Rhetoric** is like an offshoot <note>original: "paraphues," literally something that grows alongside or out of a main stem.</note> of **Dialectic** <note>the art of logical discussion</note> and of the study of character, which is rightly called **Politics** <note>Aristotle views the study of human character as a branch of political science or "the affairs of the city."</note>. For this reason, Rhetoric often slips into the guise of political science, and those who lay claim...

<summary>Aristotle defines the three pillars of persuasion: the speaker's credibility (ethos), the audience's emotional state (pathos), and the logical argument (logos). He argues that rhetoric is a hybrid discipline, growing out of both logic and political ethics.</summary>

<meta>This page continues the discussion on the relationship between rhetoric and dialectic, identifying the two primary modes of rhetorical argument: the example (rhetorical induction) and the enthymeme (rhetorical syllogism). It builds on the previous page's claim that persuasion is achieved through character, emotion, and argument.</meta>

->### ARISTOTLE<-

...taking hold of this art, some do so through a lack of education, others through boastfulness, and others for various human reasons. 

For rhetoric is a certain part of **dialectic** <term>dialektikē</term> <note>original: "dialektikē." Dialectic is the art of logical discussion and debate used to investigate the truth of a theory or opinion through questions and answers.</note> and resembles it, as we said at the beginning. For neither of them is a science regarding any definite subject or how it is constituted, but they are both instead faculties for providing arguments. Regarding their power and how they relate to one another, enough has perhaps been said. 

Concerning the methods of demonstrating a point—or appearing to demonstrate it—just as in **dialectic** <term>dialektikē</term> one method is **induction** <term>epagōgē</term> <note>original: "epagōgē." Induction is the process of reasoning from specific instances to reach a general rule.</note> and another is the **syllogism** <term>syllogismos</term> <note>original: "syllogismos." A formal logical deduction where a conclusion is drawn from two or more premises (e.g., "All men are mortal; Socrates is a man; therefore Socrates is mortal").</note>, [and a third is the apparent syllogism,] so it is here in rhetoric. 

For the **example** <term>paradeigma</term> is a form of induction, and the **enthymeme** <term>enthymēma</term> is a form of syllogism. I call an **enthymeme** a rhetorical syllogism, and an **example** a rhetorical induction. Everyone who produces a "proof" <note>original: "pisteis." Here, it refers to the means of persuasion or grounds for belief.</note> does so by demonstrating a point, either by citing examples or by using enthymemes; there is no other way. Therefore, if it is absolutely necessary to demonstrate any point whatever by either deducing or inducing (which is clear to us from our work in the *Analytics* <note>Aristotle's *Prior* and *Posterior Analytics*, his foundational books on formal logic.</note>), it follows that each of these rhetorical methods must be identical to one of the logical ones. 

The difference between an **example** and an **enthymeme** is clear from the *Topics* <note>Aristotle’s treatise on "dialectic" or everyday reasoning.</note>. For there, it was explained previously that showing something to be true based on many similar cases is called induction; in the context of rhetoric, however, this is called an **example**. But when, certain things being true, something else happens to follow from them because they are true—either universally or for the most part—that is called a syllogism there, but here it is called an **enthymeme**. 

It is clear that each of these forms has its own advantage within the art of rhetoric. For just as we have said in our methodological works...

### RHETORIC. BOOK I. CHAPTER II.

...has been said, and in these matters the same holds true. For some types of rhetoric rely on **examples** <term>paradeigma</term> <note>original: "paradeigma." A rhetorical induction, where one argues from a specific historical or fictional instance to a general rule.</note>, while others rely on **enthymemes** <term>enthymēma</term> <note>original: "enthymēma." Often called a "rhetorical syllogism," this is a logical deduction where some premises are left unstated because the audience already accepts them.</note>. Likewise, among orators, some prefer examples and others prefer enthymemes.

Now, arguments that rely on examples are no less persuasive, yet those based on enthymemes excite more applause from the audience. The reason for this, and how each should be used, we shall discuss later; for now, let us define these matters more clearly.

Since the persuasive is always persuasive to someone—and one thing is immediately persuasive and credible in itself, while another appears so because it seems to be proven by things that are—no **art** <term>technē</term> <note>original: "technē." A systematic craft or field of knowledge that follows specific principles, as opposed to mere trial and error.</note> considers the individual case. For example, medicine does not consider what is healthy for Socrates or for Callias, but what is healthy for a person of a certain type or for such-and-such groups. This is what makes it a systematic art; for the individual case is infinite and cannot be the object of scientific knowledge.

In the same way, rhetoric will not consider what is a **reputable opinion** <term>endoxon</term> <note>original: "endoxon." Beliefs that are accepted by everyone, by the majority, or by the wise.</note> for a specific person like Socrates or Hippias, but rather what is reputable for people of a certain type, just as **dialectic** <term>dialektikē</term> does. For dialectic does not draw conclusions from just anything—after all, even madmen have their own fancies—but rather from things that require discussion. Rhetoric, however, draws from things about which we are already accustomed to **deliberate** <term>bouleuometha</term> <note>original: "bouleuometha." The process of weighing options to make a decision, typically in a political or legal context.</note>.

The function of rhetoric is to deal with the kind of things we deliberate about and for which we possess no systematic arts, and to do so before an audience that cannot take in a long chain of reasoning or follow a complex argument. We deliberate about things that appear to admit of two possibilities; for no one deliberates about things that cannot have happened, or be, or turn out differently, if they suppose them to be so. There is nothing to be gained by doing so.

It is possible to reason and draw conclusions, sometimes from premises that have been previously established by reasoning, and sometimes from premises that have not been...

<summary>Aristotle defines the two core tools of rhetorical persuasion—the example and the enthymeme—and argues that rhetoric is a systematic art because it deals with general types of people and debatable, uncertain outcomes.</summary>

<meta>This page continues the discussion on rhetorical logic. It addresses why rhetorical arguments must be simpler and shorter than scientific ones, specifically focusing on the listener's capacity and the nature of human action.</meta>

# ARISTOTLE

...trained in a **syllogism** <term>syllogismos</term> <note>original: "syllogismos." A formal logical argument where a conclusion is reached from two premises.</note>, because the premises are not **reputable opinions** <term>endoxa</term> <note>original: "endoxa." Beliefs accepted by everyone, or by the majority, or by the wise.</note>.

It is necessary that of these, one type is not easily followed because of its length (for the judge is assumed to be a simple person), while the others are not persuasive because they do not proceed from agreed-upon or reputable premises. Therefore, it is necessary that both the **enthymeme** <term>enthymēma</term> and the **example** <term>paradeigma</term> deal with things which, for the most part, can be otherwise. The example serves as an induction, and the enthymeme serves as a syllogism. They are constructed from few premises, often fewer than those that make up a primary or formal syllogism.

For if any of these premises is already well-known, there is no need to state it; the listener adds it himself. For instance, to state that Dorieus <note>Dorieus of Rhodes was a celebrated athlete and son of a famous champion; he won the pankration at three successive Olympic games.</note> has won a contest where a crown is the prize, it is enough to say that he has won the Olympics. One does not need to add the premise that the prize at the Olympics is a crown, for everyone knows that.

Since few of the premises from which rhetorical syllogisms are formed are "necessary" (for most things about which we make judgments and inquiries can happen in different ways; we deliberate and reflect on human actions, and all actions are of this kind, with none of them being—so to speak—governed by absolute necessity), and since things that happen for the most part and are merely possible must be argued from premises of a similar nature (whereas necessary conclusions must be drawn from necessary premises, as is clear to us from our work on *Analytics*), it is evident that among the premises from which enthymemes are spoken, some will be necessary, but most will be things that are true only for the most part. For enthymemes are derived from **probabilities** <term>eikota</term> <note>original: "eikota." Things that are likely to happen or are generally true in human affairs.</note> and **signs** <term>sēmeia</term> <note>original: "sēmeia." Evidence or indicators used to prove a point.</note>. Consequently, it is necessary that each of these corresponds to the other.

For a "probability" is that which happens for the most part. However, it is not defined as simply as some would have it, but rather it concerns things that can be otherwise, holding the same relation to that which...

<summary>Aristotle argues that rhetoric must use brief arguments (enthymemes) and skip obvious premises because public audiences cannot follow long chains of logic. He establishes that rhetoric deals primarily with probabilities and human actions rather than absolute scientific certainties.</summary>

<meta>This page continues the classification of rhetorical proofs, picking up from the discussion on the nature of premises. It moves from the idea that human actions are rarely governed by absolute necessity to the specific logical structures used in persuasion: probabilities and signs.</meta>

### RHETORIC. BOOK I. CHAPTER II. 11

...what is **probable** <term>eikos</term> <note>original: "eikos." That which usually happens, or is thought to happen, in a certain way; a likelihood rather than a certainty.</note> relates as the universal to the particular. Of **signs** <term>sēmeia</term>, one type relates as the particular to the universal, while the other relates as the universal to the particular. Of these, the necessary sign is called a **sure sign** <term>tekmērion</term> <note>original: "tekmērion." A piece of evidence that provides a certain or "necessary" proof, making the conclusion inevitable if the premise is true.</note>; the non-necessary sign has no specific name to distinguish its difference.

By "necessary," I mean those signs from which a formal **syllogism** <term>syllogismos</term> can be formed; this is why such a sign is called a *tekmērion*. For whenever people think it is impossible to refute what has been said, they believe they are bringing forward a *tekmērion*, as something demonstrated and finished. For in the ancient language, the words for "goal" <note>original: "tekmar."</note> and "limit" <note>original: "peras."</note> mean the same thing.

Further, regarding signs, the type that relates as the particular to the universal can be shown like this: for instance, if one were to say it is a sign that the wise are just because **Socrates** was wise and just. This is indeed a sign, but it is "refutable" <note>That is, the logic can be challenged because one specific example does not prove a universal rule.</note>, even if the statement itself is true; for it does not form a valid syllogism.

The other type—for instance, if someone were to say it is a sign that a person is ill because he has a fever, or that a woman has given birth because she has milk—is a **necessary** sign. This is the only kind of sign that is a *tekmērion*; for only this kind, if the premise is true, is irrefutable.

The type that relates as the universal to the particular is, for example, if one were to say it is a sign that someone has a fever because he breathes rapidly. This, too, is refutable, even if it is true; for it is possible for someone to be short of breath without having a fever.

What a probability is, what a sign is, what a sure sign is, and how they differ has now been stated. A clearer explanation of these matters—and the reason why some signs do not form syllogisms while others have been fully reasoned out—has been defined in the *Analytics* <note>Referring to Aristotle’s work *Prior Analytics*, which deals with formal logic and the structure of the syllogism.</note>.

As for the **example** <term>paradeigma</term>, it has been stated that it is a form of **induction** <term>epagōgē</term> <note>original: "epagōgē." The process of moving from specific instances to a general conclusion.</note>, and we have mentioned what subjects it concerns. It relates neither as a part to a whole, nor as a whole to a part,

<summary>This page defines and distinguishes between probabilities and signs in rhetoric, identifying the "sure sign" (tekmērion) as the only logically irrefutable form of evidence. It explains how signs can represent relationships between particulars and universals, using examples like Socrates' justice or medical symptoms.</summary>

<meta>This page continues the technical breakdown of rhetorical proofs. It completes the definition of the "example" (analogy) and begins a crucial distinction between "commonplace" arguments (topoi) and "specific" ones belonging to particular sciences like physics or politics.</meta>

# ARISTOTELIS

...nor as a whole to a whole; but rather as a part to a part, and like to like, when both belong to the same genus, but one is better known than the other, it is an **example** <term>paradeigma</term> <note>original: "paradeigma." In rhetoric, this is an argument by analogy, using a specific historical or known instance to illustrate a similar current situation.</note>. 

For instance: that **Dionysius** <note>Dionysius I of Syracuse (c. 432–367 BC), a famous ruler who rose from a populist background to absolute power.</note> was plotting a tyranny when he asked for a bodyguard; for previously, **Peisistratus** <note>A 6th-century BC ruler of Athens who famously wounded himself and his mules to claim he needed a guard for protection, only to use that guard to seize the Acropolis.</note> asked for a guard when he was plotting, and having received it, he established himself as a tyrant; and **Theagenes** did the same in Megara <note>A Greek city-state; Theagenes was a 7th-century BC tyrant who gained power after being granted a guard by the people.</note>. 

And all others whom people know of become an example for the case of Dionysius, whom they do not yet know for certain whether he asks for this reason. All these cases fall under the same universal rule: that one who is plotting a tyranny asks for a guard. 

The sources from which these seemingly demonstrative proofs are drawn have now been stated. But there is a great difference between **enthymemes** <term>enthymēmata</term> <note>original: "enthymēma." A "rhetorical syllogism"—a form of reasoning that starts from likely premises rather than certainties, often leaving one step of the logic for the audience to fill in.</note>, and it is one that has escaped almost everyone, just as it has in the dialectic method of **syllogisms** <term>syllogismoi</term>. 

For some of them belong to rhetoric, just as others belong to the **dialectic** <term>dialektikē</term> <note>The art of logical discussion and investigation of truths through question and answer.</note> method of syllogisms; but others belong to other arts and faculties—some already established, others not yet fully grasped. This is why they escape the notice of the listeners, and the more they [the speakers] handle them in a specialized way, the more they depart from pure rhetoric or dialectic. What is being said will be clearer if explained at greater length. 

I say that dialectical and rhetorical syllogisms are those about which we speak of **places** <term>topoi</term> <note>original: "topoi." Literally "places" or "locations" where a speaker can "find" arguments. We often call these "commonplaces"—general lines of reasoning that can apply to any subject.</note>. These are the ones that apply in common to matters of justice, physics, politics, and many subjects differing in kind—for instance, the "place" of the **more and the less** <note>The argument that if something is true of a more likely case, it is also true of a less likely case (or vice versa).</note>. For there will be no more difficulty in forming a syllogism or stating an enthymeme from this "place" regarding justice than regarding physics or anything else, even though these subjects differ in kind. 

On the other hand, **specific** topics <note>original: "idia." Private or specific truths belonging to a single field of study.</note> are those which come from the premises of each individual species and genus...

<summary>Aristotle defines the "example" as a rhetorical induction moving from one specific case to another under a shared universal. He also introduces the distinction between "common" lines of argument (topoi) and those specific to specialized fields of knowledge.</summary>

<meta>This page continues the technical discussion on the nature of rhetorical premises. Aristotle is distinguishing between "common" logical tools used in any debate and "special" premises that belong to specific sciences, warning that a rhetorician who relies too heavily on specialized knowledge crosses the line into becoming a scientist.</meta>

# RHETORIC. BOOK I. CHAPTER III.

...of premises. For example, there are premises about **physics** <note>original: "physika." This refers to the study of the natural world and its laws.</note> from which no **enthymeme** <term>enthymēma</term> <note>A rhetorical argument based on likelihood rather than absolute proof.</note> or **syllogism** <term>syllogismos</term> <note>A formal logical deduction where a conclusion follows necessarily from two premises.</note> can be made regarding **ethics**; and there are other premises about ethics from which no conclusion about physics can be drawn. This holds true for all subjects. 

Now, the "common" premises will not make a person wise in any specific category of knowledge, for they do not deal with any underlying subject matter. On the other hand, the better a person selects their **specialized premises** <note>original: "protaseis." The building blocks of an argument.</note>, the more they will unconsciously create a science different from **dialectics** <term>dialektikē</term> <note>The art of investigating or discussing the truth of opinions through logical cross-examination.</note> and **rhetoric**. For if they stumble upon the **first principles** <term>archai</term> <note>The foundational truths or starting points of a specific field of study.</note> of a science, the speech will no longer be dialectic or rhetoric, but rather the specific science whose principles they have grasped. 

Most enthymemes are actually spoken from these specific and particular "species" of knowledge; fewer are drawn from the "common" types. Therefore, just as we did in the *Topics* <note>Referring to Aristotle's earlier work on logic, the *Topica*.</note>, we must here distinguish between the **species** <term>eidē</term> and the **topics** <term>topoi</term> from which enthymemes are to be taken. By "species," I mean the premises specific to each category of knowledge; by "topics," I mean those common to all subjects alike. 

Let us first speak of the species; but first, let us grasp the different **genres of rhetoric**, so that having divided them by number, we may separately take up the elements and premises for each.

## CHAPTER III.

There are three kinds of rhetoric in number; for there are exactly as many types of listeners for these speeches. A speech is composed of three elements: the **speaker**, the **subject** about which they speak, and the **person addressed**. The **goal** <term>telos</term> of the speech is directed toward this last person—I mean the **listener**. 

The listener must necessarily be either an **observer** <term>theōros</term> <note>original: "theōros." This refers to a spectator or critic who is not being asked to make a practical decision, but rather to judge the skill of the speaker or the quality of the subject.</note> or a **judge** <term>kritēs</term>. A judge is one who decides either about things that have happened in the past or things that will happen in the future. The person who judges things yet to come is, for example, a **member of the assembly** <term>ekklēsiastēs</term> <note>A citizen participating in the democratic assembly to vote on future policies or laws.</note>; the person judging things that have already happened is, for example, a **juror** <term>dikastēs</term> <note>A judge or member of a jury in a court of law.</note>. 

<summary>Aristotle defines the difference between "common" and "special" rhetorical topics and establishes his famous three-part division of rhetoric based on the listener: deliberative (future), judicial (past), and ceremonial (present/observer).</summary>

<keywords>premises, dialectics, first principles, rhetorical genres, listener, judge, observer, assembly, jury</keywords>

--- Page 25 ---
<meta>This page transitions from discussing the intellectual "species" of arguments to the three practical branches of rhetoric. Aristotle concludes his point about the audience's role—specifically how the observer or judge determines the type of speech—and begins defining the three categories: Deliberative, Judicial, and Epideictic.</meta>

->ARISTOTELIS<-

...of power, such as the observer <note>original: "theōros." In this context, it refers to a spectator or a member of an audience who judges the skill of the speaker rather than making a practical decision.</note>. Consequently, there must necessarily be three genres of rhetorical speeches: **deliberative**, **judicial**, and **epideictic** <term>epideiktikon</term> <note>Also known as "demonstrative" or "ceremonial" oratory, used for praise or blame.</note>. 

Of **deliberation** <term>symboulē</term>, one part is **exhortation** <term>protropē</term>, and the other is **dissuasion** <term>apotropē</term>; for both those who give advice in private and those who address the public in the assembly always do one of these two things. 

Of **judicial proceedings** <term>dikē</term>, one part is **accusation** <term>katēgoria</term>, and the other is **defense** <term>apologia</term>; for those involved in a legal dispute must necessarily do one or the other of these. 

Of the **epideictic**, one part is **praise** <term>epainos</term>, and the other is **blame** <term>psogos</term>. 

Each of these has its own specific **time**. For the deliberative speaker, it is the **future**; for he gives advice about things to come, either exhorting or dissuading. For the person at trial, it is the **past**; for one always accuses or defends regarding things already done. For the epideictic speaker, the most important time is the **present**; for everyone praises or blames according to existing conditions. However, they frequently make additional use of the past by recalling it, and the future by predicting it.

The **end** <term>telos</term> <note>The "telos" is the ultimate goal or purpose toward which the speech is directed.</note> of each of these is also different, and since there are three types, there are three goals. 

For the deliberative speaker, the goal is the **expedient** <term>sympheron</term> and the **harmful** <term>blaberon</term>; for the one who exhorts recommends a course of action as being better, while the one who dissuades turns the audience away from it as being worse. Other factors—such as whether a thing is just or unjust, honorable or shameful—are brought in as secondary considerations to this main goal. 

For those at trial, the goal is the **just** <term>dikaion</term> and the **unjust** <term>adikon</term>; they also bring in other considerations as secondary to these. 

For those who praise and blame, the goal is the **honorable** <term>kalon</term> <note>The Greek "kalon" implies both beauty and moral nobility.</note> and the **shameful** <term>aischron</term>; and they likewise refer other matters back to these standards. 

A sign that this is the prescribed end for each is that speakers sometimes will not dispute other points. For example, a person on trial might not dispute that an event occurred or that it caused harm; but he would never admit that he has committed an injustice; for if he did...

<summary>Aristotle defines the three branches of rhetoric—Deliberative, Judicial, and Epideictic—categorizing them by their purpose, the time period they address (future, past, or present), and their ultimate moral or practical goals.</summary>

<meta>This page continues Aristotle's analysis of the three branches of rhetoric. Having established that each branch has a specific "end" or goal—the deliberative focuses on the advantageous, the judicial on the just, and the epideictic on the noble—this section explores how speakers prioritize these goals over other concerns and introduces the common logical building blocks (propositions) they all share.</meta>

->ARISTOTELIS<-

### RHETORIC. BOOK I. CHAPTER III.

...justice was required <note>original: "edei dikēs." This concludes a thought from the previous page: while a legal speaker might admit a fact, they will never admit they are acting against justice.</note>. In the same way, those who give advice <term>symbouleuontes</term> often concede many other points, but they would never admit that the advice they offer is disadvantageous, or that they are turning the audience away from what is beneficial. However, they often give no thought to whether it is not unjust to enslave neighboring peoples, even those who have done no wrong. 

Similarly, those who **praise** <term>epainountes</term> and those who **blame** <term>psegontes</term> do not consider whether a person's actions were advantageous or harmful; rather, in their praise, they often emphasize that the person performed a noble deed while disregarding their own self-interest. For example, they praise **Achilles** because he went to the aid of his companion **Patroclus**, knowing that he must die, even though he could have lived. To such a man, a death of this kind is more noble <note>original: "kallion"</note>, whereas living would have been more advantageous <note>original: "sympheron"</note>.

It is clear from what has been said that it is necessary to first have the **propositions** <term>protaseis</term> <note>These are the basic premises or building blocks of an argument.</note> concerning these matters. For the **infallible proofs** <term>tekmēria</term>, the **probabilities** <term>eikota</term>, and the **signs** <term>sēmeia</term> are the rhetorical propositions. In general, a **syllogism** <note>A logical argument where a conclusion is reached from two linked premises.</note> is made up of propositions, and the **enthymeme** <term>enthymēma</term> <note>A "rhetorical syllogism"—a form of reasoning that starts from likely premises rather than absolute certainties, often leaving one part of the argument implied for the audience to fill in.</note> is a syllogism composed of the propositions mentioned. 

Furthermore, since it is impossible for things that cannot happen to have been done or to be done in the future—only things that are **possible** <term>dynatou</term>—and since things that have not happened or will not happen cannot have been performed or be about to be performed, it is necessary for the deliberative speaker, the legal pleader, and the ceremonial speaker to have propositions concerning the **possible** and the **impossible** <term>adynatou</term>; and whether a thing has happened or not; and whether it will be or not. 

In addition, since all speakers—whether they are praising or blaming, exhorting or dissuading, accusing or defending—try to show not only the points already mentioned, but also whether a thing is **great** or **small**, whether it is a good or an evil, noble or shameful, just or unjust, either in itself...

<summary>Aristotle explains that each type of rhetoric prioritizes a specific goal (such as nobility over advantage in praise) and outlines the shared logical requirements for all orators, including the study of possibility and magnitude.</summary>

<keywords>Achilles, Patroclus, enthymeme, syllogism, deliberative rhetoric, epideictic rhetoric, judicial rhetoric, propositions, possibility, ethics vs expediency</keywords>

->ARISTOTLE<-

...speaking, or comparing them with one another; it is clear that one must have propositions <note>original: "προτάσεις" (protaseis). In logic, these are the premises or starting points of an argument.</note> concerning magnitude and smallness, and the greater and the lesser—both in general and for each specific case. For example: what constitutes a greater or lesser good, or a greater or lesser act of injustice or justice; and similarly regarding other subjects. We have now spoken of the sources from which these propositions must necessarily be drawn. Following this, we must distinguish each of these branches individually: for instance, the subjects handled by **advice** <note>Deliberative or political rhetoric.</note>, the subjects of **ceremonial speeches** <note>Epideictic rhetoric, used for praise or blame.</note>, and thirdly, the subjects of **legal trials**.

### CHAPTER IV.

First, then, we must determine what kinds of goods or evils the advisor offers advice upon; for it is not about everything, but only about those things that are capable of either happening or not happening. Regarding things that exist or will exist by necessity, or those that are impossible to be or to happen—about such things there is no advice. 

Nor, indeed, is there advice concerning all things that *might* happen; for some good things occur by nature or by chance—even though they are among things that could either be or not be—and yet advising about them serves no purpose. Rather, it is clear that deliberation is concerned with things that are naturally referred to us, and where the origin <note>original: "ἀρχὴ" (arche). This refers to the source of action or the "starting principle."</note> of their creation lies within our own power. We continue to examine a matter only until we discover whether it is possible or impossible for us to perform.

To accurately enumerate every subject, to divide them into specific types according to how people usually conduct business, and to define them according to the truth as far as possible, is not a task for the present occasion. This is because such a study does not belong to the art of **rhetoric**, but to a more insightful and truer science <note>Aristotle likely refers here to Political Science or Ethics, which deal with the "truth" of human behavior rather than just the "persuasion" of it.</note>. In fact, rhetoric has already been granted far more than its own proper subjects of study.

<summary>Aristotle transitions into the study of deliberative rhetoric, explaining that we only deliberate and offer advice on matters that are within our power to change, rather than things governed by necessity, nature, or pure luck.</summary>

<keywords>Aristotle, Rhetoric, Deliberation, Deliberative Oratory, Propositions, Human Agency, Political Science, Persuasion</keywords>

<meta>This page continues Aristotle’s discussion of the relationship between Rhetoric, Dialectic, and Political Science. It transitions from theoretical definitions to the practical subjects of political oratory, listing the five main areas a statesman must understand.</meta>

### RHETORIC. BOOK I. CHAPTER IV.

...subjects of study <note>original: "θεωρημάτων" (theōrēmatōn). These are the theoretical principles or propositions discussed on the previous page.</note>. For what we happened to have stated previously is true: rhetoric is composed of both the science of analytics <note>The study of logical reasoning (Aristotle's *Analytics*).</note> and the branch of political science concerned with ethics. In some ways, rhetoric resembles dialectic <note>The art of philosophical debate.</note>, and in others, it resembles the arguments of the sophists <note>Professional teachers who often used clever but fallacious reasoning.</note>. 

However, the more someone tries to establish <note>original: "κατασκευάζειν" (kataskeuazein)</note> either dialectic or rhetoric not as mere faculties <note>Powers or skills (dynameis) that can be used for various purposes.</note> but as actual sciences, the more they will unwittingly destroy their true nature. By attempting to <insert>refine</insert> them in this way, they cross over into sciences that deal with specific subject matters, rather than staying within the realm of words and arguments alone. 

Nevertheless, let us now discuss those points which are useful to distinguish, but which still leave room for further investigation by political science. Roughly speaking, the most important subjects about which all men deliberate, and which those giving advice <note>Specifically, deliberative orators in a public assembly.</note> address, are five in number. These are:
1. Finances <note>original: "πόρων" (porōn), literally 'ways and means' or revenue sources.</note>,
2. War and peace,
3. The defense of the territory,
4. Imports and exports,
5. And legislation.

Regarding **finances**, the person intending to give advice ought to know the city's revenues—what they are and how large they are. This is so that if any source of income is being overlooked, it may be added, and if any is too small, it may be increased. Furthermore, he should know all the city's expenses, so that if any expenditure is unnecessary, it may be removed, and if any is too large, it may be reduced. For men become wealthier not only by adding to what they already have, but also by cutting back on their spending. 

It is not possible to gain a full view of these matters only from personal experience; it is also necessary to be well-informed <note>original: "ἱστορικόν" (historikon). In this context, it means being a researcher or having a record of the practices of other nations.</note> about the discoveries and customs of others in order to give sound advice on these matters. 

Concerning **war and peace**, the advisor must know the power of the city—how much is currently available and how much is possible...

...exist; and what kind of force is currently available, as well as what kind might be added. Furthermore, he must know what wars the city has fought, and how they were fought. It is necessary to know these things not only for one's own city, but also for neighboring cities, and for those with whom a war is likely. The goal is to remain at peace with those who are stronger <note>original: "κρείττους" (kreittous). In the Greek world of city-states, maintaining a balance of power was essential for survival.</note>, while keeping the choice of war in one's own hands when dealing with the weaker. One must also know their forces—whether they are similar or dissimilar to one's own—for in this way, one may either have an advantage or find themselves at a disadvantage. 

To understand these matters, it is necessary not only to have observed the wars of one's own people, but also the wars of others and how they turned out; for it is a law of nature that similar causes lead to similar results. 

Furthermore, regarding the defense of the territory, a statesman must not be ignorant of how it is guarded. He must know the size of the guard force, the type of troops used, and the locations of the guard posts <note>original: "φυλακτηρίων" (phylaktēriōn), referring to forts or watch-stations along the borders.</note>. This is impossible to know without being personally familiar with the land. Knowledge of the terrain allows one to increase the guard if it is too small, remove it if it is redundant, and focus more on watching the most suitable <insert>strategic</insert> locations.

Furthermore, regarding food supply <term>τροφῆς (trophēs): the management of the city's subsistence and provisions</term>, he must know how much expenditure is sufficient for the city, what food is produced locally, and what must be imported. He must know which goods require an export license and which imports are needed, so that treaties <note>original: "συνθῆκαι" (synthēkai). These were formal agreements between city-states regarding trade and mutual protection.</note> and commercial agreements can be established. It is necessary to keep the citizens free from conflict with two groups: those who are more powerful, and those who are useful for trade and provisions.

For the security of the state, it is necessary to be able to observe all these practical matters; but of no less importance is an understanding of legislation <term>νομοθεσίας (nomothesias): the art of making and maintaining laws</term>. For the safety <note>original: "σωτηρία" (sōtēria). While often translated as 'salvation' in religious contexts, here it refers to the preservation and stability of the state.</note> of the city lies in its laws. Thus, it is necessary to know how many types of constitutions <term>πολιτειῶν (politeiōn): the fundamental structure or political system of a city-state</term> there are, what is beneficial for each, and what factors naturally tend to destroy them—both factors internal to the constitution and...

For example, a **democracy** becomes weaker not only by being relaxed—to the point that it eventually turns into an **oligarchy**—but also by being over-strained to the extreme. This is just like the shape of a nose; a nose that is either aquiline <note>hooked or curved like an eagle's beak</note> or snub not only approaches the ideal middle shape when it is relaxed, but when it becomes excessively aquiline or snub, it is so distorted that it ceases to even look like a nostril.

For political advice, however, the works of those who write histories of human actions are useful. But all these things are the task of **politics**, not **rhetoric**. These, then, are the most important subjects <insert>x</insert> about which a prospective advisor must have knowledge. 

Now, let us speak again of the grounds from which one should exhort or dissuade, both regarding these subjects and all others.

### CHAPTER V.

Almost every individual in private, and all people in common, have a certain mark <note>original: "σκοπός" (skopos), a target or goal one keeps in view.</note> at which they aim in what they choose and what they avoid. This is, to put it in a nutshell, **happiness** <term>eudaimonia (εὐδαιμονία): often translated as "flourishing" or "living well," rather than just a fleeting emotion.</term> and its component parts. Therefore, for the sake of having an example, let us grasp what happiness is, simply speaking, and what its parts are; for all exhortations and dissuasions are concerned with this, the things that lead to it, and the things that are contrary to it.

...are. For we ought to do those things which produce **happiness**, or some part of it, or make it greater instead of smaller; and we must not do those things which destroy or hinder it, or produce its opposites.

Let **happiness** <note>original: εὐδαιμονία (eudaimonia) – often translated as "flourishing" or "living well" rather than just a fleeting emotion</note> then be defined as **prosperity combined with virtue**; or as **self-sufficiency of life**; or as **the most pleasant life accompanied by security**; or as **an abundance of possessions and bodies, with the power to preserve and use them**. For almost everyone agrees that happiness is one or more of these things. 

If, then, happiness is something of this sort, its constituent parts must necessarily be: **noble birth**, **a wide circle of friends**, **good friends**, **wealth**, **good children**, **many children**, and **a happy old age**. Further, it includes the **virtues of the body**, such as health, beauty, strength, stature, and **competitive skill**; as well as **glory**, **honor**, and **good luck**. Finally, it includes **virtue**, or its specific parts: **practical wisdom** <note>original: φρόνησις (phronēsis)</note>, **courage**, **justice**, and **temperance** <note>original: σωφροσύνη (sōphrosynē) – a sense of moderation and self-control</note>. 

A person would be most **self-sufficient** if they possessed both the internal and external goods; for there are no other goods beyond these. Internal goods are those of the **soul** and those within the **body**; external goods are noble birth, friends, money, and honor. Further, we believe that **influence** <note>original: δυνάμεις (dynameis) – power or capabilities</note> and **good luck** should be present, for in this way life would be most secure. 

Let us then define in the same way what each of these components is. **Noble birth** <note>original: εὐγένεια (eugeneia)</note>, for a nation or a city, means being **indigenous** <note>original: αὐτόχθονας (autochthonas) – literally "sprung from the earth itself," a point of great pride for Athenians</note> or of ancient descent, and having had leaders as its first ancestors who were distinguished, and having many descendants who have been distinguished in things that are envied. 

For an individual, noble birth comes from either the male or female line, and legitimacy from both. Just as with a city, it means that the first ancestors were well-known for virtue, or wealth, or any other of the things that are honored, and that many...

# RHETORICA. LIB. I. CAP. V.

<page-num>21</page-num>

...illustrious members of the family, both men and women, and both young and old. **Having good children** and **having many children** are clear enough concepts. For the community, "good children" <note>original: εὐτεκνία (euteknia)</note> means that the youth are numerous and excellent. This excellence consists of bodily virtue, such as stature, beauty, strength, and competitive ability. Regarding the soul, the virtues of a young person are **temperance** <note>original: σωφροσύνη (sōphrosynē) — meaning self-control or moderation</note> and **courage**. For an individual, "good children" and "many children" mean having many offspring of one's own who possess these qualities, both female and male.

The excellence of females consists, in body, of beauty and stature; in soul, of **temperance** and a love of work that is not servile <note>original: ἄνευ ἀνελευθερίας — literally "without being un-free," meaning industrious but with the dignity of a free person rather than the forced labor of a slave</note>. Similarly, we must seek for each of these qualities to exist in both private and public life, and among both men and women. For those societies where the status of women is poor, as among the **Lacedaemonians** <note>The Spartans; Aristotle often criticized their social structure, noting that the freedom and influence given to Spartan women did not contribute to the state's overall virtue or happiness in the same way it did for men</note>, they are missing out on almost half of their happiness.

The components of **wealth** <note>original: πλοῦτος (ploutos)</note> are: an abundance of coin, ownership of land and estates, and also the possession of movable property, livestock, and slaves, distinguished by their number, size, and beauty. All these things should be secure, liberal, and useful. The "useful" things are primarily those that are productive, while the "liberal" things are those meant for enjoyment. By "productive," I mean those from which an income is derived; by "meant for enjoyment," I mean those from which nothing significant results beyond the act of using them.

The definition of "security" is owning something in such a place and such a manner that the use of it is in one's own power. Whether something is truly one's own depends on whether it is in one's power <gloss>3</gloss> to alienate it; and by "alienation," I mean giving it away or selling it. In general, **wealth** consists more in use than in possession; for the actual exercise and use of such things is what constitutes wealth. **Good reputation** <note>original: εὐδοξία (eudoxia)</note> is being considered an excellent person by everyone, or possessing something that everyone desires...

...by the many, or the good, or the wise. **Honor** <note>original: Τιμὴ (timē)</note> is a sign of a reputation for doing good. Those who have already performed acts of kindness are honored most justly, though we also honor those who have the power to do good in the future. **Benefaction** <note>original: Εὐεργεσία (euergesia) — the act of being a "well-doer"</note> relates either to the preservation of life and the things that sustain it, or to wealth, or to any other goods that are difficult to acquire—whether generally, in a specific place, or at a particular time. For many people achieve honor for things that seem small, but the specific manner and the timing are the true causes of the distinction.

The **components of honor** include: sacrifices, memorials in verse or prose, prizes, sacred precincts <note>land set apart for a god or hero</note>, front-row seats at festivals, ancestral tombs, statues, and public maintenance <note>original: τροφαὶ δημόσιαι — meals provided at state expense, usually in the Town Hall</note>. There are also "barbarian" <note>To the Greeks, "barbarian" referred to anyone who did not speak Greek, particularly the Persians</note> customs, such as prostration <note>original: προσκυνήσεις (proskynēseis) — the act of bowing or kneeling before a superior</note> and yielding one's place. Gifts, too, are held in honor among all people. For a gift is both the giving of a possession and a sign of honor; this is why both the money-lovers and the honor-lovers desire them. A gift satisfies both: it is a possession, which the money-lover craves, and it carries status, which the honor-lover seeks.

The virtue of the **body** is **health** <note>original: ὑγίεια (hygieia)</note>. This should be defined as being free from disease while having full use of the body. Many people appear "healthy" in the way **Herodicus** <note>A 5th-century BCE physician and trainer known for his extremely strict and grueling health regimens</note> is said to have been, but no one would consider them happy for their health, because they must abstain from all or most human pleasures.

**Beauty** <note>original: Κάλλος (kallos)</note> is different for each stage of life. The beauty of a **youth** consists in having a body capable of enduring hardships—whether in running or in feats of strength—while being pleasant to look upon for the sake of enjoyment. This is why **pentathletes** <note>Athletes who competed in five events: jumping, running, discus, javelin, and wrestling</note> are the most beautiful, because they are naturally gifted in both strength and speed. The beauty of a man in his **prime** consists in being fit for the labors of war, while appearing pleasant in a way that is also formidable. The beauty of an **old man** consists in being capable of necessary tasks while remaining free from pain, because he possesses none of the deformities with which old age typically ravages the body. **Strength** <note>original: Ἰσχὺς (ischys)</note> is...

### RHETORICA. BOOK I. CHAPTER V.

<page-num>23</page-num>

**Strength** <note>original: δύναμις (dynamis)</note> is the power to move another as one wishes; and one must move another either by pulling, pushing, lifting, pressing, or crushing. Consequently, the **strong man** <note>original: ἰσχυρός (ischyros)</note> is strong in either all or some of these ways. The virtue of **stature** <note>original: μεγέθους (megethous)</note> consists in exceeding the majority in height, depth, and width by just such an amount that it does not make one's movements slower due to the excess.

The **athletic virtue** of the body consists of stature, strength, and speed; for even the fast person is a kind of strong person. For he who can throw out his legs in a certain way, and move them quickly and far, is a **runner** <note>original: δρομικός (dromikos)</note>; he who can squeeze and hold someone down is a **wrestler** <note>original: παλαιστικός (palaistikos)</note>; he who can push someone away with a blow is a **boxer** <note>original: πυκτικός (pyktikos)</note>; he who can do both of these is a **pancratiast** <note>original: παγκρατιαστικός (pankratiastikos) — a competitor in the 'all-powers' combat sport that combined wrestling and boxing</note>; and he who can do all of them is a **pentathlete** <note>original: πένταθλος (pentathlos) — an athlete who competed in five events: the long jump, javelin, discus, sprint, and wrestling</note>.

A **happy old age** <note>original: εὐγηρία (eugēria)</note> is the slow approach of old age accompanied by freedom from pain. For if one ages quickly, it is not a "happy" old age, nor is it if one ages slowly but painfully. This state arises from both the virtues of the body and from luck. For if a man is not free from disease and is not strong, he will not be free from suffering, nor will he be painless and long-lived; nor could he remain fortunate without these qualities. However, there is also a certain power of **longevity** apart from strength and health, for many people live to a great age without these bodily virtues. But a precise discussion of these matters is not useful for our present purpose.

**Abundance of friends** <note>original: πολυφιλία (polyphilia)</note> and **quality of friends** <note>original: χρηστοφιλία (chrēstophilia)</note> are easily understood once "friend" is defined: a friend is someone who, whenever he thinks something is good for another, is inclined to do it for that person’s sake. He who has many such people has "many friends"; he whose friends are also men of high character has "good friends."

**Good fortune** <note>original: εὐτυχία (eutychia)</note> consists in the occurrence and possession of those goods of which luck is the cause—whether all of them, the majority of them, or the most important of them. Luck is the cause of some things of which even the...
