<meta>This page continues the discussion from page 23, concluding the analysis of external and physical goods (such as health, beauty, and luck) within Book I, Chapter V, before transitioning to the definitions of "the good" in Chapter VI.</meta>

->ARISTOTLE [Book] I<-

...skills <note>original: τέχναι (technai)</note>, but many others are without skill, such as those produced by nature; though it is also possible for things to happen contrary to nature. For example, while **health** is caused by the skill of medicine, **beauty** and **stature** are caused by nature. Generally speaking, those goods that arise from **luck** <note>original: τύχη (tychē)</note> are the ones that provoke **envy** <note>original: φθόνος (phthonos)</note>. Luck is also the cause of goods that exceed expectations; for instance, if all one's brothers are ugly while he is handsome, or if others failed to see a treasure but he found it, or if a nearby arrow hit someone else but missed him, or if he was the only one not to go to a place he usually frequented while those who went just once were destroyed. All such instances are considered pieces of **good fortune**. Regarding **virtue** <note>original: ἀρετή (aretē)</note>, since the topic is most closely related to **praise** <note>original: ἔπαινος (epainos)</note>, we must define it when we compose our discussion on praise. It is now clear what things one should aim for when encouraging actions—treating them as future or already existing—and what things to discourage, for these are simply the opposites.

### CAPUT VI.
### CHAPTER VI.

Since the goal set for the person giving advice is the **expedient** <note>original: συμφέρον (sympheron) — that which is advantageous or beneficial</note>, and since people do not deliberate about the end itself, but about the means to the end; and since these means are the actions that are expedient; and since the **expedient** is a **good** <note>original: ἀγαθόν (agathon)</note>, we should grasp the fundamental elements concerning the "good" and the "expedient" in a general sense.

Let a <term>good</term> be defined as:
*   Whatever is desirable for its own sake.
*   That for the sake of which we choose something else.
*   That which all things aim for, or at least all things that have sensation or intelligence, or would aim for if they possessed intelligence.
*   Whatever reason <note>original: νοῦς (nous)</note> would assign to each person.
*   Whatever the reason inherent in each person assigns to them—this is the individual's "good."
*   That which, when present, makes one well-disposed and **self-sufficient** <note>original: αὔταρκες (autarkes)</note>.
*   Self-sufficiency itself.
*   That which is productive or preservative of such things, or that which is followed by...

<summary>Aristotle concludes his discussion on luck and good fortune before defining "the good" and "the expedient" as the primary goals of political and deliberative speech.</summary>

<keywords>luck, fortune, beauty, virtue, the good, expedient, self-sufficiency, deliberation, Aristotle's Rhetoric</keywords>

--- Page 36 ---
<meta>This page continues the logical definition of "the good" (agathon) from the end of the previous page. Aristotle is systematically categorizing things that everyone agrees are good to help the orator build persuasive arguments for what is beneficial to a city or individual.</meta>

->RHETORIC. BOOK I. CHAPTER VI.<-

...such things; and those things that prevent their opposites, as well as those things that are destructive of them <note>That is, things that destroy what is harmful are themselves good.</note>. Consequences follow in two ways: either simultaneously or later. For instance, knowledge follows the act of learning later; but life follows being healthy simultaneously. Productive causes <note>original: ποιητικά (poiētika)</note> also operate in three ways: some, in the way that being healthy is the essence of health; others, in the way that food produces health <note>as a necessary material requirement</note>; and others, in the way that physical exercise produces health, because it generally brings it about.

These points being established, it necessarily follows that the acquisition of goods is good, as is the getting rid of evils. For the latter results in the absence of evil simultaneously, while the former results in the possession of the good later. Furthermore, the acquisition of a greater good instead of a lesser one is a good, as is the choice of a lesser evil instead of a greater one; for the degree to which the greater exceeds the lesser becomes, in the first case, an acquisition of good, and in the second, a removal of evil.

It is also necessary that the **virtues** <note>original: ἀρεταί (aretai)</note> be a good; for it is by these that those who possess them are in a good state, and virtues are productive of goods and active in performing them. We must speak later about each virtue separately, defining what it is and what qualities it possesses. 

**Pleasure** <note>original: ἡδονή (hēdonē)</note> must also be a good; for all living creatures naturally desire it. Consequently, both pleasant things and **beautiful** <note>original: καλά (kala) — this term implies both physical beauty and moral nobility</note> things must be goods; for the former produce pleasure, while of the beautiful things, some are pleasant and others are desirable for their own sake.

To speak of these things individually, the following must necessarily be goods: 

**Happiness** <note>original: εὐδαιμονία (eudaimonia) — often translated as "flourishing" or "living well"</note>; for it is desirable for its own sake, it is self-sufficient, and it is for the sake of happiness that we choose many other things. 

**Justice**, **Courage**, **Temperance**, **Magnanimity**, **Magnificence**, and other such habits <note>original: ἕξεις (hexeis) — stable character traits</note>; for these are virtues of the soul <note>original: ψυχή (psychē)</note>. 

Also **health**, **beauty**, and the like; for these are virtues of the body and are productive of many things—for example, health produces both pleasure and life. Because of this, health is thought to be the best of all, since it is the cause of two of the most...

<summary>Aristotle defines "the good" by categorizing types of productive causes and listing universally recognized goods like virtue, pleasure, and happiness. He argues that these are the building blocks of persuasive political and ethical speech.</summary>
<keywords>The Good, Virtues, Happiness, Pleasure, Health, Justice, Courage, Ethics, Rhetoric</keywords>

--- Page 37 ---
<meta>This page continues Aristotle's systematic catalog of "goods"—things that are desirable for their own sake or for the sake of something else—which he began on the previous page to provide orators with a toolkit for persuasive political and ethical speech.</meta>

### ARISTOTELIS

...for many things, it is the cause of what is most precious, namely pleasure and life itself. **Wealth** <note>original: Πλοῦτος (Ploutos)</note>; for it is the excellence of acquisition <note>meaning the perfection of the ability to possess things</note> and is productive of many other goods. **Friends** and **friendship**; for a friend is desirable for his own sake and is also productive of many benefits. **Honor** and **reputation** <note>original: Τιμή (Timē) and δόξα (doxa)</note>; for they are both pleasant and productive of many things, and they are usually accompanied by the actual possession of those qualities for which people are honored. 

The **power of speech** and the **power of action**; for all such faculties are productive of goods. Furthermore, **natural talent**, **memory**, **readiness to learn**, **quick-wittedness**, and all such qualities; for these are powers productive of goods. Similarly, all the **sciences** and the **arts** are goods. And **life** itself; for even if no other good followed from it, it is desirable for its own sake. And **justice** <note>original: δίκαιον (dikaion)</note>; for it is something that benefits the community.

These, then, are the goods that are almost universally agreed upon. But in cases that are disputed, arguments may be drawn from the following: That thing whose opposite is an evil is itself a good. Also, that whose opposite benefits one’s enemies is a good. For example, if it especially benefits our enemies for us to be cowards, it is clear that **courage** is especially beneficial to the citizens. And in general, whatever our enemies desire or whatever makes them rejoice, the opposite of that thing appears to be beneficial. Thus it was well said:

-> "Surely Priam would rejoice." <-
<note>Aristotle is quoting Homer's *Iliad*. The logic is that if your enemy (King Priam) would be happy about something, it must be bad for you, and its opposite must be good.</note>

This is not always the case, however, but only for the most part; for nothing prevents the same thing from occasionally benefiting both opposing sides. Hence the saying that "misfortune brings people together" <note>original: τὰ κακὰ συνάγει τοὺς ἀνθρώπους (ta kaka synagei tous anthrōpous)</note>, which happens when the same thing is harmful to both parties. Also, that in which there can be no excess is a good; but that which becomes greater than it ought to be is an evil. And that for which much labor has been endured or much expense incurred is a good; for by that point it is an "apparent good," and such a thing is already viewed as an end <note>a final goal or purpose</note>.

<summary>Aristotle lists universally accepted "goods" like wealth, friendship, and justice, then provides logical frameworks for arguing that disputed things are good, such as by considering what would disadvantage an enemy.</summary>
<keywords>Aristotle, Rhetoric, ethics, wealth, friendship, honor, justice, logic of the good, Homeric citation</keywords>

<meta>This page continues Aristotle's list of "goods" (desirable things) used in persuasive speech. Having established that things universally recognized as beneficial are "good," he now examines more subjective or comparative goods—things that are praised by others, chosen by heroes, or easy to achieve.</meta>

### RHETORIC BOOK I. CHAPTER VI.

...is assumed to be, and is the goal of many things; and the goal is a **good**. From this principle, these famous lines were spoken:

->“And so they would leave a boast to Priam.”<-
<note>Homer, *Iliad* 2.160. Aristotle argues that if something benefits an enemy (like the Greeks retreating and leaving glory to the Trojan King Priam), then the opposite must be a "good" for us.</note>

And,

->“It is a shameful thing to remain for a long time.”<-
<note>Homer, *Iliad* 2.298. Referring to the Greeks staying at Troy for years without success; the "good" here is avoiding the disgrace of wasted effort.</note>

And there is also the proverb, “to break the water-jar at the doorstep.” <note>A Greek idiom for failing at the very last moment, just as the goal is within reach.</note> Also, that which many people strive for, and what appears to be an object of contention; for that which all strive for was defined as a good, and “the many” appear to represent "all."

And that which is **praiseworthy**; for no one praises what is not good. Similarly, that which even enemies and base men praise; for it is as if everyone already agrees, even those who have suffered because of it. Because the truth is so obvious, they are compelled to acknowledge it. Just as base men are those whom even their own friends criticize, so good men are those whom even their enemies do not criticize. For this reason, the Corinthians supposed they had been insulted by Simonides <note>Simonides of Ceos, a famous Greek lyric poet.</note> when he wrote:

->“Troy does not find fault with the Corinthians.”<-
<note>The Corinthians felt this was an insult because if an enemy has no reason to complain about you, it suggests you were not a formidable or effective opponent in battle.</note>

And whatever any of the wise, or of good men or women, has preferred; for example, as the goddess **Athena** chose **Odysseus**, or **Theseus** chose **Helen**, or the goddesses chose **Alexander** <note>Also known as Paris; this refers to the Judgment of Paris.</note>, or **Homer** chose **Achilles**. 

And in general, all things that are the object of **deliberate choice** <term>deliberate choice: (proaireta) actions or things chosen after reflection and rational calculation, rather than by impulse.</term>. Men choose to do the things already mentioned, as well as things that are bad for their enemies and good for their friends.

And things that are **possible**; these are of two kinds: things that could happen, and things that happen easily. Easy things are those that happen either without pain or in a short time; for "difficulty" is defined either by the presence of pain or by the length of time required. 

And if things happen as they wish; and they wish for either no evil at all, or an evil that is smaller than the resulting good. This will be the case if the punishment is either hidden or small. Also, things that are one's own. And things that no one else possesses. And extraordinary things; for in this way, there is greater honor.

<summary>Aristotle explores how orators can identify "goods" by looking at what is praised by enemies, what is chosen by historical heroes, and what is easily achievable or unique.</summary>
<keywords>Aristotle, Rhetoric, ethics, Priam, Homer, Simonides, Odysseus, Achilles, deliberate choice, possible goods</keywords>

<meta>This page transitions from Aristotle's discussion of specific "goods" to his foundational classification of the three branches of rhetoric. Note: The Greek text provided here actually corresponds to Book I, Chapter 3 of the *Rhetoric*, though the previous page's translation was from Chapter 6. This page defines the goals, timeframes, and methods for deliberative, judicial, and ceremonial speech.</meta>

### ARISTOTLE

...of ability, such as the <term>spectator</term> <note>original: θεωρός (*theōros*). Aristotle distinguishes between a "judge" who decides on an action and a "spectator" who simply observes and evaluates the speaker’s skill.</note>. Consequently, there must necessarily be three genres of rhetorical speeches: **deliberative**, **judicial**, and **epideictic**.

In **deliberation**, there is <term>exhortation</term> <note>original: προτροπή (*protropē*); urging someone toward an action.</note> and <term>dissuasion</term> <note>original: ἀποτροπή (*apotropē*); turning someone away from an action.</note>; for both those who give advice in private and those who address the public assembly always do one of these two things.

In a **lawsuit** <note>The judicial or "forensic" genre.</note>, there is **accusation** and **defense**; for it is necessary for those in dispute to do one or the other of these.

In the **epideictic** <note>The "ceremonial" or "demonstrative" genre, often used for speeches at funerals or festivals.</note> genre, there is **praise** and **blame**.

Each of these has its own specific **timeframe**:
*   For the **deliberative** speaker, it is the **future**; for he gives advice about things that will happen, either urging them on or turning people away from them.
*   For the **litigant** <note>The judicial speaker in a court of law.</note>, it is the **past**; for it is always regarding things already done that one person accuses and the other defends.
*   For the **epideictic** speaker, the most critical time is the **present**; for all people praise or blame based on existing circumstances. However, they frequently make use of other times as well, by recalling the past or anticipating the future.

The **goal** <note>original: τέλος (*telos*).</note> for each of these is also different; since there are three types of speech, there are three goals:
*   For the **deliberative** speaker, the goal is the **advantageous** and the **harmful**; for the one who exhorts advises a course as better, and the one who dissuades turns the audience away from it as being worse. He brings in other considerations to support this—such as whether a thing is **just or unjust**, or **honorable or shameful**—but only as secondary to the main goal.
*   For **litigants**, the goal is the **just** and the **unjust**; they likewise bring in other considerations to support these primary points.
*   For those **praising and blaming**, the goal is the **honorable** and the **shameful**; and they refer all other points back to these.

As evidence that these are the specific goals of each: sometimes a speaker will not dispute the other points at all. For instance, a man on trial might not deny that an event took place, or that he caused harm; but he would **never** admit that he acted unjustly; for if he did, he would...

<vocab>**deliberative** (συμβουλευτικὸν), **judicial** (δικανικὸν), **epideictic/ceremonial** (ἐπιδεικτικόν), **exhortation** (προτροπή), **dissuasion** (ἀποτροπή), **accusation** (κατηγορία), **defense** (ἀπολογία), **praise** (ἔπαινος), **blame** (ψόγος), **advantageous** (συμφέρον), **harmful** (βλαβερόν), **just** (δίκαιον), **unjust** (ἄδικον), **honorable** (καλὸν), **shameful** (αἰσχρόν)</vocab>

<summary>Aristotle defines the three branches of rhetoric—deliberative, judicial, and epideictic—categorizing them by their purpose, the timeframe they address (future, past, or present), and their ultimate goals (advantage, justice, or honor).</summary>
<keywords>three branches of rhetoric, deliberative, judicial, epideictic, exhortation and dissuasion, accusation and defense, praise and blame, rhetorical goals, timeframe of speech</keywords>

->### ARISTOTLE<-

And things that are suitable for them; such things are those fitting to their <term>class</term> <note>original: γένος (*genos*). In Aristotle's context, this refers to one's social standing, family line, or natural category.</note> and power. Also, things they feel they lack, even if they are small; for they choose to do these no less than great things. And things easy to accomplish; for these are possible because they are easy. "Easy" things are those which everyone, or most people, or those like themselves, or those inferior to them have successfully achieved. Also, things that will please their friends, or things by which they will annoy their enemies. And whatever things those they admire choose to do. And things for which they are naturally gifted and experienced; for they think they will succeed more easily in these. Also, those things which no <term>base person</term> <note>original: φαῦλος (*phaulos*); often used to describe someone of low character or common status.</note> would do; for these are more praiseworthy. And things they happen to desire; for such things appear not only pleasant, but also better. And individuals especially choose things toward which they are inclined: for example, the lovers of victory, if victory will result; the lovers of honor, if honor; the lovers of money, if money; and others likewise. Regarding the <term>good</term> <note>original: ἀγαθοῦ (*agathou*)</note> and the <term>advantageous</term> <note>original: συμφέροντος (*sympherontos*)</note>, these are the sources from which one must take their <term>proofs</term> <note>original: πίστεις (*pisteis*); the means of persuasion or logical arguments used by an orator.</note>.

## CHAPTER VII.

Since people often agree that both of two options are advantageous, but dispute which is *more* so, we should next speak about the <term>greater good</term> and the more advantageous. Let <term>superiority</term> <note>original: ὑπερέχον (*hyperechon*)</note> be defined as that which is as much as another and more; and the <term>surpassed</term> as that which is contained within. And "greater" and "more" are always relative to "lesser"; while "large" and "small," and "much" and "few," are relative to the size of the majority. That which exceeds is "large"; that which falls short is "small"; and "much" and "few" are defined likewise. Since we call "good" that which is desirable for its own sake and not for the sake of something else; and that which all things aim for; and that which a mind possessing <term>practical wisdom</term> <note>original: φρόνησιν (*phronēsin*); the virtue of practical reasoning and moral action.</note> would choose; and that which is productive and preservative of good, or followed by—

--- Page 42 ---
<meta>Continuing from the previous page's discussion on the "Common Topics" of degree and the definitions of goodness, this text concludes the list of motivations for human action (the end of Chapter 6) and transitions into a systematic comparison of which "goods" are superior to others (the start of Chapter 7). While the previous fragment was heavily damaged, this page provides a clear, foundational logic for rhetorical persuasion.</meta>

->### RHETORIC. BOOK I. CHAPTER VII.<-

...such things. But that for the sake of which a thing is done is its <term>end</term> <note>original: τέλος (*telos*); the ultimate goal or purpose of an action.</note>. The end is that for the sake of which all other things are done. For any individual, their own "good" is that which stands in this relation to them. It follows, therefore, that a greater number of goods is a greater good than one or a smaller number, provided that the one or the smaller number is counted as part of the total; for the total exceeds them, and that which is contained within the total is surpassed by it.

And if the greatest member of one class exceeds the greatest member of another, then the class itself exceeds the other class. Conversely, whenever a class is superior to another, the greatest member of the superior class is greater than the greatest member of the inferior one. For example, if the biggest man is bigger than the biggest woman, then men in general are bigger than women; and if men in general are bigger than women, then the biggest man is bigger than the biggest woman. This is because the proportions of superiority between <term>classes</term> <note>original: γενῶν (*genōn*); here referring to types or categories of things.</note> correspond to the proportions of superiority between the greatest individuals within them.

We also find superiority when one thing follows from another, but the other does not follow from the first. One thing may "follow" another in three ways: simultaneously, sequentially, or potentially. This is because the use of the following thing is already inherent in the use of the other. For example:
*   **Simultaneously:** Life follows from being healthy, but health does not necessarily follow from being alive.
*   **Sequentially:** Knowledge follows from the act of learning.
*   **Potentially:** Simple <term>theft</term> <note>original: ἀποστερεῖν (*aposterein*); meaning to deprive or defraud.</note> follows from <term>temple-robbing</term> <note>original: ἱεροσυλεῖν (*hierosylein*); the specific crime of stealing from a sacred place.</note>, for anyone who has robbed a temple would also be capable of committing simple theft.

Furthermore, those things that exceed a certain standard by a larger margin are greater, for they must necessarily exceed the greater thing as well. Things that are <term>productive</term> <note>original: ποιητικά (*poiētika*); the causes or means that bring something about.</note> of a greater good are themselves greater, for that is what it means to be "productive of a greater good." Likewise, that whose productive cause is greater is itself greater; for instance, if "that which is healthy" is more desirable and a greater good than "that which is pleasant," then health itself must be greater than pleasure.

Also, that which is desirable for its own sake is greater than that which is not sought for its own sake. For example, physical strength is superior to "what is healthy" (such as medicine or diet), because the latter is not sought for itself, while the former is—and we have defined the "good" as that which is sought for itself. Likewise, if one thing is an end and another is not; for the one is for the sake of something else, while the end is for its own sake—just as physical exercise is for the sake of attaining a good physical condition.

<summary>Aristotle establishes logical rules for determining which "goods" are superior to others, using principles of quantity, class-proportionality, and the distinction between means and ends. He argues that things sought for their own sake (ends) are inherently greater than things sought for the sake of something else (means).</summary>

<keywords>Aristotle, Rhetoric, ethics, the Good, ends and means, logical consequence, health vs pleasure, hierarchies of value</keywords>

--- Page 43 ---
<meta>The text continues from the previous page's discussion on how to determine which of two things is "greater" or more "desirable." Aristotle is providing a list of rhetorical "topics" or logical frameworks that a speaker can use to argue that one choice, person, or action is superior to another. This page specifically focuses on self-sufficiency, causality, and the classic tension between rarity and utility.</meta>

-># ARISTOTLE:<-

...the body. Also, that which is less in need of one or more other things is a greater good, for it is more <term>self-sufficient</term> <note>original: αὐταρκέστερον (*autarkesteron*); meaning independent or needing nothing else to be complete.</note>. That which needs fewer things, or things easier to obtain, is likewise less dependent. 

Furthermore, when one thing cannot exist or come into being without another, but the other can exist without it, the independent one is more self-sufficient and therefore appears to be a greater good. This is also true if one thing is a <term>first principle</term> <note>original: ἀρχὴ (*archē*); a starting point, origin, or fundamental rule.</note> and the other is not, or if one is a cause and the other is not, for the same reason: without a cause or a principle, it is impossible for a thing to exist or to happen. 

Between two principles, that which springs from the greater principle is greater; and between two causes, that which springs from the greater cause is greater. Conversely, between two principles, the principle of the greater thing is greater; and between two causes, the cause of the greater thing is greater.

It is clear from these arguments that a thing can be made to appear "greater" in two opposite ways. If one thing is a principle and the other is not, the principle will seem greater. But if one thing is the <term>end</term> <note>original: τέλος (*telos*); the goal or final purpose.</note> and the other is the principle, the end may seem greater; for the end is the ultimate purpose, whereas the principle is merely the start. 

This is illustrated by what the orator Leodamas <note>An influential Athenian orator of the 4th century BCE.</note> said when accusing Callistratus: he argued that the one who *planned* the deed was more guilty than the one who *committed* it, for the deed would never have been done had it not been planned. But against Chabrias <note>An Athenian general; Leodamas famously prosecuted him and Callistratus in 366 BCE regarding the loss of the town of Oropus.</note>, he argued the opposite: that the one who *committed* the deed was more guilty than the planner, for the deed would not have come to pass if there were no one to carry it out—since the very reason people plot is so that they may act.

Furthermore, that which is rare is a greater good than that which is abundant. For example, gold is a greater good than iron, even though it is less useful; the possession of it is valued more because it is harder to acquire. In another sense, however, the abundant is greater than the rare because its *utility* is superior; for what is used often is superior to what is used seldom. This is the meaning behind the saying, "Best of all is water" <note>original: Ἄριστον μὲν ὕδωρ (*Ariston men hydōr*); the famous opening line of Pindar’s first Olympian Ode, highlighting that while gold is precious, water is the most essential for life.</note>.

In general, the difficult task is greater than the easy one because it is rarer. Yet, in another way, the easy is greater than the difficult, because it is exactly as we wish it to be. Also, that whose opposite is a greater evil is itself a greater good. And that whose...

<summary>Aristotle outlines logical arguments for why one thing is "greater" than another, using concepts like self-sufficiency, causality, and the relative value of rarity versus utility. He demonstrates how a clever orator can argue both sides of an issue—such as whether the planner or the doer of a crime is more to blame.</summary>

<keywords>Aristotle, Rhetoric, self-sufficiency, first principles, causality, Leodamas, Callistratus, Chabrias, rarity vs utility, Pindar</keywords>

--- Page 44 ---
<meta>The text continues Aristotle's systematic catalog of "common topics" for comparing goods. On the previous page, he discussed self-sufficiency and the relationship between causes and effects. This page continues that logical progression, exploring how we judge the magnitude of a thing based on its results, the desire it inspires, the specialized knowledge required to understand it, and the consensus of the wise.</meta>

### RHETORIC: BOOK I, CHAPTER VII

...the loss of a thing is greater <note>original: ἡ στέρησις μείζων (*hē sterēsis meizōn*); meaning that the privation or absence of a quality is a more significant indicator of its value.</note>. Similarly, virtue is greater than the absence of virtue, and vice is greater than the absence of vice; for the former are **ends** <term>original: τέλη (*telē*); the final goals or purposes for which things are done</term>, while the latter are not.

Those things whose results are nobler or more shameful are themselves greater. Conversely, those things whose vices or virtues are greater also produce greater results; for as the causes and **first principles** are, so too are the outcomes—and as the outcomes are, so too are the causes and first principles.

Furthermore, those things are greater whose superiority is more desirable or noble. For example, keen sight is more desirable than a keen sense of smell, because sight is superior to smell as a sense. Also, being a "lover of friends" is nobler than being a "lover of money," which means that the quality of friendship is greater than the quality of wealth-seeking. In the same vein, the "excesses" or highest degrees of better things are themselves better, and those of nobler things are nobler.

Those things for which the desires are nobler or better are also greater; for greater appetites are directed toward greater objects. For the same reason, the desires for nobler and better things are themselves nobler and better.

Additionally, when the **sciences** <note>original: ἐπιστῆμαι (*epistēmai*); branches of organized knowledge or expertise.</note> are nobler or more serious, their subject matters are also nobler and more serious; for the status of a science corresponds to the status of the truth it seeks, and each science dictates its own particular truth. Proportionally, the sciences of more serious and nobler subjects are themselves more serious and noble.

Whatever would be judged—or has been judged—to be a good or a "greater" good by **wise men** <term>original: οἱ φρόνιμοι (*hoi phronimoi*); people possessing *phronēsis* or practical wisdom</term>, or by everyone, or by the majority, or by the most powerful, must necessarily be so—either absolutely or according to the standard by which they judged it.

This rule is a general one, applicable to other categories as well: the nature, the quantity, and the quality of a thing are as science and wisdom declare them to be. However, we have spoken specifically here of "goods." We have defined a "good" as that which any being would choose if it possessed wisdom. It is clear, then, that whatever wisdom declares to be "greater" is indeed greater.

Also, that which belongs to better things, either absolutely or insofar as they are better...

<summary>Aristotle outlines several logical methods for arguing that one thing is "greater" than another, focusing on the nobility of a thing's purpose, the intensity of desire it provokes, and the judgment of wise authorities.</summary>
<keywords>Aristotle, Rhetoric, virtue, vice, practical wisdom, ends and means, hierarchy of goods, consensus of the wise</keywords>

--- Page 45 ---
<meta>This page continues Aristotle's list of "common topics"—the logical tools used to compare two things and determine which is the "greater good." Having previously discussed ends and results, he now moves into psychological motivations, the consensus of the wise, and the grammatical relationships between virtues.</meta>

### ARISTOTELIS: RHETORIC

->**Page 32**<-

...for example, **courage** is a greater good than mere physical strength. Also, that which a better person would choose—either absolutely or insofar as they are a better person—is a greater good. For example, **to suffer a wrong** rather than to commit one <note>original: τὸ ἀδικεῖσθαι μᾶλλον ἢ ἀδικεῖν (*to adikeisthai mallon ē adikein*); a central tenet of Socratic and Aristotelian ethics, suggesting that the corruption of one's own soul is the greatest possible harm.</note>; for this is what a more just person would choose. 

Further, that which is more pleasant is greater than that which is less so; for all creatures pursue **pleasure** <note>original: ἡδονή (*hēdonē*)</note>, and they desire it for its own sake. Both "the good" and "the end" are defined in relation to these pleasures. A pleasure is considered greater if it is more free from pain or if it lasts for a longer time. 

Similarly, the **nobler** thing is greater than the less noble; for the "noble" is defined as that which is either inherently pleasant or desirable for its own sake. Also, those things for which we wish to be the cause for ourselves or our friends are greater goods; conversely, those things for which we least wish to be the cause are greater evils.

Moreover, things that are long-lasting are superior to those that are short-lived, and stable things are superior to those that are unstable. For the value of the long-lasting excels in its **duration**, while the stable excels in its **availability**; for whenever one wishes to use it, the stable thing remains at hand.

And as it is with **coordinates** <term>original: σύστοιχα (*systoicha*); logically related words, such as "just," "justice," and "justly"</term> and similar grammatical forms, so the comparison follows for the rest. For example, if "acting courageously" is nobler and more desirable than "acting temperately," then **courage** itself is more desirable than temperance, and "being courageous" is better than "being temperate."

Additionally, that which everyone chooses is greater than that which is not chosen by everyone. Also, what the majority choose is greater than what the minority choose; for we have defined the **good** as that which all aim for—therefore, that which is aimed at more intensely is a greater good. 

Furthermore, a thing is greater if it is judged so by our rivals, our enemies, or by those who act as judges, or those whom the judges themselves approve. In the first case, it is as if everyone acknowledges the value; in the second, it is the verdict of those in authority or those who possess specialized knowledge.

Sometimes, that in which everyone shares is considered greater, for it is a **disgrace** not to participate in it; but at other times, that which no one or only a few possess is greater, for it is **rarer** and thus more prized. 

Also, things that are more praiseworthy are greater, for they are nobler. Likewise, those things for which the honors are greater are superior; for **honor** is, in a sense, a measure of a thing's worth. Conversely, those things for which the penalties are greater are also greater in their significance. And those of the same...

<summary>Aristotle outlines various comparative measures for the "greater good," including the choices of the virtuous, the duration and stability of a thing, and the weight of public honor or consensus.</summary>
<keywords>Aristotle, Rhetoric, ethics, pleasure, courage, justice, noble, coordinates, majority opinion, honor</keywords>

--- Page 46 ---
<meta>This page continues Aristotle's exploration of "common topics" (*topoi*)—the logical frameworks used by orators to prove that one thing is better or more significant than another. Having just discussed "coordinates" and the stability of goods, he now turns to rhetorical techniques like "division" and the importance of context, effort, and natural talent.</meta>

### RHETORIC: BOOK I, CHAPTER VII

->**ARISTOTLE’S RHETORIC**<-

...and things that are either said to be great, or appear to be so, are considered greater. Also, when things are **divided into their constituent parts**, the same things appear greater, for the superiority of the whole seems to manifest in a greater number of instances. This is why the poet <note>Aristotle refers to Homer in the *Iliad* (9.591-594).</note> says that the wife of Meleager persuaded him to rise and fight by listing the horrors of a fallen city:

> All the evils that befall men when their city is captured:
> The men are slain, and fire turns the city to
> ->ashes;<-
> And others lead the children away into slavery.

The same effect is achieved by **combining** and "building up" details, as the playwright Epicharmus <note>A celebrated comic poet and early philosopher known for his structured rhetorical style.</note> did. This works for the same reason as division: the act of composition <note>original: σύνθεσις (*synthesis*)</note> demonstrates a massive superiority, and because the subject is presented as the very origin and cause of great things.

Furthermore, since that which is **more difficult** and **rarer** is greater, magnitude is also created by circumstances: the right moment <term>original: καιρός (*kairos*); the opportune moment or "the nick of time"</term>, the right age, the right place, the right time, and the available powers. For if an action is beyond one’s natural power, or beyond one's age, or beyond what is expected of one's peers—or if it is done in a specific way, or in this place, or at that time—it will possess a special greatness in terms of the beautiful, the good, the just, or their opposites. This is the source of the famous inscription for the Olympic victor:

> Formerly, bearing on my shoulders a rough 
> ->yoke,<-
> I used to carry fish from Argos to Tegea.

Similarly, the general Iphicrates <note>An Athenian general who rose from humble beginnings; his father was famously a shoemaker.</note> used to praise himself by recounting the lowly state from which he began. 

Also, that which is **natural** <note>original: αὐτοφυὲς (*autophyes*)</note> is superior to that which is acquired, because it is more difficult to possess; hence the poet says, "I am self-taught" <note>Homer, *Odyssey* 22.347; the singer Phemius argues his talent is a divine, natural gift.</note>. 

Moreover, the **greatest part** of a great thing is itself particularly great; for example, Pericles, in his funeral oration, said that "the youth had been taken from the city just as if the spring were taken out of the year." 

Additionally, things are greater if they are useful in a time of **greater need**, such as those things needed in old age or during illness. Between two things, that which is **closer to the end** <note>original: τέλος (*telos*); the ultimate goal or purpose of an action.</note> is greater. That which is a good for the individual is greater than that which is a good in the abstract. Finally, that which is **possible**...

<summary>Aristotle describes how speakers can amplify the perceived importance of a topic through techniques like "division" (listing parts) and by highlighting the difficulty, rarity, or natural talent involved in an achievement.</summary>

--- Page 47 ---
<meta>This page continues Aristotle’s list of "common topics" for comparing the relative value of goods. Having discussed how things appear greater when divided into parts or when achieved under difficult circumstances, he now moves to the distinction between "truth" and "opinion," and the value of things that are unique or irreplaceable.</meta>

<page-num>34</page-num>
<header>ARISTOTLE</header>

...[it would be considered a sign of greatness if it were bordering] on the impossible; for one task might be impossible for a specific person, while another is not. Also, things that are at the **end of life**; for the ends are more significant than the things leading to the end. And things that pertain to **truth** rather than to **opinion**. The definition of what pertains to "opinion" is that which a person would not choose if it were going to remain hidden from others. Because of this, **receiving a benefit** would seem more desirable than **doing a benefit**; for a person will choose to receive a benefit even if it remains hidden, but it does not seem they would choose to do a benefit if it were to remain hidden <note>Aristotle is being pragmatic here: people often perform "noble" deeds for the reputation, but they want the rewards of a benefit regardless of whether anyone knows.</note>. Also, those things that people wish **to be** rather than merely **to seem**; for these relate more to truth. This is why people say that justice <term>δικαιοσύνη (*dikaiosyne*); the virtue of fairness and moral rightness</term> is of little value, because it is more desirable to *seem* just than to *be* just; however, the same is not said of health.

Also, that which is **useful for more things**: for instance, things useful for life, for living well, for pleasure, and for performing noble deeds. For this reason, wealth <term>πλοῦτος (*ploutos*)</term> and health <term>ὑγίεια (*hygieia*)</term> seem to be the greatest of goods, for they encompass all of these. Also, that which is **more free from pain**, and that which is **accompanied by pleasure**; for here there is more than one good—both the pleasure and the absence of pain are present. And of two things, that which makes the whole greater when added to the same thing is the superior good. And things which **do not escape notice** when they are present, rather than those which do; for these tend toward truth. For this reason, being wealthy would appear to be a greater good than merely appearing to be so.

And the "dearly beloved" <note>original: *agapeton* (ἀγαπητόν); something unique or irreplaceable that is cherished because there is only one of it.</note>; for some, a thing is their only possession, while for others it is one among many. For this reason, the penalty is not equal if one blinds a one-eyed man and a man with two eyes; for in the case of the former, the "dearly beloved" thing has been taken away. From what sources, then, one must draw proofs when **exhorting** or **deterring**, has now been largely explained.

### CHAPTER VIII.

The greatest and most sovereign of all things for the power to persuade and to give good advice is to grasp all the **forms of government** <term>πολιτείας (*politeias*); the constitutional structures or political systems of a city-state</term>, and to distinguish the habits, laws, and interests of each. For everyone is persuaded...

--- Page 48 ---
<meta>This page transitions from Aristotle’s general discussion of "the good" and "the greater good" to the specific political context of rhetoric. He begins to categorize different forms of government, as a speaker must understand the specific goals and character of their audience's political system to be persuasive.</meta>

<page-num>35</page-num>
<header>RHETORIC. BOOK I. CHAPTER VIII.</header>

...to the **expedient**; and that which preserves the constitution <term>πολιτεία (*politeia*); the form of government or political structure</term> is expedient.

Furthermore, the authority rests with the ruling power, and these ruling powers are distinguished according to the type of constitution. For there are as many ruling powers as there are forms of government. There are four such forms of government: **democracy**, **oligarchy**, **aristocracy**, and **monarchy**. Consequently, the ruling and deciding authority must be either a part of these or the whole of them.

**Democracy** is a constitution in which the offices are distributed by lot <note>In ancient Athens, democracy was defined by "the lot" (random selection) rather than voting, to ensure every citizen had an equal chance to serve.</note>. 

**Oligarchy** is one in which the offices are held by those with property qualifications.

**Aristocracy** is one in which the offices are held by those according to their education <term>παιδεία (*paideia*); the traditional system of upbringing and education</term>. By "education," I mean that which is established by the law; for those who have remained faithful to the legal institutions rule in an aristocracy. It is necessary that these people appear to be the "best" <note>The word "aristocracy" literally means "rule of the best" (*aristoi*).</note>, which is where the name is derived from.

**Monarchy**, as the name implies, is that in which one person is the master of all. Of these, the one that follows a certain order is a **kingship**; the one that is indefinite and unrestrained is a **tyranny**.

The **end** <term>τέλος (*telos*); the ultimate goal or purpose</term> of each constitution must not be overlooked; for people choose the things that lead to their specific end. 

*   The end of democracy is **liberty**. 
*   The end of oligarchy is **wealth**. 
*   The end of aristocracy is related to **education and legal institutions**. 
*   The end of tyranny is **self-protection** <note>original: φυλακή (*phylakē*); this can mean a "guard" or "security," implying the tyrant’s primary goal is the preservation of his own power.</note>.

It is clear, then, that we must distinguish the customs, legal institutions, and interests relative to the end of each, since people make their choices by referring to this end. 

However, since means of persuasion <term>πίστεις (*pisteis*)</term> are produced not only through demonstrative argument but also through **character** <term>ἦθος (*ethos*)</term>—for we trust the speaker if he appears to be of a certain character, namely, if he appears good, or well-disposed, or both—we must have a grasp of the characters of each constitution. For the character of each constitution is necessarily the most persuasive to that specific constitution. These characters will be grasped through the same means; for characters are made manifest through...

...their **deliberate choice** <term>προαίρεσις (*proairesis*); a purposeful, moral choice or intention</term>; and choice is always directed toward the end <note>The "end" (*telos*) refers to the ultimate goal or purpose of an action.</note>. We have now discussed the goals one should aim for when encouraging an audience—regarding things as they will be or as they currently are—and from what sources we should derive proofs concerning the **expedient**. Furthermore, we have covered the character and customs associated with various forms of government, and the means and ways we can find a wealth of arguments for them, at least as far as was appropriate for the present occasion. These matters have been precisely defined in my work on the *Politics* <note>Aristotle frequently cross-references his other works; here he points to his more detailed study of constitutions and civic life.</note>.

### CHAPTER IX.

After these things, let us speak about **virtue** and **vice**, and the **noble** and the **shameful**; for these are the targets for one who offers praise or blame. It will happen that while we speak of these, we will simultaneously reveal those things from which we ourselves will be perceived as having a certain character <term>ἦθος (*ethos*); the moral character or credibility of the speaker</term>, which we noted was the second kind of proof. From the same sources, we will be able to make both ourselves and others appear trustworthy in respect to virtue. 

Since it often happens that we praise—both casually and with great seriousness—not only a human being or a god, but also inanimate objects and even the first animal one might encounter, we must grasp the propositions concerning these in the same way. Therefore, for the sake of example, let us speak about these as well. 

The **noble** <term>καλόν (*kalon*); often translated as "the beautiful," "the noble," or "the honorable"</term>, then, is that which is desirable for its own sake and is praiseworthy; or that which, being good, is pleasant simply because it is good. If this is indeed what the noble is, then virtue must necessarily be noble; for virtue, being a good thing, is praiseworthy. 

**Virtue** is, as it seems, a power providing and preserving good things; it is also a power that is beneficial in many and great ways—indeed, beneficial to all people in all circumstances. The parts of virtue are **justice**, **courage**, **temperance**, **magnificence**, **magnanimity**, **liberality**, **gentleness**, **prudence**, and **wisdom**. It is necessary that the greatest virtues are those which are most useful to others...

--- Page 50 ---
<meta>This page continues the discussion on the nature of virtue (arete) and its various forms. The previous page ended mid-sentence while defining virtue as a productive power of goodness.</meta>

<page-num>37</page-num>
<header>RHETORIC. BOOK I. CHAPTER IX.</header>

...[most hon]orable <note>The Greek text begins with the suffix of the word *timiotatas* (τιμιωτάτας), meaning "most prized" or "most honored."</note>, if indeed **virtue** is a power to do good. For this reason, people honor the **just** and the **brave** most of all; for bravery is useful to others in war, and justice is useful both in war and in peace. Next is **liberality** <term>ἐλευθεριότης (*eleutheriotes*); the virtue of being generous with one's wealth</term>; for liberal people give freely and do not fight over money, which is the thing others desire most.

**Justice** is the virtue by which everyone possesses their own property according to the law; **injustice**, on the other hand, is that by which people possess what belongs to others, in defiance of the law. 

**Courage** <term>ἀνδρεία (*andreia*); literally "manliness" or bravery</term> is the virtue by which people perform noble deeds in the face of danger, as the law commands and in service to the law; **cowardice** is its opposite. 

**Temperance** <term>σωφροσύνη (*sophrosyne*); a sense of moderation or self-control</term> is the virtue by which people conduct themselves toward bodily pleasures as the law commands; **licentiousness** is the opposite. 

**Liberality** is the practice of doing good with money; **illiberality** is the opposite. 

**Magnanimity** <term>μεγαλοψυχία (*megalopsychia*); "greatness of soul"</term> is the virtue that produces great benefits for others; **smallness of soul** is the opposite. 

**Magnificence** <term>μεγαλοπρέπεια (*megaloprepeia*); the virtue of spending large sums of money appropriately on grand, noble causes</term> is the virtue that produces greatness in expenditures; **smallness of soul** and **meanness** <note>original: "μικροπρέπεια" (*mikroprepeia*), meaning "paltryness" or a lack of appropriate scale in spending</note> are its opposites. 

**Prudence** <term>φρόνησις (*phronesis*); practical wisdom or the ability to act rightly</term> is a virtue of the intellect, by which people are able to deliberate well concerning the goods and evils previously mentioned in relation to **happiness**. 

Concerning virtue and vice in general, and their specific parts, enough has been said for the present occasion. Regarding other things, it is not difficult to see the truth; for it is obvious that the things productive of virtue must be **noble**—since they tend toward virtue—as are the things that proceed from virtue. Such things include both the signs of virtue and the works of virtue. 

Since the signs and those things which are the works or experiences of a good person are noble, it necessarily follows that all works of courage, or signs of courage, or things done courageously, are noble. So too are just things, and...

<summary>Aristotle defines the primary virtues—including justice, courage, temperance, and magnanimity—explaining how each contributes to the common good and why they are deemed "noble." He establishes that any action or sign resulting from these virtues is itself a worthy subject for praise.</summary>

<meta>This page continues Aristotle’s exploration of what constitutes the "noble" (kalon) and the "virtuous" (arete), specifically focusing on actions that are done for the benefit of others or for the sake of honor rather than self-interest.</meta>

<page-num>38</page-num>
<header>ARISTOTLE</header>

...deeds done justly; but passive experiences are not. For in this virtue <note>referring to justice</note> alone, that which is done justly is not always noble <term>καλόν (*kalon*); a central Greek concept meaning both "beautiful" and "morally fine" or "noble"</term>; rather, in the case of being punished, it is more shameful to be punished justly than unjustly. The same applies to the other virtues. 

And those things for which the prizes are honor are noble. Also, those things for which honor is the reward rather than money. And whatever a person does not for their own sake among those things that are desirable. 

And things that are good in the absolute sense, such as whatever someone has done for the sake of their country, disregarding their own interest. And things that are good by nature. Also, things that are not good for the individual themselves; for such things <note>those that are only good for the individual</note> are done for one’s own sake. 

And those things which can belong to a person after death rather than while alive; for things belonging to the living are more likely for one’s own sake. And all deeds done for the sake of others; for these are less for oneself. And all successes achieved for others rather than oneself. Also, those done for people who have done good to us; for this is just. And acts of kindness; for they are not directed toward oneself. 

And the opposites of those things of which people are ashamed; for people are ashamed of shameful things, whether in saying, doing, or intending them. As Sappho has written, when Alcaeus said:

> ->I wish to say something, but shame prevents me<-
> ->Shame [holds me back]<-
> <note>original: *Thelo ti t' eipen, alla me kolyei Aidos*</note>
> S. <note>Sappho’s reply</note> If you had a desire for things good or noble,
> And if your tongue were not stirring up something evil to say,
> Shame would not cloud your eyes,
> But you would speak about what is right.

And those things about which people feel anxiety without being afraid; for they feel this way about things that lead to a good reputation. And the virtues of those who are by nature more excellent are themselves nobler, as are their deeds—for example, those of a man rather than those of a woman. And those virtues which provide enjoyment to others rather than to oneself; for this reason, justice and ju- <note>The text breaks mid-word, likely heading toward *dikaiosyne* (justice).</note>

--- Page 52 ---
<meta>This page continues Aristotle’s catalog of things considered "noble" (kalon), transitioning from acts of self-sacrifice to the social perceptions of virtue and the rhetorical techniques used to praise or blame a person by reframing their character traits.</meta>

<page-num>39</page-num>
<header>RHETORIC. BOOK I. CHAPTER IX. 39</header>

...justice is noble. It is also considered noble to take vengeance on one’s enemies rather than to be reconciled; for to return what is owed is just, and what is just is noble; and it belongs to a brave man never to be defeated. Both victory and honor are among things noble; for they are desirable even when they bear no fruit, and they manifest a superiority of virtue. Also noble are things worthy of remembrance—and the more they are remembered, the more noble they are. And those things which follow a person even after death. And those things which are followed by honor. Also, extraordinary things. And those things which belong to a person alone are more noble, for they are more easily remembered.

And "fruitless" possessions <note>meaning possessions that do not produce an income or profit, but exist for beauty or status</note> are noble, for they are more characteristic of a free man. Also, the customs that are distinctive to each people are considered noble. And whatever things are signs of the qualities praised by each people are noble; for example, in Lacedaemon <note>Sparta</note> it is noble to wear long hair, for it is a sign of a free man, since it is not easy for a person with long hair to perform any menial labor. And it is noble not to practice any mechanical trade <term>βάναυσος τέχνη (*banausos techne*); a term for manual labor or crafts which Greeks of high status believed degraded the mind and body</term>; for it is the mark of a free man not to live for the sake of another.

We must also assume that qualities closely related to existing ones are the same, for the purpose of both praise and blame; for example, calling the cautious man "cold" or "designing," the simpleton "good-hearted," or the insensitive man "mild." And we should always characterize each person by their accompanying traits in the best possible light: for instance, calling the irascible and the manic "straightforward," and the self-willed "magnificent" and "dignified." And those who exist in the extremes should be spoken of as if they possessed the virtues; for example, the rash man is called "brave," and the prodigal "liberal" <note>meaning generous with money</note>.

For this will seem true to the majority of people, and at the same time, it allows for a persuasive (though fallacious) inference from the cause: for if a man takes risks even when there is no necessity, he would seem much more likely to do so where it is noble; and if he is profuse to anyone he meets, he will surely be so to his friends, for to do good to everyone is an excess of virtue. We must also consider the audience in whose presence the praise is given; for as Socrates said <note>Aristotle refers to a famous saying attributed to Socrates: "It is not difficult to praise Athenians when among Athenians, but one must be an orator to praise them among Lacedaemonians."</note>...

<summary>Aristotle discusses the social markers of nobility—such as the Spartan custom of long hair—and provides a tactical guide for rhetoricians to "spin" character flaws into virtues during a speech.</summary>
<keywords>justice, vengeance, Spartan customs, mechanical trades, rhetorical reframing, Socrates, virtue, nobility</keywords>

--- Page 53 ---
<meta>This page continues Aristotle’s instructions on how to construct a speech of praise. The previous page discussed how to frame specific character traits (like caution or courage) to suit the orator's purpose. Here, Aristotle focuses on tailoring praise to a specific audience and the technical distinction between "praise" and "encomium."</meta>

<page-num>40</page-num>
<header>ARISTOTLE</header>

He <note>Referring to Socrates, as quoted in Plato’s *Menexenus*</note> used to say that it is not difficult to praise Athenians when speaking among Athenians. One must, however, speak of whatever is held in honor by a particular audience as though it were actually present in the person being praised—whether the audience be Scythians, Spartans, or philosophers. And generally, one should categorize what is "honored" as something "noble," since the two appear to be close neighbors.

One should also emphasize actions that are fitting; for instance, if they are worthy of the subject's ancestors or their own previous achievements. For it is conducive to happiness and noble to acquire additional honor. One might also praise someone for acting beyond what is expected in a way that is better and more noble; for instance, if a person is moderate when they are fortunate, or great-souled <note>meaning someone with a "large soul" who acts with dignity and high purpose</note> when they are unfortunate; or if, as they grow in power, they become better and more inclined toward reconciliation. Such was the sentiment of Iphicrates <note>A famous Athenian general of humble birth</note> when he spoke of "from what beginnings to what heights I have come," and that of the Olympic victor:

-> "Formerly, bearing on my shoulders a rough [yoke]..." <note>original: "Πρόσθε μὲν ἀμφ’ ὤμοισιν ἔχων τραχεῖαν..." From an inscription for a victor who was previously a common laborer.</note> <-

and that of Simonides:

-> "She who was the daughter, wife, and sister of tyrants." <note>original: "Ἢ πατρός τε καὶ ἀνδρὸς ἀδελφῶν τ’ οὖσα τυράννων." Referring to Archedice, who despite her powerful family, remained modest.</note> <-

Since praise is derived from actions, and it is the hallmark of a person of high character to act according to a deliberate purpose, one must try to show that the subject is acting with such purpose. It is also useful if they appear to have performed these noble acts many times. Therefore, even accidents and matters of chance should be framed as results of deliberate purpose; for if many similar instances can be cited, they will be taken as a sign of virtue and intent.

Praise is a speech that makes clear the greatness of a virtue. It is therefore necessary to demonstrate that the subject's actions are of a virtuous kind. The "encomium" <term>encomium (ἐγκώμιον); a formal speech of praise directed at specific deeds or achievements, rather than general character</term>, however, is concerned with actual deeds. Circumstantial factors serve to strengthen our belief; for example, noble birth and education. For it is likely that a good man comes from good parents, and that one so brought up will be of a certain character. This is why we deliver encomiums for those who have already achieved something. Their deeds are signs of their underlying disposition; indeed, we would praise even a person who has not yet acted, if we believed them to be of such a character.

<meta>catchword: Ma-</meta>

<summary>Aristotle explains how to adapt praise to the values of a specific audience and distinguishes between praise (focused on virtue) and encomium (focused on deeds).</summary>
<keywords>praise, encomium, audience adaptation, virtue, Iphicrates, Simonides, deliberate purpose, Greek rhetoric</keywords>

--- Page 54 ---
<meta>This page continues Aristotle’s discussion on the mechanics of praise. Having established that praise should be based on what an audience values, Aristotle now explains the technical relationship between advice and praise, and introduces the concept of "amplification" to make a subject appear even more virtuous.</meta>

<page-num>41</page-num>
<header>RHETORIC, BOOK I, CHAPTER IX</header>

Blessing <note>original: *makarismos*</note> and calling one happy <note>original: *eudaimonismos*</note> are the same as each other, but they are not the same as praise or encomium. Rather, just as happiness <note>original: *eudaimonia*</note> encompasses virtue, the act of calling someone happy encompasses these other forms of praise.

Praise and **advice** share a common form. For the things you would suggest in the act of giving advice become speeches of praise <note>original: *enkomia*</note> when the wording is changed. Since we already know what a person ought to do and what kind of character they should have, we only need to take these suggestions and turn or transform the language. For example, if the advice is: "One should not take pride in things that come from luck, but in things achieved by one’s own effort."

Stated in this way, it functions as a **suggestion**; but stated like this, it becomes **praise**: "He takes pride not in things that come to him by luck, but in those achieved by his own effort." Therefore, whenever you wish to praise, look at what you would suggest as advice; and when you wish to offer advice, look at what you would praise. The phrasing will necessarily be the opposite when you transform a prohibition <note>a "thou shalt not"</note> into a statement of fact.

One should also use many techniques of <term>amplification</term> <note>original: *auxēsis*; the rhetorical art of making something seem greater or more significant</note>. For instance, point out if the person was the "only one," or the "first," or "one of a few" to do something, or if they achieved it to the "greatest degree"; for all these things are considered noble. 

Consider also the timing and the specific circumstances; these should be shown as going beyond what is merely expected. Note if they have succeeded in the same noble task many times; for this makes the achievement seem great and suggests it was not due to luck, but due to the person’s own character. 

Praise the subject if incentives or honors were invented and established because of them; or if they were the first person for whom a formal speech of praise was composed. For example, the first encomium was for Hippolochus <note>A figure from Greek history or myth, often associated with early honors</note>, and the first to have a statue set up in the marketplace <note>The Athenian Agora</note> were **Harmodius** and **Aristogeiton** <note>Famous Athenian heroes who were honored for their attempt to overthrow the tyrants</note>. 

The same applies to the opposite traits. If you find yourself lacking material regarding the person themselves, you should compare them to others; this is what **Isocrates** <note>A famous Athenian teacher of rhetoric</note> used to do because of his familiarity with the style of legal pleading. One should compare the subject to famous and distinguished people; for it is an **amplification** and a noble thing if the subject is shown to be even better than those who are already considered virtuous. It is logical that such amplification...

<summary>Aristotle explains that praise is essentially "advice" reframed as an accomplished fact and introduces "amplification" as a tool to elevate a person's deeds by comparing them to others or noting their uniqueness.</summary>
<keywords>praise vs advice, amplification, auxēsis, Hippolochus, Harmodius and Aristogeiton, Isocrates, rhetorical comparison</keywords>

--- Page 55 ---
<meta>This page concludes the study of epideictic (demonstrative) rhetoric—the art of praise and blame—and begins the investigation into judicial rhetoric, which deals with justice, injustice, and the law.</meta>

<page-num>42</page-num>
<header>ARISTOTLE</header>

...leads to praise; for superiority <note>original: *hyperochē*</note> is an excellence, and superiority is one of the "noble things." Therefore, one should compare the subject with others—if not with famous people, then with the rest of the world—since superiority is seen as a sign of virtue.

Generally, regarding the rhetorical tools common to all types of speeches, **amplification** <note>original: *auxēsis*</note> is most suitable for <term>epideictic</term> <note>ceremonial oratory that praises or blames someone</note> speeches. In these, the speaker takes the actions as being already agreed upon; the remaining task is simply to invest them with greatness and beauty.

**Examples** <note>original: *paradeigmata*</note> are best for <term>deliberative</term> <note>political oratory used to advise or persuade an assembly about future actions</note> speeches, for we judge what is to come by predicting it from what has already happened.

**Enthymemes** <note>original: *enthymēmata*; logical arguments or "rhetorical syllogisms" that lead the audience to a conclusion</note> are best for <term>judicial</term> <note>legal oratory used for prosecution or defense in a court of law</note> speeches. Because a past event is often unclear, it most requires a search for its cause and a logical proof of the facts.

This essentially covers all the sources from which praise and blame are drawn, the qualities we should look for when praising or blaming, and the basis upon which encomiums and reproaches are built. Once these are understood, their opposites become clear, for blame is derived from the opposite qualities.

### CHAPTER X.

Next, we should discuss **prosecution** and **defense**: from how many and what kind of premises their logical arguments should be built. We must grasp three things: first, the motives and the number of reasons why people commit injustice; second, their own state of mind; and third, the character and circumstances of those they treat unjustly.

Let us begin by defining "doing injustice." Let it be defined as: harming someone voluntarily <note>original: *hekontōn*</note> and contrary to the law. Now, "law" is of two kinds: **particular** and **common**. By "particular" law <note>original: *idios*</note>, I mean the written law under which a specific community is governed; by "common" law <note>original: *koinos*</note>, I mean those unwritten principles that are thought to be acknowledged by all people everywhere.

People act "voluntarily" when they act with knowledge of what they are doing and are not being forced to do it. While all things done voluntarily are not necessarily...

<summary>Aristotle concludes his guide on praise by identifying which rhetorical tools (amplification, examples, enthymemes) best suit different types of speeches, then transitions to defining legal injustice and the distinction between written and universal law.</summary>

<keywords>rhetoric, epideictic, deliberative, judicial, amplification, enthymemes, injustice, written law, common law, voluntary action</keywords>

--- Page 56 ---
<meta>This page continues Chapter X of Book I, moving from the definition of injustice to the specific motives and character flaws that lead individuals to commit wrongful acts. It begins to categorize the causes of human action.</meta>

<page-num>43</page-num>
<header>RHETORICA. LIB. I. CAP. X.</header>

...knowingly. And all things done by choice are done knowingly; for no one is ignorant of what they choose to do. The reasons why people choose to do harm and commit wicked acts contrary to the law are **vice** <note>original: *kakia*</note> and **lack of self-control** <note>original: *akrasia*; the state of acting against one's better judgment through weakness of will</note>. For if people possess a depravity <note>original: *mochthēria*</note>, whether of one kind or many, they are unjust in regard to that specific area where they happen to be depraved.

For example:
*   The **illiberal man** <note>original: *aneleutheros*; one who is stingy or lacks generosity</note> is unjust regarding money.
*   The **licentious man** <note>original: *akolastos*; one who lacks restraint</note> is unjust regarding bodily pleasures.
*   The **soft man** <note>original: *malakos*; someone characterized by laziness or an inability to endure discomfort</note> regarding ease.
*   The **coward** regarding dangers, for they abandon those facing danger with them out of fear.
*   The **ambitious man** for the sake of honor.
*   The **quick-tempered man** through anger.
*   The **lover of victory** for the sake of winning.
*   The **embittered man** for the sake of revenge.
*   The **foolish man** because he is deceived about what is just and unjust.
*   The **shameless man** through a disregard for reputation.

Likewise, each of the others is unjust regarding their respective subjects.

But concerning these matters, they are clear partly from what has been said about the virtues, and partly from what will be said about the **passions** <note>original: *pathē*; the emotions or sufferings that influence judgment</note>. It remains to state for what reasons, in what state of mind people do wrong, and to whom they do it.

First, then, let us distinguish what things we desire and what things we avoid when we attempt to do wrong. For it is clear that the **prosecutor** <note>original: *katēgorounti*</note> must consider how many and what kind of these motives exist in the opponent—motives which, when aimed at, cause everyone to wrong their neighbors—while the **defender** <note>original: *apologoumenō*</note> must consider which and how many of these motives do not exist.

Now, all people do all things either not because of themselves, or because of themselves. Of those things not done because of themselves, some they do by **chance** <note>original: *tychē*</note>, others by **necessity** <note>original: *anankē*</note>. Of those done by necessity, some are by **compulsion** <note>original: *bia*</note>, others by **nature** <note>original: *physis*</note>. So that all things they do not because of themselves are either from chance, or from nature, or from compulsion. But those things done...

<note>Aristotle is beginning his famous list of the seven causes of human action: chance, nature, compulsion, habit, reason, anger, and desire.</note>

themselves, and those things of which they themselves are the causes, some are due to **habit** <note>original: *ethos*; a settled or regular tendency or practice</note>; others,

So it follows that all things people do, they must

To further divide these actions according to age, or **states of character** <note>original: *hexeis*</note>, or other such factors

young people are prone to anger or longing, they do not act
the way they do because of their **youth**; rather, they act through anger and
longing. Nor is it because of wealth and poverty; rather, it
happens that the poor, because of their lack, long for 
money, and the wealthy, because of their power, long for
non-essential pleasures. But these people too will act
not because of wealth or poverty, but because of longing.
Similarly, both the just and the unjust, and all others 
said to act according to their states of character, will act because of all
these causes: either because of reason or because of passion; but
some do so through good habits and passions, while others do so through the opposite.
It happens, however, that such consequences follow 
from such states of character, and different ones from others; 
for perhaps it follows immediately that for the **temperate** man <note>original: *sōphrōn*; someone who is self-controlled and moderate</note>, because of his temperance, good
opinions and longings regarding pleasures follow; 
but to the **licentious** man <note>original: *akolastos*</note>, the opposites follow regarding these
same things. Therefore, such divisions must be set aside; 
one should instead examine what kinds of things usually follow from what; 
for if a person is white or black, or tall or short, nothing is fixed
as following from such traits; but if someone is young or old, or just...

--- Page 58 ---
<meta>This page continues Aristotle’s breakdown of the seven causes of human action. On the previous page, he established that individual character and circumstances like wealth or poverty are secondary to the primary psychological and external drivers of behavior. Here, he provides formal definitions for each of the seven causes: chance, nature, compulsion, habit, reason, anger, and longing.</meta>

# 45

### RHETORIC. BOOK I. CHAPTER X.

...whether a person is just or unjust, it already makes a difference. And in general, whatever circumstances cause the characters of men to differ; for example, whether one is thought to be wealthy or poor will make some difference, or being unlucky or lucky. We shall speak of these things later <note>Aristotle discusses the types of character related to fortune and age in Book II, Chapters 12–17</note>; for now, let us speak first about the remaining causes. 

Actions due to **chance** <note>original: *tychē*</note> are those whose cause is indefinite and which occur for no specific purpose, and neither always, nor for the most part, nor in an orderly way. This is clear from the definition of chance concerning these matters. 

Actions due to **nature** <note>original: *physis*</note> are those whose cause is within the things themselves and is orderly; for they result in the same way either always or for the most part. As for things contrary to nature, there is no need to examine precisely whether they occur according to some kind of nature or some other cause. Indeed, chance might also seem to be the cause of such things. 

Actions due to **compulsion** <note>original: *bia*</note> are those which occur contrary to the desire or the rational calculations of the agents themselves, yet are performed by those agents. 

Actions due to **habit** <note>original: *ethos*</note> are those which people do because they have done them many times before. 

Actions due to **reason** <note>original: *logismos*</note> are those that seem to be beneficial among the "goods" previously mentioned—either as an **end** <note>original: *telos*; the ultimate goal of an action</note> or as a means to an end—whenever the action is performed for the sake of utility. For even the **intemperate** <note>original: *akolastoi*; those who cannot restrain their physical impulses</note> perform some beneficial actions, but they do them not for the sake of utility, but for the sake of pleasure. 

Actions due to **anger** <note>original: *thymos*</note> and **wrath** <note>original: *orgē*</note> are those aimed at revenge. There is a difference between **retribution** <note>original: *timōria*</note> and **punishment** <note>original: *kolasis*</note>; for punishment is for the sake of the one suffering it <note>intended to reform the offender</note>, whereas retribution is for the sake of the one inflicting it, so that he may be satisfied. Regarding what things anger is directed at, this will be clear in our discussion of the passions <note>Book II, Chapter 2</note>. 

Actions due to **longing** <note>original: *epithymia*</note> are those that appear pleasant. What is familiar and what has become a habit is also among those things that are pleasant; for many things which are not pleasant by nature are done with pleasure once people have become accustomed to them. 

So, to sum up, everything that people do of their own accord is either good, or what appears to be good, or...

<vocab>Aristotle, Rhetoric, chance, nature, compulsion, habit, reason, anger, wrath, longing, retribution, punishment, pleasure</vocab>

<summary>Aristotle defines the seven internal and external causes of human action, notably distinguishing between the reformative nature of punishment and the self-serving nature of retribution. He concludes that all voluntary actions are motivated by the pursuit of what is perceived as good or pleasant.</summary>
