<meta>This page continues the discussion from Chapter X regarding the motives for human action. Having defined the seven causes—chance, nature, compulsion, habit, reason, anger, and longing—Aristotle now focuses on the "pleasant" and the "advantageous" as the primary goals of voluntary action. This leads directly into Chapter XI, where he provides a formal psychological definition of pleasure.</meta>

ARISTOTLE

46

...pleasant things or things that appear pleasant. Since whatever people do through themselves, they do voluntarily; and whatever they do not do through themselves, they do involuntarily; then all things done voluntarily must be either good things or apparent good things, or pleasant things or apparent pleasant things. For I also categorize the escape from evils—or from apparent evils—or the exchange of a greater evil for a smaller one, as belonging to the **good** <note>Aristotle argues that even if something is technically "bad," if it replaces something worse, we perceive it as a benefit</note>; for these are in some sense desirable. Similarly, I categorize the escape from pains—or from apparent pains—or the exchange of greater pains for smaller ones, among **pleasant** things. 

We must, therefore, determine how many and what kind of things are advantageous and pleasant. Now, the **advantageous** <note>original: *sympheronta*—the beneficial or useful</note> has been discussed previously in the section on deliberative oratory <note>Referring to Book I, Chapter 6, which deals with the aims of political and advisory speech</note>; let us now speak about the **pleasant**. We should consider our definitions sufficient if they are neither obscure nor overly detailed for each subject.

### CHAPTER XI.

Let us assume that **pleasure** <note>original: *hēdonē*</note> is a certain movement of the soul, and a sudden, perceptible settling into its natural state; and **pain** <note>original: *lypē*</note> is the opposite. If pleasure is indeed such a thing, it is clear that what is "pleasant" is that which produces the aforementioned state; while that which is destructive of it, or produces the opposite state, is "painful." 

It follows then that it is pleasant to move toward a state that is according to nature—at least for the most part—and especially when those things that happen according to nature have fully recovered their own natural condition. **Habits** <note>original: *ethē*</note> are also pleasant; for that which has become habitual is now like something natural. Indeed, habit is quite similar to nature; for "often" is very close to "always." While nature belongs to the "always," habit belongs to the "often." Also, that which is not forced is pleasant; for force is contrary to nature. Therefore, necessities are painful; and it has been rightly said:

<summary>Aristotle concludes his analysis of voluntary action by defining pleasure as a restoration to a natural state and pain as its opposite. He argues that habits are pleasant because they mimic nature through repetition, whereas compulsion is inherently painful.</summary>
<keywords>pleasure (hedone), pain (lype), natural state (physis), habit (ethos), voluntary action, psychology of rhetoric</keywords>

--- Page 60 ---
<meta>This page continues the exploration of pleasure (hēdonē), transitioning from the formal definition of pleasure as a "natural settling of the soul" to the specific things that humans find pleasant. Aristotle distinguishes between natural, bodily desires and those formed through reason or persuasion, ultimately linking pleasure to the human capacity for memory and hope.</meta>

RHETORIC. BOOK I. CHAPTER XI.

47

> For everything necessary is by nature painful. <note>original: "Πᾶν γὰρ ἀναγκαῖον πράγμ’ ἀνιαρὸν ἔφυ." A line from the poet Evenus of Paros, suggesting that compulsion is the enemy of pleasure.</note>

Thus, acts of care, effort, and intense concentration are painful; for these things involve necessity and force, unless they have become matters of habit. In that case, **habit** <note>original: *ethos*</note> makes them pleasant. Their opposites are likewise pleasant. For this reason, ease, the absence of toil, carelessness, play, rest, and sleep are among the pleasant things, for none of these are done under the pressure of necessity.

Whatever we desire is also pleasant, for **desire** <note>original: *epithymia*</note> is a reaching out for the pleasant. Desires are either **irrational** or **rational** <note>literally "without reason" (*alogoi*) and "with reason" (*meta logou*)</note>. By "irrational," I mean those desires that do not arise from a particular mental conception or opinion. These include the desires said to be "natural," such as those that arise through the body: for example, the desire for food—thirst and hunger—and the desire for every specific kind of nourishment. This category also includes the desires related to taste, sexual matters, and touch in general, as well as the senses of smell, hearing, and sight.

By "rational" desires, I mean those things people desire because they have been **persuaded**. For there are many things people long to see or possess because they have heard about them and been convinced of their value.

Since feeling pleasure consists in the perception of a certain state, and since **imagination** <note>original: *phantasia*—which Aristotle defines here as a "weak perception"</note> is a kind of faint perception, then for a person who is either remembering the past or hoping for the future, there must follow an "imagination" of the thing they remember or hope for. If this is true, it is clear that there are great pleasures for those who remember and those who hope, because perception is involved in both.

Consequently, all pleasant things must be found in one of three states: in the **present** through perception, in the **past** through memory, or in the **future** through hope. We perceive what is present, we remember what has happened, and we hope for what is to come. Now, things stored in the memory are pleasant, and this is true not only for things that were pleasant at the time they were actually present...

<summary>Aristotle explains that while effort and necessity are painful, habit and leisure are pleasant. He categorizes desires into natural bodily urges and those informed by reason, concluding that pleasure is derived from perception in the present, memory of the past, and hope for the future.</summary>

<keywords>pleasure, desire, necessity, habit, irrational desire, rational desire, imagination, memory, hope, perception</keywords>

<meta>This page continues the discussion from page 47 regarding the nature of pleasure. Aristotle is explaining that because pleasure involves a "perception" of one's state, and memory and hope are forms of perception, we can find pleasure in things that are not currently happening.</meta>

# ARISTOTLE

...were, but even some things that were not pleasant <note>Aristotle is completing a thought from the previous page: we enjoy remembering things not just as they were, but for what they led to.</note> become so if the consequence is later noble and good. Hence the saying:

-> But it is sweet, once saved, to remember one’s toils. <-
<note>original: "Ἀλλ᾽ ἡδύ τοι, σωθέντα μεμνῆσθαι πόνων." This is a famous line from Euripides' lost play, *Andromeda*.</note>

And,

-> For even in his sorrows a man finds delight, <-
-> Remembering all he has suffered and all he has done. <-
<note>original: Homer, *Odyssey* 15.400-401. Aristotle uses these classic epic verses to show that even past pain can be transformed into a present pleasure through the act of storytelling and memory.</note>

The reason for this is that it is pleasant even to simply **not possess an evil**. Things we "possess" in **hope** <note>original: *elpis*</note> are those which, when present, appear to bring great joy or benefit—or at least benefit without pain. In general, whatever brings joy when it is actually present also brings joy to those who remember it or hope for it, for the most part.

For this reason, even **getting angry** is pleasant; just as Homer wrote concerning the "spirit" or "wrath":

> Which is far sweeter than honey dripping down;
<note>original: *Iliad* 18.109. Aristotle argues that anger is pleasant because it is accompanied by the hope of revenge.</note>

For no one gets angry at someone who seems impossible to punish, nor do people get angry—or they get less angry—at those who are far above them in power. 

Furthermore, a certain pleasure follows most **desires** <note>original: *epithymiai*</note>. For people feel a kind of joy either by remembering how they succeeded in the past or by hoping they will succeed in the future. For example, those suffering from thirst in a fever find joy both in remembering how they once drank and in the hope that they will drink again. 

**Lovers**, too, find joy in talking, writing, and always doing something concerning the beloved; for in all such activities, by remembering, they feel as though they are perceiving the beloved. Indeed, this is the beginning of **love** <note>original: *eros*</note> for everyone: when they find joy not only in the beloved's presence, but also when they love by remembering them in their absence. This is why even when the absence becomes painful, a certain pleasure still arises even in **mourning** and **laments**. For while the grief exists because the person is no longer there, the pleasure exists in the...

<summary>Aristotle argues that memory and hope are inherently pleasant because they allow us to "perceive" past successes or future benefits, even finding sweetness in past toils, anger, and the remembrance of a beloved.</summary>
<keywords>memory, hope, anger, revenge, love, eros, grief, Homer, Euripides, pleasure</keywords>

<meta>This page continues Aristotle's analysis of what humans find pleasurable. Having established that memory and hope are forms of "perception" that bring joy, he now concludes a thought on why we find pleasure in mourning and moves into the psychological gratifications of revenge, victory, and social status.</meta>

# RHETORIC. BOOK I. CHAPTER XI.

...to remember and somehow see that person—both what they did and the kind of person they were. Therefore, this too has been said:

> Thus he spoke; and in all of them he stirred a desire for mourning.
<note>original: "Ὣς φάτο· τοῖσι δὲ πᾶσιν ἐφ’ ἵμερον ὦρσε γόοιο." A recurring line in Homer (e.g., *Iliad* 23.108), suggesting that even grief can be "desired" because it allows us to vividly recall a loved one.</note>

And **taking revenge** is pleasant; for where failing to attain something is painful, attaining it is pleasant. Those who are angry suffer immeasurable pain when they cannot take revenge, but they rejoice in the **hope** of it. 

And **victory** is pleasant, not only for those who love to win, but for everyone; for an impression of **superiority** arises, which all people desire, whether slightly or more strongly. Since victory is pleasant, it follows that **games** must also be pleasant—specifically those involving combat, music <note>original: *aulētikis*, referring to flute-playing or pipe-playing competitions</note>, and verbal dispute; for in these, victory often occurs. This includes games of knucklebones, ball games, dice, and draughts <note>original: *petteia*, a strategy board game similar to checkers or chess</note>. 

The same applies to more serious pursuits; some become pleasant if one is accustomed to them, while others are pleasant immediately, such as hunting and every kind of chase; for wherever there is a contest, there is also victory. Therefore, even legal practice and debating are pleasant to those who are accustomed to them and have the ability for them.

And **honor** and a **good reputation** are among the most pleasant things, because an impression is created for each individual that they are a person of a certain high character <note>original: *spoudaios*, meaning excellent or of serious moral worth</note>—especially when this is said by those whom they believe to be truthful. 

Such people include those close to us rather than those far away; friends, acquaintances, and fellow citizens rather than strangers; those living now rather than those in the future; the wise rather than the foolish; and many people rather than few. For it is more likely that the former speak the truth than the latter. After all, if someone greatly despises others—such as children or animals—they care nothing for their honor or their opinion for the sake of the opinion itself; or if they do care, it is for some other reason <note>e.g., if the opinion of a child might lead to a practical consequence, rather than being a source of pride in itself</note>.

And a **friend** is among the pleasant things; for the act of loving <note>original: *philein*</note> is pleasant; for no one...

-> ARISTOTLE'S RHETORIC. BOOK D <-
<note>The 'D' likely refers to a specific quire or section of the manuscript/printed edition.</note>

<summary>Aristotle explains why revenge, victory, and games are pleasant, linking them to the human desire for superiority. He also discusses how social honor provides pleasure, provided it comes from people we respect and trust.</summary>

# ARISTOTLE

...wine-lover <note>original: *philoinos*. This is likely an error in the source manuscript or transcription; the context of the *Rhetoric* (1371a) usually refers here to the "honor-lover" (*philotimos*), continuing from the previous page's discussion on reputation.</note>, he who does not enjoy wine; and **being loved** is pleasant. For here, too, an impression <term>phantasia: a mental image or "appearance" in the mind</term> exists of possessing the "good," which is something all sentient beings desire. To be loved is to be cherished for one's own sake.

And **being admired** is pleasant, because of the honor it brings. And **being flattered**, as well as the **flatterer**, is pleasant; for the flatterer is an apparent admirer and an apparent friend.

And **doing the same things often** is pleasant; for we have established that what is **habitual** is pleasant. And **change** is pleasant; for change is a movement toward one's natural state; for doing the same thing always creates an excess of one's established condition. Hence it has been said:

---
->— — Change in all things is sweet. — —<-
---
<note>original: *Metabolē pantōn glyky*. A quote from Euripides' play *Orestes*, line 234.</note>

For this reason, things encountered after an interval of time are pleasant—whether they are people or objects; for it is a change from the present, and moreover, what occurs only after a time is rare.

And **learning** and **wondering** <note>original: *thaumazein*, the sense of awe or "wonder" that Aristotle elsewhere calls the beginning of philosophy.</note> are generally pleasant; for in wondering, there is the **desire** [to learn], so that what is "wonderful" is an object of desire; and in learning, one is restored to their natural state of being. 

And **doing good** and **receiving good** are among the pleasant things. To receive good is to attain what one desires; but to do good is to possess [the means] and to be **superior**, both of which are things people strive for. Because being a benefactor is pleasant, it is also pleasant for people to set their neighbors right and to complete what is lacking.

Since learning and wondering are pleasant, it follows that **imitations** <term>mimēma: a representation or copy of reality, the root of 'mimetic'</term> must also be pleasant—such as painting, sculpture, and poetry—and everything that is well-imitated, even if the object of the imitation is not itself pleasant. For the pleasure does not come from the object itself, but rather...

<summary>Aristotle explains why humans find pleasure in being loved, the variety of change, and the satisfaction of curiosity. He introduces the famous concept that we enjoy "imitations" (like art or drama) because they allow us to learn and recognize things, even if the subject matter is difficult.</summary>

# RHETORIC. BOOK I. CHAPTER XI.

...[a] **syllogism** <term>syllogismos: a logical conclusion where one thing is inferred from others</term> is, that "this is that"; so that it happens that one **learns** something. And **reversals of fortune** <term>peripeteiai: sudden changes in circumstances, often used to describe plot twists in drama</term>, and **narrowly being saved** from dangers are pleasant; for all these things are wonderful.

And since what is **natural** is pleasant, and things that are **akin** are naturally suited to each other, all things akin and similar are pleasant, as a rule; for instance, man to man, horse to horse, and youth to youth. Hence the proverbs were spoken, such as "peer delights peer" <note>original: *hēlix hēlika terpei*</note>; and "like always [seeks like]" <note>original: *aiei ton homoion*</note>; and "beast knows beast"; and "always jackdaw near jackdaw" <note>Equivalent to the English idiom "birds of a feather flock together."</note>; and all other such sayings.

Since everything similar and akin is pleasant to itself, and since each person feels this most of all toward **himself**, it is necessary that all people are **self-lovers** <term>philautoi: those who love themselves; used here as a psychological observation of human nature</term>, either more or less; for all such feelings exist primarily toward one’s self. And since all are self-lovers, their own things must also be pleasant to all—for example, their works and their words. 

This is why people are, for the most part, **fond of flatterers**, and **fond of being loved**, and **fond of honor**, and **fond of their children**; for children are their own works. And to **complete what is lacking** is pleasant; for it then becomes one's own work. 

And since **ruling** is most pleasant, so also to **appear wise** is pleasant; for to be prudent <note>original: *phronein*, refers to practical wisdom or the intelligence required for leadership</note> is a quality of a leader; and wisdom is the knowledge of many wonderful things. Furthermore, since people are generally **lovers of honor** <note>original: *philotimoi*</note>, it follows that **criticizing one's neighbors** is pleasant. And it is pleasant for a person to spend time on that in which he thinks himself best, just as Euripides says:

->And to this he hastens,<-
->Allotting to it the greatest part of each day,<-
->So that he may find himself at his very best.<-
<note>This is a fragment from Euripides' lost play, *Antiope*.</note>

In the same way, since **play** is among the pleasant things, and all **relaxation**, then **laughter** is also among the pleasant things. It is also necessary that...

ARISTOTLE

...**funny things** <term>geloia: the ridiculous or humorous</term> are pleasant—including people, words, and deeds. The subject of the **ridiculous** has been defined separately in my work **On Poetics** <note>original: *Poietikēs*. Aristotle’s *Poetics* is famous for its analysis of tragedy; a second book on comedy and the "ridiculous" is believed to have existed but is now lost to history.</note>. Let this suffice as our discussion of **pleasant things**; as for **painful things**, they will be clearly understood as the opposites of these. These, then, are the motives for which people commit **wrongdoing** <term>adikia: acts of injustice or harm against the law or moral code</term>.

## CHAPTER XII.

Let us now discuss the **state of mind** of those who do wrong, and whom they target. People commit crimes when they think the act is **possible** to achieve, and specifically possible for **themselves**. They act if they believe they can escape notice, or if caught, avoid **punishment** <term>dikē: legal penalty or the judgment of a court</term>. They may also act if they expect to pay a penalty, but believe the loss will be **less than the profit** gained for themselves or those they care for.

What sorts of things appear possible or impossible will be discussed later <note>Aristotle refers here to a later section in Book II, where he discusses "the possible and impossible" as general lines of argument.</note>; for these concepts are common to all branches of rhetoric. People think they are best able to commit crimes with **impunity** when they are **skilled speakers**, men of action, or have experience in many **legal contests**. They also feel confident if they have **many friends** or are **wealthy**.

They believe they have the power to do wrong especially if they possess these traits themselves; but if not, then if they have friends, servants, or **partners** of this kind. Through these resources, they are able to carry out the act, remain hidden, and avoid the penalty. They also act if they are **friends with the victims** or with the **judges**. Friends are **off their guard** <term>aphylaktoi: unprotected or not watching out</term> against being wronged, and they often reconcile before the matter goes to trial. Judges, for their part, show favor to their friends, either acquitting them entirely or imposing only small fines.

Those who are **least likely to be suspected** are those whose physical traits or status are the opposite of the charges: for example, a **weak man** accused of assault, or a **poor and ugly man** accused of **adultery**. Also, crimes committed in **plain sight** and before everyone’s eyes are often successful; they are left **unguarded** because no one would imagine anyone would be so bold. And crimes of such...

<summary>Aristotle concludes his analysis of pleasure by noting that humor is enjoyable, then transitions to Chapter XII to examine the psychology of criminals, specifically how power, wealth, and social connections embolden individuals to commit injustice with the hope of escaping punishment.</summary>

<meta>This page continues Chapter XII of Book I, where Aristotle is categorizing the specific conditions and types of people who believe they can commit crimes or acts of injustice (adikēmata) with impunity. He is listing the strategic and psychological reasons an individual might decide that the benefits of a "wrongdoing" outweigh the risks.</meta>

### RHETORIC. BOOK I. CHAPTER XII.

...such things, and those of a kind that no one has ever done before; for these are also **unguarded**. People generally guard against habitual **wrongdoings** <term>adikēmata: acts of injustice or crimes</term>, just as they do against common illnesses; but no one is on guard against an ailment that no one has ever suffered from before. 

Criminals also target those who have **no enemies**, or those who have **many**. In the first case, the wrongdoers think they will escape notice because the victim is not on their guard; in the second, they escape notice because it seems unlikely they would attempt a crime against people who are already alert and watchful, and because they have a ready **defense** <note>original: *apologian*. This suggests the criminal can argue that they wouldn't be so foolish as to strike someone who is already expecting trouble.</note>—that they simply would not have tried it.

They also act when they have a ready **means of concealment**, a specific method, a certain place, or a convenient disposition for the task. They are emboldened when, if they fail to remain hidden, they can achieve a **dismissal of the case** <term>diōsis: a legal pushing-away or rejection of a lawsuit</term>, or a long delay, or the **corruption of the judges**. Also, if a fine is imposed, they act if they can evade the payment, or delay it for a long time, or if they are so poor they have nothing to lose. 

They are also moved to act when the **profits** are clear, large, or immediate, while the **losses** are small, obscure, or far in the future. This is also true when the **punishment** <term>timōria: retribution or legal penalty</term> is not equal to the benefit—which is how **tyranny** <term>tyrannis: rule by an absolute dictator who is above the law</term> is perceived to be. 

Further, they act if the crimes result in **actual gain**, while the penalties are merely a matter of **disgrace**. Conversely, some act when the crimes lead to a kind of **praise**—for instance, if the act happened to involve avenging a father or mother, as was the case with **Zeno** <note>original: *Zēnōni*. This likely refers to Zeno of Elea, who famously endured torture or took risks to oppose a tyrant, though the exact anecdote Aristotle has in mind regarding his parents is debated by scholars.</note>—while the penalties involve only money, exile, or something of that nature. Both types of people commit wrongs, but they have opposite characters; one values profit, the other values honor.

People also act if they have **often escaped notice** or have never been punished, or if they have **failed many times** before. For in such matters, there are those—like soldiers in war—who are ready to **renew the struggle**. 

Finally, people act when the **pleasure** is immediate and the **pain** comes later, or the profit is immediate and the loss is delayed. This is the hallmark of the **incontinent** <term>akrateis: those who lack self-control or "moral strength"</term>; and **incontinence** <term>akrasia</term> applies to everything that people desire. Conversely, people may act when the pain is immediate, but the...

<meta>This page continues Aristotle's *Rhetoric*, Book I, Chapter XII. The previous page discussed the mindset of those who commit injustice when they believe they can escape punishment or when the profit outweighs the risk. This page continues that list of motivations and begins to categorize the types of people who are most likely to be targeted as victims.</meta>

<page-num>54</page-num>
# ARISTOTELIS

...is the loss; whereas the pleasure and profit are immediate, and the punishment is distant and long-delayed. For those who are **self-controlled** and more **prudent** pursue such advantages <note>Aristotle suggests that even "prudent" people might calculate that a long-delayed punishment is worth an immediate gain.</note>. 

This also applies to those who are in a position to make it appear that they acted by **chance**, or by **necessity**, or by **nature**, or by **habit**—and in general, to make it seem as though they committed an **error** <term>hamartein: a mistake or 'missing the mark,' often seen as less morally culpable than a deliberate crime</term> rather than a deliberate **injustice** <term>adikein: a conscious act of wrongdoing or law-breaking</term>. It also applies to those who have a chance of obtaining **equity** <note>original: *epieikous*. Equity is the principle of fairness that looks at the intent and circumstances rather than just the strict letter of the law.</note>.

Also, those who are **in need** are likely to act. People are "needy" in two ways: either out of **necessity**, like the poor, or out of a desire for **excess**, like the rich. 

Furthermore, those who are **highly esteemed** and those who are **deeply disgraced** are likely to commit wrongs. The former act because they believe they will not be suspected; the latter because they feel they have no reputation left to lose.

These, then, are the states of mind of those who attempt wrongdoing. They commit injustice against people of the following types: those who possess what the wrongdoers themselves lack—whether for **necessities**, for **superiority**, or for **enjoyment**. 

They target both those **far away** and those **nearby**. In the case of those nearby, the gain is quick; in the case of those far away, the punishment is slow—as is the case with those who plunder the **Carthaginians** <note>original: *Karchedonious*. Since Carthage was a distant naval power, Aristotle uses them as an example of a victim from whom it would take a very long time for legal or military retribution to arrive.</note>. 

They also target:
*   Those who are **not cautious** or watchful, but **trusting**; for it is easy to escape their notice. 
*   The **lazy**, for it requires diligent effort to prosecute a case. 
*   The **shamefaced/modest**, for they are not the type to fight over profit. 
*   Those who have been wronged by many before but did not retaliate; these are, according to the proverb, **Mysian prey** <term>Mysōn leian: a Greek proverb referring to a defenseless person or a territory that anyone can plunder without fear of resistance</term>.
*   Both those who have **never** been wronged and those who have been wronged **often**; both are off their guard—the former because they think it will never happen to them, the latter because they think it won't happen *again*. 
*   Those who have been **slandered** or are easy to slander. Such people do not choose to go to court because they fear the judges, nor are they able to persuade the court, especially if they are already hated or envied. 
*   Those against whom the wrongdoer has a **pretext** involving their ancestors, themselves, or their friends, who have done evil or intended to do evil to the wrongdoers themselves, their ancestors, or those they care for.

<summary>Aristotle continues detailing the types of people prone to committing injustice and provides a list of vulnerable targets, ranging from the overly trusting to those who are already socially marginalized.</summary>
<keywords>Aristotle, Rhetoric, injustice, victims, Carthaginians, Mysian prey, motives for crime, equity, error vs. intent</keywords>

### RHETORICA. LIB. I. CAP. XII.

For as the proverb says, **"Wickedness needs only a pretext."** <note>original: "Προφάσεως δεῖται μοῦνον ἡ πονηρία." A common ancient Greek saying implying that a person looking to do wrong will find any excuse to justify it.</note> And they wrong both **enemies** and **friends**; for it is **easy** to wrong the latter, and **pleasant** to wrong the former. And they target those who have no friends. 

And those who are **not skilled** in speaking or acting; for such people either do not attempt to prosecute their case, or they settle the matter out of court, or they accomplish nothing even if they do go to trial. And those for whom it is **not profitable** to spend time waiting for a trial or payment; such as **foreigners** and **independent farmers** <term>autourgoi: small-scale farmers who work their own land and cannot afford to leave their crops to attend lengthy legal proceedings in the city</term>; for they are willing to settle for small amounts, and such people are easily quieted. 

And they wrong those who have **themselves committed many injustices**, or have committed the same kinds of wrongs they are now suffering; for it seems almost like *not* being an injustice when someone is wronged in the same way they were accustomed to wronging others; I mean, for example, if one were to physically assault someone who was himself habitually insulting. 

And those who have either **done harm**, or **wished to**, or are **wishing to**, or are **about to**; for this brings both pleasure and honor, and it appears close to not being an injustice at all. And those whose **favor** they wish to gain—whether they be friends, or those they admire, or those they love, or their masters, or generally those under whose influence they live. 

And those from whom they are likely to receive **fair treatment** <note>original: *epieikeias*. This refers to "equity" or "leniency," where a victim is known to be forgiving or reasonable rather than litigious.</note>. And those against whom they have already brought charges and have had prior disagreements; for such things, too, appear close to not being an injustice; as **Callippus** did in the case of **Dion** <note>Callippus was a friend of Dion of Syracuse who assassinated him, justifying the act as a "preventative" measure against Dion's supposed tyranny.</note>. 

And those who are **about to be wronged by others** if they do not do it themselves, as though there were no longer any time for deliberation; just as it is said that **Aenesidemus** sent prizes for the game of **kottabos** <term>kottabos: a popular Greek drinking game where players threw wine dregs at a target; here used metaphorically as a "congratulatory" prize</term> to **Gelon** after he had enslaved a city, because Gelon had beaten him to it, since Aenesidemus himself was about to do the same. 

And those whom, after wronging them, they will be able to perform **many just acts** for, as if easily healing the wound; just as **Jason of Thessaly** <note>A 4th-century BC ruler (tagus) of Thessaly known for his political ambitions.</note> said, it is "necessary to do some wrongs so that one can also do many just things." 

And they wrong others in matters where **everyone or many people** are accustomed to doing wrong; for they think they will meet with forgiveness. And things that are **easy to hide**; such...

<meta>This page concludes the list of conditions that encourage wrongdoers from page 55 and begins Chapter 13, where Aristotle introduces his famous distinction between the specific laws of a city and the "universal" law of nature.</meta>

<page-num>56</page-num>
<header>ARISTOTELIS</header>

And they target things that are **quickly consumed**, such as foodstuffs; or things that are **easily altered** in their shape, color, or composition. Or things that are **easy to hide** in many places; such are things that are easy to carry and can be hidden in small spaces. They also target items for which the wrongdoer already possessed many similar and indistinguishable examples. 

Furthermore, they target people who are **ashamed to speak** of the wrongs they have suffered; such as **outrages** <term>hybreis: specifically refers to acts of physical or sexual assault intended to shame the victim</term> against the women of their own household, or against themselves, or their sons. They also target those for whom a prosecution would make them seem **fond of lawsuits**; these are usually small matters or those for which people are typically forgiven. These, then, are roughly the circumstances, the types of people, and the reasons why men commit injustice.

### CAPUT XIII.

<note>Chapter 13</note>

Let us now distinguish all types of **wrongful acts** and **just acts**, starting from this point. Justice and injustice have been defined in relation to **two kinds of law**, and in two ways regarding those whom they affect. By law, I mean on the one hand **specific law**, and on the other, **universal law**. 

**Specific law** <term>nomos idios</term> is that which has been defined by each group of people for themselves; this is divided into the **unwritten** and the **written**. **Universal law** <term>nomos koinos</term> is that which is **according to nature**. For there is a certain justice and injustice by nature, which all men divine to some extent, even if there is no community or contract between them. This is what **Sophocles’ Antigone** clearly means when she says that it is just to bury Polyneices despite the prohibition, on the grounds that this is naturally just:

> For this is no thing of today or yesterday, 
> But lives forever, and no one knows from whence it came.
<note>original: "Οὐ γάρ τι νῦν γε κἀχθές, ἀλλ᾽ αἰεί ποτε / Ζῇ τοῦτο, κοὐδεὶς οἶδεν, ἐξ ὅτου φάνη." A quote from Sophocles' Antigone, lines 456-457, justifying her divine duty to bury her brother against the King's decree.</note>

And as **Empedocles** says regarding the prohibition against **killing living things**; for this is not just for some and unjust for others:

> But the law for all extends through the wide-ruling...
<note>Aristotle quotes Empedocles to illustrate that certain moral "laws" are thought to apply to all living beings, not just citizens of a specific city.</note>

<summary>Aristotle concludes his analysis of why certain crimes are committed and begins a foundational discussion on the difference between "specific" (civil) law and "universal" (natural) law.</summary>
<keywords>natural law, specific law, Antigone, Empedocles, justice, injustice, universal law, legal theory</keywords>

<meta>This page continues the discussion of legal definitions from page 56, specifically moving from the concept of "universal law" to how crimes are categorized based on whether they harm the public or an individual, and the importance of intent in legal defense.</meta>

# RHETORIC. BOOK I. CHAPTER XIII.

->*It stretches continuously through the ether and through the boundless light.*<-
<note>original: "Αἰθέρος ἠνεκέως τέταται, διά τ' ἀπλέτου αὐγῆς." This verse, likely from the philosopher Empedocles, describes the "universal law" of nature that applies everywhere, unlike the local laws of a specific city.</note>

*And as Alcidamas says in his **Messenian Oration**. Now, regarding those toward whom justice is defined, it is defined in two ways: for it is either toward the **community** or toward **one of its members** that one must do or not do certain things. Therefore, both **wrongful acts** and **just acts** can be committed in two ways: either against a specific individual or against the community. For the person who commits **adultery** or **assault** wrongs a specific person, while the person who **fails to perform military service** wrongs the community.*

*Now that all types of wrongful acts have been distinguished—some being against the community and others against one or more individuals—let us define what it means to **suffer injustice** and then speak of the rest. To suffer injustice is to have unjust acts done to one by **voluntary agents**; for "doing injustice" was previously defined as being a voluntary act. Since the victim of injustice must necessarily be harmed, and harmed **involuntarily**, the nature of these harms is clear from what was said before; for **good and evil things** have been distinguished on their own earlier, as have **voluntary acts**, which are those done with knowledge. Consequently, all **indictments** must be either against the community or against an individual; and committed either in ignorance, or involuntarily, or voluntarily and with knowledge; and of these, some are done by **deliberate choice**, and others due to **passion**.*

*Now, **anger** will be discussed when we deal with the passions. What things people choose to do, and in what state of mind, has been stated before. However, people often admit to the **deed** but do not admit to the **legal label** <term>epigraphma: the formal charge or technical name of the crime in an indictment</term>, or at least not the specific circumstances the label implies. For example, they may admit they **took** something, but not that they **stole** it; or that they **struck first**, but not that they committed **outrage** <term>hybris: a specific legal category of assault intended to humiliate the victim</term>; or that they had **sexual relations**, but not **adultery**; or that they **stole**, but did not commit **sacrilege** <note>original: hierosylia</note>, because the object did not belong to a god; or that they **encroached upon land**, but not **public land**; or that they...*

<summary>Aristotle distinguishes between crimes against individuals and those against the state, while highlighting that legal defense often relies on disputing the technical "label" of a crime rather than the act itself.</summary>
<keywords>Aristotle, Rhetoric, Alcidamas, community vs individual, voluntary vs involuntary, hybris, sacrilege, legal definitions, intent</keywords>

<meta>This page continues the discussion of legal definitions. The previous page ended with a discussion of voluntary acts and the distinction between crimes against the community versus the individual. This page moves into the necessity of defining specific crimes like theft and assault based on the perpetrator's intent.</meta>

# ARISTOTLE

<note>The text begins mid-sentence, continuing a list of actions that might be disputed in court.</note>
...[to communicate] with the enemy, but not to betray the city. For these reasons, it would be necessary to define these terms as well: what is **theft**, what is **assault**, and what is **adultery**. This is so that whether we wish to show that a crime exists or does not exist, we are able to make the justice of the matter clear.

In all such cases, the dispute is about whether the act is **unjust** and **wicked**, or not. For wickedness and the doing of injustice lie in the <term>proairesis<note>The Greek term *proairesis* refers to "deliberate intent" or "moral purpose." In Aristotle's view, an action is only truly "unjust" if it is done on purpose.</note></term>. Terms of this kind signify the intent—for example, **assault** and **theft**. If a man strikes another, he has not necessarily committed <term>hubris<note>While often translated as "pride," in a legal context *hubris* refers to an "assault" or "outrage" intended to shame the victim.</note></term>; he has only done so if he did it for a specific reason, such as to dishonor the victim or for his own pleasure. Nor has a man necessarily "stolen" if he took something in secret; he has only stolen if he took it to cause harm and to claim the object for himself. The same logic applies to other crimes as it does to these.

Since there were two kinds of **just** and **unjust** acts—for some are **written laws**, and others are **unwritten**—those which the laws expressly forbid have already been discussed. Of the **unwritten laws**, there are two kinds. 

The first kind consists of acts involving an abundance of **virtue** or **vice**, which are met with reproaches or praises, dishonors or honors, and rewards. Examples include being grateful to a benefactor, returning a kindness to someone who has helped you, being helpful to one’s friends, and other such things. 

The second kind of unwritten law fills the gaps left by the specific written law. This is what we call <term>equity<note>The Greek *epieikes* refers to "equity" or "fairness"—a way of correcting the law when it is too general to be fair in a specific, unusual case.</note></term>, which is considered to be a form of justice. **Equity** is justice that goes beyond the written law. This occurs sometimes against the will of the lawmakers, and sometimes with their will. It happens against their will when a specific case escapes their notice. It happens with their will when they are unable to provide a precise definition, but find it necessary to speak in general terms—even if the rule is not absolute, but only holds true for the most part. 

This also applies to things that are not easy to define because of their infinite variety—for instance, the act of "wounding with an iron instrument." It is difficult to define exactly what size of instrument or...

<summary>Aristotle argues that the legality of an act depends on the "proairesis" (intent) of the actor and introduces the concept of "equity" as a necessary supplement to written law.</summary>

<keywords>theft, assault, adultery, intent, proairesis, written law, unwritten law, equity, epieikes, justice, legal definitions</keywords>

--- Page 72 ---
<meta>This page continues Aristotle's analysis of legal definitions. While the previous page focused on the necessity of defining crimes like theft and assault by the perpetrator's intent (proairesis), this page introduces the concept of "equity" (epieikeia) as a way to address situations where the written law is too general to be perfectly just.</meta>

# RHETORICA. LIB. I. CAP. XIII. 59

...[to define] in what way; for an entire lifetime would fail those who try to list every specific case. If, therefore, a matter is indefinite but a law must still be enacted, it is necessary to speak in general terms. For example, if a man wearing a ring raises his hand or strikes someone, according to the literal **written law**, he is guilty and has committed a crime; but according to the truth of the situation, he is not—and this spirit of fairness is what we call <term>equity<note>original: *epieikes*. In Greek philosophy, this represents a "higher justice" or "fairness" that corrects the law when it is too broad or literal.</note></term>.

If this is what equity is, it is clear what kinds of actions are equitable, and what are not, and what kind of people are equitable. For those actions for which we should show forgiveness are the ones that are equitable.

Furthermore, equity demands that we do not treat **errors**, **crimes**, and **misfortunes** as if they were the same.
*   **Misfortunes** <note>original: *atuchēmata*</note> are those acts that are unexpected and do not arise from a bad character.
*   **Errors** <note>original: *hamartēmata*</note> are acts that are not unexpected but still do not arise from a wicked character.
*   **Crimes** <note>original: *adikēmata*</note> are acts that are not unexpected and do arise from a wicked character (for acts driven by a specific desire are the result of wickedness).

It is also equitable to be forgiving of human failings; to look not at the law, but at the **lawgiver**; and not at the literal words, but at the **intent** of the lawgiver. One should look not at the action itself, but at the <term>moral purpose<note>original: *proairesis*</note></term> of the person. One should look not at a single part of a person's life, but at the whole; not at what someone is like right now, but what they have been always, or for the most part.

Equity also involves remembering the good things one has received rather than the bad, and the good things done to oneself rather than the good things one has done for others. It means being patient when being wronged. It means preferring to settle a dispute by **reason** rather than by action, and wishing to go to an <term>arbitrator<note>original: *diaitētēs*</note></term> rather than to a court trial. For the arbitrator looks to equity, while the judge looks only to the law. Indeed, the office of the arbitrator was created for this very reason: so that equity might prevail.

Let this, then, be our definition of the nature of equity.

<summary>Aristotle defines "equity" as a correction to the written law, emphasizing the importance of a defendant's intent and long-term character over the literal wording of legal statutes. He distinguishes between accidents, mistakes, and intentional crimes to show where mercy should be applied.</summary>
<keywords>equity, justice, Aristotle, law, intent, moral purpose, arbitration, crimes, mistakes, misfortunes</keywords>

--- Page 73 ---
<meta>This page continues Aristotle’s discussion of legal and ethical wrongdoing. While the previous page defined the nature of crimes based on intent and introduced the concept of "equity" to balance rigid laws, this section transitions into how an orator can argue that one crime is "greater" than another. Aristotle shifts from defining the type of act to measuring its severity.</meta>

ARISTOTLE

### CHAPTER XIV.

**Crimes** are considered greater when they proceed from a greater degree of injustice. This is why even the smallest acts can sometimes be seen as the greatest. For example, Callistratus accused Melanopus of defrauding the temple-builders of **three sacred half-obols** <note>original: *tria hēmiōbelia iera*. An obol was a very small silver coin. While the monetary value was tiny, the fact that the money was "sacred" (belonging to a temple) made the crime a spiritual violation.</note>. In matters of justice, however, the opposite is true <note>That is, a small act of justice is not necessarily "great" in the same way a small theft of a sacred item is a "great" crime.</note>.

These judgments arise from the potential power of the act; for a man who would steal three sacred half-obols would likely commit any crime at all. Sometimes the "greatness" of a crime is judged in this way, and at other times it is judged by the actual **damage** caused. A crime is also greater when there is no equal punishment for it, but every possible penalty falls short. It is greater when there is no remedy, for such a situation is difficult or impossible to fix. Also, when the victim cannot obtain justice, the crime is "incurable," because legal justice and punishment are the intended remedies for a wrong.

A crime is also viewed as more severe if the victim—the person who suffered the injustice—punished themselves drastically <note>Likely referring to suicide or self-mutilation out of shame or despair.</note>; for the one who caused it deserves an even greater punishment. For example, Sophocles <note>This refers to the orator Sophocles, not the famous playwright.</note>, when speaking on behalf of Euctemon (who had killed himself after suffering an outrage), said he would not propose a penalty less than the value the victim had placed upon himself.

Furthermore, a crime is greater:
*   If the perpetrator acted alone, was the first to do it, or did it with very few accomplices.
*   If the same error is committed many times.
*   If the act leads to the creation of new laws to prevent it or new penalties to punish it. For instance, in Argos, they penalize the person for whom a specific law had to be passed, or those whose actions required a prison to be built.
*   If the crime is more **brutish** <term>brutish<note>original: *thēriōdesteron*. Meaning acts that are savage, animalistic, or lacking in human reason.</note></term>.
*   If it involves more premeditation.
*   If those who hear of it feel more fear than pity.

Rhetorical techniques for magnifying a crime include showing that it has destroyed or overstepped many rights at once—such as oaths, pledges of friendship, promises, or marriage rights—for this represents an accumulation of many injustices. Another factor is if the crime is committed in the very place where wrongdoers are punished, which is what **false witnesses** do; for where would a person *not* commit a crime if he is willing to do so even in a court of law? Finally, crimes are greater if they involve things for which one feels shame...

<summary>Aristotle explains the criteria for determining the severity of a crime, arguing that "greatness" is measured by the perpetrator's intent, the lack of possible remedies, the impact on the victim, and the violation of sacred or legal institutions.</summary>
<keywords>degrees of crime, Callistratus, severity of injustice, legal remedies, Sophocles (orator), Argos, premeditation, rhetorical magnification</keywords>

--- Page 74 ---
<meta>This page concludes the discussion on the relative severity of crimes and begins a new section on "non-technical proofs." Aristotle transitions from the ethical theory of wrongdoing to the practical tools used in a courtroom, such as laws, witnesses, and contracts.</meta>

RHETORICA. BOOK I. CHAPTER XIV.

...and most certainly. A crime is also greater if it is committed against the person from whom one has received a benefit; for the offender commits a greater wrong by both doing harm and failing to return a good deed. It is also greater if it violates **unwritten laws** <term>unwritten laws: *agrapha dikaia*, referring to universal moral principles or customs that are recognized even if they aren't recorded in a city's formal statutes</term>; for a superior person is one who is just without being forced by necessity. Written laws involve necessity, but unwritten laws do not. In another sense, however, a crime may be seen as greater if it goes against **written laws**; for the man who commits crimes despite the threat of terrifying penalties and fines would surely commit crimes where there are no penalties at all. So much, then, for the greater and lesser degrees of wrongdoing.

### CHAPTER XV.

Regarding the so-called **non-technical proofs** <term>non-technical proofs: *atechnoi pisteis*, sometimes called "inartificial" proofs. Unlike "technical" proofs which the orator creates through logic and emotion, these are external pieces of evidence that already exist and only need to be presented.</term>, the next step in our discussion is to run through them; for these are specific to **legal speeches** <note>original: *dikanikon*, referring to the rhetoric used in courts of law</note>. There are five in number: **laws**, **witnesses**, **contracts**, **evidence given under torture**, and **oaths**.

First, then, let us speak about laws, and how they should be used when urging or dissuading, and when accusing or defending. It is clear that if the written law is contrary to the facts of the case, one must appeal to the **universal law** and to the principles of **equity** <note>original: *epieikes*, a concept of fairness or "poetic justice" that corrects the law when it is too rigid to be fair in a specific case</note> as being more truly just. One must also argue that the phrase "to the best of my judgment" <note>Athenian jurors took an oath to decide according to the law where it was clear, but otherwise "to the best of their judgment" (*gnōmē tē aristē*).</note> means that the judge should not rely entirely on the written word. 

Furthermore, one should argue that equity always remains and never changes—nor does the universal law, for it is according to nature—but written laws change frequently. This is the basis for the defense used in **Sophocles’ *Antigone***; for she defends herself for burying her brother despite the decree of **Creon**, arguing that she acted against the king's law but not against the unwritten law:

> For these things are not for today or yesterday, but live forever;
> I was not going to [suffer the penalty] for these laws because of any man’s [decree].
<note>Aristotle is quoting lines 456 and 459 of the play *Antigone*. The heroine argues that eternal, divine laws regarding the burial of the dead override the temporary, man-made laws of a ruler.</note>

One can also argue that what is truly just is something stable and beneficial, but what merely "seems" to be a law is not; therefore, a written law that is unjust is not truly a law at all.

<meta>Continuing from page 61, where Aristotle introduced the five "non-technical proofs" used in legal rhetoric. Having just started the discussion on how to handle **laws** when they either support or oppose one's case, he now provides specific arguments for a speaker whose case is not supported by the written law.</meta>

ARISTOTLE

...for it [the "best judgment" oath] does not perform the work of the law. One should also argue that the judge is like an **assayer of silver** <term>assayer of silver: *argyrognomon*, a person trained to distinguish genuine silver coins from counterfeit ones. Aristotle uses this as a metaphor for a judge’s duty to distinguish true justice from a mere literal (and potentially "counterfeit") reading of the law</term>, so that he may distinguish counterfeit justice from the true. Furthermore, argue that it is the mark of a better person to use and abide by **unwritten laws** rather than written ones. 

One can also argue if the law in question is anywhere contrary to a well-regarded law, or even to itself; for instance, sometimes one law commands that whatever agreements are made shall be binding, while another law forbids making any agreement contrary to the law. Look also to see if the law is **ambiguous**, so that one can turn it and see in which direction it can be interpreted to fit either justice or utility, and then use it in that way.

Furthermore, argue that if the circumstances for which the law was originally established no longer exist, but the law itself remains, one must try to demonstrate this and fight against the law on these grounds. 

But if the **written law** is favorable to the case, then one must say that the "best judgment" oath <note>original: *gnome ariste*. Athenian jurors swore to judge according to the laws where they existed, and by their "best judgment" where no law applied</note> is not for the purpose of judging against the law, but so that the judge does not commit perjury if he is ignorant of what the law actually says. One should also argue that no one chooses the "good" in an absolute sense, but rather what is good for themselves; and that it makes no difference whether a law is never enacted or simply never used. 

Moreover, argue that in the other arts it is not profitable to be "wiser than the master"—for example, to outsmart a physician—for the error of the physician does not do as much harm as the habit of disobeying the one in authority. Indeed, to seek to be wiser than the laws is exactly what is forbidden in well-regarded laws. So much, then, for the subject of laws.

Regarding **witnesses**: witnesses are of two types, the **ancient** and the **recent**. Of these, some share in the risk of the trial’s outcome, while others are outside of it. By "ancient witnesses," I mean the poets and all other famous men whose judgments are known to the public. For example, the Athenians used **Homer** <note>The famous epic poet of the *Iliad* and *Odyssey*</note> as a witness...

<summary>This page explains how to argue for or against the application of a law in court, followed by a transition into the use of witnesses, distinguishing between "ancient" authorities like poets and "recent" ones.</summary>
<keywords>legal rhetoric, equity, unwritten law, best judgment, judicial oath, witnesses, Homeric authority, legal interpretation</keywords>

<meta>This page continues the discussion of "non-technical proofs" (*atechnoi pisteis*). Having concluded the section on how to argue for or against written laws, Aristotle now turns to the second type of proof: **Witnesses**. He begins by listing historical examples where ancient authorities or poets were used as evidence in legal and political disputes.</meta>

# RHETORICA. BOOK I. CHAPTER XV.

...concerning Salamis <note>Athens and Megara both claimed the island of Salamis; the Athenians famously cited verses from Homer's *Iliad* to "prove" the island historically belonged to them</note>; and the people of Tenedos recently used Periander of Corinth as a witness against the Sigeans; and Cleophon used the elegiac poems of Solon against Critias, claiming that the family had long been licentious <note>original: *aselges*; meaning undisciplined or prone to excess</note>; for Solon would never have written:

> Bid the red-haired Critias to listen to his father.

Such witnesses, then, are used for events that have already happened. Regarding future events, there are also the **oracle-tellers**; for instance, Themistocles argued that they must fight at sea, interpreting the **"wooden wall"** <note>During the Persian Wars, the Oracle at Delphi told the Athenians they would be saved by a "wooden wall." While some thought this meant a literal fence around the Acropolis, Themistocles argued it referred to the wooden hulls of their ships</note> to mean the fleet. Furthermore, **proverbs**, as has been mentioned, are a form of testimony; for example, if someone advises against making an old man a friend, the proverb bears witness: 

> Never do a kindness to an old man. 

And regarding the need to eliminate the sons along with the fathers:

> Foolish is he who, having killed the father, leaves the sons behind.

**Recent witnesses** are those well-known persons who have delivered a judgment on a matter; for their decisions are useful to those disputing the same issues. For example, Eubulus in the law courts used against Chares what Plato had said to Archibius—that admitting one is wicked has become a growing trend in the city. Also included are those who share the risk of the trial if they are thought to be lying. 

These types of witnesses, however, are only witnesses to the **fact**—whether a thing happened or not, whether it exists or does not. They are not witnesses to the **quality** of the act—for instance, whether it was just or unjust, or beneficial or harmful. However, witnesses from afar <note>meaning those not involved in the immediate case, or ancient authorities</note> are the most trustworthy regarding these matters of quality.

The most trustworthy of all are the **ancient witnesses**, for they are incorruptible. 

**Arguments regarding testimony:** For the speaker who has no witnesses, you must argue that the case should be decided based on **probabilities** <term>probabilities: *eikota*, arguments based on what is likely or "fair" rather than hard evidence</term>; and that this is what the oath "according to my best judgment" <note>original: *gnome ariste*</note> truly means. Argue also that probabilities cannot be bribed with money, and that probabilities cannot be convicted of bearing false witness. But for the speaker who has witnesses against someone who does not...

<summary>Aristotle categorizes "witnesses" into ancient authorities (poets and proverbs) and recent figures, explaining how to use their past judgments as legal evidence. He also provides a strategy for speakers lacking witnesses, suggesting they prioritize "probability" over fallible human testimony.</summary>

<keywords>witnesses, evidence, Solon, Themistocles, proverbs, probability, best judgment, legal strategy</keywords>

--- Page 77 ---
<meta>This page continues Aristotle’s analysis of "non-technical" proofs in Book I, Chapter XV of the *Rhetoric*. Having previously discussed "ancient witnesses" (poets and authorities), he now transitions to the practical use of witnesses in court and moves into the third type of proof: **Contracts**.</meta>

# ARISTOTLE

...that probabilities <note>original: *eikota*; logical arguments based on what is likely to happen</note> are not subject to legal cross-examination. Furthermore, there would be no need for witnesses if the truth could be sufficiently seen through logical arguments alone. 

**Witnesses** are of two kinds: some speak regarding the litigant themselves, others regarding the opponent. Likewise, some testify to the facts of the deed, and others to the person’s character <note>original: *ethos*</note>. Consequently, it is clear that one is never at a loss for useful testimony; for if there is no evidence regarding the specific deed—either in agreement with one’s own side or against the opponent—there is always evidence to be found regarding character, either to establish one's own moral excellence <note>original: *epieikeian*; fairness or virtue</note> or the opponent’s worthlessness. 

As for other matters concerning a witness—whether they are a friend, an enemy, or neutral; whether they have a good reputation, a bad one, or something in between; and any other such distinctions—these must be addressed using the same **topics** <note>original: *topoi*; the "places" or categories of arguments</note> from which we derive our **enthymemes** <note>a "rhetorical syllogism" or an argument based on premises that the audience already accepts</note>.

Regarding **contracts** <note>original: *synthēkai*</note>, the use of speech is limited to either magnifying or minimizing their importance, or making them appear either credible or incredible. If the contract is in your favor, you must argue that it is credible and authoritative; if it favors the opponent, you must do the opposite. 

In constructing credibility or lack thereof, the process is no different from the treatment of witnesses; for the contracts are considered credible based on the character of those who signed or witnessed them, or those who have custody of them. If the existence of the contract is admitted and it belongs to your side, you must magnify its importance. For a contract is a **private and specific law**; and while contracts do not make the law authoritative, the laws do give authority to those contracts made according to the law. 

In a general sense, the law itself is a kind of contract; therefore, whoever distrusts and invalidates a contract is effectively invalidating the laws. Furthermore, most business transactions and voluntary dealings between men are carried out via contracts; if these agreements become invalid, the mutual utility and social intercourse of men is destroyed...

<summary>Aristotle explains how to leverage witness testimony regarding character when facts are lacking and argues that contracts should be treated as "private laws" essential to social stability.</summary>
<keywords>witnesses, character (ethos), contracts, private law, legal rhetoric, credibility</keywords>

<meta>This page continues Aristotle’s discussion of "non-technical proofs" in Book I, Chapter XV. The previous page ended while explaining how a speaker should approach contracts (synthēkai) that favor their opponent. This page provides the specific counter-arguments to use against such contracts and then moves on to the fourth type of proof: Evidence obtained through torture (basanoi).</meta>

### RHETORICA. LIB. I. CAP. XV.

of men. And any other such fitting points are easy to see on the surface. ***If, however, the contract is contrary to your case and supports the opposing party, first of all, the same arguments one would use against an unfavorable law are appropriate here. For it is absurd if we believe we are not bound to obey laws when they are poorly enacted or their makers were deceived, yet we consider contracts to be binding by necessity.*** 

Next, one should argue that the judge is an arbiter of justice; therefore, the judge should not consider the contract itself, but what is more just <note>Aristotle is suggesting that "natural justice" should override a written agreement if that agreement is unfair</note>. And while justice cannot be changed, either by deceit or by force (for it exists by nature), contracts are entered into both by people who have been deceived and those who have been forced. 

In addition to these points, one should look to see if the contract is contrary to any written law or "universal law" <note>original: *koinōn*; referring to natural laws or moral principles common to all humanity</note>, and whether it conflicts with what is just or honorable. Furthermore, check if it conflicts with other contracts, whether later or earlier; for either the later ones are valid and the earlier ones void, or the earlier ones were correct and the later ones were fraudulent—whichever way is useful to your argument. 

Also, consider the matter of expediency <note>original: *symperhon*; what is beneficial or advantageous</note>, to see if the contract somehow opposes the interests of the judges, and other such things; for these points are likewise easy to observe.

**Torture** <note>original: *basanoi*; in the Athenian legal system, the testimony of enslaved persons was generally only admissible if it was obtained under torture</note> is also a form of testimony. It is thought to carry credibility because a certain necessity is attached to it. It is not difficult, then, to see the arguments regarding this and to state the possibilities. 

If the results of torture are in your favor, you should amplify their importance by arguing that these are the only true forms of testimony. But if they are unfavorable and support the opponent, one might refute their truth by speaking against the whole category of torture. You should say that people tell lies under compulsion just as much as they tell the truth—both by enduring the pain to avoid telling the truth, or by easily lying in order to make the torture stop sooner. One must be able to refer to such cases that have actually happened...

<meta>This page concludes Aristotle’s analysis of "non-technical proofs" (atechnoi pisteis) in Book I, Chapter XV. Having previously discussed laws, witnesses, contracts, and torture, the text now transitions to the final category: Oaths (horkoi). This section provides the orator with ready-made arguments for various scenarios involving the swearing of a legal oath.</meta>

->ARISTOTLE<-

...mentioned examples, which the judges can examine. 

### CHAPTER XV. (CONTINUED)

Regarding **oaths**, there are four ways to divide the subject: for a person either offers an oath to their opponent and accepts one in return, or does neither; or they do one but not the other—that is, they either offer but do not accept, or they accept but do not offer. Furthermore, besides these cases, one must consider if an oath has already been sworn, either by the speaker or by the opponent.

If a person **does not offer** the oath <note>original: *ou didōsi*; to not offer the opponent the chance to settle the case by swearing a religious oath of innocence</note>, they should argue that people commit perjury <term>perjury: the act of swearing a false oath, considered a grave religious and civil offense</term> easily. Also, they can argue that if the opponent swears, he will effectively avoid restitution; whereas if the oath is not offered, the speaker believes the judges will condemn him. The speaker should claim that the risk of the court's judgment is preferable, because they trust the judges, but they do not trust the opponent.

If a person **does not accept** an oath <note>original: *ou lambanei*; refusing to swear an oath offered by the opponent to prove one's own claim</note>, they should argue that an oath is being used as a substitute for money <note>meaning the opponent is trying to resolve a financial dispute through a ritual rather than evidence</note>. They should argue that if they were a villain, they would have sworn it already; for it is "better" to be a villain for a gain than for no reason at all. By swearing, the villain would keep the money, but by refusing to swear falsely, he does not. Thus, the refusal is due to virtue rather than a fear of perjury. 

In this case, the saying of **Xenophanes** <note>Xenophanes of Colophon, a philosopher who famously criticized the anthropomorphic gods of Homer</note> is appropriate: that this challenge is not on equal terms, as it is between an impious man and a pious one. It is as if a strong man were to challenge a weak one to a fight where they must either strike or be struck.

If the speaker **accepts** the oath, they should say they trust themselves but do not trust the opponent. Turning Xenophanes' argument around, one must say the situation is actually fair if the impious man offers the oath and the pious man is the one who swears it. It would be a terrible thing, the speaker might say, if they were unwilling to swear to the truth themselves regarding matters where they expect the judges to swear an oath before giving a verdict.

If the speaker **offers** the oath, they should argue that it is a pious act to be willing to entrust the matter to the gods. They should argue that there is no need for other judges, since the speaker is essentially allowing the opponent to be the judge of his own case. Furthermore, it is absurd to refuse to swear about the very same matters that one expects the judges to swear to.

Since it is clear how to speak in each individual case, it is also clear how to speak when these cases are combined. For instance, if the speaker is willing to **accept** but not **offer** an oath; or if they **offer** one but are unwilling to **accept**; or if they are willing both to **accept and offer**; or if...

<summary>This page details the rhetorical strategies for handling judicial oaths, offering specific arguments for whether a litigant should offer, accept, or refuse an oath based on the perceived character of the opponent.</summary>
<keywords>Aristotle, Rhetoric, oaths, perjury, Xenophanes, judicial procedure, piety, legal strategy, non-technical proofs</keywords>

--- Page 80 ---
<meta>This page concludes the final "non-technical proof" regarding oaths (horkoi) and transitions into Book II, where Aristotle shifts focus from the content of arguments to the character of the speaker and the emotions of the audience.</meta>

->RHETORIC. BOOK II. CHAPTER I.<-

...neither. In such a case, the argument must necessarily be composed of the previously mentioned points; thus, the speeches must also be constructed from those same elements. 

If an oath has already been taken by the speaker, and it is contradictory to the current case, one must argue that it is not **perjury**. For wrongdoing is a voluntary act, and perjury is a form of wrongdoing; but actions performed under force or through deceit are involuntary. In this situation, one must conclude that perjury lies in the **intention of the mind**, and not merely in what the mouth utters. <note>original: *tē dianoia, all' ou tō stomati*; Aristotle is suggesting that if one was forced or tricked into an earlier oath, their "mind" never truly consented, thus a later contradictory statement is not a religious crime.</note> 

If, however, an oath was sworn by the opponent, one should argue that the person who does not abide by what they swore subverts everything. It is for this very reason that men, after swearing an oath, make use of the laws. We demand that you, the judges, abide by the oath you took before you began to judge; shall we then not abide by our own oaths? One may also add whatever other arguments serve to amplify the importance of the matter. 

This concludes our discussion of the **non-technical proofs** <note>original: *atechnoi pisteis*; these are "pre-existing" proofs like laws and witnesses, rather than those constructed by the orator's skill.</note>.

---

# BOOK II.
## CHAPTER I.

These, then, are the sources from which one must encourage or dissuade, praise or blame, and accuse or defend. We have also seen what kinds of opinions and premises are useful for making these proofs credible. For it is regarding these subjects, and from these sources, that **enthymemes** are spoken, so as to address each specific branch of oratory individually. <term>enthymemes: rhetorical arguments that function like logical puzzles, often leaving one premise for the audience to fill in themselves.</term>

But since rhetoric is for the sake of **judgment** (for people judge the advice given in political speeches, and a legal trial is itself a judgment), it is necessary not only to look to the speech itself—to ensure it is demonstrative and trustworthy—but also to present the speaker himself in a certain way and to dispose the judge in a certain way. For it makes a great difference to the credibility of a speech, especially...

<meta>This page continues Aristotle's transition into Book II of the Rhetoric. Having finished the "non-technical proofs" (laws, witnesses, etc.) on the previous page, he now begins his famous analysis of how a speaker's character and the audience's emotions function as "technical" means of persuasion.</meta>

->ARISTOTLE<-

...credible. In deliberative speeches <note>original: *symboulais*; these are speeches intended to advise or dissuade an audience regarding future actions, such as in a political assembly.</note>, and then also in lawsuits, it is important that the speaker appears to be a certain kind of person and that the audience assumes he has a certain attitude toward them; in addition to these, it is important if the audience themselves happen to be in a certain state of mind. 

Now, the speaker appearing to be a certain kind of person is more useful in deliberative speeches; but the audience being in a certain state of mind is more useful in lawsuits. For things do not appear the same to those who love and those who hate, nor to those who are angry and those who are calm; but they appear either entirely different or different in magnitude. To the person who loves the one they are judging, that person seems either not to be doing wrong at all, or to be doing only a little wrong; but to the one who hates, it is the opposite. And to the person who feels desire and is hopeful, if a future event is pleasant, it seems both likely to happen and likely to be good; but to the indifferent or the disgruntled person, it seems the opposite. 

There are three reasons why speakers themselves are persuasive; for there are just so many things that make us believe them, apart from the formal demonstrations of the argument. These are: **practical wisdom**, **virtue**, and **goodwill**. <note>original: *phronesis, arete, kai eunoia*. This is the classic triad of "Ethos"—the speaker's character.</note> 

For people are mistaken in what they say or the advice they give either because of all three of these qualities, or because of one of them. For either because of <term>foolishness</term> <note>original: *aphrosyne*; the opposite of practical wisdom.</note> they do not form opinions correctly; or, while forming correct opinions, because of wickedness they do not say what they really think; or they may be wise and decent, but not well-disposed toward the audience. In this last case, it is possible for them to know what is best and yet not advise it. And besides these, there are no other reasons. 

It follows, then, that the person who appears to possess all these qualities is necessarily persuasive to the listeners. As for the sources from which a person might appear wise and serious, these must be gathered from our earlier divisions concerning the **virtues** <note>Aristotle refers back to Book I, Chapter 9, where he defined various moral excellences.</note>. For from those same sources, a person can present either someone else or themselves as being of such a character. Regarding **goodwill** and **friendship**, we must speak of them in our discussion of...

<summary>Aristotle explains that persuasion depends on the speaker's character (ethos) and the audience's emotional state (pathos). He identifies three essential traits for a credible speaker: practical wisdom, virtue, and goodwill.</summary>
