<meta>This page continues Aristotle's transition into Book II of the Rhetoric. On the previous page, he established that a speaker's persuasiveness depends not just on the logic of the argument, but on the character of the speaker and the emotional state of the audience. He concluded by identifying the three components of a speaker's character: practical wisdom, virtue, and goodwill. He now turns to a systematic analysis of human emotions to help the orator understand how to influence the audience's judgment.</meta>

->RHETORIC. BOOK II. CHAPTER II.<-

...we must now speak concerning the **emotions**. <note>original: *pathe*. These are the passions or "affects"—states of mind that alter our judgment.</note> The emotions are those things through which people, by undergoing a change, differ in their judgments, and which are followed by pain and pleasure; for example, anger, pity, fear, and all other such things, and their opposites. It is necessary to divide the discussion of each into three parts. I mean, for example, concerning anger: in what state of mind are people prone to anger, with whom are they usually angry, and for what reasons? For if we should possess one or two of these points of understanding, but not all three, it would be impossible to inspire anger in the listener; and the same applies to the other emotions. Therefore, just as we mapped out the propositions in the preceding sections, let us do so for these as well, and analyze them in the manner described.

## CHAPTER II.

Let **anger** <note>original: *orge*</note> be defined as a desire, accompanied by pain, for a perceived revenge, caused by a perceived slight <term>slight: *oligoria*; literally "belittlement" or treating someone as having little value</term> directed toward oneself or those near to oneself, when such a slight was undeserved. If this is indeed what anger is, it is necessary that the angry person is always angry with a specific individual—for example, with Cleon, but not with "humanity" in general. <note>Cleon was a prominent and controversial Athenian politician; Aristotle uses him as a specific example of a person someone might hate, rather than a general concept.</note> Furthermore, one is angry because that individual has done, or was about to do, something to the person themselves or to one of their own. Every instance of anger is also followed by a certain pleasure, which arises from the hope of achieving revenge. For it is pleasant to think one will obtain what one aims for; and while no one aims for what appears impossible for them to achieve, the angry person aims for what is possible for them. Therefore, it has been well said of anger:

> Which, far sweeter than dripping honey,
> Swells in the hearts of men.
> <note>This is a quote from Homer’s *Iliad* (18.109), spoken by Achilles regarding his own rage.</note>

For a certain pleasure follows for this reason, and also because people dwell in their minds on the act of revenge. The mental image <note>original: *phantasia*</note> that arises then creates pleasure, just like the images seen in dreams. Since a "slight" is the active expression of an opinion...

<summary>Aristotle defines the emotions and begins a detailed psychological analysis of anger, defining it as a pained desire for revenge triggered by an unmerited slight.</summary>

<meta>This page continues Aristotle's analysis of the causes of anger. # ARISTOTLE

...concerning that which appears to be worth nothing. (For we believe both bad things and good things are worthy of serious attention, as well as things that contribute toward them; but whatever is nothing, or very small, we assume to be worth nothing.) There are three species of **slighting** <term>slighting: *oligoria*; the act of making someone feel small or insignificant</term>: **contempt**, **spite**, and **insult**. 

For the person who shows contempt is slighting; for people feel contempt for those things which they think are worth nothing, and they slight things that they consider worth nothing. Likewise, the person who acts out of **spite** <note>original: *epereasmos*. This refers to actively thwarting someone else's plans just to be a nuisance.</note> also appears to slight; for spite is the thwarting of another's wishes, not so that something might happen for oneself, but so that it might not happen for the other. 

Since, therefore, they do not act to gain anything for themselves, they are slighting the other person. For it is clear that they do not assume the other will do them harm—otherwise they would feel fear rather than slighting—nor do they think the other can benefit them in any way worth mentioning—otherwise they would take care to be a friend. 

And the person who **insults** <term>insult: *hybris*; in Greek law and ethics, this refers to a specific kind of physical or verbal assault intended to shame the victim for the perpetrator's pleasure</term> also slights. For insult consists in doing and saying things that cause shame to the victim, not so that anything else may happen to the perpetrator beyond what has already happened, but merely for the pleasure of it. For those who give back what they received do not insult, but take revenge. 

The cause of the pleasure for those who insult is that they think, by treating others badly, they are even more superior. That is why the young and the wealthy are prone to insult; they think they are superior when they act with such insolence. The essence of insult is **dishonor**; and the one who dishonors, slights. For that which is worth nothing has no honor, whether in terms of evil or good. This is why **Achilles** says in his anger:

> He has dishonored me; for taking my prize, he keeps it, having robbed me himself. <note>original: *Iliad* 1.356. Achilles refers to Agamemnon taking his war-prize, Briseis.</note>

And,

> As if I were some dishonored vagabond; <note>original: *Iliad* 9.648</note>

suggesting that these are the reasons for his anger. People think it appropriate to be treated with great respect by those inferior to them in birth, in pow— <note>The text breaks here mid-word (*dy-*) as it continues to the next page, likely referring to *dynamis* or power.</note>

<summary>Aristotle defines three types of slighting—contempt, spite, and insult—that provoke anger, explaining that they all stem from treating someone as insignificant. He specifically highlights "hybris" as an act of shaming others to assert one's own perceived superiority.</summary>

<keywords>Aristotle, Rhetoric, anger, slighting, contempt, spite, hybris, Achilles, honor, social status</keywords>

<meta>This page continues Aristotle’s exploration of anger (*orgē*). ### RHETORIC. BOOK II. CHAPTER II.

...power, in virtue, and generally in whatever one happens to greatly excel; for example, in money the rich man excels the poor; in speaking, the skilled speaker excels the man unable to speak; the ruler excels the subject; and the man who thinks himself worthy of ruling excels the man who is worthy of being ruled. For this reason it has been said,

->Great is the anger of kings nourished by Zeus.<-
<note>original: "Thymos de megas esti diotrepheon basileon." A quote from Homer's *Iliad* (1.80). The term "Zeus-nourished" refers to the Homeric belief that kings derived their power and temper directly from the king of the gods.</note>

And,

->But even afterward he nurses a grudge, until he fulfills it.<-
<note>original: "Alla ge kai metopisthen echei koton, ophra telessē." From *Iliad* (1.82).</note>

For they are indignant because of their superiority. Furthermore, people get angry at those by whom they think they ought to be treated well; these are people whom they have benefited, or are currently benefiting—either by themselves, or through one of their own people, or through their influence—or whom they want or have wanted to benefit.

It is clear then from these things in what state of mind people get angry, and at whom, and for what reasons. As for the people themselves, they get angry when they are in pain; for the person in pain desires something. Whether someone thwarts them directly—for example, preventing a thirsty person from drinking—or if they seem to do the same thing indirectly; and whether someone works against them, or fails to work with them, or if someone else annoys them when they are in this state, they get angry with all of them. 

Therefore, those who are sick, or in poverty, or in love, or thirsty—generally those who have a desire and do not succeed—are prone to anger and easily provoked; especially toward those who slight <term>slight: *oligoria*; treating something as if it were of no value</term> their current situation. For example, the sick man is angry at those who make light of his illness; the poor man at those who mock his poverty; the soldier at those who mock the war; the lover at those who mock his love; and similarly for the rest. For each person’s current suffering <term>suffering: *pathos*; an emotional state or experience</term> paves the way for their specific anger. 

Furthermore, they get angry if they happen to receive the opposite of what they expect; for that which is contrary to expectation causes more pain, just as that which is contrary to expectation gives more pleasure if what one wishes for happens. This is why seasons, times, and dis...

<summary>Aristotle explains that anger is intensified by a sense of superiority and the frustration of specific needs or desires, especially when that frustration is unexpected.</summary>

<meta>This page continues the analysis of anger from the previous page, specifically focusing on the circumstances and types of people that provoke this emotion. Aristotle is moving from the general definition of "slighting" to the specific social contexts where it occurs.</meta>

### ARISTOTLE

### BOOK II. CHAPTER II.

...positions and ages are clear from these things—which types are easily moved to anger, and when, and where; and that when people are more involved in these states, they are also more easily moved. In themselves, then, being in these states makes them prone to anger. People get angry at those who laugh at them, mock them, and scoff at them; for these people are being <term>insulting</term><note>original: "hybrizousi." Hybris in Greek ethics is not just pride, but a specific type of insult intended to shame the victim for the perpetrator's own pleasure.</note>. And they get angry at those who cause harm in ways that are signs of insult. Such acts must be those that are done neither in retaliation for something else, nor for the benefit of those doing them; for then the act appears to be done solely for the sake of insult.

And people get angry at those who speak ill of them and show <term>contempt</term><note>original: "kataphronousi." Literally to "look down upon" or consider someone of no value.</note> for the things they themselves take most seriously. For example, those who pride themselves on philosophy are angry if someone mocks philosophy; or those who pride themselves on their appearance are angry if someone mocks their appearance; and similarly with other things. This is much more the case if they suspect that they do not possess these qualities at all, or not strongly, or that they are not perceived to have them. But when people strongly believe that they truly possess those qualities for which they are being mocked, they do not care.

And they are angrier at friends than at those who are not friends; for they believe it is more fitting to receive good treatment from them than not. And they get angry at those accustomed to honoring or caring for them, if these people then do not interact with them in the same way; for they think they are being looked down upon by these people—otherwise, they would act as they used to. They also get angry at those who do not return a kindness or do not give back an equal amount. And they get angry at those who do the opposite of what they want, if they are subordinates; for all such people seem to show contempt—some acting as if their victims are inferiors, others acting as though they are receiving favors from inferiors.

And they are especially angry at those who are of no account if they show any <term>slighting</term><note>original: "oligōrōsi." To make little of someone, or to treat them as insignificant.</note>; for the anger arising from slighting is directed toward those for whom it is not fitting to slight others, and it is fitting for inferiors not to slight their superiors. They also get angry at friends if they do not speak or act well toward them; and even more so if they do the opposite; and if they are not sensitive to their needs—as...

<summary>Aristotle explains that anger is most intense when an insult targets a person's insecurities or comes from a friend or subordinate from whom respect is expected. He defines "insult" as harm done without personal gain, intended only to shame the other person.</summary>
<keywords>anger, hybris, contempt, social status, friendship, insecurity, Rhetoric, Aristotle</keywords>

--- Page 86 ---
<meta>This page continues Aristotle's detailed taxonomy of anger. He has just explained that we feel more anger toward friends than strangers because we expect better treatment from them. Here, he explores specific behaviors and social contexts—such as being slighted in front of those we admire—that trigger an angry response.</meta>

# RHETORICA. BOOK II. CHAPTER II.

...be treated well by them, as in the case of Plexippus <note>original: "Antiphōntos Plexippos." A character from a lost tragedy by the playwright Antiphon. Plexippus was the uncle of the hero Meleager; his dismissive behavior led to a fatal conflict.</note> in Antiphon’s play toward Meleager; for failing to notice someone is a sign of **slighting** <note>original: "oligorias." This is Aristotle’s central term for the cause of anger—treating someone as if they have little or no value.</note>. We do not fail to notice the things we truly care about.

People also get angry at those who rejoice in their misfortunes, and generally at those who remain cheerful while they themselves are suffering; for this is a sign of either an enemy or someone who slights them. They are also angry at those who do not care if they cause pain; this is why people get angry at those who bring bad news. 

Furthermore, they feel anger toward those who listen to stories about their failures or who witness their flaws; such people behave like those who either slight them or are their enemies. While friends share in one's pain, everyone feels pain when their own weaknesses are exposed to view.

Moreover, people feel anger toward those who slight them in front of five specific groups:
1. Those with whom they compete for honor.
2. Those whom they admire.
3. Those by whom they wish to be admired.
4. Those before whom they feel shame.
5. Those who feel shame before them.

If someone slights them in the presence of these people, they feel a much greater anger. They also get angry at those who slight the people it would be shameful for them not to defend—such as parents, children, wives, or those under their authority. 

Anger is also felt toward those who do not return a favor, for such a slight is contrary to what is fitting. And toward those who use **irony** <note>original: "eironeuomenois." In this context, a form of mock-modesty or "playing dumb" to make someone else look foolish.</note> when the other person is being serious; for irony carries a sense of contempt. 

They also get angry at those who do good deeds for others but not for them; this is also contemptuous, as it implies that the person is not worthy of what everyone else receives. **Forgetfulness** is also a cause of anger, such as forgetting someone's name, even if it seems a small thing; for forgetfulness appears to be a sign of slighting. Forgetfulness arises from **negligence** <note>original: "ameleian"</note>, and negligence is a form of slighting.

We have now discussed the people at whom men are angry, the states of mind they are in, and the reasons why. It is clear, then, that an orator must use his speech to put his audience into the state of mind of those prone to anger, and represent his opponents as being guilty of the things that provoke anger, and as being the kind of people with whom we get angry.

<summary>Aristotle concludes his analysis of anger by listing social triggers, such as being slighted in front of peers and the negligence implied by forgetting a name. He advises the orator to use these insights to provoke an audience's indignation toward an opponent.</summary>

<keywords>anger, slighting, social status, rhetoric, irony, negligence, Plexippus, Meleager, audience emotion</keywords>

<meta>This page transitions from the causes of anger to its opposite: mildness or the settling of the emotions. Aristotle continues his systematic analysis of human passions by defining the conditions under which anger is neutralized or never arises in the first place.</meta>

# ARISTOTLE
## CHAPTER III.

Since being angry is the opposite of becoming mild, and anger is the opposite of **mildness** <note>original: "praotēti." This refers to a state of gentleness or even-temperedness.</note>, we must determine the state of mind of those who are mild, toward whom they feel mild, and by what means they are calmed. Let **mildness** <note>original: "praünsis."</note> be defined as a settling and quieting of anger.

If people feel anger toward those who **slight** <note>original: "oligorousin." To treat someone as having little or no importance.</note> them, and if slighting is a voluntary act, it is evident that they feel mild toward those who do no such thing, or who do so involuntarily, or who at least appear to do so. They are also mild toward those who intended the opposite of what they actually did, and toward those who treat themselves in the same way they treat others—for no one is thought to slight themselves.

They are also mild toward those who admit their faults and feel regret; for, taking the pain felt by the wrongdoer over their actions as a kind of punishment, their anger ceases. This is evident in how we punish servants: we punish more severely those who deny and talk back, but we stop being angry with those who admit they are being justly punished. The reason for this is that it is **shamelessness** <note>original: "anaischyntia." A lack of proper social respect or modesty.</note> to deny what is obvious, and shamelessness is a form of slighting and contempt; certainly, we do not feel shame before those whom we deeply despise.

People are also mild toward those who humble themselves before them and do not talk back; for such people seem to admit they are inferior, and those who are inferior feel fear—and no one who is afraid slights another. That anger ceases toward those who humble themselves is shown even by dogs, who do not bite those who sit down.

They are also mild toward those who are serious when they themselves are serious, for this suggests they are being taken seriously rather than being held in contempt. They also feel mild toward those who have performed greater favors for them, and toward those who ask and beg for forgiveness, as these people are acting with greater humility. Finally, they are mild toward those who are not prone to insult, mockery, or slighting—at least not toward anyone in particular, or certainly not toward good people...

<summary>Aristotle defines mildness as the settling of anger and identifies the specific behaviors—such as repentance, humility, or unintentional harm—that cause a person’s wrath to dissipate.</summary>
<keywords>Aristotle, Rhetoric, mildness, anger, slighting, shamelessness, repentance, humility, social psychology</keywords>

--- Page 88 ---
<meta>This page continues the discussion from the previous page regarding the conditions that lead to mildness (praotēs). It focuses on the psychological states that are incompatible with anger and the social circumstances that cause anger to dissipate or be diverted.</meta>

### RHETORIC. BOOK II. CHAPTER III.

...or [those who do not slight] people of the same kind as themselves. In general, we should look at the factors that produce **mildness** by observing the opposites of what causes anger. People do not feel anger toward those they fear or respect; for so long as they are in such a state, they do not get angry. It is impossible to be afraid and angry at the same time.

Also, people either do not feel anger, or feel it less, toward those who acted out of their own anger; for they do not appear to have acted out of **slight** <note>original: "oligōrian." In Aristotle's system, a slight is a voluntary act of treating something as worthless.</note>. No one who is angry slights another, because slighting is a painless act, whereas anger is always accompanied by pain. People are also mild toward those who show respect to them.

It is also clear that people are mild when their state of mind is the opposite of being angry: for instance, when at play, in laughter, at a festival, in prosperity, in success, in fulfillment—in general, in a state of freedom from pain, or refined pleasure, or reasonable hope. Furthermore, they are mild when time has passed and the anger is no longer fresh; for time puts an end to anger.

A punishment previously inflicted on someone else can also quiet a greater anger toward another. Therefore, Philocrates <note>An Athenian orator often criticized for his dealings with Philip II of Macedon.</note> was right when someone asked him—while the people were angry with him—"Why do you not defend yourself?" He replied, "Not yet." "When then?" "When I see someone else being slandered." For people become mild when they have exhausted their anger on someone else. This happened in the case of Ergophilus <note>An Athenian general tried for his failures in 362 BCE.</note>. Although the people were more furious with him than with Callisthenes <note>Another Athenian general tried and condemned around the same time.</note>, they acquitted him because they had condemned Callisthenes to death the day before.

They also become mild if they secure a conviction, or if the offenders have suffered a greater evil than the angry party would have inflicted; for they feel as if they have already obtained their vengeance. They are also mild if they believe they themselves are in the wrong and are suffering justly. For anger does not arise against what is just; in such cases, they no longer believe they are suffering undeservedly, which is what we defined anger to be. Therefore, one should first rebuke a person with an explanation <note>original: "tō logō." By using reason or speech to explain the cause of punishment.</note>; for even slaves are less indignant when punished if they understand the reason. Finally, they are mild toward those they think will not realize that the punishment comes from them and...

<summary>Aristotle explores the circumstances that neutralize anger, such as fear, the passage of time, or the exhaustion of rage on a different target. He argues that perceived justice and reasonable explanations can prevent or soothe the feeling of being slighted.</summary>

<keywords>mildness, anger, psychology of emotion, Philocrates, Ergophilus, Callisthenes, justice, vengeance, Athenian law</keywords>

<meta>This page continues from the previous discussion on the emotion of mildness or calmness (praotēs). Aristotle is concluding his analysis of how anger is neutralized and begins a new chapter on the nature of friendship (philia) and hatred (misos).</meta>

->ARISTOTLE<-

...for the things they have suffered; for **anger** <note>original: "orgē"</note> is directed at individuals. This is clear from the **definition** <note>Aristotle defines anger earlier in the *Rhetoric* as a desire for conspicuous revenge for a conspicuous slight against oneself or one's own.</note>. That is why it was rightly written:

> Tell him it was Odysseus, sacker of cities;

<note>Homer, *Odyssey* 9.504. Odysseus shouts this to the blinded Cyclops, Polyphemus. Aristotle argues that anger requires the target to know who is punishing them and why, otherwise the "revenge" feels incomplete.</note>

as if he would not have been avenged if the other had not realized both by whom he was blinded and for what reason. Therefore, people do not feel anger toward those who cannot perceive it, nor toward those who are dead, as they have suffered the ultimate end and will feel no more pain—nor will they experience the sensation which those who are angry seek to inflict. Thus the poet spoke well of Hector, when he wished to make Achilles cease his anger toward the dead man:

> For he insults the senseless earth in his fury.

<note>Homer, *Iliad* 24.54. Apollo speaks these words to the other gods, criticizing Achilles for dragging Hector's corpse around the tomb of Patroclus.</note>

It is clear, then, that those wishing to calm an audience must speak from these **topics** <note>original: "topōn." These are rhetorical "places" or strategies used to construct an argument.</note>: they should prepare the listeners to be in such a state themselves, and represent those with whom they are angry as either formidable, or worthy of respect, or as having acted as a favor, or involuntarily, or as being exceedingly pained by what they have done.

### CHAPTER IV.

Whom people **love** <note>original: "philousi"</note> and whom they **hate**, and for what reasons, let us discuss after defining **friendship** <note>original: "philian"</note> and the act of loving. Let **loving** <note>original: "philein"</note> be defined as wishing for someone the things that one considers good, for that person's sake and not for one's own, and being inclined to do these things to the best of one's ability. A **friend** <note>original: "philos"</note> is one who loves and is loved in return. Those who believe they stand in this relation to each other consider themselves friends. These things being granted, a friend must necessarily be one who shares in another's joy at good things and in their grief at painful things, not for any other reason but for the sake of the other. For all people rejoice when what they wish for happens, and grieve at the opposite; thus, pain and pleasure are signs of what one wishes. Furthermore, friends are those for whom the same things are good or bad, and those who have the same friends and...

<summary>Aristotle concludes his analysis of anger by noting it requires a conscious target, then defines friendship as wishing good for another for their own sake and sharing in their joys and sorrows.</summary>

<meta>This page continues Aristotle’s discussion on friendship (philia). Having defined the feeling of friendship in the previous section, he now lists the specific types of people we are inclined to love or like, and the reasons why we form these bonds.</meta>

<page-num>77</page-num>
<header>RHETORIC. BOOK II. CHAPTER IV.</header>

...those who have the same enemies; for it is necessary that these people 
desire the same things. So, by wishing for another person what one wishes 
for oneself, a person appears to be a friend to that other. We also love 
those who have done good <note>original: "pepoiēkotas eu." This refers to the act of "benefaction" or being a patron.</note>, either to ourselves or to those we care for; 
especially if the benefit was great, or performed eagerly, or in critical 
circumstances, and for our own sake; or those whom we believe wish to do 
good. We also love the friends of our friends, and those who love the 
people we love; and those who are loved by those we love. 

And we love those who share our enemies, and who hate the people we 
hate, and those who are hated by the people we hate; for to all such 
people, the same things seem to be good as they do to us, so that they 
wish for the things that are good for us—which was our definition 
of a friend. Further, we love those who are capable of doing good in 
matters of money and safety; therefore we honor the **liberal** 
<note>original: "eleutherious." In Aristotle’s ethics, the liberal person is one who is generous and knows how to give money to the right people in the right amounts.</note> and the brave. And we love the **just**; 
and we assume those who do not live at the expense of others to be such. 
Such are those who live by their own labor, and among these, those who 
live by **farming** <note>Aristotle frequently expresses a preference for farmers (*geōrgoi*), viewing them as the most stable and virtuous class of citizens because they are too busy to be meddlesome but self-sufficient enough to be honest.</note>; and among other professions, those who 
work with their own hands. 

We also love the temperate, because they are not unjust; and the 
peaceable, for the same reason. Also those with whom we wish to be 
friends, if they appear to wish it too. Such are those who are good in 
respect to **virtue** <note>original: "aretē." Excellence of character.</note>, and those who are well-reputed either among 
all people, or among the best, or among those we admire, or among 
those who admire us. 

Further, we love those who are pleasant to spend time and pass the 
day with; such are the easy-tempered, those who are not prone to 
point out our mistakes, and those who are not contentious or 
quarrelsome. For all such people are combative; and those who 
combat us seem to wish for the opposite of what we want. 

And we love those who are **witty** <note>original: "epidexioi." Literally "dexterous" or "tactful," referring here to social grace and the ability to handle humor without causing offense.</note>, both in making a joke 
and in taking one; for in both cases, they are striving toward the 
same... 

<summary>Aristotle catalogs the traits and social circumstances that lead to friendship, highlighting shared values, mutual enemies, and specific virtues like generosity and honesty.</summary>

<meta>This page continues Aristotle’s systematic list of the qualities and types of people that elicit the feeling of friendship or liking (philia). The previous page established that we like those who are generous, brave, and just; here, Aristotle turns to more social and interpersonal traits, such as wit, shared values, and the absence of rivalry.</meta>

<page-num>78</page-num>
<header>ARISTOTLE</header>

...to those near them; those who are able to take a joke, and who can joke **gracefully** <note>original: "emmelōs." This literally means "harmoniously" or "in tune." In Aristotle's view, a truly social person avoids being either a crude buffoon or a humorless boor.</note>. And we like those who praise the good things we possess, especially those which we ourselves fear we do not actually have. Also, we like those who are clean in their appearance, in their dress, and in their whole way of life. And we like those who are not reproachful, neither regarding our faults nor regarding the favors they have done for us; for both behaviors make a person appear prone to find fault. 

And we like those who do not bear grudges, nor preserve the memory of grievances, but are easy to reconcile; for however we see them behave toward others, we expect them to behave toward us as well. And those who are not gossips, and who do not notice the faults of their neighbors or even their own, but rather notice their good points; for this is what a good man does. 

And we like those who do not resist us when we are angry or serious; for such people are quarrelsome. Also, those who take us seriously in some way—for instance, by admiring us, or considering us excellent, or taking delight in our company. We feel this way especially regarding those qualities for which we ourselves most wish to be admired, or to be thought excellent, or to be pleasant. 

And we like those who are similar to us and pursue the same interests, provided they do not bother us and provided their livelihood does not come from the same source; for then it becomes a case of **"potter against potter"** <note>original: "kerameus keramei." This refers to a famous Greek proverb from the poet Hesiod: "potter is jealous of potter." It means that people in the same trade often become rivals rather than friends.</note>. And we like those who desire the same things as we do, provided it is possible for both to share in them at the same time; if that is not possible, the same kind of conflict arises. 

And we like those toward whom we feel such that we are not ashamed of things involving mere **reputation** <note>original: "doxa." This refers to what people think of us, rather than the objective truth of our character.</note>, provided we do not despise them. And we like those before whom we feel shame regarding things that are truly shameful. And those with whom we are in honorable competition, or by whom we wish to be emulated rather than envied; these we either love or wish to be friends with. And we like those with whom we cooperate for good ends, provided greater evils are not likely to result for ourselves. And...

<summary>Aristotle details the social graces and shared circumstances that foster friendship, such as wit, lack of grudges, and mutual admiration. He also observes that competition for the same resources or professional status (the "potter against potter" principle) can destroy potential bonds.</summary>

<meta>This page continues Aristotle’s list of characteristics and behaviors that foster friendship (philia) and shifts into a comparative analysis of anger (orgē) and hatred (misos). <page-num>79</page-num>
<header>RHETORIC. BOOK II. CHAPTER IV.</header>

...to those who love their friends equally whether they are absent or present; for this reason, everyone likes those who act this way toward the dead. And generally, we like those who are very fond of their friends and do not abandon them; for among good men, we especially like those who are good at being a friend. Also, we like those who do not put on an act <note>original: "plattomenous," meaning to mold, fashion, or feign a character.</note> before us; such are those who are willing to speak of their own flaws. For it has been said that among friends we are not ashamed of things that are merely for the sake of reputation; if, then, one who is ashamed does not truly love, then one who is not ashamed seems to be a lover of his friend. And we like those who are not intimidating, and in whom we have confidence; for no one likes someone whom they fear.

The types of friendship are: companionship, intimacy, kinship, and the like. The things that produce <note>original: "poiētika," the efficient causes or things that create the state.</note> friendship are: performing a favor, doing so without being asked, and not making it known after it is done; for in this way it appears to be done for the friend's own sake and not for some other reason.

Regarding enmity and hatred, it is clear that they should be studied by looking at their opposites. The things that produce enmity are anger, spiteful behavior, and slander. Now, anger arises from things directed against oneself; but enmity can exist even without things directed against oneself, for if we simply judge a person to be a certain kind of character, we hate them. Furthermore, anger is always directed at specific individuals, such as *Callias* or *Socrates*, whereas hatred is also directed toward whole classes; for everyone hates the thief and the <term>sycophant</term> <note>original: "sykophantēn." In ancient Athens, this referred to a professional informer or someone who brought malicious lawsuits for profit, rather than a flatterer.</note>. 

And while anger is curable by time, hatred is incurable. Also, anger aims at causing the other person pain, but hatred aims rather at doing them harm. For the angry person wants his target to feel the pain <note>He wants the target to know why they are being punished.</note>, but to the hater, that makes no difference. All painful things are felt by the senses; but the greatest evils—such as injustice and folly—are the least felt, for the presence of vice causes no pain to the one who has it. Finally, anger is accompanied by pain, but hatred is not; for the angry man feels pain...

<summary>Aristotle defines the social virtues that maintain friendship and then contrasts anger with hatred, noting that hatred is a settled disposition against whole groups while anger is a reactive impulse against individuals.</summary>
<keywords>friendship, affection, social virtue, enmity, hatred vs anger, sycophancy, classes of people, Callias, Socrates</keywords>

<meta>This page continues the comparison between anger and hatred, concluding Chapter 4, and begins Chapter 5 with a psychological analysis of fear. It builds on the previous discussion of how an orator can manipulate these emotions in an audience to define who is a friend or an enemy.</meta>

<page-num>80</page-num>

...feels pain; but the one who hates does not. And the former <note>The person feeling anger.</note>, if many things were to happen to their enemy, might feel pity; but the latter <note>The person feeling hatred.</note> would feel no pity for anything. For the person who is angry wants the one they are angry with to suffer in return <note>original: "antipathein," to suffer back or feel in response.</note>; but the one who hates wants the object of their hate to cease to exist. 

It is clear from these points, then, that it is possible for an orator to demonstrate who are enemies and who are friends, to make those who are not yet so appear to be, to dissolve the claims of those who only pretend to be friends, and to push those who are in a dispute—whether through anger or through hatred—toward whichever side one chooses for the sake of the argument. 

What things people fear, and whom they fear, and in what state of mind they feel fear, will be made clear by the following.

### CHAPTER V.

Let fear be defined as a kind of pain or agitation resulting from a mental image <note>original: "phantasia," the faculty by which an image is presented to the mind.</note> of a coming evil that is either destructive or painful. For one does not fear all evils—for instance, one does not fear whether they will be unjust or slow—but only those that are capable of causing great pain or total destruction. And even then, only if these evils do not appear far off, but close at hand, so as to be imminent; for people do not fear things that are very far away. 

Everyone knows, for example, that they will die; but because it is not near, they do not care about it. If, then, fear is this, it follows that such things are fearful as appear to have great power to destroy, or to cause harms that lead toward great pain. Therefore, even the signs of such things are fearful; for the fearful thing appears to be close. For this is what **danger** is: the approach of something fearful. 

Such things include the enmity and anger of those who have the power to do something; for it is clear that they both want to act and are able to, and thus they are close to acting. Also, injustice <term>adikia: the violation of law or fairness</term> that possesses power is fearful; for the unjust person is unjust by deliberate choice <note>original: "proairesis," a purposeful moral decision.</note>. 

And virtue when it is outraged <note>original: "hybrizomenē," referring to "hubris" or suffering an intentional insult to one's honor.</note>, if it has power, is fearful; for it is clear that when it is insulted, it always chooses to retaliate, and if it has power, it can do so now. Also, the fear felt by those who have the power to act is itself fearful; for such a person must necessarily be in a state of preparation to strike first. Since...

<summary>Aristotle distinguishes hatred from anger—noting that hatred seeks the destruction of its object—and defines fear as the mental distress caused by the proximity of a destructive evil. He identifies the specific conditions, such as the power and intent of others, that make a situation truly dangerous.</summary>

<meta>This page continues Aristotle’s analysis of fear from the end of the previous page. Having defined fear as a response to imminent harm, he now lists the specific categories of people and circumstances that typically inspire this emotion in an audience. This section is vital for an orator seeking to make an opponent appear dangerous or a situation appear dire.</meta>

<page-num>81</page-num>
<header>RHETORIC. BOOK II. CHAPTER V.</header>

...fear it. <note>This completes the thought from the previous page: because death seems far off, people do not dwell on it.</note> And since most people are rather bad, are slaves to profit, and are cowardly in times of danger, it is generally a fearful thing to be at the mercy of another. Thus, those who are aware that you have committed a crime are fearsome, as they might either denounce you or abandon you. Also fearsome are those who have the power to do wrong to those who are capable of being wronged; for, as a general rule, people do wrong whenever they can.

Those who have been wronged, or who believe they are being wronged, are also fearsome, for they are always watching for the opportune moment <note>original: "kairon," a critical or decisive moment.</note>. And those who have committed a wrong, if they possess power, are fearful because they dread retaliation <note>original: "antipathein," to suffer back or experience a corresponding pain.</note>; for we have already assumed that such a thing is fearful.

Rivals for the same prizes are also fearsome, whenever it is impossible for both to have them at once; for people are always at war with such rivals. Those who are fearsome to those stronger than ourselves are also fearsome to us; for they would be even more capable of hurting us than they would those who are stronger. For the same reason, those whom our superiors fear are also to be feared.

Also fearsome are those who attack those weaker than themselves; for they are either already fearsome or will become so as their power grows. Among those who have been wronged, or enemies, or rivals, it is not the quick-tempered and the plain-spoken <note>original: "parrhēsiastikoi," those who speak with bold, frank, or even blunt sincerity.</note> who are to be feared, but the mild, the ironic <note>original: "eirōnes," those who practice dissimulation, hiding their strength or their true intentions through self-deprecating or deceptive speech.</note>, and the unscrupulous; for because they are subtle, it is unclear if they are close to striking, and so it is never obvious that they are far from doing so.

All fearful things are even more fearful if, when a mistake is made, it is impossible to set it right—either because it is completely impossible or because the remedy is not in our own power but in the hands of our opponents. Also fearful are things for which there is no help, or where help is not easily obtained. To put it simply, those things are fearful which, when they happen or are about to happen to others, excite our pity.

These, then, are the most important things that are fearful and which people fear. Now let us discuss the state of mind in which people feel fear.

If fear is accompanied by the expectation of undergoing some destructive suffering <note>original: "pathos," a term referring to an experience, emotion, or "passive" undergoing of something, often painful.</note>, it is clear that no one feels fear who...

--- Page 95 ---
<meta>This page continues Aristotle’s exploration of the psychology of fear. Having previously established that fear is a pain or disturbance caused by the mental image of a coming evil, he now examines the specific states of mind that make a person either susceptible to fear or immune to it. This leads directly into his definition of confidence, which he treats as the logical opposite of fear.</meta>

<page-num>82</page-num>
<header>ARISTOTLE</header>
<page-type>text</page-type>

...thinking they will not suffer anything, nor suffer those specific things they do not expect, nor from those people they do not expect, nor at a time they do not expect. It follows, then, that those who feel fear are those who expect to suffer something—fearing specific people, specific harms, and at specific times. 

Those who believe they are beyond the possibility of suffering include those in the midst of great prosperity, or who believe themselves to be so; this makes them insolent, contemptuous, and reckless. Such traits are produced by wealth, physical strength, having many friends, and power. Neither do those feel fear who believe they have already suffered every horror and have grown cold <note>original: "apepsygmenoi," meaning chilled or cooled down, suggesting a loss of vital heat or emotional response.</note> toward the future—such as those who are already being led to execution <note>original: "apotympanizomenoi," referring to a form of capital punishment involving being beaten or exposed on a wooden frame.</note>. 

Rather, for someone to feel fear, there must remain some underlying hope of safety for which they are anxious. This is evidenced by the fact that fear makes people deliberate <note>original: "bouleutikous," making one inclined to take counsel or weigh options.</note>; yet no one deliberates about things that are truly hopeless. 

Therefore, whenever it is better for the audience to feel fear, the orator must make them feel that they are the kind of people who are liable to suffer; he can do this by showing that others greater than they have suffered. He should point to their equals who are suffering or have suffered, and at the hands of people they did not expect, and suffering things they did not expect, at times they did not expect.

Since it is now clear what fear is, what things are fearful, and the state of mind of those who are afraid, it is also clear from these points what **confidence** is, what things inspire it, and in what state of mind people are confident. For **confidence** <term>tharsos: a state of boldness, courage, or the absence of dread</term> is the opposite of fear, and that which inspires confidence is the opposite of that which inspires fear. Thus, confidence is the hope, accompanied by a mental image <note>original: "phantasias," a vivid mental representation or "seeing" the situation in the mind's eye.</note>, that things which bring safety are near at hand, while things to be feared either do not exist or are far away. 

Things are confidence-inspiring if dangers are far off and safety is near. Confidence also arises if there are ways to set things right, or means of help—whether many, or great, or both. It is also found in those who have neither been wronged nor have wronged others. Furthermore, confidence exists when rivals <note>original: "antagonistai," those competing for the same goals.</note> either do not exist at all or have no power; or if those who have power are our friends...

<summary>Aristotle explains that fear requires a glimmer of hope to exist, as the "already ruined" and "arrogantly wealthy" feel no dread. He then defines confidence as the hopeful expectation of safety and the absence of immediate danger.</summary>

<keywords>Aristotle, Rhetoric, fear, confidence, tharsos, phobos, deliberation, hope, security, persuasion</keywords>

--- Page 96 ---
<meta>This page concludes Aristotle’s discussion on the causes of confidence—the counterpart to fear—and begins his analysis of shame and shamelessness. It follows the previous page's discussion on how an orator can manipulate an audience's sense of security or vulnerability.</meta>

<page-num>83</page-num>
<header>RHETORIC. BOOK II. CHAPTER VI.</header>

...others <note>original: "loi," the end of "alloi" (others), continuing from the previous page's discussion on relying on allies.</note>; either because they have done good to them, or have had good done to them. Or if those whom these things benefit are more numerous, or more powerful, or both.

People are **confident** in their own state of mind if they believe they have been successful in many things and have not suffered misfortune. Or if they have frequently encountered dangers and escaped them; for humans become fearless in two ways: either by being inexperienced with danger, or by having the means to face it. For instance, in dangers at sea, those who are inexperienced with storms feel confident about what is to come, as do those who have resources to help them because of their experience. 

Confidence also arises when a situation is not frightening to one's equals, nor to those who are inferior, or to those over whom one feels superior. People believe themselves superior to those they have already conquered—either the individuals themselves, or those who were their superiors or equals. 

Furthermore, people feel bold if they believe they possess more and greater advantages that make others fear them; these include an abundance of wealth, physical strength, many friends, territory, and military preparations—either all of these or the most significant ones. They also feel confident if they have done no wrong—either to no one at all, or not to many, or at least not to the specific people they might fear. 

And generally, confidence is felt if their relationship with the gods is good, both in general and specifically regarding **signs** <note>original: "semeion," referring to omens or portents.</note> and **oracles** <note>original: "logion," divine utterances or prophetic words.</note>. For anger is a bold emotion, and the sense of being the victim of an injustice rather than the perpetrator produces anger; the divine is traditionally understood to come to the aid of those who are being wronged. Finally, people feel confident when they attack first, believing they will either suffer nothing or that they will certainly succeed. This concludes the discussion of the things that cause fear and confidence.

# CHAPTER VI.

The things of which people are **ashamed**, or for which they feel no shame, and toward whom they feel this way, and in what state of mind, will be clear from what follows. Let **shame** <term>aischyne: a sense of disgrace or social pain rather than just private guilt</term> be defined as a certain pain or disturbance regarding those things that lead to a loss of reputation...

<summary>Aristotle explains that confidence arises from a history of success, superior resources, a clear conscience, or a belief in divine favor. He then transitions to Chapter 6, defining shame as a form of pain regarding actions that damage one's reputation.</summary>

--- Page 97 ---
<meta>This page continues Aristotle’s exploration of the emotions, specifically defining shame and shamelessness. It picks up from a definition of shame as a kind of pain or agitation regarding acts that lead to a loss of reputation.</meta>

<page-num>84</page-num>
<header>ARISTOTLE</header>

...appearing to bring <term>disgrace</term> <note>original: "kakon," literally "evils" or "bad things," but here referring to shameful misfortunes</note>, whether present, past, or future. **Shamelessness** is a kind of slighting and apathy regarding these very same things. If **shame** is indeed what we have defined it to be, it follows that one must feel shame at such disgraces as appear shameful to oneself, or to those whose opinion one values. Such things are those that result from <term>vice</term> <note>original: "kakia"</note>; for example, throwing away one's shield or fleeing in battle, for these come from **cowardice**; or withholding a deposit <note>a "deposit" refers to money or goods left in someone's trust</note>, for this comes from **injustice**.

It is also shameful to have sexual relations with those one should not, or where one should not, or when one should not; for these come from **licentiousness**. Also, to seek gain from small things, or from shameful things, or from the helpless—such as the poor or the dead. Hence the proverb: "to take even from a corpse"; for these are signs of **base greed** and **illiberality** <note>original: "aneleutheria," the opposite of being a "free" or generous person</note>.

It is also shameful not to give financial help when one is able, or to give less than one can; and to accept help from those who are less well-off than oneself. Likewise, to borrow when it will look like begging, or to beg when it looks like demanding repayment, or to demand repayment when it looks like begging; and to praise someone just to seem as if you are begging; and to persist in begging even after being refused. All these are signs of **illiberality**.

To praise someone to their face is a sign of **flattery**; as is over-praising their good qualities while smoothing over their faults, or grieving excessively when another is in pain. All other such behaviors are signs of flattery.

Failing to endure hardships which elders, or the luxurious, or those in higher authority, or generally those who are weaker endure; all these are signs of **softness**. To accept benefits from another, especially to do so frequently, and then to reproach someone for the favors you have done for them; these are all signs of **small-mindedness** and **humiliation**.

To talk constantly about oneself and make grand promises, or to claim the achievements of others as one's own; these are signs of **boastfulness**. Similarly...

<summary>Aristotle defines shame and shamelessness, categorizing various social and moral failings—such as cowardice, greed, and flattery—as the specific sources of a shameful reputation.</summary>

--- Page 98 ---
<meta>This page continues Aristotle’s discussion of the causes of shame, moving from specific vices (like cowardice and greed) to the social context of shame—specifically, the importance of failing to meet the standards of one's peers and the role of the "audience" in feeling disgrace.</meta>

<page-num>85</page-num>
<header>RHETORIC. BOOK II. CHAPTER VI. 85</header>

...and the same applies to the actions, signs, and similar traits of each of the other <term>vices of character</term> <note>original: "kakion tou ethous," referring to moral failings rather than physical flaws</note>; for they are disgraceful and cause shame. In addition to these, it is shameful not to share in the fine things which everyone, or all those like ourselves, or most of them, share in. By **those like ourselves**, I mean those of the same nation, fellow citizens, those of the same age, relatives—in general, those who are on an equal footing. For it is already a cause of shame not to share in what they have; for example, in education to a certain degree, and similarly in other matters.

All these things are felt more strongly if they appear to be one's own fault; for then they clearly stem from **vice**, if one is personally the cause of the things that have happened, are happening, or are about to happen. 

People also feel shame when suffering, having suffered, or being about to suffer such things as bring dishonor and reproach. These are things that involve submitting to shameful physical services or shameful actions, among which is being the victim of <term>outrage</term> <note>original: "hybrizesthai," referring to being treated with insolence or physical/sexual violation intended to humiliate</note>. Some of these relate to **licentiousness**—whether voluntary or involuntary—and others relate to violence, when suffered involuntarily. For enduring such treatment and not defending oneself stems from a lack of manliness or from cowardice.

These, then, are the sorts of things people are ashamed of. Since **shame** is a <term>mental image</term> <note>original: "phantasia," an appearance or impression in the mind</note> concerning loss of reputation, and is felt for its own sake rather than for its eventual consequences; and since no one cares about reputation except on account of those who hold the opinion; it necessarily follows that we feel shame before those whose opinion we value. 

We value the opinion of those who admire us, those whom we admire, those by whom we wish to be admired, those with whom we are in competition <note>original: "philotimeitai," literally "striving for honor" against others</note>, and those whose opinion we do not despise. Now, people wish to be admired by, and do admire, those who possess some honorable good, or those from whom they happen to need something badly which the others have the power to give—for instance, those who are in love. They compete with their equals. And they care about the opinion of the wise as being truthful; such people include the elderly...

<summary>Aristotle explains that shame arises from failing to meet the standards of one's social equals and from being the victim of humiliating treatment. He argues that shame is inherently social, felt primarily in relation to people whose respect we desire or whose wisdom we acknowledge.</summary>
<keywords>shame, vice, equality, reputation, hubris, social standing, admiration, Aristotle</keywords>

--- Page 99 ---
<meta>This page continues Aristotle’s exploration of the social dimensions of shame, focusing on specific groups of people whose presence or opinion intensifies the feeling of disgrace. Aristotle transitions from the types of actions that are shameful to the "audience" that makes them so.</meta>

<page-num>86</page-num>

->**ARISTOTLE**<-

...and the educated. We also feel shame regarding things done **before one's eyes** and in the light of day; hence the proverb, "Shame dwells in the eyes." <note>original: "en ophthalmois einai aidō." This ancient Greek proverb suggests that the physical presence and gaze of others are the primary triggers for the feeling of shame.</note> For this reason, people feel more shame before those who will always be present with them, and those who pay close attention to them, because in both cases they are "before their eyes." 

We also feel shame before those who are not guilty of the same faults; for it is clear that their views are the opposite of our own. And we feel it before those who are not inclined to forgive those seen to be erring; for what a man does himself, he is said not to feel <term>indignation</term> <note>original: "nemesan," referring to a righteous anger or resentment at another's undeserved luck or bad behavior</note> toward his neighbors for doing; so, it follows that what he does not do himself, he will clearly feel indignation toward in others. 

We also fear shame before those likely to report things to many people; for it makes no difference if one is not seen personally, if one's actions are reported. Those likely to report things are the victims of injustice—because they are always on the watch for a chance at payback—and also **slanderers**; for if slanderers speak ill even of those who do no wrong, they will do so all the more of those who do. 

We also feel shame before those whose main pastime is the faults of their neighbors, such as mockers and **comic poets**; for these are, in a way, slanderers and reporters of news. We also feel it before those in whose presence we have never failed; for we stand in the position of being admired by them. This is why people feel shame even before those who ask something of them for the first time, as they have not yet lost their reputation among them. 

Such people include those who have recently wished to be our friends (for they have seen only our best side); this is why the reply of **Euripides** <note>The tragic playwright Euripides reportedly gave a sharp-witted response to the people of Syracuse when they asked a favor, refusing to do anything that would lower their high opinion of him.</note> to the Syracusans was appropriate. Also, among old acquaintances, we feel shame before those who know nothing of our faults. 

People feel shame not only for the shameful acts themselves, but also for the **signs** of them; for instance, not only for sexual acts, but also for the signs of having engaged in them; and not only for doing shameful things, but also for talking about them. 

Similarly, they feel shame not only before the people mentioned, but also before those who will reveal things to them, such as their servants and friends. In general, however, people do not feel shame before those whose opinion of the truth they greatly despise... <note>The text cuts off here, likely continuing with those whose judgment we ignore, such as children or animals.</note>

<summary>Aristotle explains that shame is heightened by the presence of "eyes"—specifically people we respect, those who do not share our vices, and those likely to spread rumors, like comic poets or the victims of our actions.</summary>

<meta>This page continues Aristotle’s analysis of shame, specifically examining how our relationship with an audience dictates the level of disgrace we feel. Aristotle moves from general definitions to specific historical anecdotes, illustrating how the gaze of others influences human behavior and political decisions.</meta>

### RHETORIC. BOOK II. CH. VI.

...for no one feels shame before children or animals. Nor do we feel shame for the same things before acquaintances as we do before strangers; rather, before our acquaintances, we feel shame regarding things that are truly considered disgraceful, but before those from afar, we feel it only for things defined by law or custom. 

People in the following dispositions would feel shame: first, if there are certain people present toward whom they feel as we described those whom they respect. These were, as we said, those they admire, those who admire them, those by whom they wish to be admired, those with whom they are in competition, or those from whom they need some favor—which they realize they will not obtain if they fall into ill repute. 

And these witnesses might be actually seeing them; just as Cydias <note>Cydias was an Athenian orator of the 4th century BCE, known for this specific speech regarding the island of Samos.</note> spoke to the people about the **allotment of land** <note>original: "klērouchias." This refers to a system where Athenian citizens were settled on territory taken from a conquered or rebellious state.</note> in Samos. For he urged the Athenians to imagine that the Greeks were standing around them in a circle, actually watching them, and not merely going to hear about the decree they were about to vote for. 

We also feel shame if such people are nearby, or are likely to find out about our actions. This is why people do not wish to be seen in misfortune by those who once envied them; for enviers are also admirers. Furthermore, people feel shame when they have deeds or circumstances that bring disgrace, whether these are their own, or those of their ancestors, or those of others with whom they have a close kinship. In general, they feel shame on behalf of those for whom they themselves feel responsible—this includes the people already mentioned, as well as those who look to them as followers, or those for whom they have been teachers or advisors. 

And if there are others like themselves with whom they are in competition; for out of shame before such rivals, they will do many things and refrain from doing others. Moreover, when they are about to be seen and to live openly among those who know the truth of their situation, they are more prone to being "shame-faced." 

Hence the story of Antiphon the poet. When he was about to be **executed** <term>beaten to death</term> <note>original: "apotympanizesthai." This was a brutal form of Athenian execution where the condemned was lashed to a board and either beaten or left to die from exposure.</note> by the tyrant Dionysius, he saw his fellow prisoners covering their faces with their cloaks as they passed through the gates. He said to them: "Why do you cover your heads? Is it because you are afraid one of these people might see you tomorrow?"

So, concerning shame...

<summary>Aristotle discusses how the "audience" of our actions—whether they are rivals, admirers, or strangers—determines the intensity of our shame, concluding with a grim anecdote about the poet Antiphon.</summary>

<keywords>Aristotle, Rhetoric, shame, audience, Cydias, Samos, Antiphon the poet, Dionysius, social pressure, execution</keywords>

<meta>This page concludes the discussion on shame and shamelessness from Chapter 6 and begins Chapter 7, which focuses on the definition and rhetorical application of kindness or benevolence (charis).</meta>

->ARISTOTLE<-

...these things; but concerning **shamelessness** <note>original: "anaiskhyntias." This refers to a lack of shame or a disregard for the opinion of others.</note>, it is clear that we will have plenty of arguments based on the opposites.

### CHAPTER VII.

To whom people feel **kindness** <note>original: "charis." This Greek term encompasses kindness, favor, and the gratitude felt in return. In this context, Aristotle focuses on the act of rendering a service.</note>, and for what reasons, and in what state of mind they themselves are, will be clear once we have defined kindness. Let kindness be defined as that quality by which the one possessing it is said to render a service to the one in need, not in return for anything, nor so that the one serving gains anything for himself, but so that the other person does. 

A kindness is considered "great" if it is shown toward those in great need, or for great and difficult things, or at critical times <note>original: "kairois." This refers to the 'opportune moment' or a time of crisis where help is most impactful.</note>, or if the giver is the only one, or the first to help, or helps the most. 

Needs are the **appetites** <note>original: "orexeis." General desires or impulses.</note>, and among these, the strongest are those accompanied by pain when they are not fulfilled; such are the desires, for instance, **love** <note>original: "erōs."</note>. They also occur in bodily sufferings and in dangers; for the person in danger desires safety, just as the person in pain desires relief. Therefore, those who stand by people in poverty and exile, even if the service they provide is small, are kindly received because of the greatness of the need and the timing; for example, the man who gave the **mat** <note>original: "phormon." This likely refers to a famous but lost anecdote about a man providing a simple reed mat to someone in need at the gymnasium.</note> in the **Lyceum** <note>The Lyceum was a public gymnasium in Athens where Aristotle famously established his school.</note>. 

It is necessary, then, that a service ideally addresses such needs; if not, then it should be equal or greater in importance. Since it is clear when, for what reasons, and in what state of mind kindness occurs, it is evident that from these points one must prepare their arguments: showing that some people are or were in such pain and need, and that the others provided a service of this kind in such a time of necessity.

It is also clear how one can dismiss a claim of kindness and make people appear **ungrateful** <note>original: "acharistos." Literally 'without grace' or 'unpleasant.'</note>. One can argue either that the people are serving (or did serve) for their own sake—which, as we defined it, is not true kindness—or that it happened by chance, or they were forced to do it; or that they merely gave back a debt rather than giving a gift, whether they realized it or not. In both cases, it is "something for something," and therefore not a true act of kindness.

<summary>Aristotle defines kindness (charis) as a selfless service to someone in need and explains how orators can amplify or diminish the perceived value of a favor in a rhetorical contest.</summary>
<keywords>kindness, gratitude, charis, altruism, need, Lyceum, rhetoric of favors</keywords>

RHETORICA. LIB. II. CAP. VIII. 89

...it would be a kindness. And we must examine this according to all the **categories** <note>original: "katēgorias." These are the fundamental ways Aristotle classifies reality, such as quantity, quality, time, and place. He uses them here to test if a favor is genuine.</note>: for it is a kindness either because it is this specific thing, or because of its amount, or its quality, or when it was done, or where. 

It is an indication <note>that no kindness was intended</note> if they failed to help in a smaller matter, or if they have done the same, or equal, or greater things for their enemies; for it is clear that they did not do these things for our sake. Or if the things given were worthless and the giver knew it; for no one admits to needing worthless things. 

And so, we have spoken concerning the showing of **kindness** and **ingratitude** <note>original: "akharistein."</note>.

### CHAPTER VIII.

Now let us discuss what things are **pitiable**, and whom people pity, and in what state of mind they themselves are when they feel it. 

Let **pity** <note>original: "eleos." In the Greek tradition, pity is a rational emotion triggered by seeing someone suffer beyond what they deserve.</note> be defined as a certain pain at the sight of a destructive or painful evil happening to one who is **undeserving** <note>original: "anaxiou." A key component of pity for Aristotle is the belief that the victim does not deserve their fate.</note>, which a person might expect to suffer themselves, or for one of their own to suffer; and this, when the danger appears to be near. 

For it is clear that a person who is going to feel pity must be in such a state as to think that they might suffer some evil either themselves or one of their own, and an evil of the kind mentioned in the definition, or one similar or nearly the same. 

Therefore, those who are completely ruined do not feel pity—for they think they can suffer nothing further, since they have already suffered—nor do those who think they are supremely happy; rather, they are arrogant. For if they think they possess all good things, it is clear they also think it is impossible for them to suffer any evil; for this security is also counted among the good things. 

The following types of people are those who believe they might suffer: those who have already suffered and escaped; the elderly, both because of their wisdom and their experience; the weak; and those who are more cowardly. Also, the **educated** <note>original: "pepaideumenoi."</note>, for they are capable of reasoning. Also those who have parents, children, or wives; for these are one's own, and they are liable to suffer the things mentioned. 

Pity is also not felt by those who are in a state of courageous passion, such as **anger** or **confidence**; for these states do not reason about...

<summary>This page concludes the criteria for defining a favor as a kindness and introduces the formal definition of pity, emphasizing that we pity others when we recognize a similar vulnerability in ourselves.</summary>

<meta>This page continues Aristotle's investigation into the emotion of pity (eleos), specifically looking at the psychological state required to feel it and the specific types of misfortunes that trigger it.</meta>

<page-num>90</page-num>
<header>ARISTOTLE</header>

...those who expect these things to happen; neither those who are in an **insolent** <note>original: "hubristikē." A state of arrogance where one feels invincible.</note> state of mind—for such people are too irrational to think they might suffer anything—but rather those who fall between these extremes. Nor, again, are those who are extremely fearful capable of pity; for those who are terrified do not feel pity, because they are preoccupied with their own suffering. 

People also feel pity if they believe there are some **decent** <note>original: "epieikeis." Aristotle argues that if you think everyone is wicked, you will feel they deserve their suffering rather than pitying them.</note> people; for he who thinks no one is decent will believe that everyone deserves to suffer. Generally speaking, a person is in the right state for pity when they are reminded that such misfortunes have happened either to themselves or to their own people, or when they expect such things to happen to themselves or their own. 

We have now stated the state of mind of those who feel pity. What things they pity is clear from the definition. For all painful and agonizing things that are **destructive** are pitiable; and all things that are ruinous; and all those evils of which **chance** is the cause, provided they are of significant magnitude. 

Painful and destructive things include deaths, physical outrages, bodily injuries, old age, diseases, and lack of food. Among the evils of which chance is the cause are: a lack of friends, or having few friends (which is why being torn away from friends and companions is pitiable), ugliness, weakness, and disability. Also pitiable is when an evil occurs from a source where one expected something good to happen; or when this happens repeatedly; or when a good thing happens only after the person has already suffered. An example is the gifts sent from the King <note>Referring to the King of Persia.</note> to **Diopeithes** <note>An Athenian general; the gifts arrived only after his death, making the "good" event actually a source of pity.</note> after he was dead. It is also pitiable for no good thing to have ever happened, or for there to be no opportunity to enjoy good things when they do arrive. 

These, then, are the things for which people feel pity. They feel it for their acquaintances, provided they are not too closely related by blood; for toward those very close to them, they feel as they would feel about themselves being threatened. This is why **Amasis** <note>A King of Egypt mentioned by the historian Herodotus.</note>, as they say, did not weep when his son was being led to his death, but he did weep for his friend who was reduced to begging. For the latter is **pitiable**, but the former is **terrible**. For the **terrible** <note>original: "deinon." A technical term in Aristotle's rhetoric referring to something so shocking or close to home that it inspires horror rather than the "rational" pain of pity.</note> is different from the pitiable, and it drives out...

<summary>Aristotle defines the conditions for pity, noting that it requires a person to feel vulnerable but not terrified, and lists specific misfortunes—like the death of a friend or a late-arriving stroke of luck—that trigger the emotion.</summary>

<meta>This page continues Aristotle's analysis of the emotion of pity (eleos) and transitions into Chapter IX, which introduces its moral opposite, indignation (nemesis). It explores how proximity and performance affect our emotional response to the suffering of others.</meta>

<header>RHETORIC. BOOK II. CHAPTER IX.</header>

...is disruptive to pity, and often useful for the opposite effect. Furthermore, people feel pity when the danger is close at hand.

They also pity those like themselves—in age, in character, in habits, in status, or in birth. For in all these cases, the suffering appears more relevant, as if it could happen to the observer as well. In general, we must understand here that whatever people **fear** for themselves, they **pity** when it happens to others. Since sufferings that appear close are pitiable, whereas those that happened ten thousand years ago or will happen in the distant future—being neither expected nor remembered—are either not pitied at all or not in the same way; it is necessary, then, that those who provide assistance through gestures, voices, clothing, and overall **delivery** <note>original: "hypokrisis." This refers to the art of the actor or orator in performing their speech to evoke emotion.</note> are more pitiable.

By doing this, they make the evil seem near, placing it before our eyes as if it were just about to happen or had just occurred. Things that have just happened, or are about to happen soon, are more pitiable for this very reason. So too are signs and actions—for instance, the clothing of those who have suffered and other such things. Also the words of those in the midst of their suffering, such as those already dying. Most of all, it is pitiable when people are **virtuous** <note>original: "spoudaious." Literally "serious" or "excellent" people; those who have lived a life of merit and are now suffering.</note> while facing such times. All these things increase pity by making the suffering appear near, highlighting both that the person is **undeserving** and that the suffering is right before our eyes.

# CHAPTER IX.

The emotion most directly opposed to pity is what is called **indignation** <term>indignation: (Greek: nemesis) A righteous pain felt when someone receives good fortune they do not deserve.</term>. For feeling pain at undeserved misfortune is, in a way, the opposite of feeling pain at undeserved good fortune; and both of these emotions belong to a **good character** <note>original: "ēthous chrēstou." Aristotle argues that a morally sound person should feel both pity and indignation, as both are responses to what is "undeserved."</note>. For we ought to feel sympathy for those who fare badly **undeservedly**...

<summary>Aristotle concludes his discussion on pity, noting that we pity those similar to ourselves and that a sense of "nearness" (often created by actors) heightens the emotion. He then introduces indignation (nemesis) as the righteous opposite of pity, both stemming from a sense of justice.</summary>
