--- Page 105 ---
<meta>This page continues Aristotle’s discussion on the emotions of the soul, specifically moving from the definition of pity toward its moral counterpart: indignation. It establishes the ethical framework for why we feel pain at the success of others and distinguishes between righteous indignation and the vice of envy.</meta>

### ARISTOTLE

...and to feel pity. But towards those who fare well <note>original: "eu." Referring to those who experience good fortune or prosperity.</note> undeservedly, one should feel **indignation** <note>original: "nemesan." This is a righteous form of anger or resentment felt when someone receives a benefit they do not merit.</note>; for that which occurs contrary to merit is unjust. This is why we even attribute indignation to the gods.

It might also seem that **envy** <note>original: "phthonos."</note> is opposed to pity in the same way, being closely related to or even the same as indignation. However, it is different. Envy is also a disturbing kind of pain and is directed at prosperity, but not at the prosperity of the undeserving; rather, it is felt toward those who are our equals or peers. The common element in all these cases is that the pain arises not because something bad will happen to oneself, but solely because of the neighbor's situation. For if the pain and disturbance were felt because the other person's prosperity might cause us some harm, it would no longer be envy or indignation, but **fear**.

It is evident that the opposite emotions will follow these. For the person who feels pain at those who suffer undeservedly will feel joy—or at least remain untroubled—when those who deserve to suffer actually do. For example, no virtuous person would feel pain when parricides and murderers <note>original: "patraloias kai miaiphonous." Parricides are those who kill their parents; "miaiphonos" refers to someone polluted by the blood of murder.</note> meet with punishment; indeed, we ought to rejoice at such things, just as we should at those who fare well according to their merit. Both outcomes are just and bring joy to the **decent person** <note>original: "epieikē." One who is fair-minded, equitable, and virtuous.</note>; for such a person is bound to hope that what has happened to their peer might also happen to themselves.

All these traits belong to the same moral character, while the opposite traits belong to the opposite character. For the same person who is **malicious** <note>original: "epichairekakos." One who experiences "schadenfreude," or joy at the misfortune of others.</note> is also envious. Necessarily, if someone is pained by the existence or acquisition of a good thing, they must rejoice at its loss or destruction. Therefore, all these emotions are capable of preventing pity, though they differ for the reasons already mentioned; thus, they are all equally useful for the purpose of making a situation appear not pitiable.

First, then, let us speak of **indignation**: toward whom people feel it, on what grounds, and in what state of mind.

<summary>Aristotle defines indignation as a righteous pain felt at the undeserved success of others, distinguishing it from envy, which is felt toward peers. He argues that these emotions are the moral opposites of pity and can be used by an orator to neutralize an audience's sympathy.</summary>

<keywords>indignation (nemesis), envy (phthonos), pity (eleos), justice, malice (epichairekakia), merit, Aristotle's Rhetoric</keywords>

--- Page 106 ---
<meta>This page continues Aristotle’s analysis of "indignation" (nemesis). Having previously defined it as pain felt at the undeserved success of others, he now specifies which types of "good things" provoke this emotion and why the timing of someone's success (new vs. old wealth) affects our moral reaction.</meta>

->RHETORICA. BOOK II. CHAPTER IX. 93<-

...possess it themselves; then after this, concerning the others.

It is clear from what has been said: for if **indignation** <note>original: "nemesan." This refers to a righteous form of resentment felt when someone receives a benefit they do not merit.</note> is to feel pain at apparent undeserved prosperity, it is first of all obvious that it is not possible to feel indignation at all good things. For no one will feel indignation if another is just or brave, or if they attain **virtue** <note>original: "aretē." Aristotle argues that we don't resent people for being good or moral, as these are always "deserved" traits.</note>; for neither is **pity** <note>original: "eleoi."</note> felt for the opposites of these <note>That is, we do not pity someone for being naturally cowardly or unjust in the same way we pity their physical suffering.</note>. Instead, indignation is felt regarding wealth, power, and similar advantages—things of which, to put it simply, good people are worthy, as well as those who possess natural advantages, such as noble birth, beauty, and such things.

Since that which is long-established <note>original: "to archaion." Literally "the ancient."</note> seems to be close to what is natural, it follows that we feel more indignation toward those who possess the same good if they happen to have acquired it recently and prosper because of it. For the **newly rich** cause more pain than those who have been wealthy for a long time or by descent. The same applies to those in office, those with power, those with many friends, those with fine children, and anything of the sort; and likewise if some other good comes to them because of these things. For here too, the newly rich <note>original: "neoploutoi." From which we get the term "nouveau riche."</note> who hold office because of their wealth cause more pain than those whose wealth is ancestral. It is the same with the other goods.

The reason for this is that the latter seem to hold what belongs to them, while the former do not; for that which always appears to be a certain way seems to be "true" <note>In the sense of being proper, legitimate, or the natural order of things.</note>; consequently, the others seem to possess what is not their own. And since each type of good is not deserved by just anyone, but there is a certain **proportion** <note>original: "analogia."</note> and fitness—for example, beautiful armor does not fit a just man, but a brave one, and distinguished marriages belong not to the newly rich, but to those of noble birth—if, therefore, someone who is good does not attain what is fitting, it is a matter for indignation, as it is when an inferior [competes] with a superior...

<summary>Aristotle explains that indignation is triggered by external goods like wealth and power rather than virtue. He argues that "new money" or recent success provokes more resentment than ancestral status because long-standing possession acquires a sense of natural legitimacy.</summary>

<keywords>indignation, nemesis, wealth, newly rich, virtue, merit, proportion, social status</keywords>

--- Page 107 ---
<meta>This page continues Aristotle’s exploration of "indignation" (nemesis), moving from the objects of indignation (the "newly rich" or the undeserving) to the character of the people who feel this emotion. Aristotle concludes his advice on how an orator can suppress pity in an audience before transitioning to the related, yet distinct, emotion of envy.</meta>

->RHETORICA. BOOK II. CHAPTER IX. 94<-

...to dispute; and most especially with those in the same field. Hence this has also been said:

> But he avoided battle with Ajax, son of Telamon;
> For Zeus would have been indignant with him, whenever he fought a better man. <note>original: "Aiantos d' aleeine..." This quote, usually associated with the Iliad, illustrates that even the gods find it offensive when a lesser warrior challenges a superior one.</note>

If not in the same field, then we feel indignation whenever an inferior disputes with a superior in any way—for example, if a musician disputes with a just man, for **justice** is a higher quality than **music**. It is therefore clear from these examples who people feel indignation toward, and why; for these and similar cases are the cause.

People are themselves prone to **indignation** <note>original: "nemesētikoi."</note> if they happen to be worthy of the greatest goods and already possess them; for it is not just for those who are not equals to be considered worthy of equal things. Secondly, they are prone to it if they happen to be good and virtuous people, for then they judge well and hate what is unjust. Also, if they are **ambitious** <note>original: "philotimoi," literally "lovers of honor,"</note> and desire certain achievements—especially if they are ambitious regarding those very things that others have obtained without being worthy of them. In general, those who think themselves worthy of things that they do not think others deserve are prone to feeling indignation toward those people and regarding those things.

For this reason, the **servile** <note>original: "andrapodōdeis," literally "slave-like" in character.</note>, the base, and the unambitious are not prone to indignation, for there is nothing great of which they think themselves worthy. From this it is also clear at what kind of misfortunes and failures of others we should rejoice, or at least feel no pain <note>Aristotle suggests that if we feel righteous indignation at someone's undeserved success, we will feel a corresponding "righteous joy" at their downfall.</note>. From what has been said, the opposites are obvious. Thus, if a speaker's argument puts the judges in such a frame of mind, and shows that those claiming to deserve **pity** <note>original: "eleeisthai."</note> are actually unworthy of success and deserve their current lack of it, then it will be impossible for the judges to feel pity for them.

### CHAPTER X.

It is also clear why people feel **envy** <note>original: "phthonousi." Aristotle here transitions from "righteous indignation" (pain at the success of the unworthy) to "envy" (pain at the success of equals).</note>, toward whom they feel it, and in what state of mind, if indeed envy is a certain kind of pain at the prosperity...

<meta>This page continues Aristotle’s analysis of the emotions, transitioning from "indignation" (nemesis) to "envy" (phthonos). ### RHETORICA. BOOK II. CHAPTER X. 95

...is a certain pain <note>original: "phainomenē," appearing or being perceived as pain.</note> arising from the aforementioned goods, felt toward those who are our equals, not so that the person may gain something for himself, but simply because the others possess those goods. For such people will feel envy toward those to whom they are, or seem to be, equal. By **equals**, I mean equals in birth, in kinship, in age, in disposition, in reputation, and in their existing possessions. 

Envy is also felt by those who lack only a little of possessing everything; for this reason, those who achieve great things and are successful are often envious, for they think everyone is trying to carry away what is rightfully theirs. Likewise for those who are distinguished for some particular thing, and most especially for **wisdom** or **happiness** <note>original: "eudaimonia," often translated as "flourishing" or "well-being."</note>. Also, those who are **ambitious** <note>term: "philotimoi" | literally "lovers of honor," referring to those who strive for public recognition.</note> are more prone to envy than those who are not. So too are the **pretenders to wisdom** <note>term: "doxosophoi" | those who have the reputation or appearance of being wise but lack the substance.</note>, for they are ambitious regarding wisdom. In general, those who love glory in any particular field are envious of others in that same field. The **small-souled** <note>term: "mikropsychoi" | individuals with low self-estimation or narrow concerns.</note> are also envious, for to them, everything seems to be a great matter. 

As for the things that cause envy, the "goods" have already been mentioned. For all the things that people love for the sake of glory, and the deeds they perform out of ambition, and the things they reach for to gain a reputation—and nearly all forms of good luck—all of these are objects of envy. This is especially true of things that a person either reaches for themselves, or thinks they ought to have, or things where they are only slightly superior or slightly inferior in possession to others. 

It is also clear whom they envy, for this is expressed at the same time: they envy those who are near to them in time, place, age, and reputation. Hence it is said:

->For kinship knows well how to envy.<-

And they envy those with whom they are in competition; for people compete against those I have just mentioned. But no one feels envy toward those who lived ten thousand years ago, or those who are yet to be born, or the dead. Nor does anyone envy those who live at the **Pillars of Heracles** <note>original: "Hērakleiais stēlais." This refers to the Strait of Gibraltar, representing the furthest western edge of the known world to the Greeks; here it signifies people who are geographically too remote to be rivals.</note>. Nor do they envy those whom they consider to be far beneath themselves or far above themselves, whether in their own judgment or the judgment of others. Likewise, they feel the same toward these people regarding such matters. And since people compete with their rivals...

<summary>Aristotle defines envy as a specific pain felt toward the success of our equals and identifies the "ambitious" and "small-souled" as those most prone to this emotion.</summary>
<keywords>Envy, Phthonos, Ambition, Equality, Rivalry, Aristotle's Rhetoric, Social Psychology, Ethics</keywords>

--- Page 109 ---
<meta>This page continues from page 95, finishing the discussion on the causes and objects of envy (phthonos) before transitioning in Chapter 11 to the concept of emulation (zēlos). # ARISTOTELIS

...competitors, and rival lovers, and in general those who strive for the same things, it is necessary to envy these most of all. Hence the saying: **"Potter against potter."** <note>original: "Kai kerameus keramei." A common Greek proverb (found in Hesiod) suggesting that people in the same trade are natural rivals.</note>

Also, those who have barely succeeded, or have not succeeded at all, envy those who have succeeded quickly. And they envy those whose possessions or successes are a reproach to them; these, too, are people who are close to them and like them. For it is clear that they fail to obtain the good because of these others; thus, the pain caused by this creates envy.

They also envy those who possess, or have acquired, things that naturally belonged to themselves, or which they once possessed; for this reason, older men envy younger ones. And those who have spent much to achieve a goal envy those who have achieved the same with little effort. It is also clear what such people rejoice in, and at what things, and in what state of mind they are; for just as they are pained when they do not have these goods, they will be pleased by the opposite circumstances <note>That is, they feel "Schadenfreude" or joy at the misfortunes of those they envy.</note>. Therefore, if the judges <note>In the context of Rhetoric, "the judges" (kyriōn) refers to the audience or the literal jurors whom a speaker is trying to persuade.</note> are brought into this state of mind, and if those seeking pity or claiming to deserve some benefit are the kind of people we have described, it is clear that they will not receive pity from those in power.

### CHAPTER XI.

The state of mind in which people feel **emulation** <term>zēlos | A positive form of rivalry or "zeal." Unlike envy, which wants to take a good away from someone else, emulation is a virtuous distress that motivates a person to acquire that same good for themselves.</term>, the kinds of things they emulate, and toward whom, will be clear from the following. For if emulation is a certain distress at the apparent presence of honorable goods—goods that it is possible for one to obtain—felt toward those who are similar in nature, it is felt not because another has them, but because one does not have them oneself.

(For this reason, emulation is **virtuous** <note>original: "epieikes," meaning decent, fair, or morally upright.</note> and is felt by virtuous people, while envy is **base** and felt by base people; for the emulous man prepares himself to obtain the goods because of his emulation, while the envious man seeks to prevent his neighbor from having them because of his envy.)

It follows then that those who believe themselves worthy of goods they do not have are inclined to emulation; for no one aspires to things that appear impossible. This is why the young,

<summary>Aristotle concludes his analysis of envy, noting that we envy those most like us, and begins a new chapter defining emulation as a productive, virtuous distress compared to the destructive nature of envy.</summary>
<keywords>Aristotle, Rhetoric, envy (phthonos), emulation (zelos), competition, ethics, virtue vs vice, psychological states</keywords>

--- Page 110 ---
<meta>This page continues from page 96, where Aristotle concluded his discussion on envy (phthonos). Here, he begins Chapter 11, moving to the related but distinct emotion of emulation (zēlos), which he considers a noble form of distress at the lack of good things that others possess.</meta>

### RHETORICA. BOOK II. CHAPTER XI.

...and those who are **great-souled** <note>original: *megalopsychoi*. This refers to people with high self-esteem who believe they are worthy of great things and are indeed worthy of them.</note> are of this character; as well as those who possess those goods that are worthy of honored men, such as **wealth**, a **multitude of friends**, **public offices**, and similar things. For, because they believe that being good is expected of them (since it is characteristic of those who are well-off), they strive for such goods. 

Likewise, those whom others deem worthy [of honor] are emulous. And those whose ancestors, or relatives, or associates, or nation, or city are held in honor, are emulous regarding these things; for they believe these honors are their own property and that they are worthy of them. 

If honorable goods are the objects of emulation, then it follows that the **virtues** <note>original: *aretai*.</note> must be of this kind, as well as whatever things are useful and beneficial to others—for people honor those who do good and those who are virtuous—along with those goods whose enjoyment is shared by one's neighbors, such as wealth and beauty, which are emulated more than health <note>Aristotle suggests that while health is a greater good, things like wealth and beauty are more visible and can be "enjoyed" or utilized by those around the possessor, making them more likely to trigger emulation.</note>.

It is also clear who the objects of emulation are: those who possess these and similar things are emulated. These include the qualities mentioned, such as **courage**, **wisdom**, and **authority** <note>original: *archē*. This can refer to political power or holding a leadership office.</note>; for those in authority can benefit many people. This includes generals, orators, and all who possess such powers. Also, those whom many wish to be like, or those who have many acquaintances or friends. Or those whom many admire, or those whom they themselves admire. And those whose praises and encomiums are spoken by poets or prose-writers.

They feel **contempt** <note>original: *kataphronēsis*.</note> for the opposites; for contempt is the opposite of emulation, and the act of emulating is opposite to the act of feeling contempt. It follows that those in a state of mind to emulate certain people, or to be emulated themselves, must be contemptuous of those people (and for those reasons) who possess the opposite "evils" to the honorable goods of emulation. This is why they often feel contempt for those who are merely lucky, when their good fortune comes to them without the accompaniment of those goods that are truly honored.

<summary>Aristotle defines emulation (zēlos) as a noble desire to attain honorable goods seen in others and identifies the types of people and virtues that inspire this feeling.</summary>

This allows a speaker to tailor their speech to the specific psychological makeup of their listeners.</meta>

how emotions are produced and dissolved, from which the means of persuasion arise—these have now been discussed.

## CHAPTER 12.

Let us next discuss the various types of **character** <note>original: *ethē*. In this context, Aristotle is describing the psychological profiles or "personalities" of different groups of people.</note>, considering how they are affected by the **emotions** <note>original: *pathē*.</note>, their **habits** <note>original: *hexeis*.</note>, their **age**, and their **fortunes**. By emotions, I mean anger, desire, and the like, which we have spoken of before. By habits, I mean **virtues and vices**; these also have been discussed previously, including what things each type of person chooses and what they are likely to do. The various ages are **youth**, the **prime of life**, and **old age**. By fortune, I mean **noble birth**, **wealth**, **power**, and their opposites—and in general, good and bad luck.

Young men have strong **desires**, and they are prone to do whatever they desire. Among the bodily desires, they are especially inclined to follow those of **sexual passion** <note>original: *aphrodisia*, referring to the gifts of Aphrodite.</note>, and they lack self-control in this regard. They are changeable and fickle in their desires; they desire intensely, but cease quickly. For their impulses are sharp but not deep-seated, much like the temporary thirst and hunger of the sick.

They are also **passionate** and hot-tempered, and prone to follow their impulses. They are mastered by their temper; for because of their **ambition** <note>original: *philotimia*, literally "love of honor."</note>, they cannot endure being slighted, but become indignant if they think they are being wronged. While they are ambitious, they are even more **competitive** <note>original: *philonikoi*, literally "lovers of victory."</note>, for youth desires superiority, and victory is a kind of superiority.

They value both of these more than money; indeed, they care for money least of all, because they have not yet experienced want—as the saying of **Pittacus** <note>Pittacus of Mytilene was one of the Seven Sages of Greece. This specific saying regarding Amphiaraus is lost, but likely referred to someone who did not know the value of money until they lacked it.</note> goes regarding **Amphiaraus**. They are not cynical, but rather **innocent** <note>original: *euētheis*, which can mean "simple-minded" or "good-natured."</note>, because they have not yet witnessed much wickedness. They are also **trustful**, 

<summary>Aristotle begins a famous psychological study of "character types," starting with the traits of young men, whom he describes as impulsive, ambitious, and idealistic.</summary>

...because they have not yet been often deceived. They are also full of hope; for just as those who are drunk, the young are heated by their very nature; and also because they have not yet met with many failures. They live for the most part by hope; for hope is of the future, while memory is of the past. To the young, the future is long, but the past is short. Indeed, on the first day of life, one has nothing to remember but everything to hope for.

They are also easily deceived for the reason stated: they hope easily. And they are more courageous; for they are passionate and hopeful, the former of which prevents them from fearing, while the latter makes them bold. For no one feels fear while angry, and to hope for something good is a source of confidence. 

They are also shy <note>original: *aischyntēloi*. This refers to a sense of shame or bashfulness based on social expectations.</note>; for they do not yet conceive of other standards of honor, but have been educated by the law alone. And they are **high-minded** <term>high-minded: *megalopsychia*, or "greatness of soul." In Aristotle's view, this is the virtue of those who correctly believe themselves worthy of great things.</term>; for they have not yet been humbled by the struggles of life, but are inexperienced in necessity. To think oneself worthy of great things is high-mindedness, and this belongs to the hopeful.

And they choose rather to perform **noble deeds** than useful ones; for they live more by character <note>original: *ēthei*.</note> than by calculation. For calculation is concerned with what is useful, but virtue is concerned with what is noble. They are more fond of friends and companions than people of other ages, because they delight in living together and do not yet judge anything—not even their friends—by the standard of utility.

In all their mistakes, they err on the side of excess and intensity, contrary to the **Chilonian maxim** <note>original: *Chilōneion*. This refers to the famous proverb "Nothing in excess" (*mēden agan*) attributed to Chilon of Sparta, one of the Seven Sages of Greece.</note>; for they do everything in excess. They love in excess, they hate in excess, and so in everything else. They think they know everything and are quite certain about it; this indeed is the reason they do everything in excess. The wrongs they commit are matters of **insolence** <term>insolence: *hybris*. Here, Aristotle distinguishes between crimes of passion or arrogance (*hybris*) and those of calculated malice (*kakourgia*).</term>, rather than malice. They are also compassionate—

...charitable, because they suppose everyone to be good and better than they actually are. For **wit** is nothing more than **educated insolence**. Such, then, is the character of the young.

## CHAPTER XIII.

But the elderly, and those who have passed their prime, have characters composed for the most part of the qualities opposite to these. Because they have lived for many years, have been more often deceived, have made more mistakes, and because most human affairs turn out poorly, they assert nothing with certainty, and they state everything with less conviction than they should. They "think," but they "know" nothing; and when in doubt, they always add "perhaps" and "maybe." They speak of all things in this way, but of nothing firmly. 

They are also **ill-disposed** <note>original: *kakōetheis*. This refers to a cynical disposition that puts the worst construction on everything.</note>; for malice is the tendency to assume the worst of everything. Furthermore, they are suspicious because they are distrustful, and they are distrustful because of their experience. For these reasons, they neither love nor hate with any great intensity; instead, following the maxim of **Bias** <note>Bias of Priene was one of the Seven Sages of Greece. His famous advice was to love as if one might later hate, and hate as if one might later love, to avoid the excesses of emotion.</note>, they love as if they will one day hate, and they hate as if they will one day love. 

They are also **small-minded** <term>small-minded: *mikropsychoi*. Unlike the "high-minded" youth who seek greatness, the elderly are often humbled by the hardships of life and limit their desires.</term>, because they have been humbled by life. They do not desire anything great or extraordinary, but only the basic necessities of living. They are also ungenerous <note>original: *aneleutheroi*, often translated as "stingy" or "illiberal."</note>; for property is one of those necessities, and they know by experience how hard it is to acquire and how easy it is to lose. 

They are also cowardly and fearful of everything in advance; for their temperament is the opposite of the young. They have been "chilled" by age, while the young are "hot." Thus, old age has paved the way for cowardice, for fear itself is a kind of chilling of the spirit. They are also lovers of life, and especially...

### RHETORICA. LIB. II. CAP. XIII.

...especially on their last day, because desire is directed toward that which is absent; and what they lack, they desire most. They are also more **fond of finding fault** than they should be; for this, too, is a kind of **small-mindedness** <term>small-mindedness: *mikropsychia*. A petty disposition that focuses on trivial grievances rather than large-scale virtues.</term>. 

And they live for the **useful** <term>useful: *to sympheron*. Practical self-interest or personal gain.</term>, rather than for the **noble** <term>noble: *to kalon*. That which is morally beautiful, fine, or virtuous for its own sake.</term>, more than is proper, because they are self-loving; for the useful is a good for the individual, while the noble is a good in an absolute sense. They are also more **shameless** than modest; for because they do not care equally for the noble and the useful, they disregard what others think of them. 

They are **hopeful** <note>original: *euelpides*. Though the Greek text here says "hopeful," the logic that follows—about things turning out for the worse—strongly suggests this is a scribal error for *dyselpides*, meaning "lacking in hope" or "despairing," which is the reading found in most standard editions of Aristotle.</note> due to their experience; for most human affairs are wretched, or at least most turn out for the worse; and they are also fearful because of their cowardice. They live by **memory** more than by **hope**; for the remainder of their life is short, while the past is long; and hope is for the future, while memory is for things gone by. This is also the cause of their **talkativeness**; for they spend their time speaking of the past, as they take pleasure in the act of remembering. 

Their fits of anger are sharp but weak. Their desires have either failed them or are feeble. Consequently, they are neither driven by desire nor do they act upon it, but rather act for the sake of **profit**; thus, people of this age appear **temperate** <note>original: *sōphronikoi*. Aristotle suggests this isn't true virtue, but rather a lack of impulse caused by the "enslavement" of their desires to the pursuit of wealth.</note>, for their desires have slackened and are enslaved to gain. 

They live more by **calculation** than by **character**; for calculation is concerned with the useful, while character is concerned with virtue. When they commit wrongs, they do so out of **malice** <term>malice: *kakourgia*. This implies a deliberate, "mean" mischief rather than the impulsive arrogance of youth.</term>, not out of **insolence** <term>insolence: *hybris*. The young act out of a sense of superiority; the old act out of a desire to harm.</term>. The elderly are also prone to **pity**, but not for the same reason as the young; for the young feel pity out of a love for humanity, but the old do so out of **weakness**. For they imagine that all manner of suffering is close at hand for themselves; and this belief—that one is personally vulnerable—is the essence of pity. From this arises their **wailing**...

Aristotle argues that the ideal character is found in the middle age, which combines the virtues of youth and age while discarding their respective vices.</meta>

ARISTOTELIS

...prone to pity <note>The text begins mid-word with *tikoi*, completing *eleētikoi* (compassionate/pitying) from the previous page.</note>, and are not **witty** <term>witty: *eutrapeloi*. In Greek, this implies a nimble, "well-turning" mind capable of pleasant social banter.</term>, nor fond of laughter; for a disposition toward lamentation is the opposite of a fondness for laughter. Such, then, are the characters of the young and the elderly. Consequently, since everyone welcomes speeches spoken in their own character, and those like it, it is not unclear how, by using their speeches, they themselves and the speeches appear to be of such a character.

### CAPUT XIV.

It is clear that those in their **prime** <term>prime: *akmazontes*. Literally those at their "bloom" or "peak," referring here to the middle stage of life.</term> will have a character that is between these two, removing the excess of either side. They are neither over-confident—for such a thing is **rashness**—nor are they overly fearful, but they are well-disposed toward both. They neither trust everyone nor distrust everyone, but rather judge according to the truth. And they live neither solely for the **noble** <term>noble: *to kalon*. The morally beautiful or virtuous.</term> nor solely for the **useful** <term>useful: *to sympheron*. Practical self-interest.</term>, but for both. 

And they lean neither toward stinginess nor toward extravagance, but toward what is **fitting** <term>fitting: *to harmotton*. The appropriate or suitable mean between two extremes.</term>. Likewise, they are moderate regarding both anger and desire. They are **temperate** <term>temperate: *sophrones*. Possessing self-control and sound judgment.</term> alongside **courage**, and courageous alongside temperance; for in the young and the elderly, these virtues are divided. The young are courageous but lack self-control, while the elderly are temperate but cowardly. 

To speak generally, whatever useful qualities are divided between youth and old age, those in their prime possess both; and where the others exceed or fall short, these possess the **moderate** and the fitting measure. The body is at its prime from the age of thirty to thirty-five; the soul, however, is at its peak around the age of **forty-nine** <note>original: *henos dein pentēkonta*, literally "one wanting of fifty."</note>. Regarding youth, old age, and the prime of life—and what character belongs to each—let this suffice.

<meta>This page continues Aristotle's analysis of human character (ethos) based on external circumstances. Having previously discussed how age (youth, old age, and the prime of life) shapes a person's disposition, he now moves to the "gifts of fortune," beginning with high birth and wealth.</meta>

RHETORIC. BOOK II. CHAPTERS XV, XVI.

## CHAPTER XV.

Next, let us speak in order about the goods that come from **fortune** <term>fortune: *tyche*. External factors or luck that affect a person's life regardless of their own effort.</term>, and what kind of characters they produce in men. The character of **high birth** <term>high birth: *eugeneia*. Literally "being well-born," referring to an ancient or distinguished family lineage.</term> is that the person who possesses it is more ambitious; for all people, when they already possess something, tend to pile more upon it. High birth is, in essence, the honored status of one's ancestors. 

Such people are also contemptuous, even of those who are similar to their own ancestors; this is because things are more honored and more easily boasted about when they happened long ago rather than recently. There is a distinction between being **well-born** <term>well-born: *eugenes*. Possessing excellence according to the quality of the family line.</term> and being **noble** <term>noble: *gennaios*. Not falling away from one's true nature.</term>. This "nobility" usually does not happen to those of high birth; instead, most of them are of little worth. 

For there is a kind of "crop yield" in the generations of men, just as there is in the things that grow in the fields. Sometimes, if the stock is good, extraordinary men are produced for a period of time, and then the line gives out. Gifted families often decline into "manic" or unstable characters, such as the descendants of **Alcibiades** <note>Alcibiades: A brilliant but notoriously volatile Athenian statesman and general.</note> and those of **Dionysius the Elder** <note>Dionysius I: The powerful and ruthless tyrant of Syracuse.</note>. On the other hand, steady families often decline into foolishness and sluggishness, such as the descendants of **Cimon**, **Pericles**, and **Socrates**.

## CHAPTER XVI.

The characters that follow from **wealth** <term>wealth: *ploutos*. Material riches and possessions.</term> are easy for everyone to see at a glance. They are insolent and arrogant, being affected in a certain way by the acquisition of their wealth. For they are disposed as if they possess every good thing; wealth acts as a kind of measure of the value of everything else, which is why everything seems to be purchasable by it. They are also fastidious and ostentatious: fastidious because of their luxury and the display...

<summary>Aristotle examines how high birth and wealth influence character, noting that "well-born" people are often ambitious but can decline into instability or dullness, while the wealthy tend toward arrogance because they view money as the measure of all things.</summary>

<meta>This page continues Aristotle's discussion on the character (ethos) of the wealthy, concluding Chapter XVI. It then transitions into Chapter XVII, which analyzes how the possession of power (dynamis) shapes a man's disposition, comparing and contrasting it with the influence of wealth.</meta>

->ARISTOTELIS<-

...of their prosperity. They are **ostentatious** <term>ostentatious: *salakones*. Those who make a vulgar or pretentious display of wealth.</term> and **uncouth** <term>uncouth: *soloikoi*. Originally referring to someone who speaks ungrammatically, here meaning someone socially clumsy or boorish.</term>, because they are accustomed to dwelling upon whatever they happen to love and admire, and they assume that everyone else envies the same things they do. This is a reasonable enough error for them to make, since many people are indeed in need of what the wealthy possess. 

This is also the origin of the famous remark by **Simonides** <note>Simonides of Ceos: A celebrated Greek lyric poet known for his wisdom and his interactions with various rulers.</note> concerning the wise and the rich. When the wife of **Hiero** <note>Hiero I: The powerful tyrant of Syracuse in Sicily; Simonides was a regular guest at his court.</note> asked him whether it was better to be rich or wise, he replied, "Rich; for I see the wise men spending their time at the doors of the rich." 

The wealthy also believe they are worthy of ruling, for they feel they possess the very things that make one worthy of authority. To put it in a nutshell, the character produced by wealth is that of a **fortunate fool** <note>original: *anoêtou eudaimonos êthos*. A "happy idiot" whose success comes from luck rather than merit or intellect.</note>. 

However, there is a difference between the character of those who have **newly acquired wealth** <term>newly acquired wealth: *neoploutos*. The "nouveau riche," or those who have recently gained their fortune rather than inheriting it.</term> and those whose wealth is long-standing. The newly rich possess all these vices to a greater and more offensive degree; it is as if being "newly rich" is a kind of lack of education in how to be wealthy. Furthermore, the wrongs they commit are not petty or malicious, but are born either of **arrogance** <term>arrogance: *hybris*. Insulting or violent behavior intended to shame others.</term> or a lack of self-control—for instance, acts of physical assault or adultery.

### CHAPTER XVII.

In a similar way, most of the traits associated with **power** <term>power: *dynamis*. Here referring to political influence or the authority of high office.</term> are fairly obvious. Power shares some characteristics with wealth, but in other respects, it is better. Those in power are more ambitious and more manly in their character than the wealthy, because they strive to accomplish the great deeds that their authority gives them the license to perform. 

They are also more industrious, for they are constantly in a state of care, being forced to keep a watchful eye on everything concerning their power. They are also more **dignified** <term>dignity: *semnotês*. A quality of being grave, serious, and respected.</term> rather than merely overbearing; for their high rank makes them more visible to the public, which encourages them to act with moderation. In fact, this "dignity" is essentially a softened and elegant form of seriousness. And if...

<summary>Aristotle concludes his critique of the "fortunate fool" (the wealthy) by noting that the newly rich are more prone to arrogance and vice, then begins an analysis of how political power encourages a more ambitious and disciplined character than wealth alone.</summary>

<meta>This page concludes the analysis of how external circumstances, specifically power and good fortune, shape a person's character (ethos). It then transitions into Chapter XVIII, where Aristotle shifts his focus from the psychology of the audience to the formal application of persuasion in different types of speeches and the role of the "judge" in each.</meta>

->RHETORIC. BOOK II. CHAPTER XVIII.<-

...[if they] commit injustice, they are not small-time wrongdoers, but **great wrongdoers** <note>original: *megaladikoi*. Those who commit injustice on a large scale, often because their high status or power allows them to do so.</note>. 

**Good fortune** <term>good fortune: *eutychia*. Success or prosperity that arises from external circumstances or luck rather than individual merit.</term> also shares the components of the characters we have already discussed. For the greatest strokes of perceived luck tend toward these same results; and furthermore, luck contributes to the blessing of having good children and the attainment of physical advantages. Because of their good fortune, such people are indeed more arrogant and more irrational. 

However, one most excellent trait follows from good fortune: they are **pious** <note>original: *philotheoi*. Literally "lovers of the gods."</note>, and they maintain a certain relationship with the divine, having faith in it because of the many good things they have received from luck. 

We have now discussed the characters associated with different ages and types of fortune. Since the opposites of these are easily understood from their counterparts, the character of the poor man, the unfortunate man, and the powerless man are already clear.

## CHAPTER XVIII.

Since the use of persuasive speech is directed toward a **judgment** <term>judgment: *krisis*. In rhetoric, this refers to the decision-making process of the listener, whether they are a juror, a voter, or a spectator.</term>—for there is no further need for speech regarding things we already know and have already decided—it follows that speech is used whether one is addressing a single person to exhort or dissuade them (as those do who give advice or attempt to persuade), for that single person is no less a "judge." To put it simply, the person who must be persuaded is the judge. 

This remains the same whether one is speaking against an opponent or speaking on a general thesis; for one must use speech to demolish opposing arguments, which the speaker treats as if they were a literal opponent. The same is true in **ceremonial speeches** <term>ceremonial: *epideiktikos*. Speeches of praise or blame, often delivered at festivals or funerals.</term>; for the speech is composed with the spectator acting as the judge. 

However, in a strict sense, the "judge" is only the person who decides the issues in **political contests** <note>original: *politikois agōsi*. Public debates in the law courts or the assembly where the outcome has direct legal or political consequences.</note>. In these cases, people investigate both the facts of disputed matters and the things about which they must deliberate. We have previously spoken about the characters associated with different forms of government during our discussion of deliberative speech; therefore, it should be defined...

->ARISTOTLE<-

...[what the character of the audience] might be, and how and through what means we shall make our speeches expressive of **character** <term>character: *ethos*. In rhetoric, this refers to the speaker's ability to appear credible and the way the speech reflects the moral disposition of the speaker or audience.</term>. 

Since for each specific genre of speech there was a different **end** <note>original: *telos*. The ultimate goal or purpose of the speech, such as justice in a courtroom or the advantageous in an assembly.</note>, and for all of them there are accepted opinions and premises from which speakers draw their proofs—whether they are giving advice, making a display, or engaged in a legal dispute—and since the means by which it is possible to make speeches reflect character have been defined, it remains for us to discuss the **common topics** <note>original: *koina*. These are general lines of argument that apply to all types of oratory, regardless of the specific subject matter.</note>. 

For it is necessary for everyone to make use of the "possible" and the "impossible" in their speeches; some will try to show that something will be, while others will try to show that it has happened. Furthermore, the question of **magnitude** <note>original: *megethos*. Refers to the scale or importance of an action or event.</note> is common to all speeches; for everyone uses the techniques of downplaying and amplifying, whether they are advising for or against something, or praising or defending. 

These matters having been settled, let us attempt to speak generally about **enthymemes** <term>enthymeme: *enthymema*. A rhetorical syllogism or logical argument based on probabilities and signs rather than absolute certainties.</term> and **examples** <term>example: *paradeigma*. An inductive argument that uses a specific historical or fictional case to illustrate a general point.</term>, so that by adding what remains, we may complete our original plan. 

Among these common topics, **amplification** <note>original: *auxein*. The technique of making something seem greater or more significant.</note> is most suited to display speeches, as has been said; the focus on what has occurred belongs to legal speeches, for judgment is passed on these; and the "possible" and what is "to come" belong to deliberative speeches.

### CHAPTER XIX.

First, then, let us speak about the **possible** and the **impossible**. If one of two opposites is possible to be or to happen, the other opposite would also seem to be possible; for example, if it is possible for a man to be healed, it is also possible for him to fall ill; for the same capacity governs opposites, insofar as they are opposites. Likewise, if one similar thing is possible, so is another similar thing. And if the more difficult thing is possible, so is the easier. And if the **excellent**...

--- Page 120 ---
<meta>This page continues Aristotle's exploration of the "common topics," specifically focusing on the topic of "the possible and the impossible." Following the introduction on the previous page, Aristotle here lists the logical criteria used to argue whether a certain event or state of affairs can actually occur.</meta>

### RHETORIC. BOOK II. CHAPTER XIX.

...if it is possible for something beautiful to come into being, it is also possible for it to come into being in a general sense; for it is a more difficult thing for a house to be beautiful than for it simply to be a house. 

Also, if the beginning of something can come into being, so can the end; for nothing impossible happens, nor does it even begin to happen. For example, the notion that the **diagonal of a square** <note>original: *to symmetron tēn diametron*. This refers to the mathematical proof that the diagonal of a square is incommensurable with its sides. Aristotle uses this classic example of a logical impossibility—something that cannot even begin to be "possible."</note> could be commensurable with its sides would neither begin to happen, nor could it ever happen. Conversely, where the end is possible, the beginning is also possible; for all things originate from a beginning. 

And if that which is **posterior** <note>original: *hysteron*. "Posterior" here means later in time or more developed in nature.</note> in essence or in its generation is possible to come into being, then that which is **prior** <note>original: *proteron*. The earlier stage or the necessary precursor.</note> is also possible. For instance, if it is possible for a man to come into being, it must also be possible for a boy to have been, for the boy comes first; and if a boy is possible, then a man is possible, for the boy is the starting point. 

Furthermore, those things for which there is a natural **love or desire** <note>original: *erōs ē epithymia*. Aristotle suggests that human nature rarely yearns for things that are fundamentally impossible to achieve.</note> are possible; for no one, for the most part, loves or desires things that are impossible. Also, things for which there are sciences and arts are possible to both exist and come into being. 

Whatever has its origin of generation in things that we can compel or persuade is also possible; these are the things over which we are stronger, or are the masters, or where we have friends. 

And those things whose **parts** are possible, the **whole** is also possible; and where the whole is possible, the parts are also possible. For if the front-slit, the toe-piece, and the upper-leather <note>original: *proschisma kai kephalis kai chitōn*. These are specific components of a Greek sandal or shoe.</note> can be made, then the shoes themselves can be made. And if the shoes can be made, then the front-slit, the toe-piece, and the upper-leather must also be possible to make. 

Additionally, if the whole **genus** <term>genus: *genos*. A broad category or class of things.</term> is among the things possible to come into being, then so is the **species** <term>species: *eidos*. A specific type or sub-category within a genus.</term>. And if the species is possible, then so is the genus. For example, if it is possible for a **boat** to be built, then a **trireme** <note>original: *triērē*. A standard ancient Greek warship with three banks of oars.</note> is possible; and if a trireme is possible, then a boat is possible. 

And if one of two things naturally related to each other is possible, then the other is too; for example, if the **double** is possible, then the **half** is possible; and if the half is possible, then the double is possible. 

And if it is possible for something to happen without art or preparation, it is even more possible through art and careful attention. As **Agathon** <note>Agathon was a famous Athenian tragic poet, often mentioned by Aristotle and a central figure in Plato’s *Symposium*.</note> has said:

<summary>Aristotle outlines the logical grounds for arguing "possibility," using examples from mathematics, biology, and craftsmanship to show how parts relate to wholes and causes to effects.</summary>
<keywords>possibility, logic, genus and species, trireme, Agathon, rhetoric, Aristotle</keywords>

<meta>This page continues Aristotle's Rhetoric, Book II, Chapter 19. The previous page discussed the "common topic" of the possible and the impossible, establishing the logical foundations for whether an event can occur. This page moves into the practical application of these arguments, specifically concerning past events—whether something has or has not happened.</meta>

# ARISTOTELIS

Indeed, some things are achieved through **chance**, while others involve a combination of **necessity and chance**. 

Furthermore, if a task is possible for those who are inferior, weaker, or less prudent, it is even more possible for their opposites. This is just as **Isocrates** <note>original: *Isokratēs*. A famous Athenian rhetorician and contemporary of Aristotle.</note> said: it would be a terrible thing if **Euthynus** <note>original: *Euthynos*. A figure mentioned in Isocrates' speeches; the point is that if a less capable person could learn a subject, a master like Isocrates surely could.</note> could learn it, but he himself would be unable to find it. Regarding the **impossible** <note>original: *adynatōn*. Things that cannot happen.</note>, it is clear that the arguments are simply the opposites of those already mentioned.

Whether a thing **has happened** or **has not happened** must be examined based on the following criteria. First, if the thing that is naturally less likely to happen has occurred, then that which is more likely to have occurred would also have happened. And if the thing that usually happens **later** has occurred, then that which happens **earlier** must also have occurred; for example, if a person has **forgotten** something, they must have **learned** it at some point. 

Also, if a person had both the **power** and the **will** to act, they have acted; for everyone acts when they have the desire and the capability, as there is nothing to stop them. Furthermore, they acted if they had the will and there were no external obstacles. Or, if the action was possible and they were **angry**; or if it was possible and they **desired** it. This is because, for the most part, people do what they yearn for if they have the power—the **base** <note>original: *phauloi*. People of low character or those lacking self-discipline.</note> act out of **lack of self-control** <note>term: *akrasia*. A state of acting against one's better judgment due to weakness of will.</note>, while the **virtuous** <note>original: *epieikeis*. People of fitting or equitable character.</note> act because they desire what is good.

Also, if a thing was **about to happen**, it likely did happen; for it is probable that one who is about to act actually carries it out. And if those things have occurred which naturally happen **before** an event, or for the sake of that event—for instance, if it **lightened**, it also **thundered**; and if a person **attempted** an act, they **performed** it. Conversely, if the things that naturally happen **afterwards**, or the goal for which a thing happens, have occurred, then the prior thing and that which was done for the sake of the goal must also have occurred. For example, if it thundered, it also lightened; and if a person performed an act, they also attempted it. 

Of all these sequences, some follow of **necessity**, while others hold true only **for the most part**. Regarding the fact that something has **not happened**, it is clear that the arguments are derived from the opposites of what has been said; and regarding the **future**, the same principles clearly apply. For that which is within...

<meta>This page continues Aristotle's Rhetoric, Book II, Chapter 19. This page concludes the discussion on the "possible" and "magnitude," then transitions into Chapter 20, which introduces the two main types of rhetorical proof: the example and the enthymeme.</meta>

->RHETORIC. BOOK II. CHAPTER XX.<-

...capability and the **will** to act, it will happen. This also applies to things driven by **desire**, **anger**, or **reasoning** when the person possesses the necessary power. Because of this, if someone is on the verge of acting or intends to act, the action will likely occur; for, as a general rule, things that are intended are more likely to happen than those that are not. 

Furthermore, an event will likely happen if those things have already occurred which naturally happen beforehand; for example, if it is cloudy, it is likely to rain. Also, if that for the sake of which a thing exists has occurred, then the thing itself is likely to follow; for example, if there is a foundation, then there will be a house. 

Regarding the **magnitude** <term>magnitude (megethos): the relative importance, size, or severity of a subject</term> and smallness of things, whether one thing is greater or lesser, and generally what constitutes "large" or "small," this is clear from what we have already discussed. We previously spoke in the section on **deliberative oratory** <note>Aristotle refers back to Book I, where he discussed how to argue that one "good" is greater than another for the purpose of giving advice.</note> about the magnitude of "goods" and about the greater and lesser in an absolute sense. 

Therefore, since the intended goal of every speech is a "good"—such as the **advantageous**, the **noble**, or the **just**—it is clear that every speaker must derive their methods of **amplification** <term>amplification (auxēsis): a rhetorical technique used to make an issue or merit seem more significant</term> from these categories. To search for anything beyond these regarding magnitude or superiority in the abstract is "empty talk"; for in practical use, specific facts about a case are more authoritative than general abstractions. 

Let this suffice for our discussion on the **possible** and **impossible** <note>original: *dynatou kai adynatou*</note>, whether an event has or has not happened, whether it will or will not happen, and the magnitude or smallness of things.

## CHAPTER XX.

It remains now to speak about the **common proofs** <term>common proofs (koinōn pisteis): logical tools available to all speakers regardless of the subject matter</term> available to everyone, since we have already discussed the specific ones. 

These common proofs are of two types: the **example** <note>original: *paradeigma*. A rhetorical argument based on induction from similar cases.</note> and the **enthymeme** <note>original: *enthymēma*. A rhetorical syllogism or logical argument based on probabilities.</note>; for the **maxim** <note>original: *gnōmē*. A general statement or proverb about human conduct.</note> is actually a part of an enthymeme. 

First, let us speak about the **example**; for the example is similar to **induction** <term>induction (epagogē): the logical process of drawing a general conclusion from a set of specific instances</term>, and induction is a fundamental starting point for knowledge. There are two types of examples...

<meta>This page continues Aristotle's *Rhetoric*, Book II, Chapter 20. The previous page ended while discussing "amplification," the rhetorical method of making a point seem more significant. This page begins the categorization of "examples" (paradeigmata), which are one of the two main types of rhetorical proof alongside the logical "enthymeme."</meta>

# ARISTOTLE

...of **examples** <note>original: "παραδειγμάτων" (paradeigmatōn). The start of the word is on the preceding page.</note> there are two types. One type of example consists in relating things that have happened before; the other consists in inventing them oneself. Of the latter, one is the **comparison** <term>comparison (parabolē): an analogy or illustrative parallel, often called a Socratic parable</term> and the other is **fables** <term>fables (logoi): specifically, fictional moral stories or "apologues"</term>, such as those of Aesop or the Libyans <note>Libyan fables were an ancient genre of animal stories similar to Aesop’s, often featuring African animals.</note>. 

The "historical" type of example is like this: someone might say that we must prepare against the Persian King and not allow him to conquer Egypt. For previously, Darius <note>Darius I, the Persian King who invaded Greece in 490 BCE.</note> did not cross over into Greece until he had taken Egypt; but once he took it, he crossed. And again, Xerxes <note>Darius's son, who led the massive second invasion of Greece in 480 BCE.</note> did not attack until he took Egypt; but once he took it, he crossed. Therefore, if this current king takes Egypt, he too will cross over; for this reason, he must not be permitted to do so.

A **comparison** is the Socratic style; for instance, if one were to say that officials should not be chosen by lot <note>In Athenian democracy, many public officials were chosen by random "lot" (drawing beans) rather than by election.</note>. That would be like choosing athletes by lot—not those who are capable of competing, but simply those who happen to be picked by chance; or like choosing which sailor should act as pilot by lot, as if the person who draws the lot should lead, rather than the person who actually knows how to steer.

A **fable** is like the one told by Stesichorus <note>A famous Greek lyric poet.</note> regarding Phalaris <note>A notorious tyrant of Himera in Sicily, known for his cruelty.</note>, or the one by Aesop on behalf of a demagogue. For when the people of Himera had chosen Phalaris as their **general with absolute power** <term>general with absolute power (strategos autokrator): a military leader given full authority, often a stepping stone to becoming a tyrant</term> and were about to grant him a personal bodyguard, Stesichorus, after speaking on other matters, told them a fable:

> Once a horse held a meadow all to himself. But when a stag came and ruined the pasture, the horse, wishing to take revenge on the stag, asked a man if he could help him punish the animal. The man said he would, provided the horse took a bit <note>original: "χαλινὸν" (chalinon), a bridle or bit used to control a horse.</note> and allowed the man to mount him while carrying javelins. Once the horse agreed and the man had mounted, instead of getting his revenge, the horse became a slave to the man. "In the same way," Stesichorus said, "you should take care that, in your desire to punish your enemies, you do not suffer the same fate as the horse."

For you have already...

<summary>Aristotle defines two types of rhetorical examples: historical precedents and invented illustrations (comparisons and fables). He uses the famous fable of the horse and the stag to warn against giving too much power to a leader in exchange for protection.</summary>

<meta>This page continues the discussion of rhetorical "examples" (paradeigmata). The previous page introduced the two types: historical parallels and invented parallels (comparisons and fables). It ended with a Socratic comparison about the absurdity of choosing public officials by random lot. This page concludes a fable by Stesichorus before moving on to a famous fable by Aesop and then providing technical advice on how to use these examples in a speech.</meta>

### RHETORIC. BOOK II. CHAPTER XX. 111

...you already have a bit in your mouths, having chosen a general with absolute power; but if you give him a bodyguard and allow him to mount up, you will then be slaves to Phalaris. <note>Aristotle is finishing a story about the poet Stesichorus, who warned the people of Himera against giving the tyrant Phalaris a bodyguard by telling a fable about a horse who allowed a man to arm and mount him to help hunt a stag, only to find himself enslaved to the man.</note>

Aesop, too, while speaking in Samos in defense of a **demagogue** <term>demagogue (dēmagōgos): literally "a leader of the people," though often implying one who gains power by appealing to the prejudices of the populace</term> who was on trial for his life, told this story: A fox, while crossing a river, was swept into a ravine. Unable to get out, she suffered there for a long time, and many **dog-ticks** <note>original: "κυνοραϊστάς" (kynoraistas). These are blood-sucking parasites, likely ticks or fleas.</note> attached themselves to her. A wandering hedgehog, seeing her and feeling pity, asked if he should remove the ticks from her. But the fox would not allow it. When the hedgehog asked why, she replied: "Because these are already full of me and draw very little blood; but if you take these away, others will come who are hungry, and they will drink up what remains of my blood."

"So it is with you, men of Samos," Aesop said. "This man no longer harms you, for he is already wealthy; but if you put him to death, others will come who are poor, and they will waste your public funds by stealing."

Fables are well-suited for public speaking, and they have this advantage: it is difficult to find actual historical events that are similar to our situation, but it is easier to invent fables. For one must create them just as one creates **comparisons** <note>Aristotle refers back to the Socratic parables mentioned on the previous page.</note>, provided one is able to see the likeness between things—an ability that is made easier through the study of philosophy. 

While it is easier to provide examples through fables, historical examples are more useful for **deliberation** <term>deliberative rhetoric (bouleusasthai): speech intended to advise or dissuade an audience regarding future actions</term>; for, generally speaking, the future is similar to what has happened in the past.

When using examples, if you do not have **enthymemes** <term>enthymeme (enthymēma): a rhetorical "syllogism" or logical argument based on probabilities rather than certainties</term>, you must use your examples as primary demonstrations, for they provide the proof that persuades the audience. However, if you do have enthymemes, you should use the examples as **witnesses** <note>original: "μαρτυρίοις" (martyriois). Aristotle suggests using the example as supporting evidence rather than the main logical engine of the speech.</note>, treating them as a conclusion to your logical arguments. 

If examples are placed at the beginning, they function like **induction** <term>induction (epagōgē): the process of drawing a general conclusion from a set of specific instances</term>. But induction is not very suitable for the style of public oratory, except in a few cases. If they are placed at the end, however, they function like testimony; and a witness is always a persuasive force.

<summary>Aristotle uses fables by Stesichorus and Aesop to show how fictional stories can provide political warnings, concluding that while fables are easier to invent, historical parallels are more effective for predicting the future.</summary>
<keywords>Aesop, Samos, Phalaris, fables, enthymemes, induction, rhetorical strategy, political speech, allegory</keywords>

Aristotle explains that a maxim is a general statement about human action and shows how it relates to the "enthymeme" (rhetorical logic).</meta>

<page-num>112</page-num>
<header>ARISTOTLE'S</header>

...to death. <note>The Greek text begins with the suffix "-nos," completing the sentence from the previous page: "if you put him to death, others will come who are hungry..."</note> Therefore, when placing examples at the beginning of a speech, it is necessary to use many; but when using them as a conclusion, even one is sufficient. For a single witness, if they are trustworthy, is useful. We have now discussed how many kinds of examples there are, how they should be used, and when.

## CHAPTER XXI.

Regarding the use of **maxims** <term>maxim (gnōmē): a general declaration or "moral" about human conduct or life</term>, it will become clearest what they are, what subjects they concern, and when and for whom it is appropriate to use them in speeches, once we have defined what a maxim is.

A maxim is a declaration—not, however, about particulars (such as what kind of person **Iphicrates** <note>Iphicrates was a famous Athenian general known for his military reforms and his humble origins.</note> is), but rather a general statement. Yet it is not about every kind of general truth (such as "the straight is the opposite of the curved"), but only about those things which concern human actions and what is to be chosen or avoided in conduct. Consequently, since **enthymemes** <term>enthymeme (enthymēma): a rhetorical syllogism or logical argument based on probabilities rather than certainties</term> are the reasonings concerning such matters, the conclusions and the premises of enthymemes are—once the formal reasoning process is stripped away—virtually maxims. For example:

> No man who is of sound mind should ever
> have his children taught to be excessively wise. <note>original: "Χρὴ δ' οὔ ποθ', ὅστις ἀρτίφρων πέφυκ' ἀνὴρ, Παῖδας περισσῶς ἐκδιδάσκεσθαι σοφούς." This is a quote from Euripides' play, *Medea*.</note>

This statement by itself is a maxim; but when the cause and the "reason why" are added, the whole thing becomes an enthymeme. For example:

> For apart from the other idleness they possess,
> they earn hostile envy from their fellow citizens. <note>By adding the reason (that being too "wise" leads to envy), the simple advice becomes a logical argument.</note>

And this:

> There is no man who is happy in all things. <note>original: "Οὐκ ἔστιν, ὅστις πάντ' ἀνὴρ εὐδαιμονεῖ." From Euripides' lost play *Stheneboea*.</note>

And this:

> There is no man who is truly free. <note>original: "Οὐκ ἔστιν ἀνδρῶν, ὅστις ἐστ' ἐλεύθερος." From Euripides' play *Hecuba*.</note>

This is a maxim; but when joined with what follows, it is an enthymeme:

> For he is a slave either to money or to fortune.

If, then, a maxim is what we have said it is, it follows that there are four kinds...

<meta>This page continues the classification of "maxims" (gnōmai) from the previous discussion. On the preceding page, Aristotle began defining a maxim as a general statement about human conduct, often serving as the premise or conclusion of a logical argument (enthymeme).</meta>

### RHETORIC. BOOK II. CHAPTER XXI. 113

There are several kinds of **maxims**; for they either include an **epilogue** <term>epilogue (epilogos): here used as a supplementary explanation or justification for the statement</term> or are without an epilogue. Those requiring a demonstration are the ones that state something paradoxical <note>Something "paradoxical" in this context is an idea that goes against common opinion or seems contradictory.</note> or disputed; however, those that state nothing paradoxical should be used without an epilogue. Of these, it is necessary that some require no epilogue because they are already well-known, such as:

-> To be healthy is the best thing for a man, at least as it seems to us. <-

For this is how it appears to most people. Others, as soon as they are spoken, are clear to those who reflect upon them, such as:

-> No lover exists who does not love forever. <note>original: "Oudeis erastēs, hostis ouk aei philei." This line is attributed to Euripides.</note> <-

Of those maxims that include an epilogue, some are a part of an **enthymeme** <term>enthymeme: a rhetorical syllogism, or an argument based on likelihoods rather than absolute certainties</term>, such as:

-> A man who is of sound mind should never... <note>Aristotle is quoting from Euripides' play *Medea*. The full thought, which he expects the reader to know, is: "...never have his children taught to be excessively wise."</note> <-

Other maxims are "enthymematic" in nature but are not strictly a part of an enthymeme; these are the most highly regarded. These are the ones in which the cause of what is being said is made manifest within the statement itself, such as in this case:

-> Being mortal, do not harbor an immortal anger. <note>original: "Athanaton orgēn mē phylatte thnētos ōn." This reminds the listener that since life is short, holding onto a grudge forever is illogical.</note> <-

For to say that one should not always harbor anger is the maxim; but the addition, "being mortal," explains the "why." Similar to this is the saying:

-> It is fitting for a mortal to think mortal thoughts, not immortal thoughts. <-

It is clear, then, from what has been said, how many types of maxims there are and which subject each suits. Regarding disputed or paradoxical matters, one should not use a maxim without an epilogue. Instead, one should either put the epilogue first and use the maxim as the conclusion—for instance, if someone were to say, "Because one should neither be envied nor be idle, I say that one should not be over-educated"—or, having stated the maxim first, one should then add the...

<sig>ARISTOTLE'S RHETORIC H</sig>

<summary>Aristotle categorizes maxims into those that stand alone as self-evident truths and those that require an explanation (epilogue), particularly when the statement is controversial or paradoxical. He illustrates how a maxim's power often comes from providing the "reason why" within the statement itself.</summary>

<meta>This page continues Aristotle's analysis of maxims (gnōmai) from page 113. It transitions from discussing maxims that require explanation to those that are obscure and then explores the social etiquette and rhetorical strategy of using them.</meta>

### ARISTOTLE'S RHETORIC. BOOK II. CHAPTER XXI. 114

...mentioned before. Regarding those maxims that are not paradoxical but are nonetheless obscure, one should add the "reason why" <term>the reason why (to dioti): the underlying cause or justification that makes the statement logical</term> in the most concise manner. 

In such cases, Laconian <note>Laconian refers to the Spartans, who were famous for their brief, pithy way of speaking—the origin of the word "laconic."</note> apophthegms and riddling sayings are also suitable; for instance, what Stesichorus said to the Locrians: that they should not be insolent, 

-> lest the cicadas sing from the ground. <note>original: "hopōs mē hoi tettiges chamothen adōsin." This is a riddle meaning that their land would be so devastated by war that the trees would be cut down, forcing the cicadas to sing from the dirt.</note> <-

It is appropriate for a person of older age to speak in maxims, and to do so concerning matters in which they are experienced. For a person who is not of such an age to speak in maxims is unbecoming, much like telling fables; and to do so regarding matters of which one is inexperienced is foolish and uneducated. 

There is sufficient proof of this: for rustics <note>Aristotle uses "agroikoi," referring to unrefined country-dwellers.</note> are the most fond of coining maxims and readily declaring them. To speak in universal terms about that which is not universal is most appropriate in moments of complaint or indignation; in these instances, it should be done either at the beginning or after providing the proof. 

One should also make use of hackneyed <note>Aristotle uses "tethryllēmenais," meaning "much-talked-of" or even "cliché."</note> and common maxims if they are useful; for because they are common, they seem to be correct, as if everyone were in agreement. For example, when encouraging someone to face danger even if the omens of the sacrifice are unfavorable:

-> One omen is best: to fight for one's country. <note>original: "Heis oiōnos aristos, amynesthai peri patrēs." This is a famous line from Homer's *Iliad* (12.243), spoken by Hector.</note> <-

And when facing superior numbers:

-> The god of war is impartial. <note>original: "Xynos enyalios." Another Homeric quote (*Iliad* 18.309), meaning that the luck of war can shift to either side.</note> <-

And regarding the necessity of killing the children of enemies, even if they have done no wrong:

-> Foolish is he who, having killed the father, leaves the sons behind. <note>original: "Nēpios, hos patera kteinas, paidas kataleipoi." This was a common Greek proverb regarding the cycle of blood-feuds and revenge.</note> <-

Furthermore, some proverbs are also maxims; for instance, the proverb "An Attic neighbor." <note>This refers to the reputation of Athenians as troublesome or dangerous neighbors.</note> One should also speak maxims that contradict those in common circulation—by "common circulation," I mean such as:

-> Know thyself <note>original: "Gnōthi sauton."</note> <-

and

-> Nothing in excess <note>original: "Mēden agan."</note> <-

—whenever doing so will make one's character <term>character (ēthos): the moral standing or personality projected by the speaker to gain the audience's trust</term> appear better, or when the maxim is spoken with passion. It is spoken with passion if, for example, someone...

<summary>Aristotle discusses the appropriate age and context for using maxims, the effectiveness of using common proverbs (even clichés), and when to strategically contradict famous cultural sayings like "Know thyself."</summary>
