<meta>This page continues Aristotle's discussion on the strategic use of maxims. The previous page ended while discussing "hackneyed and common" maxims. This page begins mid-sentence, describing a speaker who uses a maxim in a state of anger or indignation to challenge a conventional wisdom, specifically the famous Delphic command to "know thyself."</meta>

# RHETORICA. LIB. II. CAP. XXI. 115

...speaking in anger, might say that it is a lie that one "ought to know oneself"; for this man, at any rate, if he had known himself, would never have claimed the right to lead an army. The moral character <term>moral character (ēthos): the persona or disposition of the speaker as perceived by the audience</term> is shown to be better when one says that it is not necessary, as the saying goes, to "love as if one will later hate," but rather to "hate as if one will later love." One ought to make one's moral purpose <term>moral purpose (proairesis): the deliberate choice or intention that reveals a person's values</term> clear through the wording; if not, one should add the reason. For example, by saying that one should love, not as the proverb suggests, but "as if one will love forever," because the alternative <note>Aristotle refers here to the famous maxim of Bias of Priene: "Love as if you will one day hate."</note> belongs to a treacherous person.

Or one might say: "The common saying does not please me; for the true friend ought to love as if he will love forever." Nor does the saying "Nothing in excess" <note>original: "mēden agan." This was one of the famous Delphic maxims of ancient Greece, advising moderation.</note> please me; for one ought to hate the wicked in excess.

Maxims provide great help to speeches, primarily because of the unrefined nature <term>unrefined nature (phortikotēta): referring to an audience that prefers simple, relatable truths over complex philosophical proofs</term> of the listeners. For they rejoice if a speaker, while speaking in generalities, hits upon the specific opinions which they themselves happen to hold in particular instances. What I mean will be clear in this way—and at the same time, I will show how one should "hunt" for these maxims. For a maxim, as has been said, is a general declaration; and listeners rejoice when something is stated universally which they have already assumed in a particular case. 

For instance, if someone happens to have encountered bad neighbors or worthless children, they would welcome a speaker who says, "Nothing is more troublesome than a neighborhood," or "Nothing is more foolish than fathering children." Therefore, a speaker must aim to guess what the audience's prior assumptions happen to be, and then speak in universal terms about those specific things. This is one use of speaking in maxims <term>speaking in maxims (gnōmologeion): the practice of using pithy, universal statements to reinforce an argument</term>, but there is another that is even better: it gives the speech a moral character.

Speeches possess "character" in cases where the moral purpose is made clear. All maxims do this, because the person uttering the maxim is making a general declaration about matters of moral choice; consequently, if the maxims are good, they make the speaker appear to be a person of good character...

<sig>H 2</sig>

<summary>Aristotle explains how maxims can be used to challenge popular wisdom and how they appeal to an audience's pre-existing biases. He emphasizes that using "good" maxims helps a speaker project a virtuous moral character (ēthos).</summary>
<keywords>maxims, gnōmē, moral character, ēthos, moral purpose, proairesis, audience psychology, rhetoric, persuasion, moderation</keywords>

<meta>This page concludes the discussion of the maxim (gnōmē) and begins a new chapter on the enthymeme, the core logical structure of rhetorical persuasion. Aristotle transitions from the use of general sayings to the practical construction of arguments based on specific facts.</meta>

...ies. Let this much be said concerning the **maxim** <term>maxim (gnōmē): a general statement about human conduct or universal truths</term>, what it is, how many kinds there are, how they should be used, and what benefit they provide.

### CHAPTER XXII.

Regarding **enthymemes** <term>enthymeme (enthymēma): the "body of proof" in rhetoric; a form of syllogism that starts from premises the audience already accepts</term>, let us speak generally about the way one should look for them, and after this, the **topics** <term>topics (topoi): literally "places"—logical "locations" or categories where a speaker can find arguments for any subject</term>; for each of these is a different species. It has been stated previously that the enthymeme is a kind of **syllogism** <term>syllogism (syllogismos): a logical argument where a conclusion is reached from two given or assumed premises</term>, and in what way it is a syllogism, and how it differs from those used in dialectic. For one should not draw a conclusion from too far back, nor should one include every step of the argument; the former is unclear because of the length, while the latter is mere chatter because it states what is already obvious. 

This is the very reason why the uneducated <term>uneducated (apaideutous): those without formal training in logic or philosophy</term> are more persuasive than the educated when speaking before crowds—just as the poets say that the uneducated are "more musical" when speaking before a crowd. For the educated speak of commonalities and generalities, while the uneducated speak from what they know and from things that are close at hand. 

Consequently, one should not speak from every possible opinion, but from those that are defined—for instance, those held by the judges or those whom the judges accept. And it should be clear that the subject appears this way either to everyone or to the majority. One should draw conclusions not only from necessary truths, but also from things that happen for the most part.

First, then, one must understand that concerning whatever subject one must speak and reason upon—whether by political reasoning or any other kind—it is necessary to possess the facts belonging to that subject, either all or at least some of them. For if you have nothing, you would have nothing from which to draw a conclusion. I mean, for example, how could we advise the **Athenians** <note>original: "Athēnaiois"</note> whether to go to war or not if we do not know what their strength is—whether it is naval or land-based, or both—and how great it is, or what their revenues are...

<summary>Aristotle explains that effective rhetorical arguments (enthymemes) should avoid overly long logical chains and obvious statements, noting that simple, specific arguments often persuade crowds better than abstract ones. He emphasizes that a speaker must first master the specific facts of a case before they can construct a persuasive argument.</summary>

<keywords>enthymeme, syllogism, rhetoric, persuasion, logic, Athenians, political speech, evidence-based argument</keywords>

<meta>This page continues the discussion of how to find the "building blocks" of a persuasive argument (enthymemes). The previous page emphasized that a speaker should not argue from abstract principles alone but from things familiar to the audience. This page specifies that one must have a stock of facts about the specific subject—whether a person, a city, or an idea—in order to praise, blame, or advise.</meta>

# RHETORIC. BOOK II. CHAPTER XXII.

...ties; or whether they have friends and enemies; furthermore, what wars they have fought, and how they conducted them, and other such details. For example, it would be impossible to praise the Athenians if we did not know about the naval battle at **Salamis** <note>A decisive 480 BCE naval victory where the Greeks, led by Athens, defeated the Persian fleet</note>, or the battle at **Marathon** <note>The 490 BCE land battle where the Athenians famously defeated the first Persian invasion</note>, or the deeds performed on behalf of the **Heraclidae** <note>The "Children of Hercules"; in mythology, the Athenians were celebrated for protecting them, which served as a standard theme in Athenian political propaganda</note>, or any other such historical events. For everyone bases their praise on noble qualities that actually exist or are believed to exist.

In the same way, people also direct blame based on the opposite of these things, looking for what shameful qualities they actually possess or seem to possess; for instance, that the Athenians enslaved the other Greeks, or that they sold into slavery the people of **Aegina** and **Potidaea** <note>These were controversial actions taken by Athens against fellow Greeks during the Peloponnesian War, often cited by critics to show Athenian tyranny</note>—peoples who had fought with distinction alongside them against the **barbarian** <note>original: "ton barbaron"; a term used by Greeks to refer to non-Greek speakers, specifically the Persians in this context</note>; and they use any other such fault or error that belongs to them.

In this same manner, those who accuse and those who defend themselves conduct their cases by looking at the available facts. It makes no difference whether we are doing this regarding the **Athenians** or the **Lacedaemonians** <note>The Spartans</note>, or an individual person, or even a god. For if one were advising **Achilles** <note>The hero of the Iliad, used here as a hypothetical subject for a speech</note>, or praising or blaming him, or accusing or defending him, one must take the facts that belong to him or seem to belong to him. We use these as our source: if there is something noble or shameful, we use it to praise or blame; if there is something just or unjust, to accuse or defend; and if there is something advantageous or harmful, to offer advice.

This applies similarly to any subject whatsoever—for instance, regarding **justice**, whether it is a good or not a good thing, we must argue from the qualities inherent to justice or to "the good." Therefore, since it is evident that everyone demonstrates their points in this way—whether they reason with high logical precision or more loosely—it is clear that they do not draw their arguments from just anything, but from the facts surrounding each specific subject, and through...

<summary>Aristotle argues that persuasive speech must be grounded in specific historical or character-based facts about the subject at hand. He illustrates this by showing how Athenian history and the character of Achilles provide the necessary material for praise, blame, or legal argument.</summary>

<keywords>persuasion, Athenian history, Salamis, Marathon, Achilles, justice, ethics, rhetoric, praise and blame</keywords>

<meta>This page continues Aristotle's instructions on the "topics" of rhetoric. He has just explained that a speaker must know the specific history of a city (like Athens) to praise or blame it. Now, he moves to the general principle: a speaker needs a prepared "stock" of specific facts about any subject to create effective arguments.</meta>

# ARISTOTELIS
### RHETORIC. BOOK II. CHAPTER XXII (CONTINUED).

...of the argument; for it is clear that it is impossible to demonstrate it otherwise. It is evident that it is necessary, just as in the **Topics** <note>A reference to Aristotle's earlier work, *Topica*, which provides a system for organized logical debate</note>, first to have a selection of facts regarding each subject—both the possible facts and the most opportune ones. Concerning things that happen on the spur of the moment, one must search in the same way, looking not at indefinite things, but at the actual facts available concerning the matter of the speech. A speaker should define those facts which are as numerous and as close to the subject as possible; for the more facts one has at hand, the easier it is to demonstrate a point, and the closer they are to the subject, the more appropriate <term>appropriate: "oikeiotera," meaning facts that belong specifically to the subject rather than being general truths</term> they are and the less general they seem. 

By "general," I mean, for instance, praising **Achilles** <note>The greatest Greek hero of the Trojan War</note> because he is a human, or because he is a demigod, or because he campaigned against **Ilium** <note>The city of Troy</note>; for these things also apply to many others. Consequently, such a speaker praises Achilles no more than he does **Diomedes** <note>Another prominent Greek warrior in the *Iliad*</note>. But "specific" facts are those which have happened to no one else but Achilles, such as his killing of **Hector**, the best of the Trojans, or his killing of **Cycnus** <note>A Trojan ally who was said to be invulnerable to weapons until Achilles strangled him</note>, who prevented the Greeks from landing by being invulnerable. Or that he joined the campaign as the youngest soldier and without being bound by the oath <note>The Oath of Tyndareus, which bound most Greek kings to defend Helen's marriage; Achilles was too young to have sworn it and joined the war of his own volition</note>; and all other such specific details.

This, then, is one way of selecting arguments, and this first method is the **topical** <note>original: "topikos"; referring to the "topoi" or "places" in the mind where a speaker looks for arguments</note> one. Now let us discuss the elements of **enthymemes** <note>A rhetorical syllogism; an argument that starts from premises the audience already believes to lead them to a new conclusion</note>. I use the terms "element" and "topic" of an enthymeme to mean the same thing. But first, let us speak about those things which it is necessary to address first.

For there are two kinds of enthymemes: some are **demonstrative** <note>original: "deiktika"; arguments that prove a thing is or is not so</note>, and others are **refutative** <note>original: "elenktika"; arguments aimed at disproving an opponent's position</note>. They differ just as refutation and **syllogism** <note>A logical proof consisting of a major premise, a minor premise, and a conclusion</note> differ in **dialectics** <note>The art of philosophical conversation and logical inquiry</note>. The demonstrative enthymeme draws a conclusion from premises that are mutually agreed upon; the refutative enthymeme draws conclusions from things not agreed upon <note>Aristotle means that refutation often points out contradictions or draws conclusions that conflict with the opponent’s claims</note>. Now then, we have almost all the facts concerning each of the useful and necessary kinds...

<summary>Aristotle explains that effective persuasion requires specific facts rather than generalities, using the legends of Achilles to illustrate the point. He also introduces the two main types of rhetorical arguments: demonstrative and refutative.</summary>

<meta>This page continues Aristotle’s discussion on the "topics" (topoi) or logical locations from which a speaker can draw arguments. Having established that a speaker needs specific facts about a subject, he now transitions into a categorized list of these general lines of reasoning, beginning the famous catalog of twenty-eight "demonstrative" topics in Chapter 23.</meta>

RHETORIC. BOOK II. CHAPTER XXIII.

...the topics <note>original: "topoi," literally "places" or mental locations where arguments are found</note> are determined. For the premises have been selected concerning each individual subject; consequently, we already have the topics from which one must draw **enthymemes** <note>A rhetorical argument based on probabilities or signs, where the audience often fills in a missing premise</note>—whether they concern good or evil, the noble or the base, the just or the unjust, as well as those concerning character, emotions, and habits. These topics have been previously grasped by us in the same way.

Furthermore, let us take another approach to look at all these things in a general sense. Let us speak of them by distinguishing those that are **refutative** and those that are **demonstrative**, as well as those of "apparent" enthymemes—which are not actually enthymemes, just as they are not actually **syllogisms** <note>A formal logical deduction where a conclusion follows necessarily from two premises</note>. Once these have been made clear, let us define the methods of "solutions" and "objections," and from what sources one should bring them against enthymemes.

### C A P T E R XXIII.

One topic of the demonstrative <note>original: "deiktikōn," meaning arguments that prove a point directly</note> enthymemes is derived from **opposites**. For one must observe if the opposite quality belongs to the opposite subject. We use this to refute an argument if the quality does not belong, or to establish one if it does. For example, to argue that "to be temperate is good, because to lack restraint is harmful." Or as found in the *Messeniacus* <note>A lost speech by Alcidamas defending the independence of the Messenians against Sparta</note>: *“For if war is the cause of present evils, then one must set things right through peace.”* And:

> *For if it is not just to fall into anger against those*
> *Who have done wrong unintentionally,*
> *Then even if someone does good by necessity,*
> *It is not fitting that gratitude be owed to him.* — And:
>
> *But if indeed it is possible for lies to be*
> *Persuasive among mortals, one must also believe the opposite:*
> *That many truths happen to be unbelievable to mortals.*

Another topic is derived from **similar inflections** <term>inflections: "ptōseōn," referring to the various grammatical forms of a word root—if a quality applies to an adverb, it should apply to the noun</term>; for the quality must similarly belong or not belong to both. For example, to argue that "the just is not always good"; for if it were, then "acting justly" <note>original: "dikaiōs"</note> would always be good; but as it stands, to be put to death justly is not always desirable...

<summary>Aristotle begins a systematic list of "topics"—general lines of reasoning—used to build rhetorical arguments, focusing first on the use of opposites and grammatically related terms.</summary>

<keywords>Aristotle, Rhetoric, enthymemes, topics, logic, opposites, Messeniacus, justice, temperance</keywords>

<meta>This page continues the list of twenty-eight "topoi" or logical starting points for arguments. The previous page ended with the first topic, arguments based on opposites. # ARISTOTLE

...to die justly. Another topic is derived from **correlatives** <note>original: "ek tōn pros allēla," referring to things that exist in relationship to each other, like father and son, or buyer and seller</note>. For if "acting nobly" or "acting justly" applies to one party, then "having experienced the act" must apply to the other. Similarly, if "to command" applies, then "to have performed the act" applies. An example of this is the tax-farmer Diomedon's argument regarding the tax-contracts: "If it is not shameful for you to sell [the right to collect taxes], then it is not shameful for us to buy it."

Furthermore, if "nobly" or "justly" applies to the person who experienced the act, it must also apply to the one who did it; and if to the one who did it, then to the one who experienced it. However, there is a possibility for **fallacy** <note>original: "paralogisasthai," a false or deceptive piece of reasoning</note> here. For if a man was put to death justly, he certainly suffered justly—but perhaps not justly *at your hands*. Therefore, one must examine separately whether the sufferer deserved to suffer, and whether the doer had the right to do the deed; then use the argument in whichever way fits the case. For sometimes these two points do not harmonize. Nothing prevents a situation like that found in the *Alcmaeon* of Theodectes:

->Did no mortal man hate your mother?<-

And he says in reply:

->But we must distinguish and examine the facts.<-

When Alphesiboea asks "How?", he responds:

->They judged that she should die, but not that I should kill her.<-
<note>In Greek mythology, Alcmaeon killed his mother Eriphyle to avenge his father. This play by Theodectes is now lost, but Aristotle uses it to show that even if a punishment is deserved, the person carrying it out might not be the right one to do so.</note>

Another example is the trial regarding Demosthenes and those who killed Nicanor; for since it was judged that they killed him justly, it was thought that he died justly. There is also the case of the man killed at Thebes, concerning whom the speaker asked the judges to decide if he deserved to die—on the grounds that it is not illegal to kill a man who deserved to die.

Another topic is derived from the **more and the less** <note>Known in logic as "a fortiori." If something is true in a more likely case, it is even truer in a less likely case (and vice versa).</note>. For example: "If not even the gods know everything, then surely humans do not." For the principle here is that if a quality does not belong to the subject to which it would more likely belong, it is clear that it does not belong to the subject where it is less likely. To argue that a man "beats his neighbors, since he even beats his father" is based on the principle that...

<summary>Aristotle explains two rhetorical "topics": arguments from correlative relationships (e.g., if selling is legal, buying must be) and arguments from "the more and the less" (a fortiori). He warns that logical traps exist, such as assuming a person who deserves to die may be killed by just anyone.</summary>

<keywords>Aristotle, Rhetoric, correlatives, a fortiori, Diomedon, Alcmaeon, Theodectes, logic, justice, tax-farming</keywords>

--- Page 134 ---
<meta>This page continues the examination of "topoi"—the standard lines of argument used by orators. The previous page discussed arguments based on opposites and reciprocal relationships. This page introduces the argument from "the more and the less" (a fortiori), the argument from time, and the argument from personal character.</meta>

### RHETORIC. BOOK II. CHAPTER 23.

...if the less likely exists, then the more likely also exists. This can be used to show either that something is the case or that it is not. Further, if neither the more likely nor the less likely exists, the same logic applies. Hence it is said:

> Your father is pitiable for having lost his children,
> but is Oineus not even more so, having lost
> the most famous offspring in Greece?
<note>Oineus was the father of the hero Meleager; the quote suggests that if one father's grief is recognized, a father who lost a more famous son deserves even more sympathy.</note>

And similarly: if even Theseus did no wrong, then neither did Alexander <note>original: "Alexandros," the alternative name for Paris of Troy</note>. And if the sons of Tyndareus <note>Castor and Pollux</note> did not, neither did Alexander. And if Hector killed Patroclus, then Alexander killed Achilles. And if other artisans are not worthless, then neither are philosophers. And if generals are not worthless just because they are often defeated, then neither are the <term>sophists</term> <note>professional teachers of rhetoric and virtue, often criticized in Athens</note>. Another example: "If it is necessary for a private citizen to care for your reputation, then it is necessary for you to care for the reputation of all Greece."

Another topic is derived from the **consideration of time**. For example, as Iphicrates argued in his speech against Harmodius: "If, before I had performed my service, I had demanded the honor of a statue on the condition that I succeeded, you would have granted it. Now that I have performed it, will you not grant it? Do not, therefore, make promises when you are expecting a benefit, but take them back once you have received it."

And again, regarding the question of whether the Thebans should allow Philip <note>Philip II of Macedon</note> to pass through their territory into Attica: "If he had asked this before he helped them against the Phocians, they would have promised it. It is absurd, then, that they should refuse him passage now, simply because he has already thrown away his advantage by trusting them."

Another topic is drawn from **turning what has been said against oneself back upon the speaker**. This is a particularly effective method, as used in the *Teucer* of Sophocles, and by Iphicrates against Aristophon. Iphicrates asked him whether he would betray the fleet for money. When Aristophon said he would not, Iphicrates replied: "Then you, being Aristophon, would not betray it—but I, being Iphicrates, would?"

However, for this to work, the opponent must be the one who seems *more* likely to have committed the crime. Otherwise, it would appear ridiculous if one were to say this to a man like Aristeides <note>known as "Aristeides the Just" for his legendary integrity</note> while being his accuser. This argument is intended only to discredit the accuser's reliability.

<summary>Aristotle outlines several logical "commonplaces," including the a fortiori argument (the more and the less), the importance of temporal consistency in agreements, and the technique of turning an accusation back upon the accuser's own character.</summary>
<keywords>Aristotle, Rhetoric, topoi, a fortiori, Iphicrates, logic, persuasion, Athenian law, Philip of Macedon</keywords>

<meta>This page continues Aristotle's catalog of "topoi" or "commonplaces"—the general templates or patterns of reasoning that an orator can use to construct arguments. The previous page concluded a discussion on the argument from time and character.</meta>

### ARISTOTELIS
### RHETORIC. BOOK II. CHAPTER 23.

...rant it? <note>This completes the word from the previous page, likely concluding the argument of the general Iphicrates regarding his honors.</note> Generally, the accuser wishes to appear better than the defendant; one should therefore always refute this claim. This line of argument is altogether absurd whenever a person censures others for things they do themselves, or might do; or when they encourage others to do things they do not do themselves, nor would ever do.

Another topic is derived from **definition**. For example, the argument that the "divine" <note>original: *daimonion*</note> is nothing else than either a god or the work of a god. Yet, whoever thinks a work of a god exists must necessarily believe that gods also exist. <note>This is a famous reference to the defense of Socrates, who argued that if he believed in "divine things," he could not be an atheist.</note> And as Iphicrates argued, that the "most noble" man is the "best" man; for no nobility belonged to Harmodius and Aristogeiton <note>the famous Athenian "Tyrannicides" who helped end the rule of the Peisistratid family</note> until they had performed some noble deed. He further argued that he himself was more closely related to them: "At any rate, my deeds are more akin to those of Harmodius and Aristogeiton than your deeds are."

And as seen in the *Alexander* <note>a lost speech, likely referring to the Trojan prince Paris</note>, that everyone would agree the unrestrained are not satisfied with the enjoyment of just one body. Another example is why Socrates refused to visit Archelaus <note>the King of Macedon</note>; he said it was an "insult" <note>original: *hybris*</note> to be unable to return a favor just as one cannot return an injury. All these speakers, having defined their terms and grasped the essence of the matter <note>the "what it is" of a concept</note>, then reason about the subject at hand.

Another topic is derived from the **various senses of a word**, as discussed in the *Topics* <note>Aristotle’s manual on logic and dialectical argument</note> regarding the word "rightly."

Another is from **division**. For example: "If all men commit injustice for one of three reasons—either for this, or for that, or for this other thing—and if two of these are impossible, while even the accusers themselves do not claim the third."

Another is from **induction** <note>reasoning from specific cases to a general rule</note>. For instance, from the case of the woman of Peparethos <note>an island in the Aegean</note>: it was argued that women everywhere determine the truth regarding the parentage of children. In Athens, when the orator Mantias was disputing with his son, the mother declared the truth; and at Thebes, when Ismenias and Stilbon were disputing, the Dodonian woman <note>likely a priestess or a woman from Dodona</note> proved the son belonged to Ismenias. And because of this...

<summary>Aristotle outlines several logical "commonplaces" for oratory, including arguments based on definition, the various meanings of words, the exhaustion of possibilities (division), and general rules derived from examples (induction).</summary>
<keywords>Aristotle, Rhetoric, definition, induction, Socrates, Iphicrates, logic, legal argument, Harmodius, Aristogeiton</keywords>

--- Page 136 ---
<meta>This page continues the list of commonplaces (topoi) used to build persuasive arguments. It finishes an example regarding family names before moving into arguments based on proportion and the authority of previous judgments.</meta>

### RHETORIC. BOOK II. CHAPTER 23.

...they considered Thessaliskus <note>a diminutive form of a name</note> to be the son of Ismenias. And again, from the *Law of Theodectes*:

> "If people do not hand over their own horses to those who have poorly cared for the horses of others, nor their own ships to those who have capsized the ships of others; then, if this applies equally in all cases, one should not entrust their own safety to those who have poorly guarded the safety of others."

And as Alcidamas <note>a famous Greek teacher of rhetoric</note> argued:

> "All people honor the wise. The Parians, at any rate, have honored Archilochus <note>a famous poet known for biting satire</note>, despite his being a slanderer; the Chians honored Homer, though he was not a fellow citizen; the Mytilenaeans honored Sappho, even though she was a woman; the Lacedaemonians <note>Spartans</note> made Chilon one of their Elders, though they are the least fond of learning; the Italians honored Pythagoras, and the people of Lampsacus buried Anaxagoras, even though he was a foreigner, and they honor him even now. The Athenians prospered by using the laws of Solon, and the Lacedaemonians by using those of Lycurgus; and at Thebes, as soon as the leaders became philosophers, the city began to flourish."

Another topic is derived from a **judgment** <note>original: *krisis*</note> concerning the same, or a similar, or an opposite matter. This is most effective if the judgment is one that everyone always holds; if not that, then at least the majority; or if it is held by the wise—either all of them or most of them—or by good men. It is also effective if the judgment was made by the judges themselves, or by those whom the judges respect, or by those whose judgment cannot be contradicted (such as those in authority), or by those whom it would be unbecoming to contradict (such as the gods, a father, or teachers). An example is what Autocles said against Mixidemides:

> "If it was acceptable for the 'Awful Goddesses' <note>original: *semnai theai* — a title for the Furies/Eumenides</note> to submit to a trial at the Areopagus <note>the high court of Athens</note>, why should it not be so for Mixidemides?"

Or as Sappho argued:

> "Death is an evil; for the gods have judged it so, otherwise they themselves would die."

Or as Aristippus <note>a student of Socrates</note> said to Plato, when he thought the latter had spoken a bit too boastfully:

> "Well, at least our companion," he said—meaning Socrates—"never said anything of the sort."

<summary>This page explores rhetorical techniques based on logical proportion and the weight of authoritative precedents, citing famous poets, philosophers, and historical lawgivers.</summary>
<keywords>Theodectes, Alcidamas, Sappho, authority, logical proportion, Archilochus, Homer, Pythagoras, Solon, Lycurgus, precedent, Areopagus</keywords>

<meta>This page continues Aristotle’s catalog of "topics"—common logical patterns used to construct persuasive arguments. The previous page discussed arguments based on authority and prior judgments (krisis). This page completes that list and introduces arguments based on logical division and consequences.</meta>

# ARISTOTLE

...mentioning Socrates. And Hegesippus <note>a 4th-century BC Athenian statesman and orator</note> consulted the god at Delphi, having previously asked the oracle at Olympia whether the god there held the same opinion as his father; he did this because it would be shameful for the god to say the opposite of what his father had said. 

And concerning Helen, as Isocrates <note>a famous Athenian rhetorician and contemporary of Aristotle</note> wrote, she was a person of merit because Theseus <note>the legendary founder of Athens</note> judged her so. The same is said of Alexander <note>original: "Alexandrou," referring to Paris of Troy</note>, whom the goddesses preferred in their judgment. And regarding Evagoras <note>a King of Salamis in Cyprus</note>, Isocrates argues he was virtuous by noting: 

> "Conon <note>an Athenian general</note>, at any rate, when he met with misfortune, passed by all others and went to Evagoras."

Another **topic** <note>original: *topos*, a "place" where an orator looks for arguments</note> is derived from the **parts** of a subject, just as described in the *Topics* <note>Aristotle's earlier work on dialectical reasoning</note>. For instance, one might ask what kind of "motion" the soul is, for it must be either this specific kind or that kind. An example of this is found in the *Socrates* written by Theodectes <note>a student of Aristotle and a tragic poet</note>: 

> "Against what temple has he committed impiety? Which of the gods recognized by the city has he failed to honor?" 

<note>This argument works by dividing "impiety" into specific parts and showing Socrates is guilty of none of them.</note>

Another topic, since it usually happens that something carries with it both a good and a bad consequence, is to urge or discourage, to accuse or defend, and to praise or blame based on those **consequences**. For example, being envied is a bad consequence of education, while being wise is a good one. Therefore, one might argue one should not be educated (because it is bad to be envied), or conversely, that one should be educated (because it is good to be wise). This topic constitutes the "Art" of Callippus <note>a rhetorician whose textbook is now lost</note>, once it also incorporates the consideration of what is possible and other factors already mentioned.

Another topic is used when one must urge or discourage regarding two opposing choices, applying the previously mentioned method to both. The difference here is that while the previous topic dealt with any chance consequences, this one deals with direct **opposites** <note>original: *tanantia*</note>. 

For example, a priestess would not allow her son to speak in public, saying: 

> "For if," she said, "you say what is just, men will hate you; but if you say what is unjust, the gods will." 

The retort to this is:

> "On the contrary, one should speak in public; for if you say what is just, the gods will love you; and if you say what is unjust, men will love you."

<summary>Aristotle outlines rhetorical strategies based on the authority of past judgments, the logical division of a subject into parts, and the evaluation of conflicting consequences (dilemmas).</summary>

<keywords>Aristotle, Rhetoric, Hegesippus, Isocrates, Evagoras, Theodectes, Callippus, logic of parts, dilemma, consequences, public speaking</keywords>

<meta>This page continues the catalog of logical "topics" (topoi)—the mental "places" or frameworks an orator uses to discover arguments. The previous page ended with arguments based on the "division of parts." This page explores arguments based on contradictory motives, analogies, and identical consequences.</meta>

### RHETORICA, BOOK II, CHAPTER 23

This is the same as the common saying, "to buy the marsh and the salt" <note>original: "to helos priasthai kai tous halas." This proverb refers to a situation where one cannot get something desirable (salt) without also taking something undesirable (the swampy marsh), or more generally, accepting a "package deal" of good and bad.</note>. And what is called **distortion** <term>original: *blaisōsis*, literally "bending" or "twisting"</term> occurs when two opposite things each have both a good and a bad consequence, and these consequences are themselves opposite to each other.

Another **topic** arises from the fact that people do not praise the same things publicly and privately. Instead, in public, they most praise what is just and noble; but in private, they prefer what is to their own advantage. One should try to infer the hidden motive from the stated one; for this is the most effective "place" to find paradoxical arguments.

Another topic is derived from **analogy** <note>reasoning by comparing similar relationships</note>. For example, when Iphicrates <note>a famous Athenian general of the 4th century BC</note> was being forced to perform a **public service** <term>original: *leitourgein*, a "liturgy" or mandatory financial contribution by wealthy citizens to the state</term> for his son—who was legally too young but physically very tall—he said:

> "If you consider tall children to be men, you must also vote that short men are children."

And Theodectes <note>a tragic poet and student of Aristotle</note> wrote in his *Law*:

> "You make citizens of mercenaries like Strabax and Charidemus <note>two well-known mercenary leaders of the era</note> because of their decency; will you then not make exiles of those among the mercenaries who have committed irreparable crimes?"

Another topic is based on the idea that if the **result** is the same, then the causes from which it follows are also the same. For instance, Xenophanes <note>a pre-Socratic philosopher known for criticizing traditional views of the gods</note> used to say:

> "Those who claim the gods were born are just as impious as those who say they die; for in both cases, the result is that at some point, the gods do not exist."

In general, one can always take the consequence of either side as being identical:

> "You are about to judge not simply the man Socrates, but the pursuit itself—whether one should practice **philosophy**."

And another example: to give "earth and water" <note>the traditional Persian demand for symbolic submission</note> is to be a slave; and to participate in the "common peace" <note>likely referring to the Peace of Antalcidas (387 BC) or a similar treaty imposed by a superpower</note> is to do what one is commanded. One must choose whichever interpretation is useful for the current case.

Another topic is based on the fact that the same people do not always choose the same things later as they did before, but rather the opposite. For example...

<meta>This page continues Aristotle's list of *topoi* (commonplaces) in Book II of the Rhetoric. He transitions from arguments based on analogy to those based on the intentions behind actions and the paradoxical nature of rewards and motives.</meta>

# ARISTOTELIS

...this **enthymeme** <term>original: *enthymēma*, a rhetorical argument based on a syllogism where one premise is often implied</term>: "If we fought while in exile so that we might return, shall we go into exile now that we have returned, so that we might not fight?" For in the one case, they chose to stay in their country at the cost of fighting, but in the other, they chose not to fight at the cost of not staying.

Another **topic** <term>original: *topos*, literally a "place" or mental category from which arguments are drawn</term> is to argue that the reason a thing exists or happens is the very thing that would be the case if it *failed* to happen. For example, one might give a gift to someone only so that, by taking it away later, they might cause them grief. This is the source of the saying:

->To many, the deity—not out of kindness—<-
->Grants great fortunes, but only so that<-
->Their disasters may be more conspicuous.<-

And the line from the *Meleager* of Antiphon <note>a 5th-century BC Athenian orator and tragic poet</note>:

->Not so that they might kill the beast, but so that witnesses<-
->Of Meleager’s excellence they might become before all Greece.<-

And the line from the *Ajax* of Theodectes <note>a rhetorician and tragic poet who was a friend of Aristotle</note>, that Diomedes chose Odysseus, not out of honor for him, but so that his companion might be his inferior <gloss>3 C 3</gloss>; for it is possible he did it for this very reason.

Another topic, common to both those in legal disputes and those giving advice, is to consider the factors of **encouragement** and **discouragement** <term>original: *protreponta* and *apotreponta*</term>, and the reasons for which people either act or avoid acting. These are the factors which, if present, mean one should act; for instance, if the action is possible, easy, and beneficial either to oneself or to one's friends, or if it is harmful and damaging to one's enemies, or if the loss is less than the gain.

People are encouraged by these things and discouraged by their opposites. From these same points, they also bring accusations and offer defenses: they defend themselves using the discouraging factors and accuse others using the encouraging ones. This entire topic constitutes the whole "Art" <note>the formal system of rules or textbooks</note> of both Pamphilus and Callippus <note>early rhetoricians whose manuals Aristotle often critiques for being too limited or focused only on emotional appeals</note>.

Another topic is derived from things that seem to happen but are unbelievable: the argument is that they would not have seemed to be the case unless they were actually true, or nearly true. And this is even more persuasive; for people believe either...

<meta>This page continues Aristotle's examination of "topics" or logical patterns used in persuasive speech. The previous page ended with the discussion of motives for action. This page moves into arguments based on the "incredible" nature of truth, the identification of inconsistencies, and the importance of explaining the underlying causes of misunderstood behavior.</meta>

### RHETORIC. BOOK II. CHAPTER 23. 127

...either existing things, or what people assume to be probable. If, therefore, an assertion is unbelievable and lacks probability, it must be true; for it certainly does not appear to be true because it is "likely" or "persuasive" <note>Aristotle is explaining a clever rhetorical move: if a story is too weird to be a believable lie, it might actually be the truth.</note>. For example, Androkles of Pitheus <note>an Athenian politician and opponent of Alcibiades</note> said when attacking the law, as the people shouted in protest at his speech:

> The laws themselves need a law to correct them;
> for even fish need salt.

And yet it is neither probable nor persuasive that creatures raised in brine should need salt; or that olive-pulp <term>original: *stemphyla*, the solid remains of grapes or olives after pressing</term> should need oil; yet it is unbelievable that the very things from which oil comes should themselves need oil. 

Another refutative topic is to look for **inconsistencies** <term>original: *anomologoumena*</term>, to see if any contradiction arises from comparing all times, actions, and words. One can apply this to the opponent:

> And he says indeed that he loves you, yet he swore an oath with the **Thirty** <note>The Thirty Tyrants, a pro-Spartan oligarchy that ruled Athens with great brutality in 404 BC.</note>.

Or one can apply it to oneself:

> And he says indeed that I am **litigious** <term>original: *philodikos*, one who loves lawsuits</term>, yet he cannot show that I have ever been a party to any lawsuit.

Or one can compare oneself against the opponent:

> And this man has never yet lent anything to any of you, whereas I have paid the ransom for many of you.

Another topic is for those who have been misrepresented—whether people or things—and do not seem to be what they are; one must **state the cause** of the paradox. For there is always a reason why a thing appears a certain way. For example, when a woman was seen embracing a young man, because of her affection she seemed to be having an affair with the youth; but once the cause was stated <gloss>that he was her son</gloss>, the slander was dissolved. And as in the *Ajax* of Theodectes <note>a tragic poet and student of Aristotle</note>, Odysseus says to Ajax that though he is actually braver than Ajax, he does not appear so to others. 

Another topic is derived from the **cause**: if the cause exists, the effect exists; and if the cause does not exist, the effect does not exist. For the cause and that of which it is the cause go hand in hand, and without a cause, nothing exists. For example, Leodamas, in his defense when Thrasyboulos accused him of being a "man of the pillar" <note>original: *stēlitēs*, someone whose name was inscribed on a bronze pillar on the Acropolis as a traitor or public enemy</note>, argued that it was impossible; for he claimed he had his name chipped off during the time of the **Thirty** [Tyrants]...

<summary>Aristotle outlines rhetorical strategies for dealing with unbelievable truths, exposing an opponent's contradictions, and clearing one's reputation by explaining the hidden causes behind suspicious-looking behavior.</summary>
<keywords>rhetoric, logic, topoi, contradictions, Thirty Tyrants, legal defense, cause and effect, Leodamas</keywords>

### RHETORIC. BOOK II. CHAPTER 23. 128

...the **Thirty** <note>Referring back to the pro-Spartan oligarchy mentioned on the previous page.</note>. He replied that it was not possible; for the **Thirty** would have trusted him even more if his hatred toward the common people had been officially recorded.

Another topic: if it were possible for a matter to have been handled better in another way than what one advises, or does, or has already done, then one should examine that alternative. For it is clear that if this better way exists, the person did not act <note>The logic being: if they didn't do it the "best" way, they must not have done it at all</note>; for no one willingly and knowingly chooses what is bad. 

However, this argument is false; for it often only becomes clear later how it would have been better to act, whereas before it was unclear.

Another topic: whenever a proposed action seems to contradict past actions, one should examine them together. For example, when the people of Elea asked **Xenophanes** <note>A philosopher known for criticizing traditional views of the gods</note> whether they should sacrifice to **Leucothea** <note>A sea-goddess who was once a mortal woman named Ino</note> and sing funeral dirges for her, or not, he advised them: if they believe she is a goddess, they should not mourn her; but if they believe she is a human, they should not sacrifice to her.

Another topic: to accuse or defend based on mistakes made. For example, in the *Medea* of **Carcinus** <note>A tragic poet; this version of the myth differs from the famous play by Euripides</note>, the accusers argue that she killed her children because they cannot be found; for Medea supposedly made a mistake in "sending the children away." But her defense is that she would not have killed the children, but rather **Jason**; for she would have failed in her purpose by not killing Jason, even if she had committed the other crime of killing the children. This specific line of argument, and this form, constituted the entire "Art" <term>original: *techne*, referring to a manual or system of rhetoric</term> of **Theodorus** <note>An early teacher of rhetoric known for his technical subdivisions of speeches</note>.

Another topic is derived from a person's name. For example, as **Sophocles** says:

->"Clearly you are Iron-woman, and you bear the name to match."<-
<note>original: *Sidero*. In Greek, *sideros* means iron; he is punning on a character's name to suggest she has a "steely" or cruel heart.</note>

This is also common in hymns of praise to the gods. Similarly, **Conon** used to call **Thrasybulus** "bold in counsel" <note>original: *Thrasyboulos*, from *thrasys* (bold) and *boule* (counsel)</note>; and **Herodicus** said of **Thrasymachus**, "you are always bold in battle" <note>original: *Thrasymachos*, from *thrasys* (bold) and *mache* (battle)</note>; and to **Polus**, "you are always a colt" <note>original: *Polos* means a young horse or colt</note>; and of **Draco** the lawmaker, he said that his laws were not those of a man, but of a dragon <note>original: *Drakon* means serpent or dragon; the pun suggests his laws were inhumanly harsh</note>.

<summary>Aristotle continues cataloging rhetorical "topics," including the use of logical dilemmas (as seen in Xenophanes' advice) and the practice of drawing character traits from the etymology of a person's name.</summary>

<keywords>Aristotle, Rhetoric, Xenophanes, Medea, Carcinus, etymology, puns, Draco, Thrasymachus, logic</keywords>

<meta>This page continues Aristotle's examination of "topics" for building arguments, specifically concluding the section on names as evidence, and transitions into Chapter 24, which analyzes "apparent" or sham arguments—logical fallacies that only seem persuasive due to their presentation.</meta>

### RHETORIC. BOOK II. CHAPTER 24. 129

...for they are difficult. This is also seen in **Euripides**’ <note>A famous Athenian playwright</note> *Hecuba*, where she says to **Aphrodite**:
->And your name rightly begins the word for folly, O goddess<- <note>original: "aphrosyne" (folly) and "Aphrodite". Hecuba is mocking the goddess's name as being the root of "mindlessness" or "folly."</note>
and as **Chaeremon** <note>A 4th-century BC tragic poet</note> wrote:
->Pentheus, named after the misfortune that is to come.<- <note>original: "Pentheus" and "penthos" (grief or sorrow). In Greek tragedy, Pentheus’s name was often seen as a dark omen of his fate.</note>

Among all **enthymemes** <note>A "rhetorical syllogism" or argument based on probabilities rather than absolute certainties</note>, those that are refutative are more highly regarded than those that are demonstrative. This is because a refutative argument brings opposites together in a brief space, and when things are placed side by side, they become clearer to the listener. Of all arguments—both refutative and demonstrative—the ones that cause the most excitement in an audience are those where the listeners can foresee the conclusion from the very start (provided they are not too obvious). The audience feels a certain joy in their own cleverness for anticipating the point. This also applies to arguments that the listeners can follow closely enough to understand the moment they are completed.

### CHAPTER XXIV.

Since it is possible for a sequence of words to be a real **syllogism** <note>A formal logical deduction</note> while another is not real but only *appears* to be one, it must also be the case that one thing is a true **enthymeme** and another is merely an apparent enthymeme; for the enthymeme is a form of syllogism.

The "topics" of these apparent enthymemes are as follows. First is the one based on the style of speech. One part of this—just as in **dialectic** <note>The art of logical discussion</note>—is to state a final conclusion as if it had been logically proven, even though no actual deduction took place: "Therefore, this is not the case!" or "Therefore, that must be so!" Another trick is to use compact and antithetical language, which gives the *appearance* of an enthymeme; for this specific style of phrasing is the natural home of the enthymeme. Such a fallacy seems to arise from the outward form of the language used. For the purpose of making a speech sound logically "proven" through its style alone, it is useful to list the summaries of many separate arguments: "That he saved some, but took vengeance on others, and the Greeks..."

<meta>This page continues Aristotle's discussion from Chapter 24 regarding sham enthymemes—arguments that appear logically sound but are actually based on fallacies. It specifically addresses fallacies of ambiguity and the manipulation of combined or divided terms.</meta>

# ARISTOTELIS

### RHETORIC. BOOK II. CHAPTER 24. 130

...freed them. For each of these points was demonstrated from other premises; but when they are put together, it appears that something new arises from them as well.

One type of sham argument is based on **homonymy** <term>A fallacy where the same word is used with different meanings</term>, such as saying that a "mouse" is noble, since from it comes the most honored of all rites; for the "mysteries" are the most honored of all rites. <note>original: "mys" (mouse) and "mysteria" (mysteries). Aristotle is pointing out a pun where the word for mouse sounds like the root of the word for religious mysteries.</note> Or if someone, while praising a "dog," includes the Dog-star in heaven in the eulogy; or Pan, because **Pindar** <note>A famous Greek lyric poet</note> said:

> O blessed one, whom the Olympians call the all-various dog 
> of the Great Goddess; <note>Pindar refers to the god Pan as the "dog" or attendant of the goddess Cybele.</note>

or the argument that because it is most dishonorable to be "no dog," it is therefore clearly honorable to be a "dog." There is also the claim that **Hermes** is the most "sociable" of the gods, because he alone is called "Common Hermes." <note>original: "koinos Hermes," a proverbial expression used when someone finds a lucky treasure and is expected to share it.</note> Another example is saying that "speech" is the most excellent thing, because good men are worthy not of money, but of *speech* <note>original: "logou axios" can mean either "worthy of speech/mention" or "of great value."</note>; for the phrase "worthy of speech" is not used in only one sense.

Another type of fallacy consists in combining what is divided, or dividing what is combined. Since what is not the same often *appears* to be the same, one should use whichever version is more useful for the argument. This is the method of **Euthydemus** <note>A Sophist philosopher known for using such logical puzzles to trap opponents</note>; for instance, the claim of "knowing that there is a **trireme** <note>An ancient Greek warship with three banks of oars</note> in the **Piraeus** <note>The main port of Athens</note>," because one knows each part of that statement separately. Similarly, one might argue that a person who knows the individual letters knows the whole verse, since the verse is the same thing as its letters. 

Another example: since a double dose of medicine is unhealthy, one might argue that the single dose cannot be healthy either; for it would be absurd if two good things could make one bad thing. Used this way, the argument is refutative; but used another way, it is demonstrative: "two evils do not make one good thing." But this whole **topic** <term>A standard method or "place" for finding arguments</term> is a **paralogism** <term>A piece of false or fallacious reasoning</term>. Again, there is the remark of **Polycrates** <note>A Greek rhetorician</note> regarding **Thrasybulus** <note>The Athenian general who overthrew the Thirty Tyrants in 403 BC</note>, that "he overthrew thirty tyrants"; for he combines...

<summary>Aristotle continues cataloging logical fallacies, specifically focusing on puns (homonymy) and the "fallacy of composition," where parts and wholes are intentionally confused to create a misleading argument.</summary>

<keywords>Aristotle, Rhetoric, homonymy, Pindar, Hermes, Euthydemus, Thrasybulus, paralogism, logical fallacies, puns, composition and division</keywords>

--- Page 144 ---
<meta>This page continues Aristotle's examination of sham enthymemes—logical fallacies that appear to be valid arguments but rely on deceptive reasoning. The previous page discussed puns and the misuse of words with multiple meanings; this page moves into fallacies involving the combination or division of ideas, emotional exaggeration, and the misuse of "signs" or accidental circumstances.</meta>

# ARISTOTELIS

### RHETORIC. BOOK II. CHAPTER 24. 131

...for they are. Or the case in the *Orestes* of **Theodectes** <note>A Greek rhetorician and tragic poet who was a student of Isocrates and a friend of Aristotle.</note>; for it arises from **division**:
> It is just, if someone kills a husband,
> that she should die,
> And is it just for a son to avenge his father?
These things have indeed been done; but when **combined** <term>A fallacy where two premises are true separately, but lead to a false or immoral conclusion when joined—here, that a son is the "just" person to kill his own mother.</term>, it is perhaps no longer just. This could also be a fallacy of **omission**; for the "by whom" is removed. <note>Aristotle is pointing out that the argument leaves out the specific person performing the act; while the punishment may be just, it is not necessarily "just" for a son to be the executioner of his mother.</note>

Another topic is to establish or refute a point by **exaggeration** <term>Greek: "deinōsis," the rhetorical technique of making a deed seem especially heinous or grand to bypass logical proof.</term>. This occurs when, without having shown that the person actually committed the act, the speaker amplifies the matter; for this makes it appear as if they have done it—either when the accused amplifies the deed in their defense, or when the accuser speaks with anger. This is not a true **enthymeme** <term>A rhetorical syllogism or argument based on probabilities rather than absolute certainty.</term>; for the listener is misled into thinking the person did or did not do the act, even though it hasn't been proven.

Another fallacy is the argument from a **sign** <term>An indicator that suggests a conclusion, but does not logically guarantee it.</term>; for this too is non-syllogistic. For example, if someone were to say, "Lovers are beneficial to cities; for the love of **Harmodius and Aristogeiton** <note>A famous pair of lovers who became symbols of Athenian freedom after they assassinated the tyrant Hipparchus in 514 BCE.</note> overthrew the tyrant Hipparchus." Or if someone said, "Dionysius is a thief, for he is a wicked man." This is non-syllogistic; for while every thief is wicked, not every wicked man is a thief.

Another is through the **accidental**; such as what **Polycrates** <note>A sophist known for writing "encomiums" (speeches of praise) for unlikely subjects like salt or, in this case, mice.</note> says regarding mice, that they "rendered aid" by gnawing through the bowstrings. Or if someone should say that being invited to dinner is the highest honor; for it was because he was not invited that **Achilles** <note>The hero of the Iliad. Aristotle refers to a lost play or tradition where Achilles' anger began at Tenedos before reaching Troy.</note> was enraged at the Greeks at Tenedos; but he was enraged because he felt dishonored; it simply happened to occur over the matter of not being invited.

Another is from the **consequent** <term>The fallacy of assuming that if "B" follows "A," then "B" must have been caused by "A" or that they are identical.</term>; as in the case of **Alexander** <note>Also known as Paris, the prince of Troy who abducted Helen.</note>, that he is "great-souled"; for, looking down on the company of the many, he dwelt on Mount Ida by himself; for because great-souled men are like this, [it is assumed he is too...]

->I 2<-

<summary>Aristotle analyzes several types of sham arguments, including those that manipulate moral logic through division, those that use emotional exaggeration (deinōsis) to skip over evidence, and those that mistake accidental circumstances for primary causes.</summary>
<keywords>Theodectes, Orestes, deinōsis, sign, fallacy of the accidental, Polycrates, Achilles, Alexander of Troy, logical fallacies, enthymemes</keywords>

--- Page 145 ---
<meta>This page continues Aristotle's catalog of "sham enthymemes"—logical fallacies used in oratory to make a weak argument appear strong. The previous page concluded with the fallacy of "omission," specifically regarding who performs an act. This page explores further errors, including the "fallacy of the sign," the confusion of correlation with causation, and the misuse of probability.</meta>

# ARISTOTELIS

...one might seem high-minded <note>original: "megalopsychos"—the "great-souled" man, a character archetype in Greek ethics who is dignified and self-assured.</note>. And since he dresses elegantly and wanders about at night, he is an adulterer; for adulterers are like that. It is similar to say that because beggars sing and dance in temples, or because exiles are free to live wherever they wish, they are happy; because these behaviors belong to those who are thought to be happy, then those to whom these circumstances apply might also seem happy. *The difference lies in the "how" [the circumstances];* therefore this also falls under the fallacy of **omission**.

*Another fallacy arises from representing what is not a cause as a cause;* for instance, because something happened at the same time as, or after, another event. People take "after this" to mean "because of this" <term>Known in logic as "post hoc ergo propter hoc" (after this, therefore because of this).</term>, and this is especially common among those in politics. For example, **Demades** <note>An Athenian orator and rival of Demosthenes who favored cooperation with Macedonia.</note> claimed that the policies of **Demosthenes** <note>The famous Athenian orator who led the resistance against Philip II of Macedon.</note> were the cause of all the city's evils, simply because the war happened after he took office.

*Another is from the omission of "when" and "how";* for example, the argument that **Alexander** <note>Also known as Paris, the prince of Troy.</note> was right to take **Helen**, because her father had given her the choice of a husband. But this choice was perhaps not intended to last forever, but only for the first time; for the father's authority over her lasted only until then. 

*Or if someone were to say that hitting free men is an "outrageous assault" [hybris];* <term>In Athenian law, "hybris" was a specific criminal charge involving intentional insult and physical violation of a citizen's dignity.</term> for it is not so in every case, but only when the person strikes the first blow unprovoked.

*Furthermore, just as in eristic <term>A style of debate focused on winning the argument rather than finding the truth.</term> disputes, a sham syllogism <term>A logical argument where a conclusion is inferred from two premises.</term> arises from the confusion between what is absolute and what is not absolute, but qualified;* for example, in **dialectic** <note>The art of investigating the truth of opinions through logical discussion.</note>, one might argue that "non-being *is*," because "non-being *is* non-being"; or that the unknown is "knowable," because we can "know" that the unknown is unknown. In the same way, in **rhetoric**, there is a sham **enthymeme** based on what is not absolutely probable, but only probable in a specific way. Yet even this is not universally true, as **Agathon** <note>An Athenian tragic poet and friend of Plato and Socrates.</note> says:

> One might say this very thing is probable:
> That many improbable things happen to mortals.

For that which is contrary to probability does indeed happen; so that which is contrary to probability is, in a sense, "probable." 

<summary>Aristotle explains several rhetorical fallacies, including the confusion of correlation with causation (illustrated by the rivalry between Demades and Demosthenes) and the "probable improbable" paradox. He emphasizes that valid arguments must account for specific circumstances like time, manner, and intent.</summary>

<keywords>Aristotle, Rhetoric, logic, fallacies, Demosthenes, Demades, Agathon, probability, causation, Helen of Troy, ethics</keywords>

<meta>This page continues Aristotle's discussion on the "Art of Corax" and the misuse of probability, transitioning from the catalog of "sham enthymemes" into Chapter 25, which focuses on "refutation" (the methods of dismantling an opponent's argument).</meta>

# RHETORIC. BOOK II. CHAPTER XXV.

...contrary to probability. If this is so, then the "improbable" will become "probable." But this is not so in an absolute sense; rather, just as in **eristic** <term>A style of argument focused on winning a debate through clever verbal tricks rather than seeking the truth.</term> reasoning, the failure to specify "in a certain respect," "in relation to something," or "in a certain way" creates a fallacy <note>original: "sykophantian"—while usually meaning "slander," in this logical context Aristotle uses it to mean a deceptive or malicious misrepresentation of an argument.</note>. Here, the fallacy arises because a thing is probable not absolutely, but only in a specific sense.

The "Art" of **Corax** <note>One of the earliest Greek rhetoricians from Sicily (5th century BCE), traditionally credited with developing the first systematic approach to legal oratory.</note> is composed of this very topic. For if a man is not liable to a charge—for instance, if a weak man is accused of assault—he can defend himself by saying it is not probable <note>original: "eikos"—likelihood or probability, the standard for legal proof when direct evidence was missing.</note> that he did it. But even if he is liable—for instance, if he is a strong man—he can still defend himself by saying it is not probable, because it was so likely to *seem* probable that he would be the suspect. The same applies to other cases; a man must either be liable to a charge or not. Both circumstances appear "probable," but one is truly probable, while the other is so only in a qualified sense and not absolutely, as has been said. 

This is also what it means to "make the weaker argument the stronger" <note>This was a famous and controversial slogan associated with the Sophists, often used by critics to claim they taught people how to make lies sound like the truth.</note>. This is why people were rightly annoyed by the profession of **Protagoras** <note>A prominent Sophist who famously argued that "man is the measure of all things."</note>; for it is a deception and not the truth, but a mere appearance of probability, and it belongs to no other art than rhetoric and eristic. 

We have now discussed **enthymemes** <term>A rhetorical syllogism; an argument based on shared values or probabilities rather than certainties.</term>, both those that are genuine and those that are merely apparent.

->CHAPTER XXV.<-

Following what has been said, we must now speak of **refutations** <note>original: "lyseōn"—literally "loosening" or "undoing" an opponent's argument.</note>. An argument may be refuted either by a counter-syllogism or by bringing an objection. 

It is clear that one can create a counter-syllogism using the same "topics" already discussed; for syllogisms are formed from common opinions, and many such opinions are contrary to one another. **Objections**, however, are brought forward in four ways, just as in the *Topics* <note>Aristotle's earlier work on dialectical reasoning.</note>: either from the subject itself, from a similar case, from an opposite case, or from previous judgments. 

By "from the subject itself," I mean, for example, if an enthymeme were to state that love is a noble thing, the objection would be—

<summary>Aristotle concludes his analysis of "sham enthymemes" by criticizing the deceptive use of probability by Corax and Protagoras, then introduces the methods of refuting arguments through counter-logic and objections.</summary>

<keywords>Aristotle, Rhetoric, Corax, Protagoras, probability, eikos, eristic, enthymeme, refutation, syllogism, Sophists, logical fallacies</keywords>

--- Page 147 ---
<meta>This page continues the discussion from Book II, Chapter 25 of Aristotle's Rhetoric, moving from the definition of "probability" to specific techniques for raising an objection (enstasis) and the four logical sources of rhetorical arguments.</meta>

# ARISTOTELIS

...in two ways: either by speaking universally, stating that "every deficiency is an evil," or specifically, by noting that "Caunian love" <note>A proverbial expression for shameful or unnatural passion, named after the mythical Caunus whose sister Byblis fell in love with him.</note> would not be a recognized term if there were not also evil forms of love. 

An **objection** <note>original: "enstasis"—literally a "standing in the way," used here as a counter-instance that invalidates a general claim.</note> is also brought from the **contrary**. For example, if the **enthymeme** <term>A rhetorical argument that functions like a logical syllogism but is based on probabilities or signs rather than absolute certainties.</term> was that "a good man treats all his friends well," one might object: "but neither does the wicked man treat all his friends badly." 

Arguments are also refuted by looking at **similar cases**. If the argument was that "those who have been treated badly always hate," the objection is: "but neither do those who have been treated well always love." 

Then there are judgments issued by **famous men**. For instance, if someone used an argument that "one ought to have sympathy for those who are drunk, for they sin in ignorance," an objection could be: "Then **Pittacus** <note>One of the Seven Sages of Greece (c. 640–568 BCE) and ruler of Mytilene. He famously decreed that crimes committed while intoxicated should carry a double penalty.</note> is not to be praised; otherwise, he would not have legislated greater penalties for cases where a man sins while drunk."

Since rhetorical arguments are said to be drawn from four sources—the **probable**, the **example**, the **infallible sign**, and the **sign**—it follows that:
*   Arguments gathered from what usually happens, or what seems to happen, are drawn from **probabilities** <note>original: "eikos"—likelihoods that appeal to common sense.</note>.
*   Arguments drawn through induction based on similarity—whether from one or many instances—where one takes a universal principle and then reasons about specific cases, are drawn from **example** <note>original: "paradeigma".</note>.
*   Arguments drawn through what is necessary and actual are drawn from **infallible signs** <note>original: "tekmerion"—a piece of evidence that provides a "necessary" conclusion.</note>.
*   Arguments drawn through a universal or a particular fact, whether it is actually the case or not, are drawn from **signs** <note>original: "semeion"—a general indicator that is not necessarily conclusive.</note>.

The "probable" is not what happens always, but only what happens for the most part. It is clear, therefore, that such arguments can always be refuted by bringing a counter-objection. However, this refutation is often a matter of appearance rather than being always true. The person raising the objection does not necessarily prove that the thing is "not probable," but rather shows that it is "not necessary."

For this reason, the defendant always holds an advantage over the accuser through this logical trick. Since the accuser proves his case through probabilities, and it is not the same thing to refute...

<summary>Aristotle explains how to dismantle an opponent's argument using counter-examples (objections) and categorizes the four types of rhetorical evidence: probability, example, necessary signs, and general signs.</summary>
<keywords>refutation, objection, enthymeme, Pittacus, probability, example, signs, legal defense, logic</keywords>

--- Page 148 ---
<meta>This page continues the discussion of refuting arguments from Book II, Chapter 25 of Aristotle's Rhetoric. It focuses on how to challenge arguments based on probability, signs, and examples, transitioning into Chapter 26 regarding amplification.</meta>

# RHETORIC. BOOK II. CHAPTER XXVI.

...is not probable, or because it is not necessary. A "probable" argument—which refers to what happens for the most part—always admits an **objection** <note>original: "enstasis"—a counter-instance used to invalidate a general claim.</note>. Otherwise, it would not be merely probable, but always and necessarily true. Yet a judge often mistakenly thinks that if an argument is refuted in this way, it is either not probable or not his place to decide, thus falling into a logical fallacy as we mentioned before.

For a judge should not decide based only on necessary truths, but also on probabilities. This is precisely what it means to "judge according to one's best discretion." <note>original: "gnōmē tē aristē"—referring to the standard oath taken by Athenian jurors to decide fairly when no specific law applied.</note> Therefore, it is not enough to refute a point by simply showing it is not a "necessary" truth; one must refute it by showing it is not even "probable." This is achieved if the objection shows that the contrary happens more frequently.

Such a counter-instance can occur in two ways: regarding the time or the circumstances involved. It is most persuasive when both are present; for if something happens more often in a certain way, that is what is "more probable."

**Signs** <term>A "sign" (sēmeion) is a piece of evidence used to infer something else, like a fever signifying illness.</term> and the **enthymemes** <term>A rhetorical argument that functions like a logical syllogism but is based on probabilities or signs rather than absolute certainties.</term> derived from them can also be refuted, even if the facts themselves are true, as we explained in our first discussions. It is clear from our work on logic, the *Prior Analytics*, that every "sign" lacks the formal validity of a complete syllogism.

The same method of refutation applies to arguments based on **examples** as to those based on probabilities. If we have a single counter-example, the argument is refuted by showing it is not "necessary," or by showing that in most cases, or more frequently, things happen differently. If, however, the opponent’s examples occur more frequently and more often, one must fight back by arguing either that the current situation is not similar to those examples, or that it applies in a different way, or that it has some distinct difference.

However, **infallible signs** <term>A "tekmērion" is a "necessary sign"—evidence that proves a point beyond doubt, such as "having given birth" being an infallible sign of "having been pregnant."</term> and the arguments based on them cannot be refuted on the grounds of logical invalidity. This is also clear to us from the *Prior Analytics*. The only remaining way to refute them is to show that the alleged fact simply does not exist. If it is clear both that the fact exists and that it is an infallible sign, then the argument becomes irrefutable; for at that point, everything has been made clear through a formal demonstration.

### CHAPTER XXVI.

**Amplification** and **depreciation** <note>These are rhetorical techniques used to make a point seem more significant (amplification) or less significant (depreciation) than it might otherwise appear.</note> are not rhetorical argumen—

<meta>This page concludes Book II of Aristotle's Rhetoric. It clarifies the distinction between "topics" and "enthymemes," specifically addressing amplification and refutation, before transitioning to Book III which focuses on style (lexis) and arrangement (taxis).</meta>

->ARISTOTLE<-

...an **element**; for I use the terms "element" and "topic" interchangeably. For an **element** or a **topic** <term>A "topic" (topos, literally 'place') is a general category or a 'pigeonhole' of logic that a speaker can visit to find specific arguments for any subject.</term> is a general category under which many **enthymemes** <note>original: "enthymēmata"—the rhetorical version of a logical deduction, often leaving one premise unstated for the audience to fill in.</note> fall. 

Now, **amplifying** and **minimizing** <note>original: "auxein kai meioun"—the rhetorical techniques of making a deed seem more significant/heroic or less significant/guilty.</note> are not themselves "topics" of an enthymeme, but are actually enthymemes used to show that something is great or small—just as others are used to show that something is good or bad, just or unjust, or any other quality. These qualities are the very subjects that **syllogisms** <term>A formal logical argument where a conclusion is reached from two given or assumed premises.</term> and enthymemes deal with; so if none of those qualities (like "good" or "just") is considered a "topic" for an enthymeme, then neither is amplification or minimization.

Furthermore, **refutative** enthymemes do not constitute a different class from **demonstrative** ones. It is clear that one refutes an argument either by proving the opposite or by raising an **objection** <note>original: "enstasis"—providing a counter-instance to show that the opponent's general rule does not always apply.</note>. Both sides prove the contrary; for example, if one speaker shows that a thing has happened, the other shows that it has not; if one shows it has not happened, the other shows it has. 

Therefore, this is not a fundamental difference in the type of argument; both speakers use the same means, for they both bring forward enthymemes to prove that something is or is not the case. An **objection**, however, is not an enthymeme at all. Rather, as we described in the *Topics* <note>Aristotle refers here to his earlier work on dialectical reasoning.</note>, it consists in stating a certain opinion from which it will be clear that the opponent has either failed to reason correctly or has assumed something false. 

Regarding **examples**, **maxims**, **enthymemes**, and the whole subject of **thought** <term>original: "dianoia"—this refers to the intellectual content, the "what" of the speech, as opposed to the "how" (style).</term>—specifically, where we may find these tools and how we may refute them—let this suffice for our discussion. What remains is to discuss **style** and **arrangement**.

<summary>Aristotle concludes his study of the internal logic of a speech (Book II), arguing that amplification and refutation are types of enthymemes rather than separate logical 'topics.' He then prepares to move into Book III, which covers delivery, style, and structure.</summary>
<keywords>Aristotle, Rhetoric, topic, element, enthymeme, amplification, minimization, refutation, objection, style, arrangement</keywords>
