<meta>This page marks the beginning of Book III of the Rhetoric. Having concluded the discussion on "pisteis" (means of persuasion) and "topoi" (topics) in Book II, Aristotle now moves into "lexis" (style) and "taxis" (arrangement), while introducing the concept of "hypokrisis" (delivery).</meta>

->RHETORIC. BOOK III. CHAPTER I.<-

# BOOK III.

## CHAPTER I.

Since there are three things that must be investigated concerning a **speech** <note>original: "logon"—in Greek, this term encompasses the spoken word, the underlying reason, and the formal argument itself.</note>: first, from what sources the **means of persuasion** <note>original: "pisteis"—the proofs or modes of appeal used to convince an audience.</note> will come; second, regarding the **style** <note>original: "lexin"—the choice of words, diction, and the way language is used.</note>; and third, how one must arrange the parts of the speech.

We have already spoken about the means of persuasion and their sources—showing that they are three in number, what these three are, and why there are only these three. For all people are persuaded either by being personally moved in their emotions, or by perceiving the speakers to be a certain kind of character, or by the matter being demonstrated through logic. We have also discussed the sources from which **enthymemes** <term>A rhetorical syllogism or argument that often leaves one premise unstated, allowing the audience to fill in the blanks.</term> must be provided; for some of these are "species" of enthymemes while others are "**topics**" <note>original: "topoi"—literally 'places', these are the mental categories or general headings where a speaker looks for arguments.</note>.

Our next task is to speak about **style**. For it is not enough to simply possess the knowledge of *what* one should say; it is also necessary to say these things *as* one should. This contributes greatly to making the speech appear to be of a certain quality. 

Thus, the first subject was investigated according to its natural order—beginning with the source of persuasiveness in the facts themselves. Second is the arrangement of these facts through style. The third of these, which possesses the greatest power but has not yet been systematically attempted, is the matter of **delivery** <note>original: "hypokrisin"—this word also means 'acting' or 'theatrical performance'.</note>. Indeed, this art entered into tragic performance and **rhapsody** <term>The professional recitation of epic poems, like those of Homer, often performed at festivals.</term> quite late; for in the beginning, the poets themselves acted out their own tragedies. 

It is clear, then, that such a thing exists for rhetoric just as it does for poetry; some others have already treated this subject, including **Glaucon of Teos** <note>An early theorist or actor who specialized in the study of vocal delivery and performance.</note>. This art of delivery is a matter of the voice: how one should [use] it...

<summary>Aristotle introduces the three pillars of rhetoric—content, style, and arrangement—before focusing on the often-overlooked importance of delivery (acting) in public speaking.</summary>
<keywords>style (lexis), delivery (hypokrisis), persuasion (pisteis), arrangement (taxis), Glaucon of Teos, rhapsody</keywords>

<meta>This page continues Aristotle's introduction to Book III of the Rhetoric. He is transitioning from the "what" of a speech (logic and proofs) to the "how" (delivery and style). He picks up mid-sentence discussing the elements of vocal delivery.</meta>

<page-num>138</page-num>

<header>ARISTOTELIS</header>

...how to use [delivery] in relation to each **emotion** <note>original: "pathos"—the emotional state the speaker intends to evoke in the audience.</note>; for example, when the voice should be loud, when soft, and when intermediate; and how the **tones** <note>original: "tonois"—the pitch or inflection of the voice.</note> should be used—namely, high, low, and intermediate—and what **rhythms** are suited to each case. For there are three things that those who study delivery investigate: **volume** <note>original: "megethos"—the physical magnitude or power of the voice.</note>, **pitch** <note>original: "harmonia"—the melodic quality or "harmony" of the speech.</note>, and **rhythm**. 

Those who master these are almost always the ones who carry off the prizes in the contests. And just as in the theater, the **actors** <note>original: "hypokritai"—this term refers to stage actors, but Aristotle uses it here to describe the delivery of a public speaker.</note> now have more power than the poets, so it is in political contests, because of the corruption of our forms of government. 

A systematic art <term>A "techne" or craft; an organized body of knowledge with rules that can be taught.</term> concerning these matters has not yet been composed, since even the study of **style** <note>original: "lexis"—diction or the choice of words.</note> only advanced late. Indeed, delivery is thought to be a vulgar thing, if rightly understood. But since the entire business of Rhetoric is directed toward **opinion** <note>original: "doxan"—appearance or what the public believes, rather than absolute truth.</note>, we must pay attention to it—not because it is right to do so, but because it is a necessity. 

Strictly speaking, justice would require seeking nothing more in a speech than to avoid causing pain or providing pleasure; for it is only right to fight the case using the facts themselves, so that everything else beyond proving the point is superfluous. Nevertheless, as has been said, delivery has great power because of the corruption of the listener. 

Style, then, has a certain small necessity in every kind of teaching; for it makes some difference for clarification whether one speaks in this way or that way—though not, however, that much of a difference. Rather, all these things are for the sake of appearances and are directed at the listener; which is why no one teaches **geometry** in this manner <note>Aristotle implies that in "pure" sciences like geometry, the facts speak for themselves and don't require the "theatrical" delivery needed in politics.</note>. 

When that art of delivery is fully developed, it will achieve the same effect as **acting**. Some have attempted to say a few things about it already—for instance, **Thrasymachus** <note>A famous Sophist and teacher of rhetoric, also known as a character in Plato's Republic.</note> in his *Appeals to Pity*. Being naturally gifted at delivery is a matter of nature and is less a matter of technical rule; however, concerning **style**, it is a matter of art. Therefore, prizes are again awarded to those who are capable in this regard, just as they are to orators who excel in delivery; for written speeches...

--- Page 152 ---
<meta>This page continues Aristotle's discussion on the historical development of rhetorical style. He is explaining why prose style originally imitated poetry and why that approach is now outdated, leading into his formal definition of "good" style in Chapter 2.</meta>

<page-num>139</page-num>

<header>RHETORIC. BOOK III. CHAPTER II.</header>

...speeches have more power because of their **style** <note>original: "lexis"—the way things are said, including word choice and arrangement.</note> than because of their **thought** <note>original: "dianoian"—the intellectual content or the actual arguments being made.</note>. The poets, as is natural, were the first to set this movement in motion; for words are **imitations** <note>original: "mimemata"—representations of reality.</note>, and the voice is the most imitative of all our parts. This is why the various arts were established, such as **rhapsody** <note>The art of performing epic poetry, like Homer, from memory.</note>, **acting** <note>original: "hypokritike"—stagecraft and delivery.</note>, and others. 

Because the poets seemed to have gained their fame through their style even though what they said was simple-minded, the first **prose style** was poetic in nature, such as that of **Gorgias** <note>A famous Sicilian teacher of rhetoric known for his extremely flowery, rhythmic, and "poetic" speeches.</note>. Even now, many uneducated people think that such individuals speak most beautifully. This, however, is not the case; the style of prose is different from that of poetry. This is shown by what is happening today: even those who write **tragedies** no longer use the old style in the same way. Instead, just as they changed from **tetrameters** <note>An early poetic meter associated with dancing and a more "musical" feel.</note> to **iambic verse** <note>original: "iambeion"—the rhythm that Aristotle elsewhere notes is the most similar to the natural cadence of everyday conversation.</note> because this meter is the most like spoken language of all the meters, they have also abandoned all those words that are outside the range of normal conversation. 

The poets who wrote in the early days used these ornaments to decorate their work, and those who write **hexameters** <note>The formal meter of epic poetry like the *Iliad*.</note> still do, but the playwrights have abandoned them. Therefore, it is ridiculous to imitate those who themselves no longer use that manner of speaking. Thus, it is clear that we do not need to be precisely detailed about everything regarding style, but only about the kind of style we are discussing—prose style. The other kind of style has been discussed in the *Poetics*.

### CHAPTER II.

Let the previous points be taken as studied; and let the **excellence** <term>"Arete"—the specific virtue or functional perfection of a thing. For a knife, it is sharpness; for style, it is clarity.</term> of style be defined as being **clear** <note>original: "saphe"—the quality of being easy to understand.</note>. A sign of this is that if a speech does not make its meaning plain, it will not perform its own proper function. Style should be neither "low" <note>too plain or common</note> nor above its required dignity, but **appropriate** <note>original: "prepon"—fitting the subject matter and the audience.</note>. Poetic style is perhaps not "low," but it is not appropriate for prose. Of names and verbs, those that are **standard terms** <note>original: "kyria"—the "master" or everyday words for things, as opposed to metaphors or rare dialects.</note> make the style clear...

<summary>Aristotle argues that while prose style began by imitating poetry (like the orator Gorgias), it has evolved toward natural speech. He defines the primary "virtue" of style as clarity, achieved through appropriate and standard language.</summary>
<keywords>style (lexis), clarity (sapheia), appropriateness (prepon), poetry vs prose, Gorgias, iambic meter, rhetorical excellence (arete)</keywords>

--- Page 153 ---
<meta>This page continues the discussion from page 139 regarding the transition from poetic to prose style. Aristotle is now defining the specific elements that make prose effective: the balance between making language "foreign" enough to be interesting, but "natural" enough to be persuasive.</meta>

<page-num>140</page-num>

<header>ARISTOTLE</header>

...not humble, but appropriately adorned with the other types of words described in the **Poetics** <note>original: "peri poietikes" — Aristotle’s famous treatise on the art of poetry and drama.</note>. Varying the language from the ordinary makes it appear more **dignified**; for people have the same feeling toward language as they do toward strangers compared to their fellow citizens. For this reason, one should give the language a **foreign air** <note>original: "xenon" — meaning strange, unusual, or exotic.</note>; for people are admirers of things that are far away, and what is wonderful is pleasant.

In verse, this effect is achieved often and is appropriate there; for the subjects and the characters the speech is about are further removed from everyday life. In **plain prose** <note>original: "psilois logois" — literally "bare words," referring to speech without the accompaniment of music or the rigid structure of meter.</note>, however, these ornaments should be used much more sparingly, for the subject matter is more limited. For even in prose, if a slave were to use fine language, or a very young man, it would be quite unbecoming—and the same is true if one spoke grandly about very trivial matters. Instead, **propriety** <note>original: "prepon" — the foundational rhetorical concept of what is "fitting" or "appropriate" to the context, speaker, and audience.</note> consists in contracting or expanding the style to suit the occasion.

Therefore, one must hide the art and not seem to speak in an **artificial** way, but naturally. The latter is persuasive, while the former is the opposite; for people take offense at a speaker who seems to be plotting against them, just as they do against **blended wines** <note>A metaphor for being "cheated" or deceived by something that is not pure or honest.</note>. Consider how the voice of **Theodorus** <note>A celebrated tragic actor of the 4th century BCE known for his naturalistic and realistic delivery.</note> sounded compared to the voices of other actors; his seemed to be the speaker's own voice, while theirs sounded like they belonged to someone else. The art is successfully hidden if one composes by choosing words from **everyday language** and combining them. This is what **Euripides** <note>A famous Athenian playwright who was noted for moving tragedy away from archaic, grand language toward the more natural dialogue of the common person.</note> does, and he was the first to show the way.

Since speech is composed of **nouns** and **verbs**, and nouns have as many categories as were examined in the *Poetics*, we should use **strange words** <note>original: "glottais" — literally "tongues," referring to rare, archaic, or dialect-specific terms that sound exotic to the listener.</note>, compound words, and coined words only rarely and in few places. We will state where they should be used later; the reason "why" they should be avoided has already been mentioned: because they deviate too far from what is appropriate toward the excessive. For the style of **plain prose**, only the **standard word** <note>original: "kyrion" — the regular, literal, or "proper" name for a thing.</note>, the **specific term**, and **metaphor** are useful. The proof of this is that...

<summary>Aristotle argues that prose style must balance "foreign" dignity with natural clarity to remain persuasive. He emphasizes that the best rhetoric hides its own artifice, citing Euripides and the actor Theodorus as examples of natural-sounding composition.</summary>

<keywords>Aristotle, Rhetoric, style, propriety, prose vs poetry, Euripides, Theodorus, metaphor, naturalness, persuasion</keywords>

--- Page 154 ---
<meta>This page continues Aristotle’s analysis of style in Book III of the Rhetoric. Having previously established that prose should be neither too humble nor too poetic, he now focuses on the "metaphor" as the primary tool for achieving a style that is both clear and distinguished without appearing artificial.</meta>

<page-num>141</page-num>

### RHETORIC. BOOK III. CHAPTER II.

...these are the only words that everyone uses; for everyone, in their everyday speech, employs **metaphors**, **standard terms**, and **proper words**. <note>original: "metaphorais... oikeiois... kyriois" — Aristotle suggests that because we use these naturally, a skilled speaker can use them to craft a refined style that doesn't feel forced or fake.</note> Thus it is clear that if one composes well, the speech will have a **foreign air** <note>original: "xenikon" — a quality of being sophisticated or "distinguished," as if from a more refined place.</note> and can escape notice while remaining clear; and this, as we have said, is the very **virtue** of rhetorical speech.

Among words, **homonyms** <note>original: "homōnymiai" — words that sound the same but have different meanings.</note> are most useful to the **sophist**, for it is by means of these that he performs his verbal trickery; but for the **poet**, **synonyms** are most useful. I mean "proper" and "synonymous" terms such as "to journey" and "to walk"; for these two are both standard terms and are synonyms of one another.

What each of these is, how many types of metaphor there are, and the fact that metaphors have the greatest power in both poetry and prose, has already been discussed in our work **On Poetics**. <note>Aristotle frequently refers back to his "Poetics" as the technical foundation for his rhetorical theories.</note> However, one must labor over them all the more in prose, because prose has fewer resources than verse. Metaphor, more than anything else, provides **clarity**, **pleasure**, and that **foreign air**. Furthermore, it is a gift that cannot be "taken" from anyone else. <note>Aristotle believed the ability to see similarities—to make good metaphors—was a sign of natural genius and could not be taught like a formula.</note>

One must also use **epithets** <note>Adjectives or descriptive phrases used to characterize a person or thing.</note> and metaphors that are **fitting**; this will be achieved through **proportion**. If they are not proportionate, the impropriety will be obvious, because opposites are most visible when placed side-by-side. Rather, one must consider it like this: just as a **scarlet cloak** suits a young man, what suits an old man? For the same clothing is not appropriate for both. 

And if you wish to **praise** something, you should draw your metaphor from the better things within the same class; if you wish to **blame** it, you should draw from the worse. I mean, for example—since opposites belong to the same class—to say that a man who "begs" is "praying," or that a man who "prays" is "begging" (since both are forms of asking), is to do exactly this. This is how **Iphicrates** <note>A famous Athenian general.</note> called **Callias** a "mendicant priest" rather than a "torch-bearer." <note>original: "metragyrtēn... dadouchon." A torch-bearer was a prestigious official in the Eleusinian Mysteries, while a mendicant priest was a low-status beggar for the goddess Cybele. Iphicrates used a "lower" version of a religious title to insult Callias.</note> Callias replied that Iphicrates must be uninitiated in the mysteries, otherwise he would not have called him a mendicant priest, but...

<summary>Aristotle explains that metaphor is the most effective tool for prose because it provides clarity and distinction. He emphasizes that metaphors must be proportionate to the subject and can be used to either elevate or disparage a person depending on the "class" of the words chosen.</summary>

<keywords>Aristotle, Rhetoric, metaphor, clarity, propriety, synonyms, homonyms, Iphicrates, Callias, Poetics</keywords>

<meta>This page continues Aristotle’s discussion on the strategic use of metaphor in rhetoric. On the previous page, he established that metaphors provide prose with clarity, pleasure, and a "foreign" or distinguished air. He now explores how the choice of a metaphor can either dignify or disparage a subject depending on the speaker's intent.</meta>

<page-num>142</page-num>
<header>142 ARISTOTLE</header>

...as a **torch-bearer**; for both roles are concerned with the god, but one is honorable, while the other is held in low esteem. <note>Aristotle is illustrating how a speaker might choose a prestigious title or a lowly one for the same general activity to influence the audience's perception.</note> And where some call actors "flatterers of Dionysus," <note>original: "dionysokolakas"</note> the actors call themselves **artists**. <note>original: "technitas" — this term implies a skilled professional or craftsman, carrying much higher social status than a "flatterer."</note> Both of these terms are **metaphors**: one intended to soil a reputation, the other to do the opposite.

Even pirates now call themselves **providers** <note>original: "poristas" — a term that could also mean "procurers" or "supply officers," making their theft sound like a legitimate service.</note> to sound more respectable. Because of this, it is possible to say that a man who has committed a crime has "erred," while one who has only "erred" has "committed a crime"; or that one who has stolen has "taken" something or, conversely, has "plundered" it.

However, the phrase used by Euripides’ character Telephus, "ruling the oar," or his description of "disembarking into Mysia," is **inappropriate**. <note>In the play *Telephus*, the hero is disguised as a beggar. Aristotle argues that "ruling" is too grand a word for a common sailor's action; the metaphor is "too big" for the subject and thus feels fake.</note> It is inappropriate because the word "rule" is greater than the subject’s worth; the artifice is not successfully hidden.

There is also the possibility of error in the very syllables of a word, if they do not suggest a pleasant sound. For example, **Dionysius the Brazen** <note>original: "Dionysios ho Chalkous" — an Athenian poet and orator nicknamed "the Brazen" because he suggested the use of bronze coinage.</note> in his elegies calls poetry "the **scream** of Calliope," <note>original: "kraugēn Kalliopēs" — Calliope was the Muse of epic poetry.</note> because both poetry and screaming are types of "voice." ** But this is a **poor metaphor** because of the discordant, unpleasant sounds involved.

Furthermore, metaphors should not be fetched from afar; instead, one should give names to unnamed things by drawing from things that are **related** and of the same species, so that it is clear the terms are kindred. We see this in the famous riddle:

> "I saw a man with fire **glue** bronze upon a man;"

For the process itself has no specific name, but both it and "gluing" are types of **application**. <note>The riddle describes the medical practice of "cupping," where a bronze vessel was heated and applied to the skin to create a vacuum.</note> He therefore called the application of a cupping-glass a "**gluing**."

In general, it is possible to derive excellent metaphors from well-crafted **riddles**; for since riddles are expressed through metaphor, it is clear that a riddle's success depends on a successful transfer of meaning. Metaphors should also be drawn from "beautiful" things. The **beauty** of a word, as Lycymnius says, lies either in its actual sound or in the thing it signifies; the same is true of ugliness.

There is a third point which refutes the argument of the **sophists**. <note>The Sophists were professional teachers of rhetoric who often used clever but fallacious logic.</note> It is not true, as **Bryson** used to say, that no one ever truly speaks "obscenely," provided that the meaning remains the same whether one uses this word or that. This is a falsehood; for one word...

<summary>Aristotle explains how metaphors can be used to praise or belittle a subject and emphasizes that they must be "proportionate" and drawn from related, pleasant concepts to be effective.</summary>
<keywords>metaphor, rhetoric, Dionysius the Brazen, Euripides, Telephus, riddles, aesthetics of language, sophistry, cupping-glass</keywords>

<meta>This page continues Aristotle’s discussion on the strategic use of metaphor in rhetoric. On the previous page, he established that metaphors provide prose with clarity, pleasure, and a "foreign" or distinguished air. He now explores how the choice of a metaphor can either dignify or disparage a subject depending on the speaker's intent.</meta>

<page-num>143</page-num>
<header>RHETORIC. BOOK III. CHAPTER III.</header>

...for one word is more authoritative than another, more similar to its subject, and more suitable for putting the matter **before one's eyes**. Furthermore, words do not signify things in the same way, so one must assume that one word is more beautiful or more shameful than another. While both terms may signify the same thing—beauty or shame—they do not do so insofar as it is beautiful or insofar as it is shameful; or if they do, they differ in degree.

**Metaphors** should be drawn from things that are beautiful either in their **sound**, their **power**, the **sight** they suggest, or some other sense. It makes a difference to say, for example, "rosy-fingered Dawn" <note>original: "rhododaktylos Ēōs" — a famous Homeric epithet for the sunrise.</note> rather than "purple-fingered," or even worse, "red-fingered."

Also, in the case of **epithets**, <term>epithet: an adjective or descriptive phrase expressing a quality or characteristic of the person or thing mentioned.</term> one can apply them from a base or shameful source—for instance, "the mother-slayer"—or from a better one, such as "his father's avenger."

Consider the poet **Simonides**: when the winner of a mule-race offered him only a small fee, he refused to write an ode, feeling it beneath him to write about "half-asses" <note>original: "hēmionous" — the literal Greek word for mules.</note>. But when the man gave him an adequate reward, he wrote:

-> Hail, daughters of storm-footed steeds! <-

Even though they were also the daughters of donkeys. Furthermore, one can use **diminutives** <note>original: "hypokorismos" — a form of a word that implies smallness or endearment, often used to soften a topic.</note> to the same effect. A diminutive is that which makes both evil and good seem smaller, just as **Aristophanes** jokes in his play *The Babylonians*, using "gold-let" <note>original: "chrysidarion"</note> instead of "gold," "cloak-let" <note>original: "imatidarion"</note> instead of "cloak," and "insult-let" <note>original: "loidoremation"</note> or "sickness-let." One must be careful, however, and observe **moderation** in both cases.

### CHAPTER III.

**Frigidity of style** <term>frigidity: a rhetorical term (psychron) referring to a style that feels "cold," stiff, or overly elaborate, often failing to move the audience.</term> arises from four causes in the language used. First, in **compound words**, as when Lycophron speaks of the "many-faced heaven" or the "high-peaked earth," and...

<summary>Aristotle explains how the choice of metaphors, epithets, and diminutives can elevate or diminish a subject's status, using poets like Simonides and Aristophanes as examples. He also introduces 'frigidity,' or poor style, often caused by overly complex compound words.</summary>
<keywords>metaphor, epithets, Simonides, Aristophanes, diminutives, hypocorism, Lycophron, frigidity of style, rhetoric</keywords>

--- Page 157 ---
<meta>This page continues Aristotle’s analysis of style in Book III of the Rhetoric. While the previous page discussed the beauty and appropriateness of metaphors, Aristotle now shifts to "frigidity" (stilted or "cold" style), identifying the specific linguistic habits—like over-compounding and rare vocabulary—that make prose feel unnatural and overly poetic.</meta>

<page-num>144</page-num>
<header>ARISTOTLE</header>

...and a "narrow-passaged shore" <note>original: "aktēn stenoporon"</note>; and Gorgias used terms like "beggarly-musical flatterer" <note>original: "ptōchomousos kolax"</note>, and "those who have committed perjury and those who have sworn well."

And as Alcidamas says, "his soul being filled with rage" and "his face becoming fire-colored" <note>original: "pyríchrōn"</note>. He also thought their enthusiasm would be "result-bringing" <note>original: "telesphoron"</note>, and he established "the result-bringing persuasion of his words," and "dark-hued" <note>original: "kyanochrōn"</note> for the floor of the sea. For all these appear **poetic** because of their **compounding** <note>Ancient Greek writers often combined two words into one. While standard in epic poetry (like Homer), Aristotle argues it feels forced in a political or legal speech.</note>.

This, then, is one cause of style being **frigid** <term>frigidity: "psychron" in Greek; a term for writing that feels stiff, overly formal, or "cold" because it tries too hard to be impressive.</term>. A second cause is the use of **glosses** <term>glosses: "glōttais"—rare, archaic, or foreign words that are not part of everyday conversation.</term>. For example, Lycophron calling Xerxes a "monstrous man" <note>original: "pelōron andra"</note>, and Sciron a "predatory man" <note>original: "sinnis anēr"</note>; and Alcidamas calling poetry a "plaything" <note>original: "athyrma"</note>, and speaking of "the recklessness of nature," and a man "sharpened by the unmixed anger of his mind."

A third cause is found in **epithets**, <term>epithet: a descriptive adjective or phrase added to a name or noun to characterize it.</term> namely, using them when they are too long, ill-timed, or too frequent. For while in poetry it is appropriate to say "white milk," in prose such things are quite unseemly. If used excessively, they betray the speaker and make it obvious that it is **poetry**. 

Of course, one must use some poetic coloring, for it departs from the ordinary and gives the language a **foreign air** <note>The Greek "xenikon" implies a sense of distinction or sophistication that comes from using non-standard language.</note>. But one must aim for the moderate, since the excess does more harm than speaking at random. For while random speech lacks excellence, this over-styling possesses actual badness. 

This is why the writings of **Alcidamas** appear "frigid"; for he does not use his epithets as a **seasoning**, but as the **main course**—they are so frequent, so long, and so obvious. For instance, he does not say "sweat," but "the damp sweat"; not "to the Isthmian games," but "to the general assembly of the Isthmian games"; not "laws," but "the laws, the kings of cities"; and not "at a run," but "with a running impulse of the soul"; and not "a sanctuary of the Muses," but "having received the sanctuary of the Muses of nature"; and "the sullen thought of the soul"; and not "of favor," but "of public favor..."

<summary>Aristotle defines "frigidity" in prose as a failure of style caused by excessive compounding, the use of rare "glosses," and an over-reliance on long or frequent adjectives (epithets). He uses the flowery style of the rhetorician Alcidamas as a primary example of how over-ornamentation makes prose sound like bad poetry.</summary>

<keywords>frigidity, Alcidamas, Gorgias, compounding, glosses, epithets, poetic style, rhetoric, Xerxes, Isthmian games</keywords>

--- Page 158 ---
<meta>This page continues Aristotle's critique of Alcidamas's prose style, specifically focusing on the third and fourth causes of "frigidity" in writing: the use of overly complex epithets and inappropriate metaphors. This follows the discussion of compound words and rare terms from the previous page.</meta>

<page-num>145</page-num>
<header>RHETORIC. BOOK III. CHAPTER III.</header>

...a "creator of grace" <note>original: "charitos dēmiourgos"</note>; and a "steward of the listeners' pleasure" <note>original: "oikonomos tēs tōn akouontōn hēdonēs"</note>; and he said one "did not hide it with branches, but with the **branches of the forest**" <note>original: "hylēs kladois"</note>; and "he did not clothe the body, but the **shame of the body**"; and "the soul's desire which is a **counter-imitation**" <note>original: "antimimon"</note> (this is both a compound word and an epithet, so it becomes a piece of poetry); and "the **extraordinary** <note>original: "exedron," literally "out of its seat."</note> excess of wickedness."

Therefore, by speaking **poetically**, writers introduce the **ridiculous** and the **frigid** <term>frigidity: writing that is stiff or overly formal to the point of being ineffective.</term> through their inappropriateness, as well as **obscurity** through their wordiness. For when a writer piles words upon someone who already understands, he destroys clarity by overshadowing the meaning. 

People do use **compounds** when a thing has no name of its own and the word is easy to combine, such as "time-wasting" <note>original: "chronotribeīn"</note>. But if used too much, the style becomes entirely **poetic**. 

Consequently, the **compound word** is most useful to **dithyrambic poets** <note>Dithyrambs were wild, passionate choral hymns; Aristotle suggests their "noisy" nature suits complex words.</note>, for these are full of sound. **Rare words** <term>glōttai: archaic or foreign terms.</term> are best for **epic poets**, for they are solemn and self-assured. **Metaphor**, however, is best for **iambic poets** <note>Iambic verse was the meter of drama and most closely resembled natural speech.</note>, for they use them now, as has been said.

And there is still a **fourth cause**: **frigidity** occurs in **metaphors**. For there are also inappropriate metaphors—some because they are **ridiculous** (comic poets also use metaphors), others because they are too **solemn** and **tragic**. They become **obscure** if they are far-fetched. 

For example, Gorgias speaks of "events that are **green and full of blood**" <note>original: "chlōra kai enaima ta pragmata"</note>; or "you sowed these things shamefully and reaped them poorly" <note>original: "aishkrōs espeiras, kakōs etherisas"</note>—for this is far too **poetic**. 

And as Alcidamas calls philosophy a "**rampart of the laws**" <note>original: "epiteichisma tōn nomōn"</note>; and the *Odyssey* a "**beautiful mirror of human life**" <note>original: "kalon anthrōpinou biou katoptron"</note>. He introduces nothing of that sort as a "plaything" <note>original: "athyrma"</note> for poetry; for all these things are unpersuasive for the reasons mentioned. 

Then there is the remark of Gorgias regarding the **swallow**. When it flew over him and dropped its droppings on him, he spoke in the best tragic manner, saying: "**Shame on you, Philomela!**" <note>In Greek myth, Philomela was a princess who was transformed into a swallow. By addressing the bird as the woman she once was, Gorgias treats a natural animal act as a moral insult, which Aristotle finds absurdly over-dramatic.</note>. For if a bird did it, it was not shameful, but for a young woman, it would be.

<meta>catchword: Demo-</meta>

...for, if she had done it <note>Aristotle is likely finishing an example of a specific insult or rhetorical point from the previous page</note>, it was not shameful; but for a virgin, it is shameful. He therefore insulted him well by saying what he *was*, but not what he *is*.

### CHAPTER IV.

The **simile** <term>eikōn: an image or comparison usually introduced by "like" or "as."</term> is also a **metaphor**; for it differs only slightly. For when the poet says of ***Achilles*** that he "rushed on like a lion," it is a simile; but when he says, "the lion rushed on," it is a metaphor. Because both the man and the animal are brave, the speaker has applied the name of "lion" to ***Achilles*** by transferring the term across categories. 

The simile is also useful in prose, though only occasionally, for it has a **poetic** quality. Similes should be used in the same way as metaphors; for they are indeed metaphors, differing only in the specific way they are expressed <note>original: "tō eirēmenō," referring to the explicit comparison word "like" or "as."</note>. 

Examples of similes include:
*   The one ***Androtion*** <note>An Athenian orator and politician.</note> used against ***Idrieus*** <note>A local ruler in Caria who had been imprisoned.</note>, saying he was like "puppies let off their chains"; for just as they run at people and bite them, so too ***Idrieus*** was dangerous once released from his bonds. 
*   The way ***Theodamas*** compared ***Archidamus*** to ***Euxenus***—specifically, to an ***Euxenus*** who knew nothing of geometry. This is a comparison based on **analogy** <note>original: "ek tou analogon"</note>; for by the same logic, ***Euxenus*** would be an "Archidamus skilled in geometry." 
*   The passage in ***Plato's*** *Republic* <note>Book V, 469d.</note> stating that those who strip the corpses of the fallen are like "puppies who bite the stones thrown at them but do not touch the person throwing them." 
*   The simile regarding the **People** <note>The "Demos," the collective body of citizens in the Athenian assembly.</note>, that they are like a ship-owner who is "strong, but somewhat deaf." 
*   The one about the verses of poets: that they are like "those who have the bloom of youth but lack true beauty." For once the bloom of youth has faded, or the meter of the verse has been dissolved into prose, they no longer look the same. 
*   The remark by ***Pericles*** regarding the people of ***Samos***: that they are like "children who take their food, but do so while weeping." 
*   ***Pericles*** also said of the ***Boeotians*** that they are like "holm-oaks" <note>An evergreen oak with very hard wood.</note>; for just as holm-oaks are split by wedges made from their own wood, so the ***Boeotians*** are cut down by fighting among themselves. 
*   And ***Demosthenes*** said of the **People** that they are like "those who get seasick on ships" <note>implying they are easily swayed or unsettled by the "waves" of political rhetoric.</note>. 
*   And as Demo-

<summary>Aristotle defines the simile as a variation of the metaphor and provides a series of famous examples from Greek oratory and philosophy to show how comparisons clarify character and situation.</summary>
<keywords>simile, metaphor, Achilles, Plato, Pericles, Demosthenes, analogy, rhetoric, poetic style</keywords>

--- Page 160 ---
<meta>This page concludes the list of examples regarding similes from Chapter IV and moves into Chapter V, which defines the "purity" or correctness of language (Hellenismos) as the foundation of good style.</meta>

<page-num>147</page-num>
<header>RHETORIC. BOOK III. CHAPTER V.</header>

**Democrates** <note>original: "[-][K]ratēs." Most editions identify this as Democrates, a philosopher of the 4th century BCE.</note> likened **orators** to **nurses**, who swallow the morsels themselves but smear the children with their saliva <note>A vivid image suggesting that orators keep the real benefits of their work for themselves while giving the public only the "spit-covered" leftovers.</note>. And as **Antisthenes** <note>A pupil of Socrates and founder of the Cynic school of philosophy.</note> likened the thin **Cephisodotus** to **frankincense**, because he gives pleasure as he is consumed <note>Aristotle is punning on the fact that Cephisodotus is wasting away (perishing), just as incense is destroyed by fire to produce its scent.</note>. For all these can be described both as similes and as metaphors. It follows that all those that are highly regarded when spoken as metaphors will clearly also be similes, and similes are simply metaphors that require an additional word <note>Aristotle refers to the word of comparison, like "as" or "like."</note>. One should always apply a **proportional metaphor** <term>metaphoran tēn ek tou analogon: a metaphor based on a mathematical-style ratio, such as "A is to B as C is to D."</term> reciprocally to both terms and to things of the same kind; for example, if a **drinking cup** is the "shield of **Dionysus**," then it is also fitting to call a **shield** the "drinking cup of **Ares**" <note>This is a famous Aristotelian example: Dionysus is to the cup as Ares is to the shield; therefore, the terms can be swapped for poetic effect.</note>. Language, then, is composed of these elements.

### CHAPTER V.

The foundation of style is to **speak good Greek** <term>hellēnizein: literally "to Hellenize," meaning to use the Greek language correctly according to its rules and idioms.</term>. This consists of five points. **First**, the proper use of **connectives** <term>syndesmoi: functional words like "and," "but," "if," or "on the one hand," which link parts of a sentence.</term>, if one renders them in the natural order in which they are meant to precede or follow one another, as some rules require; for instance, the word ***on the one hand*** <note>original: "MEN."</note> and ***I, on the one hand*** <note>original: "EGO MEN."</note> require a corresponding ***on the other hand*** <note>original: "DE."</note> or ***but he*** <note>original: "HO DE."</note>. One should provide these corresponding terms while the reader still remembers the first, and not separate them by too great a distance, nor provide a connective before the one that is strictly necessary; for this is rarely appropriate. 

"But I, after he told me—for **Cleon** came, both asking and demanding—I went, taking them with me." 

In these words, many connectives have been inserted before the one that was expected to follow. If the distance to the final verb "I went" becomes too great, the meaning becomes **unclear**. This, then, is the first point: excellence in the use of connectives. **Second**, to call things by their **specific names** and not by vague, general terms. **Third**, to avoid **ambiguous terms**—unless, of course, one intentionally chooses the opposite.

<summary>This page concludes the discussion on similes, emphasizing their relationship to metaphors, and introduces the first three rules for "Hellenismos," or correct Greek usage: the proper pairing of connectives, the use of specific terminology, and the avoidance of ambiguity.</summary>

<keywords>Aristotle, Rhetoric, metaphor, simile, proportionality, Hellenismos, grammar, connectives, clarity, Democrates, Cephisodotus</keywords>

<page-num>148</page-num>
<header>ARISTOTELIS</header>

...which is what speakers do when they have nothing to say, yet pretend to be saying something significant. For such people express these things in poetry, like **Empedocles** <note>original: "Empedoklēs." A Pre-Socratic philosopher who wrote his philosophy in verse; Aristotle often criticized him for being obscure.</note>. For his long-winded circumlocution deceives, and the audience is affected just as the masses are in the presence of soothsayers; for when they speak ambiguous words, the audience nods along in agreement <note>Aristotle is suggesting that people tend to project their own meaning onto vague but impressive-sounding language.</note>.

> **Croesus**, having crossed the **Halys**, will destroy a great empire. <note>This is a famous ambiguous oracle: Croesus, King of Lydia, was told this by the Delphic Oracle. He thought it meant he would destroy the Persian Empire, but he ended up destroying his own.</note>

And because it is generally a lesser error, soothsayers speak using general categories of a matter. For one might have a better chance in a game of "odds and evens" by simply saying "even" or "odd" rather than stating exactly how many objects one holds; and similarly, one is safer saying *that* something will happen rather than *when* it will happen. This is why oracle-mongers do not specify the time. All these things are similar; therefore, unless for some such purpose <note>i.e., unless one intends to be intentionally deceptive or vague.</note>, they should be avoided.

Fourth, as **Protagoras** <note>A famous Sophist who was among the first to categorize the parts of speech and grammatical rules.</note> divided the genders of words into **masculine**, **feminine**, and **inanimate** <note>original: "skeuē" (literally "implements" or "tools"), what we now call the neuter gender.</note>; for these must also be assigned correctly:

> *She, having come and having conversed, went away.* <note>The Greek original uses feminine participles to show correct gender agreement.</note>

Fifth, in correctly naming the **many**, the **few**, and the **one** <note>Aristotle is referring to grammatical number: singular, dual (common in Ancient Greek), and plural.</note>:

> *They, having come, were beating me.* <note>The original uses plural forms to show agreement.</note>

In general, what is written should be easy to read and easy to deliver. This is essentially the same thing. This quality is lacking where there are many connecting words, or where it is not easy to punctuate, as with the writings of **Heraclitus** <note>Heraclitus was nicknamed "The Obscure" because his prose was so difficult to interpret.</note>. For to punctuate the writings of Heraclitus is a difficult task, because it is unclear whether a word belongs to what follows or what precedes it. For example, at the very beginning of his book, he says:

> *Of this **Logos** <term>Logos: can mean word, reason, or the fundamental principle of the universe.</term> existing always men prove to be uncomprehending.*

For it is unclear whether "always" should be punctuated to go with "existing" or with "uncomprehending" <note>Does the Logos exist always, or are men always uncomprehending?</note>.

Furthermore, this causes a **soloecism** <term>soloikizein: a grammatical error or impropriety where the parts of a sentence do not fit together.</term>—the failure to provide a word that fits both parts of a pair when you link them together. For example, if you use a term like "seeing" for both "sound" and "color," it does not fit both; however, "perceiving" would fit both. Inaccuracies...

<summary>Aristotle discusses the importance of clarity and grammatical correctness, criticizing the intentional ambiguity of poets and oracles. He highlights the need for proper gender and number agreement and notes how poor punctuation, as seen in Heraclitus, leads to confusion.</summary>
<keywords>Aristotle, Rhetoric, Empedocles, Croesus, Protagoras, Heraclitus, soloecism, grammar, gender, number, punctuation, ambiguity</keywords>

--- Page 162 ---
<meta>This page concludes the final point of Chapter V regarding the clarity of sentence structure and begins Chapter VI, which transitions from "Hellenism" (correctness) to "Dignity" or "Amplitude" (onkos) of style—the techniques used to make speech sound more impressive or grand.</meta>

<page-num>149</page-num>
<header>RHETORIC. BOOK III. CHAPTER VI. 149</header>

...and [clarity is achieved] if you state your point before adding details, especially if you intend to insert many things in between. For example: "I intended, having talked with him about this and that, and in such a way, to depart"; but do not say, "I intended, having talked, to depart," and then add "and then this and that, and such things happened." <note>Aristotle is advising against "dangling" information at the end of a sentence that should have been integrated earlier for better flow.</note>

### CHAPTER VI.

The following contribute to **dignity** <term>original: *onkos*; literally "bulk" or "mass," referring to a grand, expansive, and impressive style</term> of speech: first, using a definition instead of a simple name. For example, do not say "a circle," but "a plane surface that is everywhere equidistant from the center." Conversely, for the sake of **conciseness**, do the opposite: use the name instead of the definition.

Also, if something is shameful or unseemly: if the shame lies in the definition, use the name; but if the shame lies in the name itself, use the definition. <note>Aristotle suggests using whichever form sounds more polite or less offensive to the audience’s ears.</note> Furthermore, one should illustrate meanings through metaphors and epithets, though one must be careful to avoid sounding too much like a poet. 

Another technique is to make a single thing appear as many, just as the poets do. Even though there may be only one harbor, they will say "unto the Achaean harbors"; and they write:

> Behold the many-doored folds of the writing-tablet. <note>original: "Deltou men haide polythyroi diaptychai." A quote from Euripides' *Iphigenia in Tauris*. By using the plural "folds" and "doors," the poet makes a simple letter seem more significant.</note>

Also, do not link words together under one article, but give each its own: "of the woman, of that one of ours"; whereas if you want to be concise, do the opposite: "of our woman." <note>original: "tēs hēmeteras gynaikos." In Greek, repeating the article adds weight and emphasis to each word.</note> 

One should also use connecting particles; but for a concise style, speak without the particle, though the words should still be connected in sense—for example, "having gone and having conversed" versus "having gone, I conversed."

Also useful is the method of **Antimachus** <note>A Greek epic poet known for his long-winded style</note>: describing a thing by listing what it *does not* possess. He does this when speaking of **Teumessus** <note>A mountain in Boeotia</note>:

> There is a certain windy, small hill...

For by listing what a place is not, the description can be expanded indefinitely. This technique of "negative description" can be applied to both good and bad things, whichever is useful for the speaker’s purpose. This is also why poets derive names for things based on what they lack, such as "stringless" or "lyreless" music; for they create these descriptions through **privations** <term>the absence of a quality</term>. This technique is very popular when used in metaphors.

<summary>Aristotle outlines techniques for achieving a grand, dignified style ("onkos") versus a concise one, emphasizing the use of definitions, plurals, and negative descriptions.</summary>
<keywords>style, dignity, conciseness, metaphor, Antimachus, Euripides, rhetoric, definitions, grammar</keywords>

--- Page 163 ---
<meta>This page concludes Chapter VI, which discusses techniques for adding dignity or "amplitude" to speech, and begins Chapter VII, which focuses on propriety—the art of matching one's style to the subject, the speaker's character, and the audience's emotions.</meta>

<page-num>150</page-num>
<header>ARISTOTLE</header>

...expressed by analogy; for example, saying that the trumpet is a "lyreless melody." <note>original: "melos alyron." This is an example of a "privative" metaphor, where something is described by what it lacks, adding a poetic or dignified air to a common object.</note>

### CHAPTER VII.

**Propriety** of style is achieved if the language is expressive of emotion and character, and is proportional to the subject matter. 

This **proportionality** <term>original: *analogoi*; referring to the balance between the weight of the topic and the weight of the words</term> means that one should not speak in a slapdash way about grand topics, nor with solemnity about trivial ones; nor should one add ornament to a common word. To do otherwise makes the speech look like a comedy—which is what Cleophon <note>Cleophon was a tragic poet of the late 5th century BC, often criticized for using high-flown language for mundane subjects.</note> does. He said certain things as absurdly as if he were to say, "O lady Fig-tree."

Style is **expressive of emotion** <term>original: *pathētikē*; style that evokes or reflects "pathos" or feeling</term> if, for instance, in a case of insult, the language is that of an angry man; if the subject is impious or shameful, one speaks with disgust and caution; if the subject is praiseworthy, one speaks with admiration; if pitiable, with humility; and similarly for other cases. 

This appropriate language makes the matter **persuasive**. For the mind of the listener is drawn into a false reasoning <note>original: "paralogizetai"; the audience's logic is bypassed by their feelings.</note> that the speaker is telling the truth, because people in such circumstances usually feel that way. Thus, the audience thinks the facts are as the speaker says, even if they are not. The listener always sympathizes with the emotional speaker, even if he says nothing of substance; this is why many speakers stun their audience by simply overwhelming them with loud bluster.

Style is also **expressive of character** <term>original: *ēthikē*; style reflecting the "ethos" or nature of the speaker</term> when it uses signs that follow what is appropriate to each class and disposition. By "class," I mean according to age—such as a child, a man, or an old man; or by gender—woman or man; or by nationality—like a Spartan or a Thessalian. <note>In antiquity, different Greek regions were associated with different speaking styles; Spartans were famous for being brief and blunt.</note>

By "dispositions," I mean the qualities that determine a person's way of life; for not every disposition results in a specific kind of life. If, therefore, a speaker uses words appropriate to their disposition, they will create a sense of character. For a rustic man and an educated man would not say the same things, nor would they say them in the same way.

The audience is also affected by a trick that speechwriters use to excess: "Who doesn't know?" or "Everyone knows." They agree...

<meta>catchword: agree...</meta>

<summary>In this passage, Aristotle defines "propriety" in rhetoric as a balance of emotion (pathos), character (ethos), and proportionality, explaining how matching language to the speaker's identity and the subject's weight creates a persuasive effect on the audience.</summary>
<keywords>propriety, style, pathos, ethos, proportionality, Cleophon, rhetoric, persuasion, demographics of speech</keywords>

--- Page 164 ---
<meta>This page continues the discussion from Chapter VII regarding the "propriety" of style—specifically how a speaker’s delivery and choice of words must match the emotional state of the audience to remain persuasive. It then transitions into Chapter VIII, which addresses the rhythmic structure of prose.</meta>

<page-num>151</page-num>
<header>RHETORIC. BOOK III. CHAPTERS VII–VIII.</header>

...for the listener always sympathizes with the speaker’s emotion, even if he says nothing of substance. This is why a listener is often carried away by the speaker's passion, feeling a sense of shame <note>original: "aischunomenos"; the audience feels the same social pressure or emotion the speaker projects</note> so that they might share in what everyone else is feeling. 

Whether to use these techniques **opportunely** or **inopportunely** <term>original: *kairos*; the "right moment" or critical timing in rhetoric</term> is a consideration common to all forms of style. The standard **remedy** for every kind of excess is that well-known advice: the speaker should rebuke himself in advance. If the speaker seems to recognize their own exaggeration, it appears to be true and sincere, since the speaker is clearly aware of what he is doing. 

Furthermore, one should not use all these artistic devices simultaneously; in this way, the listener is "robbed" <note>Aristotle uses the term "kleptetai" (stolen/cheated) to mean that the audience is successfully deceived by the artifice because it doesn't feel forced.</note> of their suspicion. By this I mean, for example, if the words themselves are **harsh**, one should not also use a harsh voice, a stern facial expression, and the other appropriate gestures; otherwise, the artifice becomes obvious for what it is. If you use one of these but not the others, you achieve the same result of concealment. If, therefore, soft words are spoken harshly, or harsh words are spoken softly, the speech becomes unconvincing. 

Compound words, an abundance of epithets, and foreign terms are most appropriate for a speaker who is speaking **passionately** <term>original: *pathētikōs*; from "pathos," meaning with strong emotion</term>. We grant forgiveness to an angry man who calls an evil "sky-high" or "monstrous." The same applies when the speaker has already won over his audience and moved them to **enthusiasm**—whether through praise, blame, anger, or friendship. Isocrates <note>A famous Athenian rhetorician (436–338 BC) known for his polished, rhythmic prose.</note> does this at the end of his *Panegyricus*, using phrases like "fame and memory" and "those who endured." People utter such things when they are inspired; thus, the audience clearly accepts them since they are in a similar state of mind. 

This is why such language is suited to **poetry**, for poetry is something inspired. One must use it either in this way or with **irony**, as Gorgias <note>A Sophist known for his highly ornamental and rhythmic style.</note> used to do, and as is found in the *Phaedrus* <note>Referring to Plato’s dialogue, where Socrates uses high-flown, poetic language, often with an underlying layer of irony.</note>.

## CHAPTER VIII.

The **rhythm** of the language should be neither metrical (like verse) nor entirely without rhythm. If it is metrical, it lacks persuasiveness, for it seems artificial. At the same time, it distracts the listener, making them watch for the recurrence of the beat, just as children watch for the repeated cry of a herald...

<image-desc>A small decorative printer's fleuron or floral ornament is centered at the bottom of the page.</image-desc>

<summary>Aristotle explains that emotional language and poetic devices are persuasive only when the audience is already moved or when the speaker uses self-correction to appear sincere. He then begins a new chapter arguing that prose should have rhythm but should never fall into strict poetic meter, which feels fake.</summary>

<keywords>propriety, kairos, pathos, Isocrates, Gorgias, Phaedrus, irony, poetic language, prose rhythm, meter</keywords>

--- Page 165 ---
<meta>This page continues Aristotle’s Book III of the Rhetoric. The previous page ended while discussing the use of compound words and how to conceal artifice in speech. This page transitions into Chapter VIII, focusing on the specific rhythms appropriate for prose as opposed to poetry.</meta>

<page-num>152</page-num>
<header>ARISTOTLE</header>

...children anticipate the answer, such as: "Whom does the freedman choose as his patron? Cleon." <note>original: "Kleaona"; this refers to a well-known riddle or joke where the listener anticipates the name of the famous Athenian demagogue.</note> 

That which is **unrhythmical** is **unlimited** <term>original: *aperanton*; lacking a defined boundary or end</term>. However, prose must be limited, though not by **meter** <term>original: *metron*; the strict patterns of poetry</term>; for the unlimited is unpleasant and cannot be known. All things are limited by number; and the "number" of the form of language is **rhythm** <term>original: *rhythmos*</term>, of which meters are specific divisions. 

Therefore, a speech should have rhythm, but not meter; otherwise, it will be a poem. However, the rhythm should not be precisely exact. This will be achieved if it is only maintained to a certain point. 

Of the various rhythms, the **heroic** <note>The dactylic hexameter used in epic poetry like the Iliad.</note> is solemn and not suited for conversation, and it requires a musical harmony. The **iambic** <note>A rhythm following a short-long pattern.</note> is the very language of the many; for this reason, people speak in iambic rhythms more than any other meter when talking. But prose requires a sense of solemnity and the power to move the listener out of themselves. The **trochaic** rhythm <note>A long-short pattern.</note> is too much like a **kordax** <term>a suggestive, lively dance from Greek comedy</term>; this is shown by the "tripping" nature of tetrameters, which are a "running" rhythm.

There remains the **paean**, which speakers have used since the time of **Thrasymachus** <note>A famous sophist and rhetorician often mentioned by Plato.</note>, though they were not able to define what it was. The paean is a third type of rhythm and is closely related to those already mentioned; for it has a ratio of **three to two**. Of the others, one (the heroic) has a ratio of one to one; the other (iambic or trochaic) has a ratio of two to one. The ratio of one-and-a-half <note>original: *hemiolios*</note> follows these ratios, and this is the paean. 

The other rhythms should be set aside for the reasons mentioned, and because they are too metrical. The paean, however, should be adopted; for it is the only one of the mentioned rhythms that does not form a meter, and so it is the most likely to go unnoticed by the listener. 

Currently, people use only one kind of paean, both at the beginning and at the end of sentences. However, the **ending** should differ from the **beginning**. There are two types of paeans, opposite to each other. One of these is suited for the beginning, just as it is currently used; this is the one that begins with a long syllable and ends with three short ones: 

-> "Delos-born, or Lycia," <note>original: *Dalogenes, eite Lukian*</note> <-
-> and "Golden-haired Hecatus, son of Zeus." <note>original: *Chruseokoma Hekate, pai Dios*</note> <-

The other...

<meta>ornamental fleuron at bottom center</meta>

<summary>Aristotle argues that prose should be rhythmic but not metrical to remain persuasive and natural. He identifies the paean as the ideal prose rhythm because its 3:2 ratio is less obvious to the listener than the rhythms of epic or comic poetry.</summary>
<keywords>rhythm, meter, iambic, trochaic, paean, Thrasymachus, prose composition, Greek rhetoric</keywords>

--- Page 166 ---
<meta>This page continues Aristotle’s discussion of rhythm in prose from Chapter VIII and begins Chapter IX, which distinguishes between the two primary styles of sentence structure: the "running" style and the "compact" or "periodic" style. The previous page ended while introducing the paean rhythm.</meta>

<page-num>153</page-num>
<header>RHETORIC, BOOK III. CHAPTER IX.</header>

...the other <note>referring to the second type of paean rhythm</note> is the opposite, where three short syllables begin and the long one is last:

->After earth and waters, night hid the ocean.<- <note>original: "Meta de gan hydata t' okeanon ephanise nyx." This is a poetic fragment used to illustrate the rhythm of three short syllables followed by one long syllable.</note>

This rhythm creates a proper ending; for a short syllable, because it is incomplete, makes the sentence feel truncated <note>original: *kolobon*; literally "maimed" or "docked," as in an animal's tail</note>. But it is necessary to cut off the sentence with a long syllable so that the end is clear—not because of the scribe, nor because of a punctuation mark <note>original: *paragraphon*; a mark used in ancient manuscripts to show where a new thought or speaker begins</note>, but because of the rhythm itself. It has been stated, then, that prose should be rhythmical and not unrhythmical, which specific rhythms make it so, and in what arrangement.

## CHAPTER IX.

The style of prose must be either **running** <term>original: *eiromene*; "strung-together" like a chain or a row of beads</term> and made into a single unit by connecting words—just like the "preludes" in dithyrambs <note>Dithyrambs were passionate choral hymns; their opening sections were notoriously loose and unstructured.</note>—or **compact** <term>original: *katestrammene*; "turned back on itself"</term> and similar to the "antistrophes" <note>The second part of a choral ode which mirrors the complex structure of the first part.</note> of the ancient poets.

The running style is the ancient one; for example: "This is the exposition of the inquiry of Herodotus of Thurii." <note>Aristotle quotes the famous opening line of the *Histories* of Herodotus.</note> This style was used by everyone in the past, but now it is used by few. By "running," I mean a style that has no end in itself until the subject being discussed is finished. It is unpleasant because it is infinite <term>original: *apeiron*; lacking a defined boundary or limit</term>; for everyone wants to see the end in sight. For this reason, runners only pant and lose heart at the turning-posts <note>The *kampter* was the pillar at the end of the stadium track; runners were most exhausted there because the goal was not yet the final finish line.</note>; for if they see the finish line before them, they do not tire as easily. 

This, then, is the running style. The **compact style** is that which consists of **periods** <term>original: *periodos*; literally a "circuit" or a "path that returns to the start"</term>. By a "period," I mean a sentence having a beginning and an end within itself, and a length that can be easily taken in at a glance. Such a style is pleasant and easy to learn. It is pleasant because it is the opposite of that which is never-ending, and because the listener always feels they have a grasp on something, since the sentence is always finished for them. But to foresee nothing and make no progress is unpleasant. It is easy to learn because it is easy to remember; and it is easy to remember because it has a **number** <term>original: *arithmos*; here meaning a structured, predictable rhythm or measure</term>...

<vocab>Aristotle, Rhetoric, running style, compact style, period, rhythm, Herodotus</vocab>

<summary>Aristotle concludes his study of prose rhythm by recommending the paean as a way to clearly mark the end of sentences, then introduces Chapter IX by distinguishing between the rambling "running" style of older writers like Herodotus and the more effective "periodic" style.</summary>
<keywords>rhythm, paean, running style, periodic style, Herodotus, prose structure, Greek rhetoric</keywords>

--- Page 167 ---
<meta>This page continues Aristotle’s analysis of the "compact" or "periodic" style of prose. Having previously defined the period as a sentence with a beginning and an end that can be grasped as a whole, Aristotle now explains why this style is more effective for memory and clear communication.</meta>

<page-num>154</page-num>
<header>ARISTOTELIS</header>

...style in periods, which is the easiest of all to remember. For this reason, everyone remembers verse better than random prose; for it has a number <note>The Greek *arithmos* here refers to the meter or rhythm that provides a predictable structure.</note> by which it is measured. The period must also be completed by the thought, and not be cut off prematurely, like the iambic verses of Sophocles:

> This is Calydon, land of the Pelopian soil. <note>original: "Kalydon men hēde, gaia Pelopeias chthonos." This is a famous example of a misleading line break. Calydon is actually in Aetolia, not the Peloponnese, but the phrasing makes it sound like the speaker is claiming it is part of the Peloponnese.</note>

For the opposite is assumed due to the division of the verse, as in the case mentioned: that Calydon belongs to the Peloponnesus. A period is either divided into members <term>original: *kola*; the constituent parts of a complex sentence</term> or it is simple. A period with members is a style that is both complete and divided into parts, and is easy to say in one breath—not because of an arbitrary division, like the period from Sophocles mentioned above, but as a completed whole. A member <term>original: *kolon*</term> is one of the two parts of such a period. By "simple," I mean a period consisting of only one member. 

It is necessary that both the members and the periods be neither curtailed <note>original: *meiurous*; literally "mouse-tailed," referring to a sentence or verse that tapers off weakly or ends abruptly.</note> nor too long. For that which is too short often causes the listener to stumble; for it is inevitable that when they are still rushing forward toward a point further on, and the rhythm—the limit of which they already anticipate—is suddenly pulled back by a stop, a jolt or "stumbling" occurs because of the resistance. 

Long periods, on the other hand, make the listener feel left behind, much like people who take a walk and turn back only after they have overshot the finish line; for they leave behind those who were walking with them. Similarly, periods that are excessively long become a speech that resembles a musical prelude <term>original: *anabolē*; a long, wandering opening in ancient music that lacked a fixed structure</term>. Thus, the joke that Democritus of Chios made against Melanippides—who composed long, wandering preludes instead of structured choral responses—comes true:

> A man works evil for himself in working evil for another;
> And the long prelude is worst for the one who composed it. <note>These lines are a parody of the poet Hesiod. Democritus uses a pun on *anabolē*, which can mean both a "musical prelude" and a "delay" or "postponement" of justice.</note>

This criticism also fits those who use excessively long members. But as for those with excessively short members, a true period does not even form;

<summary>Aristotle explains that periodic sentences are easier to remember because of their structured rhythm. He warns against sentences that are either too short (causing the reader to "stumble") or too long (causing the reader to feel "left behind").</summary>

<keywords>Aristotle, Sophocles, Democritus of Chios, Melanippides, period, colon, musical prelude, prose rhythm, Rhetoric</keywords>

--- Page 168 ---
<meta>This page continues Aristotle’s discussion of the "periodic" style of prose, transitioning from the general definition of sentence structure to the specific types of "members" or clauses that make up a sophisticated period. It concludes a sentence from the previous page regarding the pitfalls of overly short phrasing.</meta>

<page-num>155</page-num>
<header>RHETORIC. BOOK III. CHAPTER IX.</header>

...it leads the listener to stumble. <note>original: "προπετῆ οὖν ἄγει τὸν ἀκροατήν." This completes the thought from page 154 that excessively short clauses make a listener feel as if they are tripping forward rather than following a steady rhythm.</note> Of the style consisting of members <term>original: *kola*; the building blocks of a complex sentence</term>, one kind is **divided**, and the other is **antithetical**. 

The **divided** style is like this: *“I have often wondered at those who gathered the great festivals, and established the athletic contests.”* <note>Aristotle is quoting the opening of Isocrates’ "Panegyricus," a famous speech calling for Greek unity.</note>

The **antithetical** style is that in which, in each of the two members, either an opposite is placed against an opposite, or the same word is linked to opposites. For example: *“They benefited both: those who remained behind and those who followed; for to the latter they acquired more than they had at home, and for the former they left behind enough at home.”* 

The opposites here are *remaining* and *following*, and *enough* and *more*. 

Likewise: *“So that both to those needing money and those wishing to enjoy it,”* where *enjoyment* is set against *acquisition*. 

And again: *“It often happens in these situations that the wise fail and the foolish succeed.”* 

*“Immediately they were deemed worthy of the prizes for excellence, and not much later they gained the empire of the sea.”* 

*“To sail through the mainland, and to march across the sea, by bridging the Hellespont and digging through Mount Athos.”* <note>A reference to the Persian King Xerxes, who famously dug a canal through the Athos peninsula and built a bridge of ships across the Hellespont to invade Greece.</note> 

*“And though they were citizens by nature, they were deprived of their city by law.”* 

*“For some of them perished miserably, while others were saved shamefully.”* 

*“In private, to use barbarians as household servants; in public, to overlook many of the allies being enslaved.”* 

*“Either to have them while living, or to leave them behind when dead.”* 

And what someone said against Peitholaus and Lycophron in the law court: *“These men, while they were at home, were selling you; but having come to us, they have bought you.”* <note>Peitholaus and Lycophron were tyrants of Pherae in Thessaly; the speaker is using a wordplay on "selling" (betraying) and "buying" (bribing or corrupting).</note> 

For all these examples achieve what has been described. This kind of style is pleasing because opposites are most easily recognized, and when placed side-by-side they are even more recognizable; also because it resembles a **syllogism** <term>a logical argument where a conclusion is drawn from two premises</term>; for a refutation is a gather... <note>The text cuts off mid-word: "συνα[γωγή]" (a bringing together or collection of opposites).</note>

<summary>Aristotle defines "divided" and "antithetical" sentence structures, providing a series of examples (largely from the orator Isocrates) to show how contrasting ideas make a speech more memorable and persuasive. He notes that antithesis is particularly effective because it mirrors the structure of a logical proof.</summary>

<keywords>Aristotle, Rhetoric, Antithesis, Isocrates, Period, Clauses, Persuasion, Logic, Hellespont, Xerxes, Peitholaus, Lycophron</keywords>

--- Page 169 ---
<meta>This page continues the discussion from page 155 regarding the structure of the "periodic" style. Aristotle is concluding his technical breakdown of how clauses (members) can be balanced against one another before moving into Chapter 10, which explores what makes speech elegant or witty.</meta>

<page-num>156</page-num>
<header>ARISTOTELIS</header>

...is a bringing together of opposites. Such, then, is **antithesis**. **Parisosis** <term>par-ih-SOH-sis; from 'isosis', meaning equalization</term> occurs if the members <term>kola; the distinct parts of a sentence</term> are of equal length. **Paromoiosis** <term>pa-roh-moy-OH-sis; similarity of sound</term> occurs if each of the members has similar sounding extremities. This must happen either at the beginning or at the end of the clauses. At the beginning, the similarity is always in whole words; but at the end, it consists of the final syllables, or inflections of the same word, or the repetition of the same word. 

Examples of similarity at the beginning: *“For he received from him an estate (*agron*) that was idle (*argon*).”* <note>The Greek words 'agron' (field/estate) and 'argon' (untilled/idle) create a pun through their similar sound.</note>

->Gifts they received, and by words they were persuaded.<- 
<note>This is a quote from Homer’s Iliad (9.526), showing balanced beginning sounds in the original Greek.</note>

Examples of similarity at the end: *“They thought that he had fathered a child, but that he had been the cause of it.”* <note>Aristotle is pointing out the rhyming verb endings: 'tetokenai' and 'gegonenai'.</note> *“In the greatest anxieties, and in the smallest hopes.”* An example of an inflection <term>ptosis; a change in the grammatical case of a word</term> of the same word: *“He is worthy of being set up in bronze, though he is not worth a copper.”* <note>Aristotle plays on the word 'chalkous' (a bronze statue) and 'chalkou' (a copper coin).</note> An example of the same word: *“When he was alive you spoke ill of him, and now [that he is dead] you write ill of him.”* An example of a similar syllable: *“What ill would you have suffered if you had seen an idle man?”* <note>A play on 'deinon' (terrible/ill) and 'argon' (idle).</note>

It is possible for a single sentence to possess all these features at once: to be an **antithesis**, to have **balanced clauses**, and to have **similar endings**. The various ways periods can begin have been nearly all enumerated in the *Theodecteia*. <note>A lost work on rhetoric by Theodectes, a friend and student of Aristotle.</note> There are also **false antitheses**, such as those Epicharmus <note>A comic playwright known for his parodies of philosophical language.</note> used to write:

->At one time I was among them; at another time I was<-
->with them.<-
<note>This is a "false" antithesis because being "among" them and "with" them means the same thing; there is no real contrast in meaning, only in the sound of the words.</note>

### CHAPTER X.

Now that these matters have been defined, we must discuss the source of **elegant sayings** <term>asteia; literally 'of the city,' meaning sophisticated or witty</term> and those that are **highly regarded**. To produce them is the role of a naturally gifted or a well-practiced man; but to explain them is the task of this inquiry. Let us therefore speak of them and list them, beginning with this principle: 

Learning things easily is naturally pleasant to everyone, and words signify something; therefore, those words which create knowledge in us are the most pleasant.

<summary>Aristotle concludes his technical analysis of sentence balance (parisosis) and sound similarity (paromoiosis) before beginning a new chapter on the origins of wit and elegance in speech, linking pleasure to the ease of learning.</summary>

<keywords>Aristotle, Rhetoric, Antithesis, Parisosis, Paromoiosis, Wit, Sophistication, Theodectes, Epicharmus, Linguistics</keywords>
