<meta>This page transitions from the technical mechanics of sentence structure (antithesis and balanced clauses) into Chapter 10, where Aristotle explores what makes speech "elegant" or "witty" (asteia). He focuses on how the mind finds pleasure in learning through metaphors and vivid imagery.</meta>

<page-num>157</page-num>
<header>RHETORIC. BOOK III. CHAPTER X. 157</header>

...gives the most pleasure. Now, foreign or rare words <note>original: "glōttai"</note> are simply unknown to us, while ordinary words <note>original: "kyria"</note> we already know. It is **metaphor** that accomplishes this best. For instance, when a poet calls old age “stubble,” <note>original: "kalamēn"; a reference to the Odyssey where Odysseus describes himself as a shadow of his former self, like withered straw.</note> he creates a kind of learning and knowledge through a shared category <note>original: "dia tou genous"</note>, for both old age and stubble have "lost their bloom."

Poetic **similes** <note>original: "eikones"</note> achieve the same result; therefore, when they are well-crafted, they appear elegant. As we have said before, a simile is simply a metaphor that differs by an addition <note>specifically, the addition of a comparative word like "as" or "like"</note>. This makes it slightly less pleasant because it is longer; it does not directly say "this *is* that," and so the listener's soul does not immediately seek the connection.

It follows that both the style of speech and the rhetorical arguments <term>enthymemes; a type of logical argument used in persuasion where some premises are implied</term> are most elegant when they provide us with **quick learning**. This is why superficial arguments are not highly regarded—for we call "superficial" those things that are obvious to everyone and require no search for meaning. Likewise, we reject those that, once spoken, remain unintelligible. Rather, the most successful arguments are those where understanding occurs at the very moment of speaking—even if we had no prior knowledge—or where the mind follows the meaning only a short moment later. In these cases, a process like learning takes place; in the other cases, neither occurs.

In terms of the **thought** expressed, these are the types of arguments that win favor. In terms of the **style**, elegance is achieved through the structure of the sentence, specifically if it is expressed **antithetically**. For example: *“Regarding the peace that was common to others as a war against their own private interests.”* Here, "war" is set in opposition to "peace."

Regarding the choice of words, elegance comes from using **metaphor**—provided it is neither too far-fetched (which makes it hard to grasp) nor too superficial (which fails to move the listener). Furthermore, the words should "bring the matter before the eyes," <note>original: "pro ommatōn poiei"</note> for the listener ought to see the actions as if they are happening now, rather than in the future. 

One must therefore aim for these three things: **metaphor**, **antithesis**, and **vividness** <term>energeia; literally "activity" or "energy," referring to language that makes inanimate things seem alive and moving</term>.

Of the four types of metaphors, the most popular are those based on **analogy**. For example, Pericles said that the youth who had perished in the war had vanished from the city as if the spring were taken out of the year... <note>Aristotle is quoting a famous funeral oration by the Athenian statesman Pericles.</note>

<summary>Aristotle argues that elegant speech relies on "quick learning" facilitated by metaphors, similes, and antithesis. He introduces the concept of "bringing things before the eyes" (vividness) as a key tool for a persuasive speaker.</summary>

<keywords>Aristotle, Rhetoric, metaphor, simile, enthymeme, antithesis, vividness, Pericles, learning, elegance</keywords>

...[y]outh had been swept away from the city, it was as if someone had **taken the spring out of the year**. <note>Aristotle is finishing a quote attributed to Pericles regarding the loss of young men in war.</note> And Leptines, speaking of the Lacedaemonians <note>Spartans</note>, said that the Athenians should not allow Greece to be **rendered one-eyed** <note>original: "heterophthalmon"; he argued that Athens and Sparta were the "two eyes" of Greece, and losing one would leave the nation sightless.</note>.

And Cephisodotus, when Chares was eager to submit his accounts regarding the Olynthian war, grew indignant, saying that Chares was trying to submit his accounts while **holding the people in a chokehold** <note>original: "pnigma"; implying Chares was pressuring or suffocating the public's judgment.</note>. On another occasion, when urging the Athenians to sail for Euboea to provide supplies, he said they must go out with **"the decree of Miltiades"** <note>referring to the famous, decisive decree that led to the victory at Marathon; he meant they should act with the same legendary speed and courage.</note>. 

And Iphicrates, when the Athenians made a truce with Epidaurus and the coastal regions, was angry, saying they had **stripped themselves of the rations of war**. And Peitholaos called the *Paralos* <note>one of the two sacred state triremes of Athens</note> the **"cudgel of the people,"** and Sestos the **"grain-bowl of the Piraeus."** <note>Sestos was a strategic point for the grain trade; the Piraeus was the port of Athens.</note> 

And Pericles urged that Aegina be removed, calling it the **"eyesore of the Piraeus."** <note>original: "lēmēn"; literally the "rheum" or "crust" in the corner of a diseased eye. Aegina was an island visible from the Athenian port that was often a hostile rival.</note> 

And Moerocles said he was no more a rogue than a certain "respectable" man he named; for while that man practiced his roguery at **thirty-three percent interest**, Moerocles only did so at **ten percent**. <note>A joke playing on the "respectability" of money-lenders versus the perceived "dishonesty" of politicians.</note> 

And the iambic verse of Anaxandrides concerning daughters who were taking a long time to marry:

> "My daughters are **past the deadline** for their weddings." <note>original: "hyperēmeroi"; a legal term for a debtor who has missed their payment date.</note>

And the remark of Polyeuctus to a certain Speusippus who had suffered a stroke and could not keep still: he said that Speusippus was unable to stay quiet though fate had **bound him in a five-holed pillory of disease**. <note>A "five-holed pillory" was a wooden instrument of punishment that bound the neck, arms, and legs; Polyeuctus is using it as a metaphor for the debilitating nature of the stroke.</note>

And Cephisodotus called the triremes <note>warships</note> **"painted mills,"** <note>likely because they "ground down" the city's wealth to maintain.</note> and the Dog <note>a nickname for a Cynic philosopher, likely Diogenes</note> called the taverns the **"common mess-halls of Attica."** <note>original: "pheiditia"; this was a sarcastic reference to the austere, mandatory communal meals of the Spartans.</note> 

Aeschion said that the Athenians had **"poured the city out"** into Sicily; for this is a metaphor and brings the scene **before the eyes**. <note>original: "pro ommatōn"; a key term for Aristotle meaning "vividness."</note> He also said, **"so that Greece cried out"**; this too is a kind of metaphor and brings the event **before the eyes**. 

And just as Cephisodotus urged them to be careful not to turn their many assemblies into **"confluences."** <note>original: "syndromas"; a term suggesting a disorganized mob or a rushing together of waters rather than a structured political meeting.</note> And Isocrates...

<meta>This page continues Aristotle’s catalogue of witty and vivid metaphors used by famous Athenian orators. He is specifically illustrating the concept of "setting a scene before the eyes" (pro ommaton), which makes an audience feel as though they are witnessing the events described.</meta>

<page-num>159</page-num>

...towards those gathering in the great festivals. And as it is said in the Funeral Oration <note>Aristotle refers to a famous speech commemorating the war dead, likely one attributed to Pericles or Isocrates</note>, that it was fitting for Greece to **shave her head** <note>original: "keirasthai"; a traditional sign of mourning</note> at the tomb of those who died at Salamis, for their freedom was **buried together with their valor**. For if the speaker had merely said it was "fitting to weep because their valor was buried," it would be a metaphor and a vivid image <note>original: "pro ommaton," meaning "before the eyes"</note>; but the addition of "freedom with their valor" creates a certain contrast <note>original: "antithesis"</note>. 

And as Iphicrates said: **"The path of my words lies through the middle of the deeds done by Chares."** This is a metaphor based on analogy <note>original: "analogian"</note>, and the phrase "through the middle" makes the idea vivid. 

Also, the saying that one should **"call upon dangers to come to the aid of dangers"** is a vivid metaphor. 

And Lycoleon, speaking in defense of Chabrias, said: **"Not even respecting his bronze statue, which stands as a suppliant for him."** <note>Chabrias was a famous general; his statue in the marketplace depicted him in a defensive kneeling position, which Lycoleon poignantly compares to a person begging for mercy.</note> This was a metaphor suited to the moment, not for all time, but it set the scene before the eyes; for while Chabrias was in danger of being condemned, his statue "pleaded" for him—the inanimate becoming animate, a living reminder of his service to the city. 

And the phrase: **"Practicing in every way to be small-minded"**; for "to practice" is a way of "making something grow." 

And the saying that **"God kindled the mind as a light in the soul"**; for both the mind and light reveal things. 

**"We do not end our wars, but postpone them"**; for both postponement and a peace of this kind involve things that are yet to come. 

And to say that **"treaties are a far more beautiful trophy than those won in battles"**; for battle trophies are for small successes and a single stroke of luck, but treaties represent the conclusion of the whole war. Both are signs of victory. 

Also, the claim that **"cities pay a great penalty to the censure of men"**; for a "penalty" <note>original: "euthyna," referring to the formal audit or "settling of accounts" required of Athenian officials</note> is a form of just damage.

Thus, it has been shown that elegant sayings <note>original: "asteia"</note> arise from metaphor...

### CHAPTER XI.

We must now define what we mean by "setting before the eyes" <note>original: "pro ommaton"</note> and what actions produce this effect. I say that things are "set before the eyes" when they signify things being in a state of **activity** <term>energeia: a technical term in Greek rhetoric meaning "vividness" or "actualization," where inanimate objects are described as if they are alive and moving</term>. 

For example, to say that a good man is **"square"** <note>original: "tetragonon"</note> is a metaphor—since both a square and a good man are "complete" or "perfect"—but it does not signify activity. However, the phrase **"having his prime in full bloom"** represents activity. Likewise, the phrase **"you, like a creature set free"** <note>original: "apheton"; often used of animals dedicated to a god that are allowed to roam freely</note> is activity. And in the line, **"thereupon the Greeks, darting forward with their feet,"** the word "darting" <note>original: "aixantes"</note> is both activity and metaphor.

Homer also frequently uses this technique of describing inanimate things as if they were animate through the use of metaphor. In every case, he is esteemed for creating this sense of activity, as in these examples:

> The **shameless stone** rolled back down to the plain. <note>From the myth of Sisyphus, who was cursed to roll a boulder up a hill for eternity.</note>
> And, The arrow **flew**.
> And, **Longing** to fly.
> And, They stood in the ground, **craving** to taste flesh. <note>Referring to spears that missed their mark and stuck in the earth.</note>
> And, The point of the spear rushed **eagerly** through his breast.

In all these instances, because the objects are treated as living beings, they appear to be active. For "acting shamelessly," "being eager," and the rest are forms of activity. Homer has applied these qualities through metaphors based on analogy: for as the stone is to Sisyphus, so is a shameless person to the one they offend.

He also achieves this in his most celebrated similes involving inanimate objects:

> Curving, white-crested; some in front, others
> ->behind;<-

For he makes everything move and live. And this activity is a form of **imitation** <term>mimesis: the representation of the real world in art and literature</term>. One should use metaphors, as has been said...

<summary>Aristotle defines the rhetorical concept of "setting before the eyes" (vividness), explaining that it is achieved by using metaphors that attribute life and "activity" (energeia) to inanimate objects.</summary>

<meta>This page continues Aristotle's analysis of what makes speech "witty" or "elegant," moving from the concept of "vividness" (setting a scene before the eyes) to the use of metaphors that bridge distant concepts and the effectiveness of linguistic surprises or puns.</meta>

RHETORICA. BOOK III. CHAPTER XI. 161
L
ARISTOTLE'S RHETORIC

...previously, metaphors should be drawn from things that are related but not obvious; just as in philosophy, it is the mark of a keen mind to discern similarities even in things far apart. For example, Archytas <note>A Pythagorean philosopher and mathematician</note> said that an **arbitrator** and an **altar** are the same thing, for the victim of an injustice flees to both for refuge. Or if one were to say that an **anchor** and a **hook** <note>original: "kremastran," a hook for hanging pots or clothes</note> are the same; they are indeed the same kind of thing, differing only in that one acts from above and the other from below. To speak of "leveling" cities involves a similarity between things far apart: the equality of a physical surface and the equality of political powers.

Most witticisms are achieved through metaphor and by **deceiving the listener beforehand**. It becomes clearer to the listener that they have learned something because the conclusion was contrary to their expectation; the soul seems to say to itself: "How true! And yet I missed it." Among clever sayings, those are most elegant which do not mean what they literally say—such as the remark of Stesichorus <note>A lyric poet; this phrase was a warning that invaders would lay waste to the land</note> that **"the cicadas will sing to themselves from the ground."** <note>Because the trees would be cut down, leaving the cicadas no branches to sit on.</note>

Well-constructed riddles are pleasant for the same reason: they provide a process of learning and employ metaphor. This is also what Theodorus <note>A famous rhetorician known for his stylistic innovations</note> calls "speaking new things." This occurs when the conclusion is a **paradox** and not—as he puts it—in accordance with previous expectation, but rather like the distorted words used by comedians. This same effect is produced by jokes that depend on a change of a single letter, for they deceive the audience. This happens even in verse, when the listener's expectation is subverted:

> He strode along, and under his feet were... **chilblains**. <note>original: "chimetla." The audience expects "sandals" (pedila), which sounds similar in Greek.</note>

The listener thought the poet was going to say "sandals." For this to work, the meaning must be clear the moment the word is spoken. Wordplay based on a single letter does not say what the speaker seems to be saying, but rather twists a name; for example, the joke of Theodorus against Nikon the lyre-player: **"A Thracian [slave] art thou."** <note>original: "Thrattei se." This is a pun. It sounds like the phrase "It disturbs you," but by a slight shift in pronunciation, it means "You are a Thracian woman."</note> He pretends to say "It disturbs you," and thus deceives the listener, for he means something else. This is pleasant for the listener once the point is understood; however, if one does not know that Nikon is of Thracian descent, the wit will not be apparent. And the...

<summary>Aristotle explains that wit arises from "learning" through metaphors that connect distant ideas and through "deceiving" the audience's expectations via puns and paradoxes. He provides examples of how unexpected word endings and linguistic shifts (paronomasia) create a pleasing intellectual surprise.</summary>
<keywords>metaphor, Archytas, Stesichorus, Theodorus, witticisms, puns, paradox, linguistic deception, Nikon the lyre-player, learning</keywords>

<meta>This page continues Aristotle's discussion on the mechanics of wit, specifically focusing on how speakers use puns, double meanings, and unexpected turns of phrase to provide a sudden "learning" experience for the audience.</meta>

# ARISTOTLE

...you want to "Pers-ize" him <note>original: "persai." This is a pun by Theodorus mentioned on the previous page, playing on the Greek word "to destroy" and the name of the Persians.</note>. Both meanings must be stated appropriately; and the same applies to **witticisms** <term>asteia: literally "urbanities," referring to sophisticated or clever speech</term>. For instance, one might say to the Athenians that their **dominion** of the sea was not the **beginning** of their troubles, for they benefited from it. Or, as Isocrates said, that their **dominion** was the **beginning** of troubles for the city <note>original: "archē." Aristotle is playing with the double meaning of "archē," which means both "political rule/empire" and "beginning/source."</note>. In both cases, the speaker says something that one would not expect to be said, and yet it is recognized as true. To simply say that a "beginning is a beginning" is not clever; but Isocrates does not say it that way. He uses the word **beginning**, but denies the meaning one first expects, applying it in a different sense.

In all such cases, if a **homonym** <term>homonymia: a word that sounds the same but has different meanings</term> or a metaphor is introduced appropriately, the result is successful. For example, "Endurable is not endurable" <note>This likely refers to a person named Anaschetos (meaning "Endurable"); the phrase suggests the man himself is anything but endurable.</note> denies the homonym, but does so appropriately. And:

->You would not be more of a stranger than you ought to be,<-
->A stranger;<-

or "not more than you ought," which is the same thing. This implies that a guest should not always be a **stranger** <note>original: "xenos." This word means both "guest" and "stranger/foreigner."</note>, for that too is "alien." The same applies to the celebrated line by Anaxandrides <note>A Middle Comedy poet known for his clever wordplay</note>:

->It is a noble thing to die before doing anything worthy of death.<-

For this is the same as saying, "It is worthy to die when one is not worthy of death," or "It is worthy to die while not being deserving of death," or "while not doing things worthy of death." The form of the expression in these examples is the same; but the more briefly and antithetically it is stated, the more effective it becomes. The reason is that **learning** <term>mathēsis: the process of gaining new insight</term> happens more effectively because of the contrast, and more quickly because of the brevity.

There must always be present either a focus on the person addressed or a correctness of expression—if what is said is true and not superficial. For these qualities can be separated; for example, "One should die having committed no wrong." This is true, but it is not **witty**...

<summary>Aristotle explains how puns on words with double meanings, like "archē" (empire/beginning), create wit by surprising the listener with a sudden, logical insight. He emphasizes that brevity and contrast are essential for making a saying memorable and intellectually satisfying.</summary>
<keywords>Aristotle, Isocrates, Anaxandrides, wordplay, puns, homonyms, learning through contrast, Rhetoric, Greek wit</keywords>

--- Page 176 ---
<meta>Continuing Aristotle's discussion of wit and clever speech (asteia) from Book III, Chapter XI of the Rhetoric. The previous page discussed puns, double meanings, and homonyms as sources of wit. This page moves into similes (eikones) and their relationship to metaphor, with concrete examples drawn from Athenian literary and rhetorical life.</meta>

# ARISTOTLE

...the worthy man ought to marry the worthy woman — but that is not clever. It is clever only if both meanings are held at once. <note>Aristotle is making a point about wordplay: saying "the worthy should marry the worthy" is merely a moral truism. The wit emerges when the phrase simultaneously carries a double or ironic edge.</note> "He is worthy indeed of death, being one who is not worthy of death" — here the tension between the two uses of "worthy" creates the effect. The more of these elements a phrase contains, the wittier it appears: for instance, if the words are themselves metaphors, and metaphors of a particular kind, combined with **antithesis** <term>antithesis: the placing of contrasting ideas side by side for rhetorical effect</term>, and **balanced structure** <term>parisosis: a rhetorical figure in which clauses are made approximately equal in length and rhythm</term>, and if the phrase has **vividness** <term>energeia: literally "actuality" or "being-at-work"; used by Aristotle to describe language that makes things appear before the mind's eye as if in action</term>.

**Similes**, as has been noted in the earlier discussion above, always win approval and are in a sense **metaphors** — for they are always expressed in terms of two things, just like the proportional metaphor. For example: a shield, we say, is the **goblet of Ares** <term>Ares: the Greek god of war</term>; and a bow is a **lyre without strings**. To express it this way — both terms together — is not simple. But to call the bow simply a "lyre," or the shield simply a "goblet," is simple. <note>Aristotle distinguishes between a full simile ("the shield is the goblet of Ares") and a bare metaphor ("the goblet"), arguing the full form retains both comparison terms and so is richer.</note>

People also form similes in the following way: comparing a flute-player to an **ape**; or a near-sighted man to a **lamp sputtering in the rain** — for in both cases things are drawn together. <note>The humor in these comparisons comes from an unexpected but apt convergence of characteristics: the grimacing flute-player resembles a monkey; the squinting man's flickering gaze resembles a guttering flame.</note>

A simile works well when it involves **metaphor** <term>metaphora: the carrying-over of a name from one thing to another</term>. For instance: to compare a shield to the goblet of Ares; to compare a ruined building to the **rags of a house**; and to say that **Niceratus** <term>Niceratus: a real Athenian figure, apparently known for his passion for rhapsodic recitation</term> is a **Philoctetes** <term>Philoctetes: the hero of Sophocles' tragedy, who was bitten by a snake and left in filth and misery on the island of Lemnos</term> bitten by **Pratys** — as **Thrasymachus** <term>Thrasymachus: the famous sophist and rhetorician of Chalcedon, a prominent character in Plato's *Republic*</term> compared him, having seen Niceratus defeated by Pratys the rhapsode <note>A rhapsode was a professional reciter of epic poetry, especially Homer.</note> in a contest, yet still walking about with his hair long and unkempt. <note>Philoctetes, stranded and suffering, was famously disheveled; Niceratus, humiliated in a recitation contest yet stubbornly keeping his dramatic appearance, struck Thrasymachus as a comic parallel.</note>

It is precisely in such comparisons that **poets** most often fail — and also, when successful, most win glory. I mean when they produce something like:

> Just like parsley, he carries his legs curled.
> Just like **Philammon** <term>Philammon: a famous boxer of antiquity</term> sparring with the **punch-bag** <term>korykos: a leather punching bag used in athletic training</term>.

All such expressions are **similes**, and similes, as has been stated many times, are metaphors.

**Proverbs**, too, are metaphors from one species to another. For example: if someone brings a thing upon himself expecting to benefit from it and is instead harmed — as the **Carpathian** <note>The island of Carpathus, in the southeastern Aegean, was associated with this proverb.</note> says of the **hare** <note>The proverb "like the Carpathian and the hare" referred to someone who introduced rabbits to their island thinking them a benefit, only to be overrun and ruined by them — a classic tale of an imported good turning into a pest.</note> — for both parties have experienced the thing that is spoken of. <note>The point is that proverbs work rhetorically as compressed metaphors: they transfer a lesson from a particular case to a general one, illuminating the present situation by analogy.</note>

This, then, is the source from which **clever sayings** are drawn, and...

Aristotle also explains how proverbs operate as species-to-species metaphors, grounding abstract rhetorical theory in memorable, concrete cases.</summary>

<meta>Continuing from the end of Book III, Chapter XI, where Aristotle discusses the effectiveness of wit and the "bringing-before-the-eyes" (energeia) through metaphors and similes. This page concludes that discussion and moves into Chapter XII, which addresses the different styles of speech appropriate for writing versus public performance.</meta>

# ARISTOTLE

...the reason for this has already been almost entirely explained. There are also **hyperboles** <term>hyperbole: a rhetorical exaggeration not intended to be taken literally</term> that are well-regarded, which are essentially **metaphors**. For instance, of a man with a black eye, one might say: "You would have thought he was a basket of mulberries." <note>original: συκαμίνων τάλαρον (sykaminon talaron). The purple bruising of a black eye is compared to the deep red-purple color of ripe mulberries.</note> For a black eye is something reddish-purple. However, this is quite an extreme exaggeration. The expression "just like this or that" is also a hyperbole, differing only in the way it is phrased:

->Just like Philammon <note>Philammon of Scarpheia: a celebrated Olympic boxer</note> sparring with the punching-bag; you would have thought he *was* Philammon himself fighting the bag.<-

->Like curly celery <note>original: σέλινου οὖλα (selinou oula)</note> he wears his legs; I thought they were not legs, but celery, they were so curly.<-

There are also hyperboles that are juvenile, for they express a great vehemence of spirit. This is why people use them most when they are angry:

->Not if he gave me as many gifts as there are grains of sand and dust.
The daughter of Agamemnon, son of Atreus, I will not marry,
Not even if she vied in beauty with golden Aphrodite,
Or in handiwork with Athena.<-
<note>Aristotle is quoting Achilles’ famous refusal to rejoin the Trojan War from Homer's *Iliad*, Book IX. Achilles uses these hyperboles to show his absolute disdain for Agamemnon's peace offering.</note>

The Attic orators <term>Attic orators: the famous public speakers of classical Athens, such as Demosthenes or Isocrates</term> use this device most of all. Consequently, it is unseemly for an older man to speak this way.

## CHAPTER XII

We must not overlook the fact that a different style of language fits each genre of speech. For the **written style** <term>graphikē: literally "graphic style," designed for a reader to study in private</term> is not the same as the **style of public debate** <term>agōnistike: literally "competitive style," designed for oral performance in a large venue</term>; nor is the style of political oratory the same as that of the law courts. It is necessary to understand both. For the one is a matter of knowing how to speak correct Greek; the other is a matter of not being forced into silence when one wishes to communicate something to others—a fate that befalls those who do not know how to write down their thoughts. The **written style** is the most precise, whereas the **style of debate** is the most suited to delivery <term>hypokritikōtatē: literally "most actor-like," referring to the use of voice and gesture to persuade</term>. Of this latter style, there are two kinds: one is **ethical** <term>ēthikē: character-driven, intended to portray the speaker as a certain kind of person</term> and the other is **emotional** <term>pathētikē: intended to stir the passions of the listeners</term>. Therefore...

<keywords>hyperbole, Philammon, Homer, Achilles, style of writing, style of debate, ethics, emotion, Attic orators</keywords>

<meta>Continuing from the end of the previous chapter, Aristotle is transitioning from the discussion of metaphors and vivid language to Chapter XII, which distinguishes between the style of writing intended for reading and the style intended for public debate or theatrical performance.</meta>

### RHETORIC. BOOK III. CHAPTER XII.

And **actors** look for plays of this kind, just as **poets** look for such actors. On the other hand, those writers whose works are meant to be read are carried in people's hands—such as **Chaeremon**, who is as precise as a **professional speechwriter** <term>logographos: a professional who wrote speeches for others to deliver in court</term>, and **Lycymnius** among the **dithyrambic poets** <term>dithyramb: a wild, passionate choral hymn often honoring the god Dionysus</term>.

When compared, the works of the "writers" <note>Those who write for the page rather than the stage.</note> seem thin and narrow in the heat of actual competitions. Conversely, the speeches of the orators, though they were highly effective when well-spoken, seem **amateurish** when they are held in the hands for reading. The reason for this is that their style is suited specifically to the **contest** <term>agon: a public struggle or debate, such as in a court or the assembly</term>. 

For this reason, theatrical elements, when stripped of their **delivery** <term>hypokrisis: the art of acting or rhetorical delivery</term>, fail to perform their proper function and appear foolish. For example, the use of **asyndeton** <term>asyndeton: the omission of connecting words like "and" or "but" between phrases</term> and the practice of repeating the same thing over and over are rightly rejected in the written style; however, in the style of public debate, even the orators use them, for they are designed for delivery. 

When repeating the same idea, one must necessarily vary the delivery; this, in a sense, clears the path for the acting: 

->“This is the man who robbed you; this is the man who deceived you; this is the man who finally attempted to betray you.”<-

This is what the actor **Philemon** used to do, both in **Anaxandrides’** play *The Madness of Old Men*, when he says “Rhadamanthys and Palamedes,” and in the prologue of *The Pious*, when he says “I.” For if a speaker does not act out such lines, he becomes like “the man carrying the beam.” <note>original: ὁ τὸν δοκὸν φέρων. An idiom for someone who is stiff, wooden, or unnatural in their movements.</note>

It is the same with **asyndeta**: “I came, I met him, I entreated him.” For the speaker must act these out and not say them as a single, continuous phrase with the same character and tone. Furthermore, asyndeton has a unique quality: many things seem to be said in the same span of time. For a **conjunction** <note>original: σύνδεσμος (syndesmos). Aristotle views connectors like "and" as things that bind multiple ideas into a single unit.</note> makes many things into one; therefore, if it is removed, it is obvious that the opposite will happen—the one thing becomes many. 

Thus, it produces **amplification**: “I came, I conversed, I made many pleas.” The speaker seems to overlook everything they have said or are currently saying. This is also what **Homer** intends to do in the passage...

<summary>Aristotle distinguishes between the "written style" (precise and literary) and the "style of debate" (designed for performance), noting that rhetorical devices like repetition and asyndeton are effective in person but fail on the page.</summary>
<keywords>rhetoric, delivery, acting, asyndeton, performance vs reading, Chaeremon, Philemon, Anaxandrides, Homer, amplification</keywords>

<meta>Continuing from the previous page's discussion on rhetorical devices like asyndeton and repetition, Aristotle now uses a famous example from Homer’s Iliad to illustrate how repeating a name can magnify a person’s importance in the mind of the audience.</meta>

# ARISTOTLE

->“Nireus, then, from Syme;
Nireus, son of Aglaia;
Nireus, the most beautiful.”<-
<note>original: "Νιρεύς δ’ αὖ Σύμηθεν· Νιρεύς Ἀγλαΐης· Νιρεύς, ὃς κάλλιστος." These lines from Homer's Iliad (2.671–673) are the only mention of Nireus in the entire epic.</note>

For when much is said about a person, it is necessary that their name be mentioned many times. Therefore, if the name is mentioned many times, it creates the impression that much has been said. Thus, the poet magnified Nireus by mentioning him only once <note>meaning in one specific passage</note> through a **fallacy** <note>Aristotle uses the term "paralogismos" to describe this logical trick where the audience assumes quantity of mentions equals quantity of information</note>, and he created a lasting memory of him, even though he never speaks of him again later.

The style of **political oratory** <term>demegorike lexis: the style used for addressing the public assembly</term> is exactly like **impressionistic painting** <term>skiagraphia: literally "shadow-painting," a technique meant to be viewed from a distance where broad strokes create an effect that disappears up close</term>. For the larger the crowd, the more distant the view; therefore, in both cases, fine details are a waste of effort and appear worse. 

But the style of **legal oratory** <term>dike: the style used in a court of law</term> requires more precision. This is even more true when the case is heard by a **single judge**, for this is the smallest unit of rhetoric. In such a setting, it is easier to see what is relevant to the matter and what is not; the heat of the **contest** <term>agon: the competitive struggle of a debate</term> is absent, so the judgment can remain pure. 

For this reason, the same orators do not achieve fame in all these different styles; where there is the greatest need for **acting** <term>hypokrisis: the art of delivery and vocal performance</term>, there is the least amount of precision. This occurs where the voice is used, and especially where a loud voice is required.

The **ceremonial style** <term>epideiktike lexis: "display" oratory used for speeches of praise or blame</term> is the most suited for writing, for its purpose is to be read. The second most suitable for writing is the **legal style**. 

To further divide style into categories like "pleasant" and "magnificent" is a waste of effort. Why should it be these any more than "temperate," "generous," or any other moral virtue? For it is obvious that the qualities we have already mentioned will make it "pleasant," provided that the **virtue of style** has been correctly defined. For why else must it be **clear** and not **lowly**, but **appropriate**? 

If it is wordy, it will not be clear; nor will it be clear if it is too brief. It is obvious that the middle ground is what fits. And the qualities mentioned—the skillful blending of **ordinary language** and **exotic words**, the use of **rhythm**, and the **persuasiveness** that comes from being **appropriate**—will make the style pleasant. Concerning...

<summary>Aristotle compares different styles of speech to painting, noting that public speeches require broad strokes while legal and written texts require precision. He argues that a good style is naturally "pleasant" if it balances clarity, appropriateness, and rhythm.</summary>

<keywords>Aristotle, Rhetoric, Nireus, delivery, impressionism, legal oratory, political oratory, ceremonial oratory, clarity, appropriateness</keywords>

<meta>Continuing from the previous page where Aristotle discussed how certain stylistic choices, like repetition, can create a powerful impression on an audience, we now move from how things are said (style) to how a speech is organized.</meta>

RHETORICA. BOOK III. CHAPTER XIII. 167

...and so, the discussion regarding **style** has been completed, both in general terms for all types and specifically for each genre. It remains, then, to speak about **arrangement**.

## CHAPTER XIII.

A speech has two essential parts: for it is necessary to **state the matter** about which one is speaking, and then to **prove it**. Therefore, it is impossible to state a case without proving it, or to prove it without having first stated it. For one who proves, proves something; and one who states a case beforehand does so for the sake of proving it. 

Of these two parts, the first is the **statement** <term>prothesis: the initial presentation of the proposition or the facts of the case</term>, and the second is the **proof** <term>pistis: the logical arguments or evidence used to persuade the audience</term>. It is as if one were to divide the speech into the **problem** and the **demonstration**.

Nowadays, however, people make ridiculous divisions. For a **narrative** <term>diegesis: a detailed account of events</term> surely belongs only to **legal oratory** <term>dikanikos: speeches given in a court of law</term>. But in **ceremonial** <term>epideiktikos: speeches of praise or blame at public events</term> or **political oratory** <term>demegoria: speeches given in the public assembly</term>, how can there be a "narrative" in the sense they describe, or "arguments against the opponent," or an "epilogue" <note>Aristotle is criticizing other teachers of rhetoric who try to force every speech into a rigid, multi-part structure that doesn't fit every occasion.</note> for demonstrative points? 

The **introduction** <term>prooimion</term>, the **comparison** <term>antiparabole</term>, and the **recapitulation** <term>epanodos</term> only occur in political speeches when there is a formal dispute. For **accusation** <term>kategoria</term> and **defense** <term>apologia</term> often occur in those settings, but not in **deliberation** <term>symboule: the act of giving advice on future courses of action</term>—except perhaps for the conclusion. 

Furthermore, these parts are not even required for every legal speech; for example, if the speech is short or the matter is easy to remember, such parts would only add unnecessary length. Therefore, the only truly necessary parts are the **statement** and the **proof**. 

These two are the essential ones; but at most, a speech might consist of an **introduction**, a **statement**, a **proof**, and a **conclusion** <term>epilogos</term>. The arguments against an opponent are actually part of the proofs, and the "comparison" is really an amplification of one's own position, which makes it a part of the proofs (since the person doing this is proving something). The introduction and the conclusion do not prove anything; rather, they serve to remind the audience of what has been or will be said.

If one were to follow the detailed divisions made by the followers of **Theodorus** <note>Theodorus of Byzantium was a 5th-century BCE rhetorician famous for inventing many technical terms for the sub-sections of a speech</note>, one would have to include a "narrative," a "supplementary narrative," a "preliminary narrative," a "refutation," and a "supplementary refutation." But one must speak of a specific type...

<summary>Aristotle argues that a speech fundamentally only requires two parts: the statement of the case and the proof. He critiques other rhetoricians for creating overly complex and unnecessary divisions that do not apply to all types of oratory.</summary>
<keywords>arrangement, statement (prothesis), proof (pistis), narrative (diegesis), legal oratory, political oratory, Theodorus of Byzantium, structure of a speech</keywords>

--- Page 181 ---
<meta>Continuing from the previous page, Aristotle is finishing his critique of rhetoricians who create overly complex and unnecessary divisions of a speech. He now transitions into a detailed analysis of the first major part of any discourse: the introduction.</meta>

...naming and distinguishing the parts. If one does not do this, the terminology becomes empty and nonsensical—much like *Lycymnius* does in his manual <note>Lycymnius of Chios was a 5th-century BCE rhetorician known for using flowery and often confusing technical terms.</note>, when he coins names such as “wafting,” “wandering off,” and “branches.”

### CHAPTER XIV.

The **introduction** <term>prooimion: the opening of a speech, from the Greek words meaning "before the song"</term> is the beginning of a speech; it is what the “prologue” is in poetry and the “prelude” is in flute-playing. For all these are beginnings and, as it were, a **clearing of the path** <note>original Greek: "odopoiesis"</note> for what is to come. 

The musical prelude is similar to the introductions used in **ceremonial speeches** <term>epideiktikos: speeches of praise or blame delivered at public festivals or funerals</term>. Just as flute-players, after playing whatever opening piece they have mastered, connect it to the “keynote” <note>original Greek: "endosimon" — the opening note or signal given by a lead performer to set the pitch for the rest of the ensemble</note>, so too should one write in ceremonial speeches. The speaker should say whatever he wishes at the start, then strike the keynote and link it to the main body of the speech. 

Everyone follows this pattern, using the introduction of *Isocrates’* *Helen* as an example; for there is nothing in common between the “eristics” <note>Philosophers or speakers who engage in competitive, hair-splitting arguments</note> and the character of *Helen*. At the same time, if the speaker moves away from the primary subject <note>original Greek: "ektopise" — to go out of place or digress</note>, it is fitting so that the entire speech does not seem to be of a single, repetitive type.

The introductions for ceremonial speeches are generally drawn from **praise** or **blame**. For instance, *Gorgias* begins his Olympic speech with: **“You are worthy of being admired by many, O men of Greece”**; he is praising those who brought together the great national assemblies. *Isocrates*, on the other hand, uses blame, criticizing the fact that while people have honored physical excellence with great rewards, they have established no prize for those who possess wisdom.

Introductions can also be drawn from **advice**, such as the idea that one should honor good men—which is why the author praises *Aristides*—or those who are neither famous nor wicked, but are good men who remain obscure, such as *Alexander* <note>Also known as Paris, the son of King Priam of Troy</note>, the son of *Priam*. This last example is used when giving advice.

Furthermore, introductions may be drawn from the techniques used in **legal oratory** <term>dikanikos: speeches given in a court of law, either for the prosecution or the defense</term>. These are derived from things relating to the audience—specifically if the speech concerns something that is unexpected, or difficult, or...

<summary>Aristotle defines the "prooimion" (introduction) by comparing it to musical preludes and poetic prologues, explaining how ceremonial speakers use praise or blame to lead into their main topic. He emphasizes that the introduction serves as a path-clearing exercise to prepare the audience for the core arguments.</summary>
<keywords>prooimion, introduction, ceremonial oratory, epideictic, Gorgias, Isocrates, musical analogy, praise and blame, legal oratory</keywords>

--- Page 182 ---
<meta>Continuing from the previous page, Aristotle explains that the introduction (prooimion) serves as a "keynote" to link the speaker's initial remarks to the main subject. He is currently illustrating how this technique is used across different genres of performance and speech.</meta>

...as is commonly said by many, so that it may be excused; like **Choerilus** <note>Choerilus of Samos was an epic poet of the 5th century BCE; he famously complained that the "field of poetry" had already been fully harvested by earlier poets like Homer.</note> wrote:

> “But now, when all has been shared out.”

The introductions of **ceremonial speeches** <term>epideiktikos: speeches of praise or blame delivered at public festivals or funerals</term>, then, are derived from these sources: from praise, from blame, from exhortation, from dissuasion, or from matters directed toward the listener. These **starting notes** <note>original Greek: "endosima" — the pitch or signal given by a lead musician to an ensemble</note> for the speech must be either related or unrelated to the subject. 

Regarding the introductions of **courtroom speeches** <term>dikanikos: forensic or judicial rhetoric used in legal settings</term>, one must understand that they serve the same purpose as the prologues of dramas and the introductions of epic poems. For the introductions of **dithyrambs** <note>Dithyrambs were passionate choral hymns and dances performed in honor of the god Dionysus.</note> are similar to those of ceremonial speeches:

> “Because of you and your gifts, or the spoils...”

In speeches and epic poems, the introduction is a **sample** <note>original Greek: "deigma"</note> of the subject, so that the audience may see in advance what the discourse is about and their minds may not be left hanging; for whatever is indefinite leads the mind to wander. The speaker who places the beginning into the listener’s hand, as it were, enables them to follow the story. For this reason, Homer wrote:

> “Sing, goddess, of the wrath.” <note>The opening line of the *Iliad*.</note>
> “Tell me, Muse, of the man.” <note>The opening line of the *Odyssey*.</note>
> “Lead me to another tale, how from the land of Asia a great war came to Europe.” <note>A line from an epic poem, likely by Choerilus, regarding the Persian Wars.</note>

Tragic poets also make the subject of the drama clear, even if not immediately. Euripides does this almost at once, but Sophocles also makes it clear somewhere in the prologue; for example:

> “My father was Polybus.” <note>A reference to the opening scene of Sophocles' *Oedipus Rex*.</note>

Comedy does the same. Thus, the most necessary and specific function of the introduction is to make clear what the **purpose** <term>telos: the end goal or final objective</term> of the speech is. Therefore, if the matter is already clear or of minor importance, an introduction should not be used. The other types of introductions used by speakers are merely **remedies** <note>original Greek: "iatreumata" — therapeutic measures or "medical treatments" for an audience that might be bored, hostile, or distracted.</note> and are common to all parts of the speech.

<summary>Aristotle explains that the primary purpose of a speech's introduction is to provide a "preview" of the subject to prevent the audience's attention from wandering. He compares the structure of rhetorical introductions to those found in epic poetry, tragedy, and music.</summary>
<keywords>prooimion, introduction, forensic rhetoric, epideictic rhetoric, Homer, Sophocles, Euripides, purpose of speech</keywords>

->ARISTOTLE<-

...having stirred the audience's interest beforehand, he begins immediately: "Elis, happy city." <note>original: "Elis, polis eudaimōn." Elis was a city-state in the Peloponnese, famous as the administrator of the Olympic Games.</note>

## CHAPTER XV. <note>original Latin: "CAPUT XV"</note>

Regarding **prejudice** <term>diabole: translated here as prejudice or slander; in Greek rhetoric, it refers to any hostile impression or false accusation an opponent creates in the minds of the audience</term>, one method is to use those arguments by which one might dispel a disagreeable suspicion; for it makes no difference whether the suspicion was voiced by someone or not, so this rule is universal. Another way is to meet the points of dispute by showing either that the act did not happen, or that it was not harmful—or at least not to this person—or that it was not so great as alleged, or not unjust, or not significant, or not shameful, or of no great importance. For these are the points on which a dispute turns. 

An example is **Iphicrates** <note>Iphicrates was a celebrated Athenian general known for his tactical innovations.</note> against **Nausicrates**; for he admitted that he did what the other said and that he caused harm, but he denied that he acted unjustly. Or one may offer a trade-off if one has done something unjust: if the act was harmful, it was at least noble; if it was painful, it was at least beneficial, or something of that sort. 

Another method is to argue that the act was a **mistake** <term>hamartēma: a fault or error in judgment</term>, or an **accident** <term>atychema: a stroke of ill-fortune beyond one's control</term>, or a matter of **necessity**. For instance, **Sophocles** <note>This likely refers to the tragic playwright Sophocles, who was active in Athenian public life even in his eighties.</note> said he trembled, not as his accuser claimed—to appear like a frail old man—but out of necessity; for it was not by his own choice that he was eighty years old. 

One can also "trade off" the motive, arguing that one did not intend to cause harm, but intended this other thing instead, and that the harm complained of was merely an accidental byproduct. He might say it is only just to hate him if he had acted specifically so that this harm would happen. 

Another method is to see if the accuser themselves is involved, either now or in the past, or those close to them. Another is to see if others are included in the charge who are admittedly not guilty of the slander; for instance, if so-and-so is "pure" of the crime of adultery, then surely this person [and that person are also innocent]. 

Another is to check if the accuser has slandered others, or if others slandered the defendant, or if people were suspected without formal slander (just as the defendant is now) and were later shown to be innocent. Another method is to counter-slander the accuser; for it is absurd if the accuser themselves is untrustworthy, yet their words are to be believed.

<summary>Aristotle outlines various rhetorical strategies for dispelling slander and prejudice in a courtroom, suggesting that a speaker can deny the act, justify its intent, or discredit the accuser.</summary>
<keywords>rhetoric, slander, prejudice, legal defense, Iphicrates, Sophocles, motive, Athenian law</keywords>

--- Page 184 ---
<meta>Continuing the discussion from Chapter 15 on ways to remove prejudice or slander. Aristotle explores how past trials and the interpretation of motives can be used to defend oneself or attack an opponent, before transitioning to the rules of narrative in Chapter 16.</meta>

...this person's words will be credible. Another method is to appeal to a previous **judgment** <note>original: "krisis." Aristotle suggests using a prior legal or social decision to support one's case.</note>; as **Euripides** did against **Hygiainon** in the trial regarding the **Exchange of Properties** <term>Antidosis: a legal process in Athens where a wealthy citizen challenged another to either take over a costly public service or exchange all their property with them</term>. When Hygiainon accused him of impiety, claiming he encouraged perjury by writing:

> My tongue has sworn, but my mind remains unsworn. <note>original: "hē glōss' omōmoch', hē de phrēn anōmotos." This is a famous line from Euripides' play *Hippolytus*.</note>

Euripides replied that Hygiainon was acting unjustly by bringing judgments from the **Dionysiac contest** <note>The theatrical competitions held during the festival of Dionysus, where plays were judged by a specific panel.</note> into the law courts; for there he had already given an account of his words, or would give one if the other wished to accuse him. 

Another method is to attack the act of **slander** <note>original: "diabolē"</note> itself, showing how great an evil it is, and specifically that it creates "other trials" <note>Meaning it drags in irrelevant issues or forces the jury to judge the speaker's character rather than the facts of the case.</note>. A topic common to both the accuser and the defender is to speak of "signs" or symbols. For example, in the play *Teucer*, **Odysseus** argues that Teucer is a relative of **Priam** <note>The King of Troy, the enemy of the Greeks.</note>, because **Hesione** was his sister. But Teucer argues that his father, **Telamon**, was an enemy to Priam, and that he did not betray the spies to the Trojans. 

Another method, for the one slandering, is to praise a little and then blame at length, or to praise many great things and then blame one significant thing concisely, or to put forward many good qualities and then blame the one thing that actually bears upon the case. Such speakers are the most "artful" and the most unjust; for they attempt to cause harm by using good qualities, mixing them with the bad. 

It is common to both the slanderer and the one clearing himself—since the same action can be performed for many different reasons—that the slanderer must interpret the motive maliciously, taking it in the worse sense; whereas the one clearing himself must interpret it in the better sense. For example, when **Diomedes** chose **Odysseus** to accompany him: one might say he did so because he considered Odysseus the best of men; but another might say it was not for that reason, but because Odysseus was the only one who was not a rival, being so inferior. And let this be enough regarding the subject of **prejudice**.

## CHAPTER XVI. <note>original Latin: "CAPUT XVI"</note>

**Narrative** <term>diegesis: the part of a speech where the speaker recounts the events or facts of the case</term> in **ceremonial speeches** <term>epideictic: a branch of rhetoric used for praise or blame, such as funeral orations or festival speeches</term> is not continuous, but proceeds in parts. For one must [recount] the actions...

<meta>Continuing the discussion from the previous page regarding the "non-artistic" and "artistic" elements of a speech, Aristotle now moves into Chapter 16 of Book III, focusing on the narrative or the statement of facts (diegesis).</meta>

### ARISTOTELIS

...to go through the elements from which the speech is composed. For the speech consists of one part that is **non-artistic** <term>Non-artistic (atechnon): facts or evidence that the speaker does not "create" through skill, but simply employs, such as witnesses or laws.</term>—since the speaker is not at all responsible for the actions themselves—and another part that comes from **art** <note>original: "ek tēs technēs." This refers to the speaker's rhetorical skill in presenting those facts.</note>. This artistic part involves showing either that a fact exists (if it is doubted), or its quality, its quantity, or even all of these together. 

For this reason, one should sometimes not narrate everything in sequence, because it is difficult to remember the proof when it is presented in that way. "From these facts, he is shown to be brave; from these others, wise or just." This kind of speech is simpler, whereas the other [sequential] kind is complex and not plain. One should merely remind the audience of well-known facts; for this reason, many subjects do not require a **narrative** <term>Narrative (diegesis): the section of a speech where the speaker recounts the facts of the case to the audience.</term> at all. For example, if you wish to praise **Achilles**, everyone knows his deeds; you only need to make use of them. But if you are speaking of **Critias** <note>Critias was a leading member of the "Thirty Tyrants" in Athens. Unlike the legendary Achilles, the specific details of his life or a particular case involving him would require a formal retelling.</note>, you must provide a narrative, for not many people know of him.

** Now, it is currently said, quite ridiculously, that the narrative should be "fast." This is like the man who, when the baker asked him whether he should knead the dough hard or soft, replied: "What? Is it impossible to do it well?" It is the same here. For one should not narrate at great length, just as one should not make a long introduction or present proofs at great length. For excellence here does not consist in being "fast" or "concise," but in **moderation** <note>This reflects Aristotle’s famous "Golden Mean"—finding the appropriate balance between extremes.</note>. This means saying as much as will make the matter clear, or will make the audience believe that the event happened, or that harm was done, or that an injustice was committed, or that the events are of whatever magnitude you wish the audience to believe. For the opponent, you should do the exact opposite.

One should also slip into the narrative <note>original: "paradiēgeisthai." Literally to "narrate alongside" or interweave minor details into the main story.</note> whatever contributes to one's own virtue. For example: "I was always advising him to do what was right, telling him not to abandon his children." Or, you can highlight the other person’s vice: "But he answered me that wherever he was himself, there would be other children." This is the reply that **Herodotus** <note>A reference to Herodotus, *Histories* 2.30, regarding Egyptian soldiers who deserted their king and cynically claimed they could easily replace their families elsewhere.</note> says the revolting Egyptians gave. Or, include whatever is pleasant to the judges. For a defendant, the narrative is usually shorter. The points of dispute...

<summary>Aristotle argues that the "narrative" of a speech should not be judged by its speed or brevity, but by its effectiveness in making the case clear and establishing character. He suggests that well-known facts require only a reminder, while less familiar subjects require a full explanation.</summary>
<keywords>rhetoric, diegesis, narrative, Achilles, Critias, moderation, Herodotus, persuasion, character, virtue</keywords>

--- Page 186 ---
<meta>Continuing from the previous page, Aristotle is discussing the narrative (diegesis) of a speech. He has just dismissed the idea that a narrative must always be "fast," arguing instead for "appropriateness." He now transitions into how to handle facts that are either disputed or admitted, and how to infuse the narrative with character.</meta>

### RHETORIC. BOOK III. CHAPTER XVI.

->RHETORICA. LIB. III. CAP. XVI.<-

...either that the deed did not happen, or that it was not harmful, or not unjust, or not so significant; therefore, one should not dwell on what is already agreed upon, unless it contributes to one of these points—for instance, admitting that a deed was done, but arguing it was not **unjust**. Furthermore, one should recount past events only if, by not being described as they happened, they fail to move the audience to pity or indignation. An example is the **Story of Alcinous** <note>original: "Alkinou apologos." This refers to Odysseus’s long account of his travels. Aristotle notes that in Homer's *Odyssey*, Odysseus later summarizes these many books of adventures for his wife **Penelope** in only sixty lines.</note>, which was told to **Penelope** in sixty verses. Another example is how **Phayllus** treated the Epic Cycle, or the prologue in the play *Oeneus*.

The narrative should also be **ethical** <term>Ethical (ēthikēn): In this context, it means the speech should reveal the "character" or moral nature of the person speaking.</term>. This is achieved if we know what creates **character** <note>original: "ēthos."</note>. One way is to make **moral purpose** <term>Moral purpose (proairesis): A deliberate choice that reveals a person's values; it is not just what someone does, but why they choose to do it.</term> clear; the quality of the character is determined by the quality of this purpose. The purpose, in turn, is defined by its end goal. This is why mathematical discourses have no "character," because they have no moral purpose—they do not deal with the "purpose for which" <note>original: "hou heneka," or the "final cause." Math deals with proofs, not human motivations.</note> something is done. Socratic dialogues, however, do have character, for they discuss such human matters.

Other "ethical" traits are those that naturally follow each character type; for example, "he went on talking while he walked," which reveals a certain **boldness** and **rudeness** of character. Also, one should speak not as if from the intellect—as is the current fashion—but as if from moral choice: "I wanted this, for I had chosen it; and even if it brought me no profit, it is better this way." For the former belongs to a **prudent** person, while the latter belongs to a **good** person. Prudence consists in pursuing the **useful**, while goodness consists in pursuing the **noble**.

If a statement seems unbelievable, you must then provide the reason. Sophocles provides an example of this in his play ***Antigone***, where she says she cared more for her brother than for a husband or children; for if those are lost, they might be replaced, but:

> With mother and father gone to Hades, 
> No brother could ever be born again.

If you have no specific reason to give, you should at least state that you "know it sounds unbelievable, but that is simply the kind of person you are"—for people generally do not believe someone would act without an underlying motive.

<summary>Aristotle explains that a narrative should focus only on disputed facts and must be "ethical," revealing the speaker's moral purpose (proairesis) and character (ethos) rather than just their intellect.</summary>
<keywords>narrative (diegesis), character (ethos), moral purpose (proairesis), Alcinous, Penelope, Sophocles, Antigone, rhetoric</keywords>

--- Page 187 ---
<meta>The previous page discussed how to make a narrative "ethical" by showing the speaker’s moral purpose. Aristotle noted that mathematical discourses lack character because they lack a moral goal. This page continues the discussion on using emotional cues and character traits to make a narrative persuasive before transitioning into the nature of proofs.</meta>

### ARISTOTLE

...acting willingly, except for what is to one’s own advantage <note>The text continues the thought that people do not usually act against their interests unless there is a specific moral choice involved</note>. Furthermore, one should speak from the emotions, recounting both the consequences and the signs that the audience recognizes, as well as the traits that belong specifically to oneself or to the opponent. For example: "And he went away, giving me a sidelong glance" <note>original: "hypoblepsas." A vivid description of a suspicious or hostile look</note>. And as Aeschines says of Cratylus <note>Cratylus was a philosopher known for his belief that everything is in flux; Aeschines here describes his physical agitation</note>, that he was "hissing and shaking his hands." These details are persuasive because these signs that the audience knows become tokens of the things they do not yet know. Many such examples can be found in the works of **Homer**:

> "Thus he spoke, and the old woman covered her face with her hands." <note>A reference to Odysseus’s nurse, Eurycleia, in the *Odyssey*</note>

For those who are beginning to weep typically cover their eyes. You should immediately introduce yourself as a certain kind of person, so that the audience views both you and your opponent in a specific light—but do this without being noticed. That this is easy to do can be seen from those who bring reports; even though we know nothing of the events, we still form some sort of impression of the messenger. One must use narrative in many places throughout a speech, and sometimes even at the beginning.

In public speaking <note>referring to "deliberative" oratory in a political assembly</note>, there is very least narrative, because no one narrates the future. But if there is a narrative, it will be of things that have already happened, so that by being reminded of them, the listeners might deliberate better about the future—either by using those past events to attack someone or to praise them. However, in such cases, the speaker is not performing the specific work of a counselor <note>Aristotle distinguishes between the role of a political adviser and a storyteller</note>. If a point seems unbelievable, you must promise to give the reason immediately and arrange your explanation as the audience desires. For example, the **Jocasta** of **Carcinus** <note>Carcinus was a Greek tragic poet; this version of the Oedipus myth is lost to us except for these fragments</note> in his *Oedipus* always makes such promises when the seeker asks about her son. So too does the **Haemon** of **Sophocles**.

### CHAPTER XVII.

**Proofs** <term>Proofs (pisteis): The logical or emotional means by which a speaker persuades an audience.</term> must be demonstrative. One must provide demonstration because the dispute usually concerns one of four points; the proof must be brought to bear specifically on the point being contested.

<summary>Aristotle explains how to use emotional cues and vivid descriptions to reveal character in a narrative, then discusses why narratives are rare in political speeches. He concludes by introducing the formal requirements for rhetorical proofs.</summary>

<keywords>Aristotle, Rhetoric, character, emotions, Homer, Aeschines, public speaking, Jocasta, Sophocles, proofs, pisteis</keywords>

--- Page 188 ---
<meta>This page continues Aristotle’s discussion on the arrangement of a speech, specifically moving from the narrative portion to the "proofs" or arguments. In the previous page, he discussed how to make a narrative reveal character and emotion. Here, he begins Chapter 17, focusing on how the nature of the proof changes depending on the branch of rhetoric being used (judicial, epideictic, or deliberative) and warning against the over-reliance on formal logic when it might alienate the audience.</meta>

### RHETORIC. BOOK III. CHAPTER XVII. 177

For instance, if a person disputes that an action occurred, then during the trial, they must focus their **proof** primarily on this point. If they admit the act but dispute that it caused "harm," they must prove that; or if the harm was less than claimed, or if the act was "justified." <note>Aristotle refers here to the "stasis" theory—the specific point at issue in a legal case, such as Fact, Definition, Quality, or Jurisdiction.</note> Similarly, the same applies if the dispute is about whether the event actually took place. 

One must not forget that in this specific type of dispute <note>the dispute over whether a fact occurred</note>, it is necessary that one of the two parties is acting wickedly. In such a case, ignorance cannot be blamed, as it might be if the parties were merely disputing whether an action was "just." Therefore, one should spend more time on this point of fact; in other cases, one need not. 

In **epideictic** <note>ceremonial or "display" oratory, such as a funeral oration or a speech of praise</note> speeches, for the most part, the "amplification" will be used to show that actions are noble and useful. In these cases, the facts themselves are usually taken on trust; proofs are rarely brought for them unless the actions are hard to believe or if someone else has been blamed for them. 

In **deliberative** <note>political or assembly</note> speeches, a speaker might dispute that a future event will happen, or argue that while what is proposed *will* happen, it will not be "just," or not "useful," or not as important as claimed. One must also watch for any lies the opponent tells regarding matters outside the main issue; for these appear as evidence <note>original: "tekmeria," meaning signs or necessary proofs</note> that they are lying about the rest of the case as well.

**Examples** <note>original: "paradeigmata"</note> are most suited for deliberative speeches, while **enthymemes** <note>a form of rhetorical syllogism or logical deduction based on probabilities</note> are better suited for judicial speeches. Deliberative speech concerns the future, and so we must necessarily draw examples from things that have happened in the past. Judicial speech, however, concerns what is or is not the case, which allows for more direct demonstration and necessity—for what has already happened is fixed by necessity.

One should not deliver enthymemes one after another in a continuous string, but should mix them in with other material. If you do not, they damage one another, for there is a limit even to quantity. 

> "O friend, since you have said as much as a wise man would say." <note>original: "tosa... hosa." Aristotle quotes Homer, *Odyssey* 4.204. Menelaus is praising the young Peisistratus for speaking with the right *amount* of wisdom, not just the right *kind*.</note>

Notice he says "as much" (*tosa*), but not "such things" (*toiauta*). Furthermore, do not seek an enthymeme for every single point; if you do, you will act like some of those who practice philosophy, who use logic to prove things that are already better known and more certain than the very arguments they use to prove them. And when you are trying to evoke emotion...

<summary>Aristotle explains how the type of evidence (examples vs. logical deductions) should vary based on whether a speech is political, legal, or ceremonial. He cautions against using too much formal logic, which can confuse the audience or state the obvious.</summary>

# ARISTOTELIS
### RHETORIC. BOOK III. CHAPTER XVII (CONTINUED). 178

...do not state an <term>enthymeme</term> <note>original: "enthymema" — a rhetorical argument based on logic or probability rather than absolute certainty</note>; for it will either drive out the emotion, or the enthymeme will have been spoken in vain. For simultaneous movements drive each other out and either vanish or become weak. Nor, when the speech aims for an ethical tone, should one seek to use an enthymeme at the same time; for a logical proof has neither <term>character</term> <note>original: "ethos"</note> nor moral purpose. 

One should use <term>maxims</term> <note>original: "gnomai" — short, general statements of wisdom or truth</note> both in the narrative and in the proof; for this conveys character. For example: "And I have given, even knowing this, that one ought not to trust." If speaking with <term>passion</term> <note>original: "pathetikos" — referring to the appeal of pathos or emotion</note>: "And I do not regret it, although I have been wronged; for while the profit remains with him, justice remains with me." 

Public speaking <note>deliberative or political oratory</note> is harder than pleading in court <note>judicial oratory</note>, and naturally so, because it deals with the future. In court, one deals with the past, which is already known even to diviners, as Epimenides the Cretan said—for he did not prophesy about things to come, but about things that had already happened but were obscure. Moreover, the law serves as the starting premise in judicial cases; and once one has a starting point, it is easier to find a proof. 

Deliberative rhetoric does not allow for many diversions—such as attacking an opponent, speaking about oneself, or aiming to create emotion. Indeed, these should be used least of all, unless the speaker departs from the main subject. If you find yourself at a loss for arguments, you should do what the orators in Athens do, and what Isocrates does; for even while advising, he manages to accuse—for instance, he accuses the Spartans in his *Panegyricus*, and Chares in the speech *On the Peace*. 

In **epideictic** <note>ceremonial or display</note> speeches, one should weave praise into the speech as episodes, just as Isocrates does; for he is always introducing some new person. This is what Gorgias meant when he said that "speech never fails him"; for if he speaks of Achilles, he praises Peleus, then Aeacus, then the goddess <note>Achilles' mother, Thetis</note>. Likewise, he might praise courage, or this and that quality. This is the nature of the technique. Therefore, if you have logical proofs, you should speak both ethically and demonstratively.

<s>

<summary>Aristotle explains that logical arguments (enthymemes) can undermine emotional appeals and should be balanced with character-driven maxims. He also compares the difficulty of political oratory, which predicts the future, with judicial oratory, which analyzes the past.</summary>
<keywords>enthymeme, ethos, pathos, maxims, Epimenides, Isocrates, Gorgias, epideictic rhetoric, judicial rhetoric, deliberative rhetoric</keywords>

<meta>This page continues the discussion of rhetorical proofs, specifically how to structure arguments when logical proofs are lacking and how to handle an opponent's existing arguments. It bridges the gap between the formal "proof" (pistis) and the use of "character" (ethos) in persuasion.</meta>

# RHETORIC. BOOK III. CHAPTER XVII.
<sig>M 2</sig>

If you lack <term>enthymemes</term> <note>original: "enthymemata" — logical arguments based on probability</note>, speak with an <term>ethical tone</term> <note>original: "ethikos" — focusing on the speaker's character and moral standing</note>; for it is more fitting for a virtuous person to appear good than for his speech to be logically precise. Of the various enthymemes, those that <term>refute</term> <note>original: "elenktika" — arguments used to disprove an opponent</note> are more highly regarded than those that <term>demonstrate</term> <note>original: "deiktika" — arguments used to prove one's own case</note>; this is because anything that creates a refutation makes it more obvious that a logical conclusion has been reached, as opposites are more easily recognized when placed side-by-side. 

Arguments directed against an opponent are not a different category of speech; rather, they are part of the <term>proofs</term> <note>original: "pisteis"</note>—some are used to dismantle the opposition by objection, others by counter-deduction. In both <term>deliberative</term> <note>political oratory</note> and <term>judicial</term> <note>courtroom oratory</note> speeches, one should generally present one's own proofs first, and then meet the opposing arguments by dismantling them and pulling them apart beforehand. 

However, if the opposition’s case is particularly multi-faceted, one should address the opposing arguments first—just as Kallistratos <note>A famous 4th-century BCE Athenian orator and statesman</note> did in the Messenian assembly; for he first cleared away what they were going to say before speaking his own part. If you are speaking second, you must first address the opponent's speech by dismantling and counter-arguing it, especially if it was well-received. For just as the mind does not welcome a person who has already been prejudiced against, it feels the same about a speech if the opponent seems to have spoken well. One must therefore "make room" in the listener for the speech that is about to follow. This is achieved by clearing away the opponent's points; therefore, after fighting against either all, or the most important, or the most popular, or the most easily refuted points of the opponent, you should then make your own points credible.

> I will first become an ally to the goddesses;
> For I, Hera— <note>Aristotle is quoting Euripides' play "The Trojan Women," lines 969-971, where Helen defends herself by first addressing the silliest arguments of her accuser.</note>

In these lines, the speaker first touched upon the most foolish point. So much, then, for the subject of proofs. As for <term>character</term> <note>original: "ethos"</note>: since saying certain things about oneself might be seen as envious, or lead to long-windedness, or invite contradiction—and saying them about someone else might be seen as abusive or boorish—one should put the words in the mouth of another speaker. This is what Isocrates <note>A famous Athenian teacher of rhetoric</note> does in...

<summary>Aristotle explains that refuting an opponent's argument is often more effective than simply proving one's own, especially if the opponent has already made a strong impression. He also introduces the technique of using a third party to deliver praise or criticism to avoid appearing arrogant or rude.</summary>
<keywords>refutation, Kallistratos, ethos, character appeal, Euripides, Isocrates, courtroom oratory, political speech, making room for arguments</keywords>
