ARISTOTLE

...to Philip, and in the *Antidosis*; and as Archilochus <note>A Greek lyric poet known for his sharp, satirical iambic verse</note> rebukes; for he represents the father speaking about his daughter in the iambic poem:

->**Of money**<-
> Nothing is unexpected, nor is anything to be sworn impossible. <

And he represents Charon the carpenter in the iambic poem whose beginning is:

> The wealth of Gyges is nothing to me. <

And as Sophocles represents Haemon speaking on behalf of Antigone to his father <note>Creon, the King of Thebes</note>, as if he were reporting the words of others. One must also transform <term>enthymemes</term> <note>logical arguments based on probability</note> and sometimes make them into <term>maxims</term> <note>original: "gnomas" — general truths or aphorisms</note>; for example: "Prudent people should settle their differences while they are successful; for in this way they would gain the greatest advantage."

To put it in the form of an <term>enthymeme</term>: "If it is necessary to settle differences when the reconciliation is most beneficial and advantageous, then people should settle them while they are successful."

# CAPUT XVIII.
## CHAPTER XVIII.

Regarding <term>interrogation</term> <note>original: "erotesis" — the rhetorical technique of asking questions to trap an opponent</note>, it is opportune to use it, most especially when the opponent has already stated one thing such that, if one more question is asked, an absurdity results. For example, Pericles <note>The famous Athenian statesman</note> questioned Lampon <note>A prominent soothsayer and priest</note> concerning the initiation rites of the Savior Goddesses. When Lampon replied that it was not possible for the uninitiated to hear of them, Pericles asked if Lampon knew them himself. When he said he did, Pericles asked: "And how is that, since you are uninitiated?" <note>Pericles traps the priest: if the rites are secret, even admitting knowledge to an outsider is a contradiction of that secrecy, or he exposes a flaw in the priest's own standing.</note>

Secondly, use it when one premise is obvious and it is clear that the opponent will grant the second to the questioner. Having learned the one premise, one should not ask about the obvious one, but simply state the conclusion. For example, when Meletus denied that Socrates believed in the gods, Socrates asked if he [Socrates] spoke of anything "divine." When Meletus agreed, Socrates asked if "divine beings" <note>original: "daimones"</note> were not either children of gods or something divine. When Meletus said yes, Socrates said: "Is there anyone, then, who thinks there are children of gods..." <note>Socrates uses logic to show that if he believes in "divine things," he must logically believe in "gods," contradicting Meletus’s accusation of atheism.</note>

<summary>Aristotle discusses using third-party voices and maxims to soften arguments, then introduces techniques for rhetorical interrogation to expose contradictions in an opponent's logic.</summary>

<keywords>interrogation, maxims, Archilochus, Sophocles, Pericles, Socrates, enthymeme, rhetoric, logic</keywords>

<meta>This page continues the discussion on rhetorical interrogation (erotesis). The previous page ended with Pericles questioning the soothsayer Lampon about the secrecy of initiation rites.</meta>

->RHETORIC. BOOK III. CHAPTER XVIII.<-

...but the gods no? <note>This phrase completes the anecdote from the previous page where Lampon is trapped by a question regarding the gods.</note> **Furthermore**, [interrogation should be used] when one is about to show the opponent saying contradictory things, or something paradoxical. **Fourthly**, when there is no other way to answer except in a sophistical <note>using clever but fallacious arguments</note> manner; for if the opponent answers, "In a way it is, and in a way it is not," or "some parts yes, and some parts no," or "in one respect yes, but in another no," the audience boos them as if they were at a loss.

**Otherwise**, do not attempt it; for if the opponent successfully objects, you will seem to have been defeated. For it is not possible to ask many questions because of the inherent weakness of the listener. **Therefore**, one must compress <term>enthymemes</term> <note>logical arguments based on probability or signs rather than certainties</note> as much as possible.

**To answer**, one must respond to ambiguous questions by making distinctions through logic, and not by being too brief. To those things that seem contradictory, one should offer the solution immediately in the response, before the opponent asks the next question or draws a <term>syllogism</term> <note>original: "syllogisasthai" — a logical conclusion drawn from premises</note>; for it is not difficult to foresee where the argument is headed. **This** point, as well as the nature of the solutions, should be clear to us from the *Topics* <note>original: "topikon" — Aristotle's earlier work on the art of dialectical reasoning and debate</note>.

**And** when drawing a conclusion, if the opponent frames the <term>conclusion</term> <note>original: "symperasma"</note> as a question, one should state the reason. For example, **Sophocles** <note>The famous playwright, who here acts in his capacity as a political commissioner</note> was asked by **Peisander** if he had voted, like the other commissioners, to establish the Council of the Four Hundred <note>The oligarchic council that briefly overthrew the Athenian democracy in 411 BC</note>. He said, "Yes." "What then?" said Peisander, "did these measures not seem wicked to you?" "Yes," he replied. "Therefore, did you not do these wicked things?" "Yes," he replied, "for there was no better alternative."

And like the **Laconian** <note>A man from Sparta</note> who was undergoing an audit of his term in the <term>ephorate</term> <note>original: "ephoreias" — a high executive office in the Spartan government</note>. When asked if he thought the others had been put to death justly, he said "Yes." "Therefore," the other said, "did you not establish the same laws as they did?" And when he admitted it, the other said, "Then would it not be just for you to be put to death as well?" "Certainly not," he replied. "For they did those things because they had taken money, but I did not; I did it because it was my judgment."

**Therefore**, one should not continue questioning after the conclusion, nor should one put the conclusion itself in the form of a question, unless the truth is overwhelmingly in your favor.

**Regarding** wit <note>original: "geloion" — the laughable or the ridiculous</note>, since it seems to have some use in public debates, and as **Gorgias** <note>A famous Sophist and teacher of rhetoric</note> said, one should...

<summary>This page discusses the strategic use of interrogation (erotesis) in rhetoric, providing rules for when to trap an opponent with questions and how to answer them. Aristotle uses historical examples of political questioning to illustrate how to handle accusations and logical traps.</summary>

<meta>This page continues the discussion of rhetorical strategy, transitioning from how to handle an opponent's interrogation and humor to the final section of a speech: the epilogue.</meta>

->ARISTOTLE<-

...as Gorgias <note>original: "Gorgias" — a famous Greek Sophist and rhetorician</note> said, one should destroy the opponent's seriousness with laughter, and their laughter with seriousness—a correct observation. We have already stated how many types of humor there are in the *Poetics* <note>original: "peri poietikes" — Aristotle's famous treatise on literature; the section on comedy is now lost</note>. Some of these are suitable for a gentleman <note>original: "eleuthero" — literally a 'free man,' referring to someone of noble character and education</note>, and some are not. Therefore, one must choose the type of humor that is appropriate to one's own character. 

**Irony** <term>irony</term> <note>original: "eironeia" — here meaning a self-deprecating or subtle humor</note> is more gentlemanly than **buffoonery** <term>buffoonery</term> <note>original: "bomolochias" — crude, slapstick, or vulgar joking</note>; for the person using irony creates a joke for their own sake, whereas the buffoon does so for the sake of others.

## CHAPTER XIX.

The **epilogue** <term>epilogue</term> <note>original: "epilogos" — the conclusion or "outro" of a speech</note> is composed of four elements:
1. Making the audience well-disposed toward yourself and ill-disposed toward the opponent.
2. Amplification <note>making things seem more significant</note> and minimization <note>making things seem less significant</note>.
3. Stirring the audience into various emotions.
4. Refreshing the memory <note>recapitulation of the main points</note>.

**Naturally**, after one has proven that they are telling the truth and the opponent is lying, it follows to praise, to blame, and to refine the points made. One must aim for one of two things: to show that you are good to the audience (or good in general), and that the opponent is bad to them (or bad in general). We have already discussed the "topics" <note>general categories of argument</note> from which one should establish people as being virtuous or wicked.

**Following this**, once the facts have been proven, it is natural to proceed with amplification and minimization. For the facts must be agreed upon if one is to speak about their magnitude; just as the growth of physical bodies proceeds from something that already exists. We have previously laid out the topics for how one must amplify or minimize points.

**After these things**, since the nature and importance of the case are clear, one should lead the listener into the emotions. These are: **pity**, **indignation** <note>original: "deinosis" — the act of making a crime or situation seem especially horrific or severe</note>, **anger**, **hatred**, **envy**, **emulation** <note>original: "zelos" — a positive form of emulation or jealousy</note>, and **strife**. The topics for these have also been mentioned previously.

**Consequently**, what remains is to refresh the memory of what was said before. This should be done in the same way as is often suggested for the introductions...

<summary>This page concludes the discussion on the proper use of humor in rhetoric and outlines the four essential components of a speech's epilogue: building goodwill, amplification, emotional appeal, and recapitulation.</summary>

<keywords>Gorgias, irony, buffoonery, epilogue, amplification, minimization, emotions, pity, anger, memory, rhetoric</keywords>

<meta>This page continues Aristotle's discussion on the components of the epilogue, specifically focusing on the fourth part: the recapitulation or "refreshing of the memory." It follows the previous page's introduction to the four goals of a concluding statement.</meta>

### RHETORIC. BOOK III. CHAPTER XIX.

...to others) - they speak incorrectly; for they tell us to repeat things often so that they may be easily learned <note>original: "eumathe" — easy to learn or understand</note>. In the former case <note>the introduction</note>, one must state the facts so that the subject of judgment is not overlooked; but here <note>in the conclusion</note>, one should state in summary the proofs that have been demonstrated.

The starting point is to show that one has performed what was promised; therefore, one must state both what was said and why. This is done by a comparison with the opponent. One can compare either everything that both sides said on the same subject, or not directly point-for-point. 

"But he said these things about this matter, while I said those things, and for these reasons."

Or one can use **irony** <term>irony</term> <note>original: "eironeias" — here referring to a rhetorical technique of mocking or challenging an opponent's claims</note>, for example: "For he said these things, but I said those." And: "What would he have done if he had proven these points, and not those?" 

Or one can use questioning: "What has not been proven?" or "What has this man proven?" 

Either do it this way, or by comparison, or following the natural order <note>original: "kata physin"</note> in which the points were originally spoken; and then, if you wish, treat the opponent's arguments separately. 

For the very end of the speech, a style without connecting words <term>asyndeton</term> <note>original: "asyndetos" — a rhetorical device where conjunctions like 'and' or 'but' are omitted to create a sense of urgency or finality</note> is appropriate, so that it is clearly an epilogue and not just more speech-making: 

->**"I have spoken; you have heard; you have the facts; now judge."**<- 
<note>original: "eirēka, akēkoate, echete, krinate"</note>

<summary>Aristotle concludes his treatise on Rhetoric by explaining how to summarize arguments in the epilogue and recommends ending with a rapid-fire, disconnected style to emphasize finality.</summary>
<keywords>Aristotle, Rhetoric, Epilogue, Summary, Comparison, Irony, Asyndeton, Recapitulation</keywords>
